## Chapter 0 — 14 Sentences to Take to the Table

*Memorize these. When there's no time to think at the table, the decision comes out of these fourteen sentences. The first 10 are the core; 11–14 were written in from v5's tournament execution.*

1. **One pair in a bloated pot is a bluff-catcher — AA included.**
   This is your root error. Three bustouts came from here.

2. **If a hand weaker than yours won't pay on the river, the jam is not for value.**
   Overpair on a bad river: check-call in a small pot, check-fold in a big pot.

3. **Chops don't jam.**
   If the villain went all-in, he has removed the hand playing for a split from his range.

4. **A bluff passes three criteria: blocker, connectivity, board ownership.**
   Passing all three means 'bluffable'; the move is chosen by the owner of the board.

5. **Deceptive medium hands are small-pot winners and big-pot losers.**
   JTs, KQ, KJ, 97s. Their role is open/flat/BB-defend; never big-pot initiator.

6. **Equity is won on paper, money is won at the table.**
   Playability comes before equity. If KQo's 45% can't be realized, it's a fold.

7. **Before every hand, stack mode first, then range.**
   Playing a 130bb range at 45bb is the most expensive habit.

8. **My OOP 3-bet range is distinctly tighter than my IP range.**
   Because OOP, one pair in a bloated pot is a bluff-catcher. Connects to Chapter 1.

9. **Live, profit comes from wide value 3-bets, not bluff 3-bets.**
   The field doesn't fold. Drop the balance, profit from the imbalance.


10. **At 25–30bb value comes from fold equity, not from completing cards.**
    The hand you're looking for isn't one that can develop, but one that's already good: an ace, broadway, a pair. Chapter 5.

11. **The size you bet on the turn is the price of the decision you'll make on the river.**
    The second barrel buys up the luxury of a river check-fold. Chapter 11.

12. **In ICM, a marginal call = the tournament-life version of the root error.**
    Risk premium raises the bluff-catcher's pay-off threshold. Chapter 12.

13. **Multiway, one pair is one class below what it is HU.**
    Every additional player multiplies the chance that someone has the nuts. Chapter 13.

14. **A decision made on tilt comes from the wound, not the range — label the wound first.**
    The other rules only engage once the tilt is noticed. Chapter 16.

## Chapter 1 — Bluff Selection

### 1.1 The three criteria

If a hand is going to be bluff fuel, it must pass these three. If one is missing it's not a bluff, just lost chips.

| Criterion | What you're looking for | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Blocker** | A card that takes the villain's strongest hands out of his range | Holding A♠: you're blocking the nut flush |
| **Connectivity** | Structure that touches the board and can develop | An OESD or FD — not just 'two high cards' |
| **Board ownership** | Whose range the board hits | T98, 765 are yours; dry A-K-7 is the villain's |

### 1.2 The J2s lesson — the suited trap

Being suited doesn't make a hand bluff fuel. J2s has no blocker value (it takes away nobody's nuts), its connectivity is weak (a 9-card gap between the two cards), and even when it completes it carries bottom-flush risk. The word suited is fooling you: what you're looking for isn't suited, it's connected with blockers.

The same trap runs in reverse with offsuit broadways: KJo, QJo — blocker value but no connectivity. These aren't bluff fuel either.

### 1.3 Move selection: who owns the board

Even a hand that passes the three criteria can be played with the wrong move. The critical distinction:

> **Check-raise bluffs are made on boards that hit YOUR range** (T98, 765, J-middle connected).
> **On boards that hit the villain's range** (A-K high dry), draws call quietly.

Why: A♦ K♠ 7♣ smashes CO's open range (AK, AQ, AJ, KQ, A7s, 77, AA, KK). How many A-K combinations are in your BB defense range? Few. Your check-raise has low credibility → good hands call, only air folds. To win air's pot you'd be risking your gutshot in a bloated pot. Calling solves everything: a cheap turn, stacking him with a hidden straight if a T comes, the option to turn aggressive if a spade comes, a cheap exit if it bricks.

### 1.4 Who not to bluff

- **Rec / station type:** doesn't fold. Think value bet, not bluff.
- **Short stack:** the call price is small relative to his stack, he's in auto-call territory.
- **A player committed to the pot:** psychologically, fold is closed off.
- **A big stack playing for survival on the bubble:** will pay you off for free.

---

### 1.5 Exception: If the Pot Arrived via Check-Down, Pick the Bluff from the BOTTOM


B1.1's blocker-first criterion is for nodes where money goes in. But if the pot reaches a late street via a check-down where both sides have shown weakness (flop check-check, turn check → river), the criterion reverses on the IP side:

- Villain's range is capped and bluff-catcher-heavy → you don't need the best blocker to make him fold.
- Pick the bluff from the very BOTTOM of your range (zero showdown value, blockerless trash); take marginal hands that carry a blocker to showdown by checking (they sometimes win).

> **At a node where money goes in, B1.1's trio (blocker / connectivity / board ownership) selects the bluff fuel; at a check-down RIVER node, showdown-worthlessness selects the bluff.**

The capped-range logic works on both sides: because IP checked back both the flop and the turn, his range is also capped → OOP, too, selects the bluff at this node by showdown-worthlessness, not by blocker. On both sides, the blocker is only a tiebreaker (a preference between two trash hands of equal showdown value). This is the hand-selection layer for the B19.3-19.4 (probe/stab) lines.

### 1.6 Perceived Bluff ≠ Real Bluff — Pick the Bluff from OUTSIDE the Opponent's "I'm Blocking It" List


1.1 selects the blocker against villain's strongest hands. At a river jam, a second list comes into play: the hands villain THINKS are your bluff class. On a K-Q-T-3-x line with three streets of betting plus a river jam: the weak Q9/T7-type hands OOP is supposed to call in balance instead think "this guy bluffs with AT/AQ," count the T/Q as a blocker, and lean toward folding (I'm blocking his bluff → his range is value-heavy → fold); meanwhile the real bluff, the A5s/A4s type, gets paid by those folds precisely because no catcher blocks it.

Keep two lists: (a) the class villain thinks is your bluff; (b) what you're actually bluffing with. Build (b) from OUTSIDE of (a) — his "I'm blocking it" folds pay your bluff, his "I can't block it" calls pay your value; turn AT/AQ into a bluff less often. This mirrors 32.6's card-matching rule: there, you CARRY the card of villain's premium bluff-catcher; here, you DON'T carry the card he thinks is his blocker. Mostly against the pool; borderline against a reg who counts on the heuristic *(fold rate: calibrate)*.

> **Your real bluff range sits outside villain's bluff-catch heuristic.**

## Chapter 2 — Deceptive Medium Hands

> **JTs, KQ, KJ, 97s: small-pot winners, big-pot losers.**
> Their roles are open / flat / BB-defend. Their role is NEVER big-pot initiator (3-bet, stack-off fuel).

Why: when JTs makes top pair it has a kicker problem. When KQ makes top pair it's in the shadow of AK/AQ. Even when 97s makes two pair, it's exposed from above. These hands leak money out of the villain's marginal hands while the pot is small (single-raised); when the pot grows (3-bet+), the range facing them narrows and strengthens — they get dominated.

**One sentence:** *Same hand — when the pot size changes, the winning side changes.*

### 2.1 The KQo case — the difference between equity and playability

**42bb, Day 2. HJ (a reg, ~22%) opened, you're in the CO with KQo. Correct decision: fold.**

KQo's raw equity against HJ's 22% range is ~45% — not bad. But you can't realize that equity:

- The AK, AQ, KK, QQ, AA in HJ's range turn your best flops into a grave: you flop a K → you pay AK three streets. You flop a Q → you pay AQ.
- So your best scenarios are your most expensive traps.
- At 42bb, if you 3-bet and eat a 4-bet you can't continue; calling and playing OOP has no initiative either.

**The lesson: equity is won on paper, money is won at the table.**

## Chapter 3 — Stack Modes and Opening Ranges

*The first question to ask before any hand: which mode am I in? The range is chosen after that.*

| Mode | Character | What drops / what rises |
|---|---|---|
| **80bb+** | Standard charts. LJ ~18% → BTN ~45% | Suited connector and small pair value peaks |
| **40–60bb** | First tightening. Size stays fixed, range narrows | The offsuit bottom band drops (KTo, QJo, J9o) |
| **25–40bb** | Serious mode shift | Suited connectors lose value, A-x gains value. 'Clear decision vs a jam' filter |
| **15–25bb** | The open-jam boundary | Open 2x from some positions, direct jam with others |
| **<15bb** | Jam / fold | No move outside the range exists |

### 3.1 Critical threshold: medium pairs below 30bb

Below 30bb, medium pairs like 77–TT are not hands to play post-flop — they are jam-or-fold hands. If the set doesn't come you don't have the stack to pay three streets; and if it does, with a short stack you can't get maximum anyway.

### 3.2 The ICM layer

> **30bb on the bubble ≠ 30bb on Day 1.**
> As the money approaches, the same stack doesn't play the same range. The short stacks on your left are protecting you, the big stacks are squeezing you.

- If there are short stacks to your left: widen your opening range, they're playing to survive.
- If there are big stacks to your left: tighten, they can 3-bet you with impunity.
- On the bubble the middle stack is the most fragile position — you can neither jam like a short stack nor apply pressure like a big one. Patience in this mode.

---

## Chapter 4 — 3-bet and Call Ranges by Position

*★ Added in v3. Written assuming 100–150bb and an 8-handed table.*

### 4.0 The logic of this chapter

Your root error is misclassifying one pair in a bloated pot. That error surfaces on the river but is born preflop: what we call a bloated pot is, 90% of the time, a 3-bet pot. So building your 3-bet range correctly is the most efficient way to lower the FREQUENCY of the root error — it delivers results faster than improving your hand reading.

**Every range is tested with two questions:**

1. If I 3-bet and see a flop, will I be comfortable when I make one pair? If not, I'm not 3-betting — I flat or I fold.
2. Will I be OOP? In an OOP 3-bet pot, one pair is by definition a bluff-catcher. That's why OOP ranges are distinctly tighter than IP ranges.

### 4.1 The live tournament adjustment

You will not apply solver ranges as-is. There are three systematic deviations in the EPT field:

| Live player behavior | Your adjustment |
|---|---|
| Folds very little to 3-bets | Lower your bluff 3-bet frequency, widen your VALUE 3-bets |
| Almost never 4-bet bluffs | When you see a 4-bet, take QQ and below seriously; AK is not an automatic 5-bet |
| Opens are wide, coldcalls plentiful | The squeeze is your most profitable move; drop bluff 3-bets in multiway pots |

> **Live, the money comes from wider value 3-bets, not bluff 3-bets.**
> Theory forces you toward balance; the field gives you permission to profit from imbalance.

### 4.2 Sizing — memorize, don't think

| Situation | Size |
|---|---|
| **IP 3-bet** | 3× the open (3.5× is fine live) |
| **OOP 3-bet (blinds included)** | 4× the open |
| **For each coldcaller** | add +1 open size |
| **Squeeze (open + 1 caller)** | 4.5–5× |
| **4-bet IP** | 2.2× the 3-bet |
| **4-bet OOP** | 2.5× the 3-bet |

### 4.3 3-bet ranges — position matchups

*The hands in the bluff row were chosen for flop playability, not blocker theory.*

**vs UTG / UTG+1 open — discipline zone**

| Position | VALUE | BLUFF |
|---|---|---|
| LJ / HJ | QQ+, AKs, AKo | none |
| CO | QQ+, AKs, AKo (JJ mixed) | A5s |
| BTN | JJ+, AKs, AKo, AQs (mixed) | A5s, A4s |
| SB | QQ+, AKs, AKo | none |
| BB | QQ+, AKs, AKo | A5s (rare) |

**Flat (IP only, 100bb+):** TT–77, AQs, AJs, ATs, KQs, KJs, QJs, JTs, T9s, 98s. If 150bb+, add 66–22 and 87s, 76s.

**What you will not do:** coldcall a UTG open with AJo, KQo, KJo. These hands are either 3-bet or fold — played in between, they are exactly the hands that inflict the root error.

**vs LJ / HJ open**

| Position | VALUE | BLUFF |
|---|---|---|
| CO | JJ+, AQs+, AKo | A5s, A4s |
| BTN | TT+, AQs+, AKo, AQo (mixed) | A5s–A3s, KJs, QJs |
| SB | JJ+, AQs+, AKo | A5s, A4s |
| BB | TT+, AQs+, AKo, AQo | A5s–A3s, KJs, T9s (rare) |

**Flat IP (CO/BTN):** 99–22, AJs, ATs, KQs, KJs, KTs, QJs, QTs, JTs, T9s, 98s, 87s, 76s

**vs CO open**

| Position | VALUE | BLUFF |
|---|---|---|
| BTN | TT+, AJs+, AQo+, KQs | A5s–A2s, KTs, QTs, J9s, T8s |
| SB | JJ+, AJs+, AQo+, KQs | A5s–A3s, KJs |
| BB | TT+, ATs+, AQo+, KQs, KJs | A5s–A2s, K9s, QTs, J9s |

**BTN flat:** 99–22, AJo, KJo, KTs, QJs, QTs, JTs and all 65s+ suited connectors

**vs BTN open — steal defense**

| Position | VALUE | BLUFF |
|---|---|---|
| SB | 88+, ATs+, AJo+, KJs+, QJs | A5s–A2s, K9s, Q9s, J9s, T8s, 87s |
| BB | 99+, ATs+, AQo+, KQs, KJs | A5s–A3s, K9s–K7s, QTs, J9s, T8s, 76s |

**SB flat: almost none.** Flatting from the SB at 100bb is a losing position — 3-bet or fold. Exception: at 200bb+ with a weak, paying BTN, flatting 66–22 and suited connectors is acceptable.

**BB flat: very wide.** You're closing the action, the price is good. All suited hands, all pairs, most broadways, all suited connectors.

**vs SB open (BB only) — the field's most profitable 3-bet spot**

- **VALUE:** 88+, A9s+, ATo+, KTs+, QTs+, JTs
- **BLUFF:** K7s+, Q8s+, J8s+, T8s, 97s, 86s, 65s
- **Flat:** every remaining playable hand. The SB range is wide, you're IP — few reasons to fold.

### 4.4 The three conditions for a coldcall

A coldcall is a harder decision than a 3-bet because you're giving up the initiative. If all three aren't met at the same time, don't flat:

1. **Position:** you're IP or you're closing the action (BB).
2. **Depth:** for set-mining, effective stack at least 15× the call. At 100bb a 3bb call → 45bb+ behind: fine. The same call at 40bb: not.
3. **A paying villain:** someone who will pay you off when you make your set. Set-mining against a tight reg is unprofitable.

**Multiway warning:** if there's an aggressive player behind you who can 3-bet, flatting a tight hand leaves you open to a squeeze. In that case either upgrade to a 3-bet or fold.

### 4.5 Your response to a 3-bet

| Response | Hands |
|---|---|
| **4-bet value** | KK+, AKs (against a late-position 3-bettor) |
| **4-bet mixed** | QQ, AKo — yes against a late-position 3-bet, no against UTG |
| **4-bet bluff** | A5s, A4s — VERY rare live. If nobody folds, a bluff 4-bet is burning money |
| **Flat (IP, 150bb+)** | JJ, TT, AQs, KQs; suited connectors if the price is below 3× |
| **Fold** | OOP all offsuit broadways: AJo, KQo, ATo — trash against a 3-bet |

> **A 4-bet pot is a bloated pot.**
> Taking flop+turn value there with AA and jamming a bad river is an exact replay of Case 3 (the 245 board). The rule doesn't change: if a hand weaker than yours won't pay on the river, the jam is not for value.

### 4.6 The squeeze — the single most profitable move live

The coldcaller's range is tight but weak: he can't 4-bet, he folds most of his hands.

- **VALUE:** JJ+, AQs+, AKo
- **BLUFF:** A5s–A4s, KQs, AJs (blocker + playability)
- **SIZE:** 4.5× IP, 5×+ from the blinds

If the coldcaller is tight, widen the bluff side. If the coldcaller is a fish (doesn't fold), cut the bluffs entirely — value squeeze only.

### 4.7 The stack-mode top layer

| Mode | 3-bet character | Flat | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **200bb+** | Value-heavy, few bluffs | Widest | Implied odds peak. Offsuit broadway value minimal |
| **100–150bb** | Tables apply as-is | Wide | Standard mode |
| **60–100bb** | More polarized, bluffs increase | Narrows | Set-mining weakens |
| **40–60bb** | Linear / merged | Almost none | 3-bet or fold |
| **25–40bb** | 3-bet = commit | None | A hand you 3-bet must be able to continue against a 4-bet |
| **<25bb** | Jam / fold | None | No 3-bet-fold structure exists |

**The most common mistake:** playing a 130bb range at 45bb — flatting small pairs, 3-bet bluffing suited connectors. Both lose money at 45bb.

### 4.8 BB Defense — The Ante Price

In an ante structure, BB defense is the most generous spot in your math: there's already a lot in the pot and your call adds little. That's why the BB defend range is very wide — but "getting in cheap" does not replace postflop discipline.

**Price math (example: 2.25× open, with antes):** 2,000 BB, 4,500 open → the pot already holds 4,500 (open) + 2,000 (your BB) + 1,000 (SB) + ~2,000 (antes) ≈ 9,500. You need to add 2,500. → 2,500 / 12,000 ≈ **~21% equity is enough.** Almost any two cards clear this raw threshold.

| Decision (vs a 2.25× open) | Hands |
|---|---|
| **Call (defend)** | All suited (incl. T3s), connected/1-gap offsuit (86o, 97o, J8o), all Ax, most Kx, 22+ — roughly 55–60% of hands |
| **Fold** | J3o, Q4o, K2o–K5o, 92o — both disconnected and dominated junk. Price rescues equity, not playability |
| **3-bet instead of call** | The bluff side from 4.3 (A5s–A2s, K8s–K6s type) + value. Keep this aggression vs late opens; ante pots are worth stealing |

**Two critical nuances:**

1. **Getting in cheap ≠ continuing cheap.** Calling 2,500 and then flipping to "I'm already in the pot" mode after flopping second pair is the BB version of your root mistake. The entry price does not affect postflop decisions — every street is a fresh decision.
2. **Tighten multiway.** With an open + one caller the price is still good, but dominated hands (A7o, K9o) lose value; keep the suited/connected side, fold the weak offsuit Ax.

Once the stack drops below 40bb (Mode C/D) this wide defend narrows — the stack can't afford to see cheap flops and fold, so the "call-to-jam" transition begins. This table applies at the 56bb restart.

---

### 4.0-EK The 'one pair = bluff-catcher' boundary in a 3-bet pot


The sentence "in an OOP 3-bet pot one pair is by definition a bluff-catcher" is a preflop range-construction rule (it keeps the OOP 3-bet range tight). Postflop the bluff-catcher boundary follows the ROLE, not the position: it holds for the capped/calling side at SPR ≳2 (at SPR ≲1.5 even his top pair mostly drops into the call-off class — it doesn't start the pot but it does call a jam; see 11.0-EK-2) — when the 3-bettor is OOP (blind vs. late position, the most common case), the caller is IP and the boundary applies to him. The overpair/TPTK of the 3-bettor who bloated the pot with range + nut advantage, however, is mostly in the jam class at shallow SPR (30–45bb, SPR ≲2; mid-connected boards where you hold the nut advantage) *(calibrate)*; an underpair does not enter this class. At SPR ~3–4 and above (≳4–5 at 100bb) the flop-jam class mostly disappears; on dry/low boards (8-6-2 / K-7-2 type) an overpair is still mostly a stack-off hand *(calibrate)*, on connected two-broadway boards the nut advantage erodes → overpair/TPTK becomes a bet-and-reassess / pot-control class *(stack-off threshold: calibrate)*. The root error is unchanged and belongs to the one who bloated the pot: the license depends on SPR and board texture; deep, on a board where the nut advantage has eroded, the 3-bettor who stacks off with one pair regardless of the board is committing the root error (see B32.1, B32.3).

### 4.2-EK The 100bb-with-ante blind node: SB 3-bet mid-sized + high equity, BB big — and where SB flat actually lives


4.2's "OOP 4×" row gives both blinds a single size; in a 100bb ante'd MTT the two split apart *(multipliers: calibrate)*:

- **SB's 3-bet is mid-sized.** The BB sits behind, and the BB's reply to an SB 3-bet is practically 4-bet-or-fold — an SB facing two ranges doesn't go to a big size; a mid size already folds out the BB's medium pairs/weak suited broadways cheaply. Material: the top of the range + high-equity suited connectors like T9s + a medium-pair mix *(full list: calibrate)*. The bottom of the range — low-kicker suited like J7s — is NOT a 3-bet, low equity doesn't justify forcing money; offsuit broadway like ATo/KJo goes to a 3-bet-fold mix rather than a flat *(calibrate)*.
- **BB's 3-bet is big.** Against a single range (BTN) it uses the full AK/AQ/KK–TT size; EV is close across the 5–6× band — if the pool is relatively size-inelastic and over-folds, it's mostly correct to buy the bluff cheap with a small size and take the value with a big one; this is a size-tell, exploitable against a reg who reads prices *(calibrate)*.
- **SB flat.** 4.3's "SB flat is almost nonexistent" line stands as discipline; in a 100bb ante'd game a theoretical SB flat exists, but flat = the BOTTOM hands above, 3-bet = high equity. The reflex "the bottom of my calling range → 3-bet it for balance" is a leak in the wrong direction *(borderline — run it in your own sim)*. At 40bb an SB flat range exists; its postflop execution is 26.5-EK-3.

### 4.6-EK The two faces of the squeeze: bluff material unblocks the opponent's FOLD block; the trap hand as the third player depends on the node


**Material.** 4.6's A5s/KQs/AJs list is correct when the cold-caller is IP and tight. In a BB squeeze over an SB call (100bb), the target is the SB's capped range — offsuit broadway + suited medium — and it folds often; the size, though, is still BIGGER than a single-opponent 3-bet: there's dead money in the pot, two opponents to fold out, and size is added per caller — a small squeeze hands the SB exactly the cheap call it's looking for. The bluff comes from broadway-less suited hands like 67s/97s/86s/75s plus a bit of offsuit Ax that UNBLOCK the combos with which the SB most often FOLDS to the squeeze (the ATo/KTo/QTo/JTo it flatted the open with). T8s/J9s block that block themselves (all four contain a T; J9s cuts JTo), and so do KQs/AJs. The test: "whose block am I unblocking?" The pool finds this squeeze rarely and plays tighter than the SB should *(calibrate)*.

**Third player.** "There's a raise plus a call, over-call with JTs" is a trap: the cold-caller's KQs–QTs and the opener's JJ+/AJ dominate you. Ask first: does the cold-caller's range have a TOP? In balance, the CO flats part of its AK/QQ/JJ/TT against an LJ open — the trap hand isn't the strongest one but the premium that aggression rewards least (JJ improves relative to the range once 3-bet; AA is what KQ/AQ continues into). The pool doesn't build this trap from the CO: it 3-bets its premiums at 100%, so its calling range settles into 22–99 + suited broadway → NO top → against a medium suited connector it's a JAM, not an over-call (14.1-EK-3). The reverse node: at 100bb, BTN flats AA and 4-bets JJ/QQ against a BB squeeze — denying the SB's cheap third-player call *(frequencies: calibrate)*.

### 4.9 Marginal suited/offsuit groups (Kx/Qx/Jx): open or fold, flat only from the BB


The low suited group — K3s–K6s, Q3s–Q6s, J3s–J6s — carries a single practical rule in tournament NLH: **open it or fold it; flat only from the BB.** Flatting these hands in position against a raise is their worst possible use. *(Rough baseline: ante'd 8-max, 30–60bb, no heavy ICM — ICM and big sizing tighten the boundary further; these are not solver law, they are a safe working boundary.)*

| Spot | Suited (low Kx/Qx/Jx) | Offsuit (same) |
|---|---|---|
| BTN first-in | Mix/open: K2s+, Q4s+, J5s+ (Q2s/Q3s, J3s/J4s borderline) | K6o opens, K5o borderline; K3o-K4o and Qxo/Jxo mostly fold |
| CO first-in | K5s/K6s, Q6s (Q7s/Q8s a safer cutoff), J7s+ open; the rest fold | All fold |
| HJ and earlier | K6s borderline, the rest fold | Fold |
| SB first-in | All limp/raise (no fold); the bottom Qxs/Jxs fold under ICM | K3o-K6o limp/raise; Q5o/Q6o, J5o/J6o mix; the bottom fold/mix |
| vs BTN/CO raise | **No flat:** part of K5s/K6s is a 3-bet bluff (K blocker), the rest fold | Almost pure fold — not as good a 3-bet bluff as the suited version |
| BB vs BTN/CO 2x | Most of it defends (call) | K3o-K6o mostly call (vs BTN); Qxo/Jxo narrows (opener/sizing/ICM) |
| BB vs early/UTG-HJ | As sizing grows, Q3s/Q4s, J3s/J4s can fold | All default fold |

**Why offsuit is far harsher:** the suited version produces flushes and strong draws, its equity realizes well, and the K-blocker plus playability make it a reasonable 3-bet bluff. Offsuit carries none of that; the hand it most often produces is a weak top pair open to domination. **Short stack:** below 20bb it's a jam candidate from the BTN/SB (suited survives deeper) — but at 15bb from the BTN don't make low Kxs an AUTOMATIC jam (mostly minraise/fold plus some jam); Q3s/J3s isn't an automatic jam even at 10bb.

**Postflop — where the root error lives:** these hands are flush-draw and bluff hands, not top-pair hands. With K6s on a K-high flop, one pair does NOT go into a 100bb pot — it's not an automatic stack-off, but it also doesn't always turn into a bluff (small pot, pot-control, bluff-catch). The non-suited version isn't even a natural bluff candidate.

### 4.10 The Ax split: suited opens early, offsuit AJo+/ATo+


The basic split: **in early position, low suited Ax can be opened; low offsuit Ax is mostly a fold.** A2s in early position is usually worth more than A8o — it carries nut-flush, wheel, and 3-bet/4-bet bluff potential; offsuit carries none of that.

| Position (first-in) | Suited Ax | Offsuit Ax |
|---|---|---|
| UTG | A2s-A5s open/mix, A6s-A8s mix, A9s+ open | AJo+ open, ATo mix; A9o and below fold |
| UTG+1/LJ | A2s+ opens | ATo+ opens, A9o borderline |
| HJ | A2s+ opens | A9o+ opens, A8o mix |
| CO | A2s+ opens | A8o+ opens (A7o mix, below that depends on opponent) |
| BTN | All suited Ax | Almost all Ax (A2o-A5o mixed in some solves) |
| SB | All limp/raise | Nearly all of it; the weakest fold under ICM |

**Against a raise:** low suited Ax (especially A2s-A5s) are the best 3-bet bluff candidates — ace blocker + nut-flush + wheel; from the BTN sometimes a flat too. Low OFFSUIT Ax against a raise is mostly a fold outside the BB — it isn't flatted. AQo is a 3-bet/call mix, AK a value 3-bet. **Short stack:** at 10-15bb the raw equity and blocker value of a strong offsuit Ax rises, and the solver jams part of it — but "jam every Ax from UTG" is wrong.

**Postflop:** on an A-high flop, low-kicker Ax is mostly a small-bet/check and pot-control hand; there's no automatic three streets or 100bb stack-off with a weak Ax. **A5o on Day 1A, betting 75% into the flop and 75% into the turn on an AT2-J board** was exactly the example to avoid (B7 Case 9): top pair was there, but the kicker was weak, and two big bets needlessly bloated the pot.

**Short rule:** CO: A2s+ / A8o+. BTN/SB: all Ax. Against a raise, low suited Ax is a call/3-bet mix, low offsuit Ax folds outside the BB. With top pair and a weak kicker, grow the pot under control.

### 4.11 Suited broadways (KQs/KJs/QJs) and a PLO-6 KQJ note


**Opening:** KQs, KJs, and QJs all three open from EVERY position (8-max UTG included; QJs is borderline from UTG but the solver still opens it). Position doesn't decide whether to open — it decides the response to a 3-bet once you have.

**Against a raise — position is everything:**
- **From BTN/CO against an early open:** KQs/KJs are a 3-bet–flat mix, QJs mostly flats. At 100bb, weight KQs toward 3-betting (even though it's dominated by AK/AQ, fold equity plus position make up for it). KJs/QJs are the best hands to flat in position: pot control, implied odds, board coverage.
- **From the blinds:** all three defend from the BB (KQs 3-bet/call, KJs/QJs call). From the SB there's NO flat — KQs 3-bets, KJs is borderline, QJs folds to an early open / 3-bets a BTN open.
- **OOP against an early open (MP/HJ, UTG opened):** KQs is 3-bet-or-fold (flat is weakest), KJs/QJs generally fold. This is the smaller-scale version of the WSOP AQs bust (B7 Case 8): an OOP flat is a "let me see a flop" hand, and that flop is rarely easy.

**Response to a 3-bet (you opened):** in position, call with all three (100bb). OOP, KQs calls, KJs/QJs call/fold by size; there's no 4-bet with either — the 4-bet-bluff role belongs to A-blocker hands (A5s/A4s). **Tournament adjustment (<40bb):** the flat disappears, KQs reverts to 3-bet/jam, KJs/QJs call in position, fold OOP.

**PLO-6 note:** KQJ simply being suited by itself means nothing — what the remaining three cards do decides it. KQJ + two double-suited + a connected card is a CO/BTN open, a call to a 3-bet; **KQJ + danglers is a fold even from the CO** — it's exactly these "half-pretty" hands that made −$13.5K from the CO (B15.5 L1/L2).

## Chapter 5 — The 25–30bb Band: Battle Plan

*★ Added in v4. The tournament's most frequently visited and most chip-expensive band. Chapter 4's tables were for 100–150bb; this chapter tells you what to play once those tables go invalid.*

### 5.0 The character of the band

> **In this band value comes from FOLD EQUITY, not from completing cards.**
> The hand you're looking for isn't one that "can develop" but one that's ALREADY GOOD: an ace, broadway, a pair. The engine of speculative hands (implied odds) doesn't run at 28bb — when you make your set or straight there's no stack left to get paid with, and in the 85% of cases where you don't complete, you're helpless.

Decision order in two seconds: MODE → POSITION → HAND. When a suited connector arrives at 28bb, you don't even reach the third step.

### 5.1 Opening ranges (nobody in ahead of you)

*Size 2–2.2×. At this depth there's no point opening bigger.*

| Position | Opening range |
|---|---|
| **UTG / UTG+1** | 77+, ATs+, AJo+, KQs |
| **LJ / HJ** | 55+, A8s+, ATo+, KTs+, QJs |
| **CO** | 33+, A5s+, A9o+, K9s+, QTs+, JTs |
| **BTN** | 22+, all A-x, K7s+, K9o+, Q9s+, J9s+, T9s |
| **SB** | 22+, A2s+, A7o+, K9s+, KTo+, QTs+ |

### 5.2 3-bet = JAM

In this band a 3-bet means commit. There is no such structure as "3-bet then fold" — you go directly all-in.

| Against whom | Jam range |
|---|---|
| **Early position open** | TT+, AQs+, AKo |
| **CO / BTN open** | 88+, ATs+, AQo+, KQs |
| **Chip leader / folds to jams** | 77+, A9s+, AJo+, KQs (widened) |

**NO FLATTING in this band.** Not from the SB, not from the BB, not IP. Chapter 4's wide flat tables belong to the 100bb+ world.

### 5.3 Calling a jam

If the villain went all-in: 99+, AJs+, AQo+. Below that is not a call at 28bb — either you make the jam yourself or you fold.

### 5.4 The fold list

**Unconditional folds in this band:**

- All suited connectors: T9s, 98s, 87s, 76s, 65s, 54s
- All suited gappers
- Weak offsuit broadways: KJo, QJo, JTo
- FLATTING small pairs (jamming is a separate matter)

### 5.5 Field case — three hands, one pattern (GGMasters $150, July 2026)

In the 28bb band the table asked the same question with three different hands. In all three the correct answer was fold, and the decisive factor was the mode, not the hand.

| Hand | Position | Situation | Decision |
|---|---|---|---|
| **T9s** | SB | Offered a call for 40K, 28bb behind | FOLD — flatting from the SB is a losing position |
| **54s** | BB | 30K into a 73K pot, 28bb behind | FOLD — the implied-odds engine isn't running |
| **87s** | BB | 30K into a 73K pot, 28bb behind | FOLD — better hand, same decision |

**A correct-decision example from the same session:** a ~23bb jam from the BTN with TT. The chip leader (1.08M) considered a call and folded; the pot was taken. Chapter 3's rule was applied — below 30bb, medium pairs are not post-flop hands, they are jam-or-fold hands.

> **Rule: The question waiting for you in this band is not "is this hand good enough" but "is this hand playable at this stack".**
> Folding speculative hands preserves the ammunition you'll use to jam with a real hand.

### 5.6 Villain-reading note

If you've seen the chip leader fold to a jam once, widen your jam range against that player's opens. A profile that opens wide and folds to jams is the most profitable target at the table in this band. Priority order: (1) jam when that player opens, (2) open from BTN/CO and collect the blinds.

---

### 5.1-EK The SB row is conditional in BvB: weak Ax/Kx and small pairs aren't a fold, they're limp material


5.1's SB row (22+, A2s+, A7o+, K9s+, KTo+, QTs+) reads as raise-or-fold; at the BvB node, the weakest offsuit Ax (wheel kicker) and lower-medium offsuit Kx that fall below the table's floor aren't a fold — they're **limp** material *(calibrate)*. The 25–30bb of this band is the upper end of B33.1's shallow layer: at its lower end (~25bb) a blocker limp-jam is in play; at its upper end limp-jam becomes far too steep an overbet of the pot, and the material shifts to limp-call — limp-jam belongs roughly to ≤25bb *(calibrate)*. A limp-3-bet is a commit in this band too: a size that won't be folded (what's left behind ≈ the pot) *(calibrate)* — there's no foldable small limp-3-bet, 5.2's "3-bet = JAM" doctrine applies at the limp node as well. Limp-call, though, is a BvB-limp-specific exception to 5.2's "no flat" rule (the last decision that closes the action, plus a price against a small iso); the flat ban against an open still applies exactly as written. Small pairs show up as raise in the row, but at this depth in BvB they partly shift toward limp *(calibrate)*. Premiums mostly stay in the raise; a low-frequency trap limp to protect the limp range is borderline *(calibrate)*. Read the row as a "raise range," not "the SB's whole game"; for the limp's content see B33.1's shallow layer and B33.2.

### 5.1-EK-2 The ICM layer: the limp slice CLOSES against an aggressive covering BB; opening size vs. a short BB is 2.2–2.5x, not a min-raise


5.1-EK's limp material is for chipEV BvB. Two ICM corrections; the B3/B5 tables don't change.

- **The limp closes.** With an aggressive BB on your left who covers you, and with ICM on, the SB's limp isn't a range-widening tool — it's a door that opens you up to an iso plus a capped-range penalty from the big stack → raise-only, tight; weak Ax/Kx and small pairs go not to the limp but to the open-jam slice at short effective stacks (≈≤15bb), and to a fold deeper — the tree is at 12.2-EK-4. With a short BB, the limp is forbidden a second time: a short, capped stack jams wide into a limp, you fold — and you've handed a free flop on top of it. The limp survives only when the BB is weak and passive *(calibrate)*.
- **Sizing against a short BB.** If BB ≤5bb, a min-raise carries no fold equity — priced, he pays with every hand (the flip side of 30.6); 2.2–2.5x BB starts folding out a slice of the range, and it squeezes the SB too. Scale it by position (BTN biggest), the range a touch NARROWER — not wide range plus big size; 3x is the border *(calibrate)*. One note on B3's opening-size rows: the short-BB exception is 12.2-EK-5.

### 5.2-EK If the opponent didn't jam: 4-bet jam from the BTN into the SB's BIG non-all-in 3-bet


At ~30bb effective, the SB's correct 3-bet is mostly ALL-IN (5.2); a big non-all-in 3-bet should be rare in balance. The pool does the opposite: it 3-bets big with the hands it should be jamming. That range is polarized and mostly **capped** — the value side is AK/mid-high pairs, the bluff side is offsuit broadway/suited Kx; AA-KK has mostly either been jammed or gone in small *(borderline — some pools 3-bet AA big too, watch for it)*. As the size grows, dead money increases, but the SB is also committing — the stack-to-pot remaining shrinks, fold equity DROPS relative to a small 3-bet but doesn't hit zero; the profit comes from the sum of the bluff half folding, the dead money, and, when paid off, live equity against an AA/KK-less (capped) range — the capped claim is conditional, watch for it *(borderline — calibrate)*. If AA/KK/QQ are scarce in the range, your own K and Q are live overcards; suited Kx/Qx jams better than expected.

**Rule:** In the 30bb band, when you see a big non-all-in 3-bet from the SB, set your BTN 4-bet-jam range wide — suited Ax/Kx, pairs, broadways *(boundary: calibrate)*. If the SB's 3-bet is small-sized or a jam, return to 5.3. This is a pool exploit: the moment you start seeing AA/KK at full frequency in the SB's big non-all-in 3-bet, pull the widening back.

### 5.2-EK-2 A SHORT BB grants the SB a 3-bet-FOLD license — a slice that doesn't exist at equal stacks


5.2's "3-bet = JAM" is for equal stacks: the SB's non-all-in 3-bet gets jammed by both the BTN and the BB, and can't be folded. If the BB IS SHORT, the math changes: the SB pays the BB's jam with almost every hand on price (effective stack small); the only real threat left is the BTN's 4-bet jam — and because the BB acts before the BTN, by the 4-bet node it has mostly already folded. The result: low-EV hands that would be jammed at equal stacks (A7o/K9o/QJo type *(calibrate)*) move into a 3-bet-FOLD slice; the value 3-bet (QQ+/AK) earns more since the bluff slice balances it. The license also has a boundary in the BTN: a BTN that sniffs out the 3-bet-fold slice gets more fold equity from its 4-bet jam and WIDENS — the license holds only while the BTN isn't jamming enough; against one that adapts, cut the bluff slice back *(calibrate)*. Side effect: the SB's marginal flats (small suited connectors, medium offsuit broadway) drop out — a short BB jam punishes the flat; the higher-card flats remain. On the BTN side: there's no overcall against this 3-bet, only jam or fold — since there's a lot of bluff in the 3-bet, don't narrow your jam range on the assumption "3-bet = value." The same mechanism applies to a CO open: an SB 3-bet-plus-jam mix, with even less flat. It's a relative of 22.11's escape hatch — a raise that buys a fold option; a short BB lowers the price of that option *(boundary: calibrate)*.

### 5.2-EK-3 Two footnotes to "NO FLAT": an SB call slice under 3-handed ICM (borderline) — and 5.2-EK's pool condition


The rule stands; two footnotes.

1. **3-handed + ~30bb SB + an extremely wide-opening BTN (ICM):** the BTN covers you, the chip leader BB sits behind — and yet an ICM sim still gives the SB a CALL with suited broadway / small pairs / some suited Ax: with the opener this wide, the stack a jam risks is large relative to the dead money (12.1-EK), while a call sees a flop at a shallow SPR. *Borderline — calibrate.* This does NOT touch 22.8-EK: that's the rule for the BB, who closes the action, against the chip leader; here the seat is SB with a player still behind. In 8-max and against a normal opener, NO FLAT stands exactly as written.
2. **5.2-EK's widened 4-bet jam depends on the pool.** "A wide jam from the BTN, pairs included, into the SB's big non-all-in 3-bet" rests on the pool's 3-bet being capped. If the 3-bet range is balanced/polarized and premiums are at full frequency (HU, against a good reg), the medium pair's jam drops out: mostly 66–88 slow, 22–44 and 99–JJ jam, QQ+ slow *(calibrate)* — see B31.7-EK. Same hand, two conditions, two answers; the moment you start seeing AA/KK at showdown in a big 3-bet, close 5.2-EK.

### 5.3-EK Against a tight re-jam, the OFFSUIT value combo count sets the call price — ATs/KQs fold


5.3's line (99+/AJs+/AQo+) stands; the reasoning behind it is combo counting. The pool's ~30bb re-jam is tighter than theory (typically medium pairs + strong offsuit broadway + A-blocker suiteds ≈ 7% *(calibrate)*). The math "the range has KTs/QJs/A5s in it, ATs holds up" counts wrong: suited bluffs are 4 combos each, while the offsuit AJ/AQ/AK that dominate ATs are 12 combos each, plus the pairs. A hand that only offsuit hands dominate (ATs/KQs type) is a fold to a tight jam — even if the jammer widens its suited material; KQs's loss is twice 88's gain *(calibrate)*. The opener/flatter's line is one notch wider — around 88+/AJs/AQo *(calibrate)*; AJo folds, 88 is borderline.

The loop: the tighter the pool jams, the tighter its callers get; against a tightening caller, adding bluffs to the jam becomes profitable (14.1-EK-2). Against an opponent who non-all-in 3-bets AA/KK, the jam is capped → the call opens up (the mirror of 5.2-EK).

> **When calling a jam, count offsuit value combos, not suited bluffs.**

## Chapter 6 — Draws on the Turn: Bet or Free River

*Poker's most frequent turn question. The decision depends on three factors.*

### 6.1 Is there fold equity

- Semi-bluffing a station type = burning money. He won't fold, you're left with your naked equity → check, take the free card.
- Against a reg and when the board fits your range → bet. You get two ways to win: making him fold OR completing.

### 6.2 Quality of the draw — does it get paid when it completes

| Draw | Decision | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| **Nut flush draw** | Bet-weighted | You get action when it completes, your blockers are strong |
| **Low flush draw (97s)** | Check-weighted | Even when it completes, risk of paying off a higher flush |
| **Open-ender (OESD), straight board** | Both are legitimate | Hidden strength — gets paid a lot when it completes. Choose based on villain |
| **Gutshot** | Check | Almost never semi-bluff fuel |

### 6.3 IP / OOP distinction

- **IP:** checking really does mean a free card — if villain checks too, you saw the river for free.
- **OOP:** a check doesn't guarantee a free card. If villain bets, the 'free' plan collapses. OOP, either bet your draw or check it with a call plan — don't check thinking 'I'll see it for free'.

---

### 6.2-EK The Gutshot Line Is SUSPENDED at Two Polar Nodes — the Draw's Job Is Blocking, Not Completing


6.2's "gutshot → check" rule is for the general turn-draw decision: fold equity is uncertain, the draw alone isn't fuel. It's suspended at two nodes; at the first, the gutshot's job is to block villain's continuing class (TPGK/overpair); at the second, it's to fold out weak pairs/overcards/weak draws and carry fold equity — in neither case is it to complete the card:

- **Effective-nut overbet (11.1-EK):** a gutshot with an overcard blocks villain's top-pair-good-kicker and overpair combos (it does NOT block two pair — for that you need to hold a board rank), and folds out his pairs and weak draws — a natural bluff for the polar overbet range.
- **The fluid turn after a check-raise (26.6):** a semi-bluff jam candidate — it denies equity by folding out weak pairs/overcards/weak draws; it only folds out a draw that dominates you when the SPR is high enough *(borderline)*.

Condition: the node must genuinely be polar, and villain's layer heading toward a fold must be wide *(calibrate)*. Against a station, and at a medium-sized bet node, 6.2 stands exactly as written: gutshot checks.

## Chapter 7 — Case Autopsies

> **The root error of all four cases is the same:**
> Misclassifying one pair (including AA) in a bloated or multiway pot.

### Case 1 — A4s river call ($50K High Roller, WSOP 2026)

**What happened:** You saw the chop mechanic correctly, but skipped a filter and made the hero call.

**The skipped filter:** Chops don't jam. If villain went all-in, he has removed the hand playing for a split from his range.

**Rule:** Seeing a mechanic correctly isn't enough — also ask whether villain's move is consistent with that mechanic.

### Case 2 — KTo top pair check-raise all-in ($10K 6-Handed)

**What happened:** Check-raise all-in with top pair on a wet board; villain called with the QQ he had flatted.

**Correct play:** Check-call, then reassess. Top pair on a wet board is not a big-pot starter.

**Rule:** One pair is not stack-off fuel. The road to a bloated pot turns your hand into a bluff-catcher.

### Case 3 — AA river jam (PokerOK $108 Mystery Bounty, July 2026)

**Board:** 2-4-5, river 6. Your hand is AA. Villain has 66 (river set).

**What was correct:** Flop and turn value bets — both were right.

**The mistake:** Jamming the remaining stack on the bad river 6. On that river no hand weaker than yours pays — every three, straight, set has you beat; one pair passes. There is no value target.

> **Rule: if no hand weaker than yours will pay on the river, a jam is not value.**
> Overpair on a bad river: check-call into a small pot, check-fold into a big pot.

*Chapter 4 link: the reason this hand became a bloated pot was born preflop. Building your 3-bet range correctly directly lowers the odds of this case repeating.*

### Case 4 — T6s trip-ten river check-raise all-in (WSOP Online ME Day 2 prep)

**What happened:** T6s in the BB, called a BTN min-raise. Flop A-T-3, call 1/3 pot. Turn 5 check-check. River T (board A-T-3-5-T) → trip tens, kicker 6. You checked, BTN fired a thin bet, you check-raised all-in.

**Correct play:** Check-call. On this runout your trips are a bluff-catcher: no weaker hand calls the all-in (every ten outkicks you, boats are ahead); the raise folds out the bluffs and only gets paid by hands that beat you.

**Rule:** A good-looking single/strong hand (trips included) turns into a bluff-catcher on a paired/bloated board — the exact mirror of Case 2 (KTo). (B9.2 protocol: this hand from the table was logged as a case.)

---

## Chapter 9 — Training Protocol

> **Command: "prepare me" / "drill" / "quiz me"**
> Socratic Q&A begins: 5–8 decision questions, one at a time, the next question isn't asked before your answer arrives.

### 9.1 Question mix

| Weight | Topic |
|---|---|
| **50%** | Root error — one pair / bloated pot classification |
| **35%** | Stack mode and range selection (Chapters 3, 4, 5) |
| **15%** | Bluff selection and draw decisions |

### 9.2 Rules

- Questions answered wrong are asked again 1–2 days later in a different disguise.
- Hands you bring from the table are processed as cases and added to Chapter 7.
- These chapters' candidate sentences were logged into B0 as s.12–15; new permanent rules coming from the table get added to B0, and the heading is updated.
- If the answer is right, the reasoning is asked too — a correct decision with wrong reasoning is half a point.
- Camp opens at the start of August; the EPT starts August 16.

---

### 9.3 The Drill's Two Modes and the A-Game Log


**Volume preflop, curiosity-cut postflop.** Run the preflop drill on volume — phone in hand, low energy, no questions asked. The rule reverses postflop: every wrong answer AND every surprising right answer STOPS the drill — open the class view, find the threshold where the action turns, write it up in the three-part format (27.4), then continue. "My combo played it correctly, I passed" is the drill's root error — you learned nothing.

**A-game log.** Two lines at the end of every session through the series: how I played (a grade plus a number), and what I did that day (sleep, pre-session mental load, the hour). The point isn't health tracking, it's capturing the A-game's inputs — after a few days, two or three recurring variables remain *(calibrate: yours may come out different)*. Write the law you find into the series plan (B16.4) and don't violate it.

### 9.3-EK The Range Recall Ritual — "Am I Playing This" First, Frequency Last


The opening of both the preflop drill and every postflop session is a two-minute blind recall: state the position, close your eyes, read the range in grid order — offsuit Ax → Kx → Qx → Tx → pairs → suited — and for every hand let the FIRST question be "am I playing this hand?", not "3-bet what percent?" The second layer: "does this hand have a 3-bet premium, from which criterion?" Frequency precision is the last layer; if the layer order breaks, memorized percentages replace memorized decision classes and can't be recalled at the table. The same ritual applies to villain's side too: if you can't answer "CO opens — which hands is he playing?", you're not ready to drill that spot — go back to the chart first.

**Rule:** In the drill, the order is fixed: am I playing it → what's its premium → frequency; someone who knows the first two without the third still makes few mistakes, someone who's memorized the third without knowing the first two collapses at the table.

### 9.3-EK-2 The Offsuit-Floor Anchor — Remember Every Position in One Line, Pick Your Bluff One or Two Pips Below


The shortcut to memorizing an entire opening range wholesale is to anchor on the range's lowest offsuit line: roughly, LJ bottoms out at offsuit Tx, HJ at offsuit 9x, CO at offsuit 8x; on the BTN the floor practically disappears — almost every offsuit Ax gets opened (down to an A2o–A5o mix) *(floor lines: calibrate; see 4.10)*. This one line also tells you postflop where villain's air/bluff block comes from — on a dry high-card board like K-2-6, CO c-bets his floor-line offsuit 8x at a high frequency *(frequency: calibrate)*; if your 8x blocks that block, your check-raise candidate slides one notch lower. The third use is preflop bluff selection: your 3-bet/squeeze bluff candidates mostly come from kickers one or two pips BELOW that floor (if HJ's floor is 9x, think A8s/A7s type), because the test is: "am I folding out the hand that would dominate me on a call?" — if yes, the bluff is profitable, not trash.

**Rule:** Memorize one offsuit-floor line per position; derive villain's air, your own check-raise notch, and your 3-bet bluff candidates all from that same line.

### 9.3-EK-3 Drill Score Isn't a Thermometer — Stop on Direction and Sizing-Class Errors, Not on RNG Noise


9.3 says stop on every postflop surprise; this has a mirror-image leak too — a practice that stops because you picked the "wrong" side on the solver's mixed node, trying to push the score to 95, sells the forest for a tree. In the postflop pack, scores in the 80s are a healthy starting point *(calibrate)*; what you're chasing isn't the number but the picture: on this board, roughly how often does my range bet, at which sizing class, and what does its check hold. Let what stops you be direction errors (bet/check, call/raise) and sizing-CLASS errors; not the mix's percentage difference. What plays the turn/river well isn't memorized frequency either, it's memory of the flop picture — knowing "I bet half my range on this flop" tells you both ranges on the next street.

**Rule:** In the drill, stop on direction and sizing-class errors; don't stop on mix/RNG noise — what matters isn't the score you've banked but the picture of "what is my range doing on this board."

### 9.4 The Report-Reading Protocol — Start from the Extremes, Diff Against a Hypothesis, Run a Null Test


Paging through thousands of boards in a solver report in sequence isn't study, it's sightseeing. Protocol: (1) PREDICT first — look at the preflop ranges, write down "which board class belongs to whom," then open the report. (2) Start from the extremes: the boards you "never check" and the ones you "check most" — the leak is almost always at the extremes; mid-frequency boards already play the way you feel they should. (3) When comparing two depths or positions, don't sweep every board: build a hypothesis ("A-high must have changed at 30bb"), filter to look only at that class, and also verify the class that should NOT have changed (null test) — two numbers side by side, the difference in one glance. (4) If the output doesn't confirm your prediction, don't throw the framework out; find the variable with the question "which single factor shifted the sizing/frequency?"

**Rule:** Don't enter the report without a prediction, don't dawdle outside the extremes; when hunting for a difference, use hypothesis + counter-test, not a full sweep.

### 9.5 Mass Priority in Range Comparison — Offsuit Blocks + Pairs; Ratio and Saturation


When comparing two ranges, the eye drifts to the suited ladder; the mass isn't there. Offsuit combos come in 12s, pairs in 6s — the difference between two ranges is written almost always by offsuit blocks and pairs; suited differences are usually 1–4 combos and don't move the postflop needle. Two more corrections: (a) think in RATIO, not absolute combos — "6% versus 2.5%" looks small but means 2.5x; (b) saturation — a narrower range carries every shared hand at a bigger share: if neither player ever folds 66, the one playing at half the width holds it roughly twice as often. This trio tells you who the board works for: whose offsuit block connected, what the ratio is, and who holds it denser.

**Rule:** Don't hunt for the range difference in the suited corner — the mass is in the offsuit blocks and pairs; read the difference by ratio and saturation.

### 9.4-EK In an ICM Report, Equity Realization Doesn't Have to Sum to 100% (3+ Handed)

In a cEV aggregate report, the two players' equity realization is a mathematical complement — if one is below 100%, the other is above it; it's a zero-sum game. In an ICM postflop report this breaks whenever at least one player outside the hand is still at the table (3+ handed): on most boards BOTH sides realize equity below 100%, and on some boards that go very well for you, a SINGLE side can rise above 100% — because the difference leaks to the players outside the hand at the table, it isn't split between the two sides. Read the report with that expectation: don't get suspicious and think "both came out low, is something wrong?" — that's ICM postflop's normal signature, don't carry the zero-sum cEV intuition over there. At the HU final table (once only 2 players remain) there's no third party to leak to — there ICM is a linear complement in $EV too, and just like in cEV, both sides dropping below 100% together is a sign you should be suspicious of.

**Rule:** In an ICM postflop aggregate report, the two sides' equity realization not summing to 100% — whenever at least one player outside the hand is still at the table — is not an error; at HU this rule reverses, and the zero-sum reading applies.

### 9.4-EK-2 Prune the Deviation From the Tree Before You Bring It to the Table

If you're considering dropping the big sizing/shove at an ICM postflop node for a smaller, lower-variance line, test it in the report before you bring it to the table on a "feeling": pull that big sizing out of the tree entirely, and compare the rest of the strategy's EV against the old one by hand class (in the grid). Write your expectation down in advance: the BOTTOM of your range (weak hands) picks up a small EV gain, the TOP (fragile-strong hands like overpairs, top pair) loses a bigger one, and net range EV drops — measure how much each class loses in your own tree, then calibrate the result. If the deviation really targets a single hand class (e.g., one specific marginal value hand), the damage can stay small; if it's a blanket policy change like "I'll just play smaller overall," in the grid it comes out of the top hands' pocket most of the time *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** Before bringing a sizing/line change to the table, pull that option out of the tree and write your prediction — the bottom of the range gains a little, the top loses more — then verify how much each hand class gains and loses in your own grid (9.4's predict-then-counter-test backbone).

### 9.6 Build Your Own Shortcut Formula for Situation-Sensitive ICM Spots on a Break

Some ICM situations — especially spots near the bubble, where a small percentage change in the field swings the answer hard — don't hold up to pattern-extraction by paging through sim after sim: put two or three similar sims side by side and the output jumps around so much that the "pattern" doesn't show itself; you end up having learned one spot's answer but can't carry it to the neighboring spot. In this kind of situation, do something different on the break: from the real structure sheet (the FULL payout ladder — the amount at every paid place, not just the total prize — plus the total chip count) build your own DUMMY stack distribution — set the total equal to the real chip count, place a few bigs, a group of averages, and a few shorts — run it through an ICM calculator once, and derive a shortcut formula from the resulting $EV numbers that you can apply by hand at the table (see 20.6-EK-4). The total prize plus the number of paid places alone isn't sufficient input: what actually drives ICM pressure is the FLATNESS of the ladder — the same total and the same number of paid places can produce very different rung shapes; a flat/linear ladder raises the premium, a top-heavy ladder lowers it (see 20.3-EK-3). This is different from memorizing one spot's answer: it produces a TOOL you can reuse at the table again and again, not a one-time ANSWER.

**Rule:** In ICM spots where pattern-matching breaks down (a small situational change swings the answer hard), don't page through sims one by one — build your own dummy-stack table with the FULL payout ladder, run it through a calculator once, derive a formula you can use by hand from it, and reuse that at the table.

## Chapter 11 — Bloated Pot, Turn Discipline and River Execution

*★ v5. The MISSING HALF of the root error. B0/B4/B7 establish the DIAGNOSIS "one pair in a bloated pot = bluff-catcher"; this chapter gives how to play the hand once the diagnosis is made: when the pot is "bloated", whether to fire the barrel on the turn, at what price to call/value on the river. All three documented eliminations are the subject of this chapter.*

### 11.0 "Bloated pot" — a numerical definition

Until now "bloated pot" was used qualitatively (≈ 3-bet pot). When depth changes, this intuition fails: at 250bb a single-raised pot can enter the stack-off zone by the turn; at 100bb a 3-bet pot is already there. The correct measure is SPR (stack ÷ pot).

> **Deep, a bloated pot is defined by SPR, not by the number of bets.**

| SPR (on the flop) | Typical pot | Role of one pair |
|---|---|---|
| **< 1** | 4-bet pot / short stack | Decision was made preflop — commit |
| **1–4** | 100bb 3-bet pot | Bluff-catcher. Do NOT START a big pot |
| **4–8** | 40–70bb single-raised / live multiway bloated open | Two streets of value + control; careful on the third street |
| **> 8** | 100bb+ standard single-raised pot (incl. 200–300bb Main Day 1) | Thin value can be taken — but a pot that sees a re-raise suddenly drops into the 1–4 band |

SPR is arithmetic, universal. Which EXACT hand class stacks off at which SPR becomes clear with your execution data — *(calibrate: assign an SPR to the 3 elimination hands)*.

**Rule:** Read the SPR first, then assign the role. Deep (SPR>8), demoting one pair to bluff-catcher too early and missing value is a mistake; in mid-shallow (SPR 1–4), stacking off on the old deep reflex is one too.

### 11.1 Turn discipline — the second-barrel criterion

B6 covered the turn only for DRAWS. The turn decision for value/marginal hands goes here. The root error is usually born on the turn: you bloat the pot yourself with the second barrel and end up as a bluff-catcher on the river (in Case 3 the turn bet was CORRECT — B7; but that bet bloated the pot — when firing the second barrel your river plan must be ready in advance).

| My hand | Blank / low turn | Overcard to me | Board paired | Draw-completing |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Overpair** | Bet (controlled) | Careful — count the SPR | Check leaning | Check / reduce size |
| **Top pair good kicker** | Thin bet | Check-call | Check | Check-fold leaning |
| **Top pair weak kicker** | Check | Check | Check-fold | Check-fold |
| **Air + blocker** | Barrel candidate | Barrel candidate | Give up | Give up (the card arrived) |

Sizes *(calibrate)* — but the direction is fixed: every time you grow the pot, your buying power for the check-fold luxury on the river shrinks.

> **Before firing the second barrel, ask: can I state my river plan? Am I leaving a pot size at which I can check-fold on a bad river?**

**B0 c.12:** *The size you fire on the turn is the price of the decision you'll make on the river.*

### 11.2 River — bluff-catch: at what price to call

B0 c.2 says "check-call in a small pot, check-fold in a big pot" — a coarse tendency. When villain bets, the decision reduces to three variables:

| Villain size | What bluffs does he have? | What value weaker than me bets this size? | Blocker | Tendency |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **≤ 50% pot** | Many (thin value + bluffs) | Plenty | Less important | Call direction |
| **50–100%** | Shrinking | Narrowing | Important | Borderline — blockers decide |
| **Overbet** | Polarized: nuts or air | Almost none | Critical | One pair → fold; only blocker-holding bluff-catchers call |

**Rule:** As the size grows, villain's range shifts toward value, and one pair resolves from bluff-catcher into FOLD. "The reg overbet" = read it as polarized; panic hero-calls and forgetting chops (Case 1) are this spot's mistakes.

### 11.3 River — thin value: who pays

All of the book's value filters so far were NEGATIVE ("don't jam if he won't pay"). The positive command was missing: if someone pays, BET the THIN value. This is the other face of B0 c.2 — same filter, opposite direction.

| My hand class | Rec / station | Reg |
|---|---|---|
| **Top pair good kicker** | Small value bet (he pays) | Thin bet / check-call |
| **Second pair** | Small value bet | Check-call |
| **Two pair, small board** | Value bet | Check-call — the reg pays this size with better |

**Rule:** If the question "which hand weaker than mine pays?" HAS an answer — however thin — bet. In the rec-heavy Main, missed thin value is direct chip loss.

### 11.4 Bad river catalog

With an overpair/strong one pair, when these cards arrive the "no value target" alarm goes off:

- **Fourth of the low cards / straight-completing** (Case 3: river 6 on 2-4-5 — trips, straight, set have all passed you)
- **Third flush card** (if you don't hold a flush)
- **Board pairing** (door to set / full house)
- **Overcard over yours** (villain's top pair beats you)

On these cards: check-call in small pots, check-fold in big pots. **NEVER jam** — jam value exists only if a hand weaker than yours will pay.

*Root-error link: this entire chapter is the execution layer of B0 c.1-2. B4 reduces the error's FREQUENCY preflop; B11 delivers the EXECUTION when the error moment arrives.*

---

### 11.0-EK Not bloating, but bloating WITHOUT A PLAN


> **What commits the root error isn't bloating the pot — it's bloating it without a CLOSING PLAN.**

Growing the pot on the turn isn't itself the mistake — growing it without knowing in advance what you'll do on which river is the mistake. The distinction is in the PLAN, not the SIZE (a planned medium-sized double-barrel is legitimate; an unplanned overbet is a mistake):

- **Unplanned double-barrel:** stringing along turn + river with a medium size on a "we'll see" basis → you end up stuck as a bluff-catcher on every dirty river, exposed to check-raise. This is the root error.
- **Planned single-street:** on a dynamic board where equity is clean, firing the turn as one big size (overbet included) and locking in a DECISION for EVERY river in advance. IP, if villain checks the hand closes with a check-back; if he leads, your response (the call/fold threshold) must already be locked in on the turn too — not "no decision," but "every decision already made." OOP, "check" alone isn't enough: your response to a bet (the call/fold threshold) must also be locked in on the turn, or you fall into exactly the bluff-catcher spot B11.1 warns about. The size carries the river plan in both cases.

The distinction is tested with one question (B11.1's second-barrel criterion): *"Can I state my river plan RIGHT NOW?"* If the answer IP is "check-back if checked to, call this size / fold that size if led into," or OOP "call this size / fold that size," the big single size is legitimate; if the answer is "I'll see how it goes," don't bloat the pot.

### 11.0-EK-2 The SPR 1-4 row is for the capped side; the 3-bettor's top pair counts streets by depth


B11.0's table row "SPR 1-4: one pair is a bluff-catcher, do NOT START a big pot" holds for the capped side — the side whose pot got bloated — at SPR ≳2; at SPR ≲1.5 (at a 30-60bb baseline, 3-bet pots mostly fall into the SPR ~1.5-3 band; ≲1.5 only around ~30-40bb and at OOP 3-bet sizing — *calibrate*) the capped side's top pair also mostly drops into the call-off class — the threshold falls for both sides; the capped side still does NOT START the pot but does not fold to a jam *(calibrate)*. For the range-and-nut-advantaged 3-bettor, at SPR ≲2 on a medium-connected board top pair/overpair is mostly a jamming class (see B32.1); at 100bb (SPR ≳4-5 at OOP 3-bet sizing — at the row's upper bound; in an IP 3-bet pot it can exceed ~5 and fall outside the row — *calibrate*) in an A-high 3-bet pot top pair is mostly a "two streets + river control" hand (best kicker plus a capped opponent puts a third street at the margin) — a third street means shoving the stack in with one pair, and the B11.4 catalog applies across that third street (see B32.2). The bluff-catcher definition reverts onto the bloater's top pair the moment a B11.4 card falls on that street (turn included) or they get raised; on the river they're already in control/bluff-catch mode by plan anyway.

### 11.0-EK-3 The TURN version of the SPR≈1 row: not a JAM with value, a half-stack bet


The B11.0 table says "commit" at SPR<1 — correct, but it doesn't say the size. On the turn, around SPR 1, jamming an overpair/strong top pair on "I'm committed anyway" is a mistake — especially the reflex of "scary card, just shove" purely because an overcard or a draw appeared. Commit tells you the money is going in eventually; WHICH street, at WHAT size it goes in changes villain's continuing range. The range that calls a flop bet is loaded with bottom pair, weak middle pair (and, if the bet was small, gutshots and high overcards too); this range snap-folds to a jam — exactly the class you're crushing. Half to two-thirds pot *(calibrate)* pushes that class to indifference: part of it pays (your value), part of it folds (fold equity for your bluffs). The protection loss is marginal: half-pot gives a draw a worse direct price than jam anyway; its implied odds are already small since only a half-stack remains behind — you pay that remainder to it on the river, but the total is capped; a flush draw is mostly left indifferent — with half the stack going in on the turn, you're jamming every river card anyway, the geometry already commits you. An overcard turn doesn't break this either: that range mostly doesn't hold that card.

On a scary turn too, half-stack is the default (protection + thin value). Jam only for a single reason: villain's continuing range is draw-HEAVY and half-pot would give that range a direct price — typically when the turn adds MORE THAN ONE live draw to the range (flush + double gutshot), more often OOP + at SPR≤1; if the turn COMPLETES the flush, the B11.1 table applies: check / reduce size, not jam. A minority case *(marginal — calibrate)*. On the bluff side: the overcard+draw combos that carry the most equity against villain's continuing range — at SPR 1 you get paid off often, so pick equity, not blockers; a hand that holds the turn card is already top pair, on the value line.

**Scope:** B21.5's rule that "at shallow SPR an in-between size ties up chips → jam or fold" belongs to the ICM + defense/float context; this entry is the value-aggressor's turn in chipEV. Different seat, no contradiction.

### 11.0-EK-4 One rung up the SPR ladder: at turn SPR 1.5–2, value bets 2/3 — villain puts the rest in FOR you


11.0-EK-3 covered SPR≈1: no fold equity, half-stack, equity picks the bluff. One rung up (turn SPR ~1.5–2; ≈30bb, a big c-bet got called, a single flush draw arrived) the default is **2/3** — not half, and not jam either *(band boundary: calibrate)*:

- **Jam's rationale is fake.** "Stack his top pair" — after 2/3 what's left behind is less than a min-raise; whether top pair calls or jams, since the remainder is small the money mostly goes in on the river anyway.
- **The class jam costs you:** the bottom pocket pair / wheel Ax / broadway that picked up a gutshot on the turn that call 2/3 but fold to jam — exactly the class you're crushing. A small size already strips out backdoor-less overcards (KTo/ATo).
- **Drift within the class:** top pair with the best kicker goes big; weak kicker goes small; an overpair holding the high flush card leans small — it wants to keep the draw it dominates (the QJ♦ type) in the pot; an overpair not holding that card leans big/jam to charge the draw *(calibrate)*.
- **Bluff:** fold equity exists → an overcard that holds a card in villain's CALL class (flush draw); a missed Kx/Qx you don't block folds. At 11.0-EK-3 equity picked the bluff, at this rung it's the blocker — that's the ladder.

Break point: at SPR ≲1, or when the turn adds a SECOND draw, jam weighting appears *(marginal)* — the same mechanism as 26.6's "flowing nut → jam," different seat.

### 11.1-EK Turn overbet — PFR IP, after the flop c-bet gets called: effective nuts and bluff selection


18.2-EK said "nut advantage + capped → overbet"; this entry gives the turn-card mechanics. The overbet's leverage isn't the true nuts but the effective nuts — the hand your opponent's range structurally cannot beat you with. BB arrives largely stripped of the combos it 3-bet preflop and check-raised on the flop (some sets/two pair stay in via slow-play, some big pairs stay via flatting — effective nuts is range-relative, not absolute); your AK/AQ, your overpair, your top pair fills that gap and plays "unbeatable" on the turn. The single rule: **the overbet card is one that completes the class that went to 3-bet/x-r in villain's range, while adding nothing to the remaining (capped) class.** Examples: on a high+low disconnected flop, a second high card that doesn't complete a straight (K-8-x to A: AA/AK is on your side — BB's mostly went to 3-bet; in BB, Ax is only top pair, no straight); on a low flop, a high card villain missed (folded to that card on the flop, you c-bet); on a medium-high flop, a low blank (villain has no two pair with that card, and its overpair mostly went to 3-bet) *(deep, single-raised pot, IP PFR; calibrate)*. The negative of the same rule is the closing card — a card that completes villain's NON-capped class: on a two-high-card flop, a third Broadway card — K-Q-x to A/J/T included (BB's flatted JT/QT/AT/KJ-type combos become a straight or two pair; even an A completes JT's Broadway; only the case where a nut straight is made with a 3-bet-range combo is marginal), on A-x-x a low straight-completer, a flush-completer, an unpaired low card pairing up on a paired flop (9-9-4 to a 4: the check-calling 4x becomes a full house; K-7-7 to a K turns PFR's AK/KQ into a big full house — this closes, it doesn't open). On a closing card, reduce BOTH frequency AND size — check a lot; if you do bet, the range goes MERGED: nuts + still-ahead set/two pair/strong top pair value + a small/medium size with few bluffs that block villain's completed class *(calibrate)* — as the size shrinks it goes merged, not polar (18.2-EK's overbet-polar / small-merged axis); this is the turn version of 11.3-EK. Frequency is preserved only on a brick where the range advantage persists.

**GAPS, not nuts, select the bluff.** The overbet is polar: a medium-strength hand (a turned second pair, a weak top pair) neither folds villain out nor is ahead — it belongs at a small size or a check. A bluff candidate should not block the hands villain folds, should block the strong class villain keeps, and should have clean outs if called — the best class is a **turned bottom pair** and an overcard-plus-gutshot (6.2-EK). One big size is enough *(calibrate)*; the edge opens from fold equity, not from raw equity.

### 11.1-EK-3 Turn after the c-bet gets called: four counter-intuitive rules — the mono board's fourth suit card, big flop → small turn, barreling an A turn, the turned-pair bluff


B18's flop sizes give rise to four rules on the turn that fall outside the 11.1 table *(all calibrate)*:

1. **Monotone flop + fourth suit card (IP vs BB):** the range polarizes — the nut flush goes big; against it, air that does NOT block a pair (that doesn't hold villain's foldable 8-x/5-x/Q-x) bluffs at the same size; a hand holding a pair (a K-8 type) is not a bluff — it blocks villain's fold. The pool heavily under-bluffs this turn. OOP PFR vs BTN it's the reverse: fourth suit card → range checks; on a brick → thin SMALL value including second pair/medium pocket pair — the pool polarizes and skips the thin value (18.2-EK-9).
2. **A-high board, a big flop bet gets called → turn goes SMALL:** the target is the remaining bottom/middle pairs; the "bet big, continue big" reflex is wrong (18.2-EK-8).
3. **Dry-medium board, a big c-bet gets called, turn brings an A → mostly BARREL:** the big flop bet already swept weak Ax out of the caller's range — the A is the aggressor's card, the range advantage is yours; continue barreling including Ax for value, a small-medium size is enough (9x/TT pays). What checks isn't Ax — it's overpair/9x that dropped to second pair beneath the A; check-calling with Ax is only a low-frequency mix *(calibrate)*.
4. **High board, low turn — bluff priority is turned-pair > high gutshot > bottom pocket pair:** the trash that holds the turn card blocks villain's new two pair; a bottom pocket pair blocks his first folding block (weak pair + gutshot) — the pool over-bluffs bottom pocket pairs. River value is narrow (two pair+/straight) → a single all-in size; when the board has no K (a Q-T high runout type), bluff from K-x: it blocks the premium catcher (K-Q) without blocking T-x's fold (the "turned-pair bluff" line for B1; the catcher side is 26.4-EK).

### 11.1-EK-2 "Will the class that pays bet on its own?" — two nodes for checking with the nuts (against a reg)


If the class that pays a bet is going to bet into your check anyway, betting only costs you the money from villain's bluffs. The test is one question; two nodes:

- **A turn that ties the nuts (IP).** On K-J-T, KQ gets a small bet called, turn brings an A: you have Broadway, but it chops with AQ; villain's second-best class (AK/AJ/set) wants to bet big itself on the river, and turns T9/J8 into a bluff. Against AK you're getting one street either way — and that street comes on the river, at his size, from two sources → KQ/AQ and two-pairs mostly CHECK the turn *(calibrate)*.
- **The worst river for your range after a small turn lead (OOP).** K-7-3, small c-bet called, turn 5 small lead called, river A: villain holds Ax (yours went to a raise on the flop). CHECK the range, straight (6-4) included: Ax bets into your check → raise; missed Q9/QT-suited big-bluffs → these only get harvested by checking; if you jam, only Ax pays — and it was going to bet anyway.

**Boundary (23.11):** if villain doesn't bet the river (a passive pool/station), both nodes close — delayed value collapses, so take the money on the turn instead. Villain type dictates the answer here, not the board.

### 11.2-EK The price-threshold is SUSPENDED at two nodes (even in chipEV)


B11.2's size→call-direction table rests on a single assumption: villain's BETTING range HAS a bluff class that fires this size (indifference = the balance between paying off the bluff-catcher and folding). At two nodes, indifference can't be established: the bluff class is either structurally absent (drained) or present but blocking your call range — the price-threshold breaks down in either case, even without ICM:

- **A river drained of bluffs:** if the river completes every draw (bluff candidates convert to value) or villain's line structurally contains no bluff candidate on this street, the bet range he fires on the river is almost pure value. Villain still fires a BET, but since no bluffs remain in it, paying it off costs you a net loss, not indifference → B11.2's price-call doesn't apply here, the theoretical fold rate is overwhelming.
- **A blocker-heavy / monotone big-money node:** if villain's natural bluffs hold the card your strong/call range needs (a monotone board where villain holds the lone flush card), then whenever villain holds a bluff, your chance of holding the strong hand DROPS — he's the one holding the card you need. Because his bluffs block your call range, they succeed more often than normal → even in theory, defending BELOW MDF is correct. B21.4 gave this for ICM; by the blocking mechanism it also applies in chipEV at this node.

> **The price-threshold assumes "a bluff class fires this size." If villain's bet is bluff-free (drained) or his bluffs block your call, even a cheap price is a trap.**

### 11.2-EK-2 The attacking side of blocker mechanics


B11.2-EK's second node (villain's bluffs block your call range) describes the defending side. The same mechanic on offense is "card-matching": fire your overbet bluff holding the card villain's premium bluff-catcher carries — holding that card reduces villain's "matching" call combos, mostly raising the fold share of his remaining range (fold equity goes up, though it's not guaranteed). On a monotone board where the flush GETS THERE, a bottom pocket pair plus the flush card is therefore a natural overbet bluff; when the flush MISSES, bluffs that hold the flush card mostly go to the bottom of the list — they block villain's missed draws that would otherwise fold; if a non-blocking candidate exists, pick that one first (see B32.6).

### 11.3-EK Layer-targeted sizing: shrink when the nut layer sits in villain's range


If your value mass sits in the middle layer and the nut layer sits in villain's range, SHRINK the size: a small size collects payment from villain's weak-pair/draw layer, caps your loss against his set/two pair (nut) layer, and cuts off his raise leverage — you end up not being the one who started the bloated pot. Apply this size as the ONE size across your entire betting range, nuts included, so the range stays protected against a raise. This isn't a memorized rule but a compass: the size chooses which layer of villain's range you want to push to indifference *(calibrate)*.

### 11.3-EK-2 The flop twin of layer-targeted sizing


The rule "size chooses which layer of villain's range you want to push to indifference" holds on the 3-bet-pot flop too: first ask "which class do I want to fold out?", then "at what size is that class indifferent?" On a paired board where trips is live for the caller (a 9-7-7 type), the caller's middle layer mostly calls a small bet and approaches indifference around half pot *(calibrate)*; on a paired board where the high single card sits in your range (an A-J-J type), that layer mostly already folds to a small size (see B32.4).

### 11.3-EK-3 IP sizing on the river: WHO are you opening the action to — medium hands check or 2/3+, the nuts go 2/3 or all-in


The 11.3 table's rec column says "small value bet"; the reg column's mechanism is as follows.

**There's no third-pot with a medium hand.** Pot 10, bet 3, a caller who's 60% against the range → your gain is ~6% of the pot — the best case. A small bet opens a raise node against a reg; the small range's weak value + bluffs → the range most exposed to a raise, and your gain drops to 1–2%. In solver lines like bet-check-bet and bet-bet-bet, IP's river size is 2/3 or all-in; a third-pot is ~zero *(calibrate)*. Medium hand: check or 2/3+. Exception: a station who doesn't raise (11.3's small bet); 19.6's blocker-bet is OOP's deviation.

**With the nuts, villain's RAISE capacity picks the size.** Ask "who raises 2/3?" — if his range holds a strong catcher he thinks is "unbeatable" (overpair/set), it raises on its own; a medium Ax calls 2/3 and folds to a jam → 2/3 collects both. Against a passive villain who doesn't raise, YOU have to put the money in yourself → all-in *(depth threshold: calibrate)*.
