**Max size under three conditions:** you're arriving with lots of bluffs (a big call on the flop, checked-through on the turn) + villain arrives with a capped catcher + your value beats all of his catchers → all-in, 2.5–4× included; bluff volume doesn't shrink the size, it GROWS it. B11.4's "NEVER jam" is for one pair; this is nut/polar value. If all-in isn't in your tree, your flop/turn sizes are lying too (29.2).

### 11.2-EK-3 The "what would I do if he checked" test — deciding whether to raise a small bet

B11.2 gives how the price-threshold shifts as the size grows; there's a separate shortcut for the decision of whether to RAISE a small bet (especially at blocker-bet size): ask "what would I do if villain had checked?" If the answer against a check is a clear VALUE bet (an unhesitating bet against a check — on the river there's no protection motive, no card left to come) AND it's clear which hands weaker than mine could pay my raise, then villain's small bet has only SKIPPED that bet — run your own line, raise. If the answer against a check is only a THIN bet (though you'd want to bet a little bigger yourself) or there's no weak hand that would pay my raise, then just CALL villain's small bet too — if I raise, only better hands keep paying me, while I fold villain's bluffs and weak calls out of the pot.

**Rule:** When deciding whether to raise a small bet, ask two questions: "what would I do if he checked" and "which hands weaker than mine pay my raise" — if both are clear, raise; if even one is unclear, call.

## Chapter 12 — ICM and the Final Table Battle Plan

*★ v5. Promotes B3.2's 6-line "ICM layer" to a load-bearing spine. SHR €100K and HR €10.3K small fields + steep payouts: nearly every Day 2 decision is under ICM. The most expensive ICM mistake is exactly the ROOT ERROR itself — calling off with one pair in a bloated pot, wrong in chipEV and multiplicatively wrong under ICM.*

### 12.0 When ICM turns on in this tournament

Same 28bb, same hand, same position: in the SHR it's an ICM decision, in Main Day 1 a pure chipEV decision. Make this distinction in advance, NOT at the table.

| Event | Field character | When ICM effectively turns on | Bubble ≈ FT? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **SHR €100K** | Reg-heavy, very small field *(verify at the table)* | Early — from mid Day 1 | Yes, nested |
| **PLO HR €25K** | Small field | Early–mid | Close |
| **HR €10.3K** | Reg-heavy, small field | Mid — steep as the money approaches | Close |
| **Main €5.3K** | Rec-heavy, big field | Late — chipEV until Day 3 | No, separate |

Field size / payout percentages are NOT MADE UP — read them from the lobby based on registration count. Step 0 is added to the decision order: **"Is ICM on in this event?"**

### 12.1 Risk premium — the ICM correction to B5's jam/call tables

B5.2/5.3 ranges are pure chipEV. ICM's number-one asymmetry: **jamming is much cheaper than calling** (jamming has fold equity; calling has none).

> **In a call spot the FIRST question isn't "is it the bubble?" but "am I COVERED?"** What squeezes you isn't the bubble but being covered (bust when you lose). Against someone who covers you (chip leader), calls tighten HARD; against a jam from a short who does NOT cover you (you don't zero out even losing), you call WIDE even on the bubble. *(Emre's leak to calibrate: lumping the two together as "bubble = tight".)*

| Situation | JAM range | CALL range (vs jam) | Jam at the chip leader |
|---|---|---|---|
| **ICM off (per 12.0)** | B5 as-is | B5 as-is (99+/AJs+/AQo+) | B5 as-is |
| **Bubble / near the money** | Tighten one notch | Tighten TWO notches | Widen (if they're locked) |
| **FT pay-jump active** | Tighten two notches | Tightest — fold hard when covered | Depends on role (12.2) |

Notch contents in drills with ICMIZER/solver *(calibrate)* — no made-up thresholds.

> **Jam and call ranges DIVERGE under ICM. Symmetry is a chipEV assumption.**

**Emre calibration (2026-08-10)** — harsh bubble, wide BvB jam from someone covering you, ~22bb: **CALL = 88+ · AJs+ · AQo; A9s / KQs FOLD.** Driver: cover + bubble = losing means bust €0, a marginal edge isn't worth tournament life (A9s reverse-dominated, KQs a flip). *If you're NOT covered (jammer is shorter than you, you don't bust even losing) the line is much wider; if the jammer tightens, the call gets even tighter. The exact threshold is verified from the lobby with ICMIZER.*

**Drill add-on (2026-08-10, session 2)** — the NOT-covered side, where the leak actually lives: not covered, 22bb in the BB, bubble, wide short jam — folded **both A9s and KTo** ("range not enough"). Both are calls (KTo ~54% vs a wide 13bb BTN jam, needing ~44% for the price; A9s clearer still). The **not-covered fold reflex is the sticky half of the leak** — it survived the covered/not-covered contrast in the very same session. So the not-covered CALL floor sits *at least* as wide as A9s/KTo here; the full range stays `(calibrate)` with ICMIZER, but the error is the fold, not the call. Table cue: **before folding to any jam, ask "am I covered?" — if No, the call is much wider than it feels.**

### 12.2 Final table — role matrix

At the FT the correct behavior depends on STACK ROLE, not the HAND.

| My role | Someone shorter than me at the table: YES | Someone shorter than me: NO |
|---|---|---|
| **Short (<15bb)** | Be the first jammer, watch the ladder | Don't fold up — jam correctly; locking up is also a bust (to the blinds) |
| **Middle** | Most fragile — know whose bust you're waiting for, don't open pots with those who cover you | You're effectively the shortest — 12.5: if everyone's locked, WIDEN your jam range, don't fold up |
| **Deep, covering** | Aggression is free — crush without penalty | Don't get into an ego war with the chip leader |

### 12.3 The ladder — when it's real money

If the next pay jump is LARGE relative to your stack and someone shorter than you is at the table: wait, ladder the jump. If the jump is micro: play chipEV.

Payout ladder *(fill from the lobby — threshold numbers are not made up)*:

| Place | Prize | Difference vs previous place |
|---|---|---|
| *(fill in)* | *(fill in)* | *(fill in)* |

### 12.4 Bubble hunting map

The bubble isn't defense; it's the tournament's highest-chipEV window — IF you're on the RIGHT side of it.

| My stack | Locked middle stack | Short stack | Big stack covering me |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Big (covering)** | Rob them — widen opens + 3-bet pressure | Normal | No ego — bluff 3-bets off |
| **Middle** | Selective robbery — ONLY the locked middle YOU cover; otherwise B3.2: patience | Normal | Run |
| **Short** | — | — | Run, wait for the correct jam |

Rule: **the locked-up middle stack is the most profitable target at the table.** Rec/reg field difference: in the Main, targets abound; in SHR/HR everyone knows this, so target selection is selective (same as B5.6's opponent-reading pattern).

### 12.5 Short-stack ICM exception — CAUTION

⚠️ **"Tighten under ICM" is NOT ALWAYS correct.** When everyone is locked, the short stack's correct play is to WIDEN — nobody wants to pay you off. In this band the "tighten in general" reflex is actively wrong advice.

**<15bb jam card** *(calibrate — ≤6 rows: only BTN/SB/BB + "first in"; chipEV jam range + ICM correction column; no full Nash table is written, the "check at the break" character is preserved)*.

**B0 c.13:** *A marginal call under ICM = the tournament-life version of the root error.*

---

### 12.0-EK Late registration cuts both ways — while it's open the premium is LOWER than the calculator says; as it closes, the dollar-EV of sitting down can be HIGHER than the buy-in


Root error: (a) taking the calculator's premium at face value while late registration is still running, (b) rejecting the late-reg decision on the reflex "if I'm late I'll be playing a short stack." (a) The calculator treats the field as closed; while registration is open, incoming players grow the pool and the number of paid places and push the bubble further away — the real premium is a tick lower than shown *(calibrate)*. Correction: compute assuming the final field size and the final payout table, or step the displayed premium down one notch. (b) The mechanism is premium mechanics itself: every elimination raises the survivors' stack-dollars — the late sitter gets the same chips for the same buy-in as everyone else, but at the value the eliminated field has pushed up *(roughly a few percent; calibrate)*. In favor: most of the field eliminated, chips gathered in one giant (a single big leader instead of two medium leaders raises EV noticeably), flat payouts / large min-cash, no pre-reg incentive, freezeout. Against: top-heavy payouts, high rake, pre-reg chip bonus, many other late registrants (every additional late sitter erodes your share) — and your own edge: with a short stack the edge doesn't get realized, you arrive at the bubble short. Rule: late reg is a number on the structure sheet, not a feeling — compute it on break; if your edge is big and the structure slow, sit from the start (24.2); if your edge is small and two events clash, the dollar-EV of sitting late is usually positive *(calibrate)*.

### 12.1-EK-3 At ≥20bb, if the players behind cover you there is NO open-jam range — once its top goes to raise-call, the jam range dies


Don't build two ranges side by side as "at 20bb, jam or raise." Conditions: ICM on, short-handed table (3-handed type), the blinds cover you, small ante. Your raise-call range (the top of your range — vs the SB's jam; one notch wider vs the BB's jam *(lines: calibrate)*) moves your top into the raise; the jam range left behind (candidates like medium suited Ax / small pairs) is TOPLESS, and opponents pay it off noticeably wide *(calibrate)* → every jam is minus. Result: open-jam is zero; the raise share is small *(frequency: calibrate)*, its top raise-calls, the rest raise-folds — you play for the dead money risking 2bb, you don't put 20bb at risk. Two mirrors: (a) if the opponent's seat carries a raise range, his jam is topless → your call vs his jam widens *(calibrate)*; (b) if you carry no jam range, the SB's marginal re-jam is unprofitable — raise-fold also narrows the opponents' jam ranges. As the ante grows, as the stack shortens, as the players behind get shorter than you, the jam range comes back. B3's "15–25bb: direct jam with some" line and 17.8 Mode D's "part of the opens go in as a direct jam" belong to a chipEV / covering-player context; under this condition list they close — the tables don't change.

### 12.1-EK The 30bb gate — fold equity is not a jam license; the license comes from the RATIO of the stack risked to the reward


"The villain opens very wide, folds 90% to my jam → shove wide" is a chipEV intuition. Under ICM, while covered, what limits the jam's EV isn't the raise-fold gap but the RATIO of the stack risked to the reward: if you lose, your tournament life is gone; when you win, the dead money you collect is small relative to your stack. Against the same wide opener, against the same narrow call range, jam width opens up with the stack band: narrow at ~30bb (medium pairs and up + the best Ax/broadway), one notch at ~25bb, hard below ~20bb, wide at ~15bb — under ICM still not "any two," mostly *(bounds: calibrate)*. Rule: against a wide opener, read jam width FIRST from the stack band, THEN from fold equity. Above 25bb while covered, even if he folds 90%, the jam is NOT the first option — the jam may be EV+, but the non-all-in 3-bet (12.2-EK-2) that captures the same fold at far less stack risk dominates it; the ICM premium magnifies that difference. In that band the tree shifts to the non-all-in 3-bet and selective calls; the place where fold equity alone is the license is below ~20bb. Borderline note: 30bb is "few jams," not "no jams" — mostly. This is how 12.1's "tighten one/two notches" row reads inside the band; the table doesn't change.

### 12.1-EK-2 Jam texture → call material: PAIRS against an any-two jam, Ax against a narrow/blocker jam


"He's shoving even 7-2s, how can I fold AQ?" Most of a very wide jam range is low-unconnected trash; against that texture a medium-high pair dominates both cards, and AK/AQ is ~60-65% favorite against two live undercards (below the pair's ~70% vs undercards — but not a flip); the trash in the wide range offers no hands that dominate AK. ICM (loss = bust) magnifies the ordering: a high pair's call EV can climb above AK's *(calibrate)*; the real dropouts are small pairs (22-66) and AJo/ATo/KQ. As the jam texture shifts toward an Ax/Kx-heavy blocker mix, the table turns: high Ax finds hands it dominates, pairs drop into flips → AQ/AK gain value in the call; in a narrow, PAIR-heavy (TT+/AK) jam, AQ and medium pairs drop together. The rote "the wider the villain, the wider I go" is right for the threshold, incomplete for the ordering: in a widening jam, pairs rise faster than Ax. 12.1's "88+/AJs+/AQo" row is for one texture; read two columns: wide/any-two jammer → keep 88+/AK/AQo, drop small pairs (22-66) and AJo/ATo/KQ; narrow/blocker (Ax/Kx) jammer → Ax-heavy, drop small-medium pairs. The cover map comes first (12.1): this distinction organizes the INSIDE of the range, not its threshold.

### 12.1-EK-4 How a small premium shapes the range — flat is the first casualty, the trap closes, and the villain's CALL rate picks the 3-bet's texture


Root error: "The premium is 2%, I'll ignore it." A small premium first cuts opponents' continue range against the 3-bet; that gives the 3-bet more fold equity per chip. The consequences come together: (a) flat is the first slice to drop in every seat — 3-bet frequency flat or up, fold up; (b) the trap closes — big pairs turn to the non-all-in 3-bet instead of flat/jam, because with the villain narrowing his stack-off there is less to induce, and getting squeezed from behind pays a premium; (c) the 3-bet's block changes — medium value (medium-high pairs, good suited broadways) drops from 3-bet to call because the stack-off threshold has risen; suited connectors drop to call because they more than fully realize their equity in the call, the bluff slice shifts to single-high-card offsuit blockers; the size grows. But it isn't the premium that picks polarity — it's the villain's CALL rate against the 3-bet: in the deep, premium-laden spot it's polar because the call rate collapses; in a shallow-mid spot where the villain still calls wide (his own premium small, the flat cheap) the bluffs stay linear — the strongest hands that can't be flatted, not bottom Ax. Rule: before building the 3-bet — "how much does the villain call my 3-bet?" Few calls → polar-big, offsuit blocker bluffs; many calls → linear-small, bluffs from strong hands that can't be flatted. The premium polarizes only indirectly, by pushing that rate down.

### 12.1-EK-5 Wide against the same short's JAM, tight against his NON-ALL-IN 3-bet — the pair book turns with texture; ICM dissolves "committed"


Root error: answering the villain's all-in and his small 3-bet with the same hand list. The short's jam range carries the middle block (medium pairs, medium suiteds, wide Ax) → the medium-high pair dominates it and lives in the call book. The same short's non-all-in 3-bet is polar: the top + blockers that can fold; the medium pair is either dominated or folds nobody out, and it blocks the folds too → the same hand calls the jam, FOLDS to the 3-bet — it looks like a paradox, but it comes from texture. The second layer, with a giant behind who covers you: hands that in chipEV are committed the moment you call are NOT committed under ICM — pay off the short's jam with the medium pair, fold when the giant re-shoves; the giant, for his part, has to shove with a stack that will fold you out, which is why his range narrows after your call (the top pay-off bound moves up). Rule: against the short, read the TYPE of the action first — all-in → pair-heavy wide call; small 3-bet → the top + fold; with a cover behind, the "call → fold if a rejam comes" book is a separate line — chipEV's "I called, so I'm going with it" reflex is a bust here.

### 12.1-EK-6 The OPENING PERCENTAGE, not the position label — if you opened 39% you're the BTN, pay off the SB's rejam accordingly; "value raise → fold to the jam" is not an inconsistency


Root error: "I opened from the HJ, the SB shoved, KQo is weak for an HJ range → fold." Mechanism: pre-FT, with the whole table short/premium-laden, the HJ open is effectively a BTN range (~39%); the SB (short, low premium against you — 3% on you, 11% on him *(calibrate)*) builds his jam off YOUR OPENING PERCENTAGE: every pair, suited broadways, A5o, KJo, KQo. Against him you're close to chipEV → the call book is wide: KQo, A8o, K9s/Q9s, every pair *(calibrate)*. Play by the label and you over-fold; the SB robs you. If the villain shoves narrower than theory (isn't finding A5o/22), cut the bottom calls, keep the body. Second arm: under ICM the raise range is not "committed" — hands like AT/KQs/A9o get raised (iso or open) because they dominate the CALL range, and they fold when the villain's polar JAM arrives; the same hand has two answers at two nodes — there is no "I raised, so I have to pay." "Spending" a hand at this node is normal; the raise-fold value slice is thicker under ICM than in chipEV *(calibrate)*. The wide-range version of the 22.11 escape hatch (the raise that buys a fold option with AK).

### 12.1-EK-7 The deep ICM jam is hyper-fragile to the villain's CALL — a blind paying off a few combos wide zeroes the jam range; once the jam goes, the OPEN must tighten too


Root error: carrying the solver's "25% open-jam at 37bb" output to the table. Mechanism: the deep open-jam (30–40bb, linear payouts, blinds behind at ceiling premium) works in theory because the blinds fold even QQ/AKs *(calibrate)*. But that call range isn't real: paying off with QQ/JJ he's mostly dominating, and when he wins he becomes the runaway leader (22.2-EK-2 arrival role) — what's correct in practice and in future-EV is the slightly wider call. Adding JJ+/AKs/some TT to the blinds — two or three combos — takes the jam frequency from 25% to zero *(calibrate)*. The chain: once the jam goes, those hands fall to raise-fold; the raise-call/raise-fold gap opens, the players behind get profitable rejams → the open range must tighten too (fold rate roughly from 43% to 60% *(calibrate)*). If the Q4s/Q5s jam doesn't exist, J4o/T4o can't open either. Rule: with a deep stack, the "the players behind are forced to fold" jam only if you have SEEN that the players behind really are rocks; against a villain who pays even one combo wide, cut the jam AND tighten the open one notch — both together. 12.1-EK's 30bb gate is "few jams"; this line gives why the "few" is fragile and drags the open along with it. The same warning applies to the short-SB's 60bb "squeeze-jam against a capped flat": in theory every suited Ax, in practice if the villain isn't flatting QQ/AK, that jam doesn't exist.

### 12.1-EK-8 Similar short stacks + runaway leader = chipEV toward each other — the call range vs a jam follows the jam's TEXTURE, not its FREQUENCY; A8o flats, A5o is not a jam


Root error: "short vs short, low premium → open the chipEV chart" — right direction, wrong calibration. Mechanism: two similar shorts, leader far away → the premium between them ~zero (satellite table); but three corrections. (1) If the payouts are linear and there is a third, shorter stack at the table, the premium climbs back up (from 4% to 11% *(calibrate)*) — not "short-short = zero premium," but "short-short + no other short + top-heavy = zero." (2) Build the call vs the jam on the jam's contents, not its percentage: 16% call against a strong-wide range that jams 36% but carries A9s/KJs/AQ; 21% call against a weak-narrow range that jams 18% but keeps its top in the raise *(calibrate)* — a high frequency doesn't make the range weak. (3) The BB's answer to the BTN open, when the opening short-similar stack's range is 22% (a lojack range, not BTN): A8o FLAT (too weak to jam, too strong to bluff), the jam material is medium pairs/AQo/the worst of the suited broadways, the bluff 3-bets are A2o/A3o/K5o, the rest of offsuit Ax folds. Rule: between similar shorts, move toward chipEV but check three things: is there a shorter stack at the table, are the payouts linear, what is inside the jammer's range — if all three come back "yes/strong," two notches tighter than the chipEV chart. The FT short-vs-short application of 12.1-EK-2's "jam texture → call material" line.

### 12.1-EK-9 The expensive jam range is "all or nothing" — if the players behind pay a tick wide it drops to zero; the cut jam goes to FOLD, not to raise


Root error: assuming the open-jam range is fixed; shoving on "the sim says jam this much" without observing the call discipline of the players behind. Mechanism: when the jam's risk (stack × premium) is large — mid-deep stack, players behind at high premium but covering you — the profit margin is razor thin; ONE player behind calling ONE combo (JJ/AK) wide takes the jam slice to zero *(calibrate)*. When risk/reward is good (short stack, players behind also short, dead money large relative to the stack), the same wide call only thins the jam — roughly by a third *(calibrate)* — the most profitable jams remain. Second arm: hands dropped from the jam do NOT move to the RAISE. If they do, the raise-call/raise-fold gap opens and the players behind crush you with rejams without penalty → those hands go to fold, total folding rises a few points *(calibrate)*. Third arm: against the wide call the jam TEXTURE turns — the bluff-jams (small suited Ax, suited broadways, 55) are erased; the medium value that "gets called by worse" (medium pairs, AT) stays in the jam. So against a wide payer the jam range shrinks while growing value-dense. **Rule:** before an expensive jam, one question — "is there someone behind who pays a tick wide?" Yes, or don't know → move the marginal slice from jam to FOLD (not to raise), keep the value-jam, cut the bluff-jam. The extra fold is cheap; the wrong jam is a bust.

### 12.2-EK The short-medium BB's most profitable FT spot: the cold 4-bet jam window


The reflex "there's an opener, there's a 3-bettor, I'm short → fold" leaves money on the table in this three-way. The window stands on three legs: (1) the opener is a middle stack under ICM pressure — he's already open-jamming his strongest hands against the big stack's 3-bet, so his opening range is **topless**; (2) the 3-bettor is a big stack that covers him, big sizing and polar — value the minority, offsuit trash the majority; (3) you are in the BB short-medium: the dead money is big relative to your stack, but your stack is too long for the 3-bettor to pay off with a bluff on "it's cheap anyway" *(lower threshold: calibrate)*. Your jam makes the 3-bettor ditch his bluffs, and the opener almost never pays — his top already went to the open-jam, and the remaining range folds in the sandwich with the 3-bettor behind him. One notch longer in stack and the window closes: the dead money shrinks, the jam turns into value *(upper threshold: calibrate)*.

Material: suited Ax/Kx (the A/K blocker thins the 3-bettor's few value combos), pairs (the bluffs hold no pairs, the opener's call is narrow), strong broadways. If the 3-bettor is small-sizing/linear, or you're very short, there is no window.

**Scope:** 22.8-EK's "jam-or-fold vs the small ICM 3-bet, QQ+" line is the answer of the covered OPENER; here you are in the third seat and the 3-bet is BIG-polar — don't let the "I saw a 3-bet → tight" reflex kill this window. It is a deviation from equilibrium: apply it when the observed dynamic is there (the big stack seen 3-betting the same middle stack over and over AND the middle stack seen open-jamming his premiums — his raise range topless); if the second observation is missing, the opener may still be raising QQ+/AK and will pay off the cold jam → cut the material down to suited Ax/Kx blockers, drop the small/medium pairs and the broadways (QQ+/AK are value anyway, the jam stays).

### 12.2-EK-2 The BB's 3-bet book under ICM in two seats: when covering, bluffs from the offsuit bottom; when covered, value is KK+, premiums FLAT


The middle stack mostly answers a 3-bet with jam/fold or flat; the small 4-bet is rare — that fact shapes both seats.

**The COVERING BB (deep, opener a middle stack):** the value top is narrow (around KK+/AK *(calibrate)*), the bluffs come from the very BOTTOM offsuit. Two reasons: suited hands more than fully realize their equity against the covered opponent (21.3-EK-2) → more valuable in the call book, not spent on bluffs; the villain's raise-fold range is mostly suited Ax/Kx → a small offsuit hand that doesn't block it is a good bluff. The A-blocker is borderline: the A cuts both the fold range (Axs) and the jam range (AA/AK), net effect a wash — the "I hold an A → bluff" reflex is not automatic here.

**The COVERED middle BB (30-50bb, opener the leader):** there is no inducing — a 3-bet with TT-QQ/AQ buys a stack-off against better than itself when the jam comes. Value is effectively KK+ *(calibrate)* (AK borderline, mostly a flat); QQ-TT/AQ FLAT — not 22.8-EK's "no flatting with marginals," but the PREMIUM flat that protects the range. The bluff slice is nearly closed *(single digits: calibrate)*. Bands *(thresholds: calibrate)*: above 25bb, little rejamming; at 20-25bb high pairs + AK/AQs go to the jam — suited Ax/Kx and small pairs are BORDERLINE, the ICM premium against the leader-opener drops most of them; below ~15bb suited Kx drops too — the covered short stack's risk premium pushes the rejam threshold up. Even if the opener opens very wide, the value slice mostly stays fixed (QQ/AK can re-enter at the margin); what really changes is the bluff slice.

### 12.2-EK-3 Under ICM the small-medium pair (66-88, 99 borderline) dies in the rejam / 3-bet-fold book — the Ax blocker passes it


"88/99 is a good hand, I shove / I 3-bet" — that reflex picks the most expensive material under ICM. In a rejam at the opener, the small-medium pair folds out no good hand (the top pays anyway) and gets no bad ones to pay: when called it's crushed by overpairs and flips against Ax with overcards; all it folds out are blocker hands of the KQ/AJs type. In the same spot, A4o/A9o: live equity against overpairs + blocks the opener's calling top (AA/AK/AQ) and unblocks his folding slice → more folds; in high-ICM-pressure, shallow spots the Ax blocker's rejam EV mostly passes the small-medium pair's *(calibrate)*. 3-bet-fold likewise: the target is folding out the villain's raise-fold top (ATs/KQs); the pair's postflop value largely melts in a bloated 3-bet pot and on overcard boards. Rule: under ICM the small-medium pair lives in the CALL book (sees a flop, can fold to an overcard), dies in the rejam/3-bet-fold book; that book's material is Ax blockers + the highest pairs (TT and up on that side — TT stays as rejam material). Exception *(borderline)*: if the opener's range is stuffed with small pairs/suited connectors, the medium pair turns into a value-rejam. No contradiction: in 12.2-EK pairs are cold 4-bet jam material because the dead money in the pot (open + 3-bet) is larger, the gain per fold grows, and the polar 3-bet range's bluff share is high relative to the opener's fold slice; the first-in open-jam is a separate book (12.2-EK-4); the 22.11 escape hatch's "AK-type" raise-fold slice does not cover the medium pair.

### 12.2-EK-4 The squeezed middle stack's tree is RAISE-ONLY — the limp is a range-widening tool, banned in tight mode


The middle stack that wants to play tight under ICM escapes into the limp — wrong. The limp exists to WIDEN a range (it gets chipEV-profitable weak hands in cheaply); tight mode is raise-only, because the raise carries fold equity, while the limp opens an iso to the aggressive big stack behind + the postflop capped-range penalty. If there's a short BB, the limp is banned a second time: the short slams a wide jam on the capped limp, you fold — and on top of that you've handed him a free flop. The tree is three slices: (1) raise-call only JJ/QQ+ *(calibrate)*; (2) OPEN-JAM (~15-25bb effective; at 30bb+ this slice turns into raise-fold/fold) = strong-but-not-nut hands you do NOT want to have to fold to a rejam (AQ/AJ/AT, 88-TT types *(calibrate)*) — raise and the aggressive SB rejams, you fold, equity wasted; end it with the jam; (3) raise-fold nearly none. VPIP roughly halves *(calibrate)*; the remaining EV comes from the collision effect (22.11). Sizing: the raise slice is SMALL (min-raise ~2x *(calibrate)*) — when the slice consists of nothing but JJ/QQ+, asking for folds is the jam slice's job, not the raise slice's; premium hands invite the aggressive SB/BB's rejam. The chip leader does the reverse — iso the middle stack's limp with suited connectors (hands that don't get blown off by a limp-jam), not with offsuit trash; 28.3's bounty limp is a separate context. 5.1-EK's BvB limp line is for chipEV; with a covering aggressive BB + ICM that slice closes.

### 12.2-EK-5 If the BB is ≤5bb the open size is 2.2-2.5x, NOT a min-raise — "I'm committed anyway" is wrong


"A min-raise is enough, I'll pay off his jam anyway" zeroes the fold equity: a ≤5bb BB pays off a min-raise with nearly any hand on price alone (the other face of 30.6). Move the size up to 2.2-2.5x and a slice of the BB's range *(roughly a fifth — calibrate)* starts folding; the SB also defends tighter (the BB's reopen threat). Grade it by position: late position toward the top of the band, early position near the bottom *(ends: calibrate)* — the more players behind who can jam, the higher the big size's cost of "catching a jam." The range NARROWS a tick along with the size (ICM narrows it already): narrow range + big size; not wide range + big size. 3x and above is borderline — the field over-folds to 3x, and folds just as much as needed to 2.5x; a psychological threshold, calibrate. Rule: if the BB is in the "will pay with anything" band, grow the size, narrow the range, no limp (12.2-EK-4). B3's open-sizing tables don't change; this line is only the short-BB exception.

### 12.2-EK-6 Against the linear small 3-bet, the cold 4-bet is NON-ALL-IN — and 4-bet-fold with medium pairs/AQ *(borderline)*


Root error: "I got 3-bet, with a medium pair it's jam or fold." Mid phase, a short BTN small-3-bets the opener; you're big in the SB but the BB covers you. The short's small 3-bet carries no fold equity against the opener → his range is linear and strong, no suited-connector bluffs. Against it, your continue range must be even more linear (the top tenth) and non-all-in: the short's answer to the 4-bet is either a call with medium hands or a polar jam — a jam stronger than his heads-up rejam (big pairs + AK + suited Ax). Medium pairs and AQ, which in chipEV never fold to that jam, become 4-bet-folds here; only the highest pairs stack off. Rule: against the small-linear 3-bettor, make the 4-bet a size that "buys a fold option," pay off with the very top, write medium pairs/AQ into 4-bet-fold — the mirror of 12.2-EK's cold-jam window against the polar-big 3-bettor: there you jam because the 3-bet is bluff-heavy; here you raise + fold because the 3-bet is linear. *(Borderline — wants solver verification; if the covering BB behind goes away, the 4-bet-fold slice narrows.)*

### 12.2-EK-7 Against the polar opener (his jam is the middle, his raise top+trash) no pair rejam — wide flat + plenty of 3-bet bluffs; even while covered, K-high calls the any-two JAM


Root error: the premium-laden short/middle stack min-raised, "88 is a good hand → shove." Mechanism: the high-premium short/middle opener open-JAMS his medium hands (medium pairs, suited Ax, suited broadways); his raise range stays two-ended — the top (99+/AQ+) and raise-fold trash (K4s/Q5s/J3o types). Against that range a pair rejam dies both ways: the top slice calls (you're either crushed or a big favorite — no hand to deny), the bottom slice folds anyway; a small pair on top of that BLOCKS the trash (holding 22 reduces 72o/62o, the top's share grows). The correct tree: (1) jam nearly zero — neither pairs nor suited Kx; (2) wide FLAT (K7s/Q8s/J8s — cheap, the trash folds postflop); (3) 3-bet bluffs WIDER THAN NORMAL (K4/K5/Q2/J2 types at full frequency — *calibrate*): the premium gap is in your favor, the villain folds his raise-fold trash to the non-all-in 3-bet too; the value slice is TT+/AQ 3-bet-call. The counter-hand: when the same opener JAMS any-two, even while being COVERED the call is wide — pot odds (~43%) + premium (~15%) = ~58%; against K-high suited any-two ~60% → KTs/KJo/A7o/66 call *(calibrate)*. "He covers me → tighten hard" is range-blind; the threshold is compared against equity vs the range. On the FT bubble, against the same polar SB raise, 44-TT is a CALL in the BB (the jam only folds out what it dominates and is crushed when called — the call's EV is clearly above the jam's); the jam material is low pairs + offsuit Ax + J8/T9 (folds out his K8/Q8 raises). 12.2-EK-3's "the medium pair dies in the rejam" has a different mechanism (it folds out no good hand); the engine here is the opener's polarity + trash-blocking, and the answer is different too: flat + overbluff.

### 12.2-EK-8 Whether suited Ax or suited broadway/pair gets jammed — the width of the villain's 3-bet-CALL range chooses; and the "one pip" sensitivity


Root error: the rote "I hold an A → blocker → jam" (or its reverse, "A5s is always a bluff-jam"). Mechanism: the rejam/4-bet-jam's profit has two parts — equity when called + blocking the villain's CALL range. If the villain's 3-bet-call range is WIDE (99/TT/AQ included — medium premium, a covering 3-bettor), the A blocker blocks little value and lots of bluffs (the bluffs are already Ax); when called, A5s is dominated by everything while JTs/QTs flips against TT/AQ → jam the suited broadways + pairs, click/flat the suited Ax. When the villain's call range is NARROW (KK+/AK; the stone-bubble spot where the CO folds even JJ/TT/AQ), the table turns: A5s blocks AA/AK and is live against KK; AJo, meanwhile, blocks the fold range (AJ/AT/KQ) and never flips when called → suited Ax pure jam, AJo/KQo/suited Kx FOLD. Sensitivity: in the narrow-call spot, if the villain pays one pip wider (adds TT/JJ/AQs — *calibrate*) the suited-Ax bluff-jam slice closes ENTIRELY, the jam drops to value only (JJ+). The population pays wider than theory in narrow-call spots → against humans play the linear value-jam, not the Ax-blocker jam; the Ax-blocker jam only once you've seen the villain's fold discipline. The same rule at the shallowest (~8bb): you'll get called anyway → linear jam (A9s included), blocker weight zero. Two pairs don't flip equally: TT is a few points better than 77 against AK; in a premium-laden spot that gap is the jam/fold line *(calibrate)*.

### 12.2-EK-9 The covering 3-bettor — size SMALL, bluff material = "the best hands that aren't profitable flats" + offsuit Ax; good flats are not spent on 3-bets


Root error: sizing up the 3-bet in ICM ("pressure → big size") and 3-bet-folding AQ/AJ/KQ. Mechanism: the premium gap runs in your favor (villain's premium is 2–3x yours) → villain folds to a small 3-bet at the same rate; a big size only grows your own risk. The ICM calculator clearly prefers a ~2x–2.4x size on top of the min-raise (~6bb) over 8–10bb *(calibrate)*. Material: (a) offsuit Ax (A2o–A7o) at full frequency — blocks value, low playability, nothing wasted by not flatting it; (b) "just BELOW the profitable flat" — KTo/QTo/JTo, K5s/J7s/Q7s: the flat doesn't profit, in the 3-bet they carry blocker + fold equity; (c) PROFITABLE flats of the AQ/AJ/KQ/K7s/Q8s type are NOT spent on 3-bets — flatting keeps the range they dominate in the pot. The opposite seat (the covered opener, medium premium ~10%): no over-folding to the small 3-bet — the jam slice is wide (suited/offsuit Ax, pairs), and the price + low premium opens up the call as well (J9o/K9o/T8o borderline — *calibrate*); suited Ax FLATS IP (dominates villain's Ax bluffs, has outs against KK) — not a jam. Two deep stacks with mutual double-digit premiums: 3-bet value KK+/AK, QQ FLATS both the 3-bet and the 4-bet, 4-bet AA/KK + suited Ax bluff; AA is not a jam/trap but a CLICK — pull KK into the stack-off, combinatorially that's what he holds most.

### 12.2-EK-10 The bluff kicker — the high card should block villain's VALUE, the low card should not block his FOLD range; but do block the low card villain CONTINUES with


Root error: "A5o is the better hand, so it's a better bluff than A2o." Mechanism: the EV of a bluff-jam/3-bet comes from villain's FOLD frequency; the second card must NOT BLOCK the hands villain will fold. Against a limper A2o > A5o: villain already limp-folds 72o/62o, the 2 doesn't block those; the 5, on the other hand, blocks 75s/65s (which he would limp-call) — the difference is small, the direction is clear *(calibrate)*. For the same reason the 3-bet bluff against an opener is A3o/A4o/K4o; the AT/QT 3-bet bluff is a separate lane — because it folds out AJ/QJ (the folding broadways). The reverse: if villain raise-JAMS his low connectors (a 12bb opener raise→jams 87s/76s, raise-folds 54s), then A8/A7 > A5/A4: the 8/7 blocks his continue range, the 5 only touches his raise-fold. Rule: in a 3-bet/jam bluff, pick the second card from villain's CONTINUE range, not his fold range — in both directions the question is "which cards does he continue with?". The ICM-preflop version of B1's bluff selection and B32's "unblock his folds" lines.

### 12.2-EK-11 In ICM, a SMALL 4-bet ("click") instead of the jam — even at 15bb; it induces the bluff-jam and buys a fold against two jams


Root error: reflex all-in with a strong hand after getting 3-bet — "I'm committed anyway." Mechanism: villain's ICM 3-bet is polar (narrow value + many offsuit bluffs). If you jam, value pays and the bluffs fold — the jam's only gain is the bluff's fold. Against a small 4-bet, value still pays BUT the bluff slice, where it would have folded to a jam, turns into a 5-bet-bluff-jam (K7o/A3o types) — money a jam could never extract. A polar 3-bet range almost never calls the click, so the click carries no "sees a cheap flop" risk. The second gain is multiway: click with QQ → if one jam comes, call; if two jams come (one shoves, the other re-shoves), fold — the ranges pile so heavily onto AA/KK that even QQ/AK gets thrown away; had you jammed, that option didn't exist (the 3-bet node of 22.11's escape hatch). Material: value QQ+/AK, bluff-click offsuit Ax (the A-highs you raised); "size close to half the stack" *(calibrate)*. Condition: villain must be FINDING 5-bet bluff-jams — against one who doesn't (either folds or shoves only with value) the click induces nothing; jam yourself so the occasional AQs call doesn't get away. Rule: in ICM it's not "jam or fold" but "click or fold" — jam only against a passive villain and at pot-committed shallowness. 12.2-EK-2 is the BB's 3-bet playbook; this line is the OPENER's answer to a 3-bet; 22.8-EK's "jam-or-fold" line is for the COVERED short — at similar stacks the tree turns to the click.

### 12.2-EK-12 Against a narrow-polar opener, jam is ZERO — 3-bet-or-call; even TT/AJo is 3-bet-CALL, because the jam and the 3-bet produce the same continue range


Root error: "I'm 13bb, how do I not jam AK?" — the short-stack reflex. Mechanism: if the opener opens very tight (early position, many covering stacks, around 10% *(calibrate)*) and the MIDDLE of his range has already gone to open-jam, the remaining raise range is "the top + raise-fold trash". There is no hand a jam folds out of this range: the top pays either way, the trash folds to a small 3-bet too. The jam's only effect is to chase away from the table the hands that would 4-bet-bluff-jam over a 3-bet (AQ/A5s types) — the money you dominate. That's why neither the short BB nor the short in position plays jams: value 3-bet (JJ+/AK) + 3-bet-CALL TT/99/AJo (jamming folds out nothing; if you 3-bet to call, the opener's trash folds, and if a jam comes back you meet the top — but you were going to meet it anyway) + high-card bluffs (KQs/KJs/KQo — the opener's raise-fold comes from Ax; don't block Ax). For the same reason a very short BB (8–9bb) CALLS the polar min-raise with medium pairs (55–JJ), doesn't jam: the pair dominates all the bluff-raises, and a jam folds them out. Rule: "the seat is BTN but the range is 10%" — play the range, not the position: no jam against the narrow-polar opener, click + call; jam only against a linear-wide range (equity denial). Once the range linearizes (medium hands in the raise), it's the exact opposite: medium pairs and suited broadways go back to the jam, to fold out the wide opener's entire middle.

### 12.2-EK-13 Premium 20%+ and similar big stacks: 3-bet = AA only (KK FLATS), giant size, bluff offsuit Ax — AK is an open-JAM even at 36bb


Root error: the deep-FT chipEV rote of "KK/QQ 3-bet-value, AK 3-bet-call". Mechanism: against a similar big stack that covers you, with the premium near its ceiling (20%+ *(calibrate)*), the only hand you can 3-bet-call is AA — even KK doesn't want the 70% stack-off against A5s, so KK FLATS; QQ/JJ flat. With value down to a single hand, the size goes GIANT (roughly a third of the stack *(calibrate)*): it either folds villain out or commits him, no in-between. Bluff: the offsuit Ax you can't 3-bet-call (A-blocker + cheap when it folds). AK's tree is closed: its flat is weak, its 3-bet-call a flip → open-JAM even at 36bb; a pinch of A2s/A3s as cover alongside the jam. The opener's answer is symmetric: to the giant polar 3-bet, KQs/99 FOLD, ATo/AJo/A9s CALL (dominates the Ax bluffs, blocks AA), 4-bet-jam suited Ax (AA blocker + overcard), KK/QQ doesn't jam (either a fold or AA — it calls the click). Against the jam, the JJ/QQ call is borderline break-even — for the leader, taking a flip and ending up short is negative in future-EV, mostly a fold. Rule: with similar big stacks and the premium at its ceiling, it's "flat or AA": the 3-bet's name is AA, the jam's name is AK, the rest flats/folds; size giant. 12.2-EK-2's "value KK+, AK borderline flat" line is the medium-premium (30–50bb, covered) version; once the premium passes 20%, KK too drops to a flat and AK moves from flat to jam.

### 12.2-EK-14 3-bet SIZE by the premium relationship — giant to the leader, medium to an equal, minimum to a short you cover; as the size shrinks, value widens and the bluff turns to high cards


Root error: a single 3-bet size ("3x, everywhere"). Mechanism: the size prices the DIFFERENCE between villain's premium against you and your premium against him. (1) Against a leader who covers you (your premium is higher than his): value squeezed down to AA, bluff offsuit Ax → giant size (around a third of the stack *(calibrate)*) — a small size hands him a cheap call + position, and you can't realize. (2) Against a middle stack with the premium close/in your favor: medium size (roughly a quarter of the stack *(calibrate)*), KK enters the value, multiply the Ax bluffs — villain has to fold to the 3-bet more than you do, so combo-level over-bluffing is legitimate. (3) Against a short opener you cover (his premium is higher than yours): the SMALLEST possible size — his answer is jam-or-fold anyway, the small size buys the fold equity for free and is cheap when it has to fold; value widens all the way to 99+/AQ, the bluff is high-card (AT/KQ/KJ/QJ — blocks the opener's 4-bet-jam pairs, his raise-fold comes from Ax), the flat range NARROWS (at low SPR the strong hands go to 3-bet-call, so the weak flats are left unprotected). Rule: 3-bet size = the sign of the premium difference: premium against you → size up and shrink value to AA; premium in your favor → size down and widen value to 99/AQ. This is exactly the preflop face of 21.3-EK-2's river size lever.

### 12.2-EK-15 4-bet-jam material is locked to the 3-bettor's CALL range — narrow (AA/KK/AK) → suited Ax; wide (99/TT/AQ) → suited broadway, suited Ax "bad"


Root error: the habit of "4-bet-jam with A4s in every spot." Mechanism: a 4-bet-jam's EV comes from two things — what it folds out, and what happens when it doesn't get the fold. The 3-bettor's call range NARROW and top-heavy (AA/KK/AK, deep and high premium): the A-blocker erases a large part of that range (AA + AK), and when called there's an overcard → suited Ax jams; suited broadway, by contrast, gets crushed by KK/AK. The 3-bettor's call range WIDE and linear (99/TT/JJ/AQ — shallow, premium in your favor, a 3-bet against a short opener): the A-blocker now blocks little value but most of villain's bluffs are Ax → suited Ax blocks his FOLDS and, when called, is dominated even against 99; KJs/KQs, on the other hand, blocks KK/AK, unblocks his Ax bluffs, and flips against the pairs → suited broadway jams; AJs folds but KJs shoves *(calibrate)*. Mirror: 3-bet BLUFF selection hangs on the same lock — deep, suited Ax (the AA/AK it blocks outweighs); shallow, offsuit broadway/AT (blocks the opener's 4-bet-jam pairs, cheap when it folds); middling suited Ax (A7–A9s) is bad in both worlds: it blocks too many raise-folds. Rule: before jamming, name villain's CALL range: if it's "AA/KK/AK", hold an A; if it's "99/AQ", hold a K/Q. 12.2-EK-2's "20–25bb suited Ax/Kx jam" band is the narrow-call case; in the wide-call case the Ax drops out.

### 12.2-EK-16 If the pair's RANK blocks villain's raise-folds, the jam gets worse — 99 < 66; "the 9 blocks the calls, the 8 the folds" → K9s jams, K8s calls


Root error: pricing the jam only by equity against the range that calls. Mechanism: the jam's hidden variable is "how many combos of villain's FOLDS do I hold". If the wide opener's raise-fold slice is built from mid-high offsuits like A9o/K9o/Q9o/J9o, then 99 blocks most of those folds → the jam's fold equity melts, 99 is WORSE than 66 as a jam; 44/55 jams while 66/77 folds, because the opener raise-folds A6o/A7o but jams A4o/A5o *(calibrate — combo-based, spot-specific)*. The same law flips for Kx: if the opener raise-CALLS 9x (K9/Q9/J9 pays) but raise-FOLDS 8x (K8o folds), then the 9 blocks the call range (good) and the 8 blocks the folds (bad) → K9s JAMS, K8s CALLS — the two look like "the same hand"; they aren't. Third face: the short BB's jam over a wide big-stack open — JTo/T9s/Q4o jam (folds out dominators like KT/QT/A9), KTo/K9s CALL (the opener folds the Kx you dominate and pays only with the better ones); K2s jams, K9s doesn't — "the WORSE Kx jams, the better one sees a flop." Rule: ask of every card in the jam candidate: does it block villain's call (good) or his fold (bad)? Rank by those two lists, not by the hand. The exact combos can't be worked out at the table; two rotes suffice: "the medium pair blocks the raise-folds" and "bad Kx jams, good Kx calls."

### 12.2-EK-17 If the opener plays "top + pure trash" (middle in the jam), blocker logic INVERTS — the 3-bet bluff is 52o, A2o's deuce becomes a blocker, the trap shuts down


Root error: picking the 3-bet bluff from A/K blockers in every spot, setting the trap in every spot. Mechanism: when a covering big stack opens into short/locked blinds, his range is three parts — the top raise-calls, the middle OPEN-JAMS, the bottom (62o/72o/T3o) raise-folds. Against this range: (1) a K/Q/J blocker blocks only the raise-FOLDS (the top is AA/KK, the trash without high cards) → the 3-bet bluff comes from low cards that UNBLOCK the fold range, like 52o/42o/63o; value is Ax only (blocks AA); pairs 66–KK don't 3-bet, they FLAT — a 3-bet into an "AA or fold" range is pointless, while the flat crushes the trash on the flop, and the defense is very wide (down to 53o *(calibrate)*). (2) A2o's rote of "the 2 doesn't block folds" ends: if the opener raise-folds his deuces, the 2 blocks exactly those folds → A2o folds, A7s/A8s 3-bets (better kicker when called); if the opener opens 60%+ and raises every deuce, the 2's share drops and A2o goes back to 3-betting *(calibrate)*. (3) Short-vs-short very shallow (8–9bb), against the polar min-raise every Ax AND every Kx jams — the K doesn't block the bluffs, the opener jams his own Kx; even Q2o/J2o jam because the Q/J block raise-folds and are an overcard when called *(calibrate)*. (4) The trap: the polar raise range does NOT stack off with a pair (even K5o that catches top pair doesn't pay) → the AA/KK trap's value drops, end it with a 3-bet; against a linear range (KTs/JTs in the raise) the trap comes back. General law: as the premium rises, trap the top of the range less. Rule: read villain's raise-fold slice — if it's high-card, A/K blocker bluffs; if it's trash, low-card unblock bluffs; if he jams his medium hands, no 3-bet with pairs — flat.

### 12.2-EK-18 The covering big stack IP against a short opener — wide FLAT (over-realization + squeeze protection); the flip side: against a capped flat range the deep SB squeeze-jams size-independent


Root error: carrying the "no flatting in ICM" rule (22.8-EK — it is for the COVERED player) over to the COVERING seat; or 3-betting the short as the leader. Mechanism: the short opener's premium against you is high, yours ~0 → on the flop he's condemned to check-fold, you realize with a bet; and no squeeze comes either — the players behind carry a premium against the FLATTER (you), the opener is tight → you'd call a small squeeze with a strong range, and a big squeeze commits them against the short → everyone goes quiet. Result: even against a big 3x size, suited connectors, suited 9x, all pairs, offsuit broadways flat *(calibrate)*; to the 3-bet only the trash that can't flat (not AT/KJ/QTo — those make money in the flat — the QTo/KTo/A9o class). Condition: being a genuinely RUNAWAY leader (several times villain's stack); a 50bb "big" stack can't find half of this flat *(calibrate)*. SB mirror: an SB who isn't the leader but carries a high premium FLATS TT/JJ/AQs/AJs against a wide-weak-polar BTN open — it can't stack off, but the range is weak and the flat is protected from the squeeze (the SB version of 12.2-EK-2's premium flat). The flip side (you're the opener, a short behind flatted your open): if the flat range is CAPPED (QQ+/AK in the 3-bet), the deep SB behind squeeze-JAMS "against two dead BBs" — whatever the size (even a 60bb jam), because the flatter never pays and the opener's call range is tiny *(calibrate)*; and the shortest BB over-calls that jam wide (77/AJs), because no one behind is going to pay. Rule: against a short opener the leader FLATS IP, doesn't 3-bet; if the flat has no cover (a slice of premiums), you hand the deep SB a free squeeze-jam — keep the cover or trim the flat.

### 12.2-EK-19 The BB's correct answer to a polar short opener is zero jams; if the BB jams anyway, the opener plays "water on the fire" — tighten, put the medium pair on induction


Root error: the covering BB's reflex 3-bet-jam (small pairs, Ax, suited Kx) over a short/middle opener's open — to "scare" the covered player. Mechanism: the high-premium covered opener's range is structurally polar — the middle slice (small pairs, suited broadway, medium Ax) has already gone to open-jam; what remains in the raise is top + bottom. A jam has no fold equity against a polar range (the top pays, the bottom folds anyway) → the BB's theoretical answer is the non-all-in 3-bet (narrow value, bottom offsuit bluffs — 12.2-EK-2) + a very wide call; jam nearly zero. The population doesn't do this; it jams. The opener's correct adjustment is not aggression-for-aggression but tightening: (1) tighten the played range one notch — the bottom of suited Ax, suited 9x, Q7s-type hands can't realize their equity; (2) move the medium pairs (88-TT) from open-jam to RAISE-CALL — if the BB's jam is weighted toward small pairs/A5, the medium pair takes the call as the dominator; jamming wastes that EV; (3) once the medium pairs leave the jam, the bluff-jams leave too (A3s/A4s/ATo) — no value left to protect, fold. If on top of that the BB starts calling wide too (K6s, T8o), the tightening hardens: the range shrinks by roughly a third *(calibrate)*, raise-call widens to medium pairs+/ATs, raise-fold nearly disappears — no fold equity, getting jammed on is frequent. **Rule:** as the covered opener, if villain jams too much or pays too much, your fold equity has melted → fewer hands, more induction, zero bluff-jams. Exception *(borderline)*: if the BB's jam has opened up to suited Kx/broadway, a KQs-type hand itself goes back to jamming — if it doesn't jam, it gets jammed on.

### 12.4-EK Executing the "rob" — the covering big stack's three levers — and read the payout ladder for the OPPONENT


12.4's "rob them — widen opens + 3-bet pressure" cell breaks down into three concrete arms *(calibrate all of them)*:

1. **Size.** Against a short BB (≲12bb), the size is chosen by whether the BB has a CALL vs the min-raise: against a short who calls with every hand for the price and commits on the flop, not a min-raise but 2.5–3x — the jam (rejam) rate is size-independent, and the big size turns the cheap calls the min-raise invites into folds (12.2-EK-5); against a short playing pure jam-or-fold (no call at all) the size stays small — a big size doesn't add folds, it only loses extra when you get jammed on. Against ICM-locked MIDDLE stacks too, pressure is sold with frequency, not size (21.7-EK).
2. **Material = the answer of the players behind.** If the players behind are jam-or-fold, blocker offsuits (K6o/Q8o types — no flop will be played, the blocker buys fold equity); if there's a deep caller behind, suited/playable (K5s > K9o).
3. **The wide flat freezes the table.** The covering deep stack's speculative flat from the CO (53s types) makes the BTN/SB squeeze expensive through their own risk premium → mostly an IP flop + a license for three streets of pressure on the BB. Against the second stack's open, 3-bet value KK+ (QQ/JJ flat — his 4-bet-jam is suited Ax/AK/KK+); the range is protected by suited Ax's 5-bet-jam. Postflop tiered: flop small, turn half, river jam.

**Target selection:** read 12.3's ladder not for yourself but for the OPPONENT — the step's size relative to his bankroll and his stack tells you who will turn to stone (16.6's fifth step); the stone-frozen locked middle stack is the most profitable target.

### 12.4-EK-2 The overfold's second wave — when villain folds one notch too much to the jam, it's not just the jam that grows: the LIMP dies


Root error: stopping at "the BB over-folds to the jam → I'll shove a bit wider." The covering SB's ICM limp range exists for the medium suited hands that want to be neither a raise nor a jam (its jam gets dominated, its raise eats a jam). Once the BB starts folding one notch too much to the jam *(calibrate)*, exactly those medium hands turn into profitable jams — the limp's reason to exist disappears; the vacated raise slice fills with value and trash, the strategy collapses to "raise or jam", limp near zero. One more click of overfold and the fold too drops to zero. One exception remains: high-card hands that dominate villain's call range (medium Kx/Qx types) don't jam even against the overfold — they raise; a jam leaves only better hands in the call and folds out the ones they dominate; the profit comes from the call of the hands you dominate. Rule: when you see the BB's overfold to the jam, change three things together — bring the medium suiteds into the jam range, shut down the limp, widen the raise; keep the dominating high-card hands in the raise. A single-notch read changes two-thirds of the strategy; failing to see it is failing to turn 22.1-EK's "the population tightens up" observation into money.

### 12.4-EK-3 Open/jam material — blocker or playability: players behind short/postflop rare → blocker; deep/defending wide → suited


Root error: a single "opening range" rote (always toss A5o and keep T9s — or the reverse). Mechanism: in ICM the range's shape depends on WHERE the hand ends. Players behind short and premium-laden (condemned to jam/fold) → the hand rarely sees a flop, it ends in walk/jam/fold → blocker-heavy: A5o/A7o/K4o open, T9s/J9s/Q8s fold (the blocker thins both villain's value and bluff jams). Players behind deep with the BB's premium close to yours (defends wide, frequent flops) → playable suited: J5s/Q4s/Q3s open, naked blocker offsuits drop. The same principle in jam material: when the covering leader jams into high-premium blinds, "one high + one low SUITED" (J5s/Q6s/K4s) jams — it blocks the call range (99+/ATs), and when called has an overcard + flush equity; T9s/98s, by contrast, RAISES — the BB pays with hands you dominate. And the BB's call vs the jam: ATs > AQo — the offsuit combos are the same hand; the suit's extra equity against pairs/Q-x is worth more than the Q's extra domination *(calibrate)*. In the short BB (~8bb) the reverse: you will get called → LINEAR jam (A9s included), call ~0 (even K8s/87s/KTo fold — at that SPR a dominated hand is condemned to stack off). Rule: "are the players behind jammers or callers?" → jammers: blocker; callers: suited; if you yourself will get called: raw strength. The 12.4 hunting map doesn't change; this line is its "with what" column.

### 12.4-EK-4 A leader who widens his opens but not his CALL vs the jam is rejam heaven — the gap is opened by observation, not by the sim


Root error: "the leader opens five points wider → rejam a bit wider." The sim confirms this but yields a small difference; the real money is elsewhere. Mechanism: the sim, when it widens the open, ALSO widens the call vs the jam (down to QJo/A7o) → the rejam gain is limited, a few points *(calibrate)*. The real population widens the open easily; it doesn't widen the call with A5o — raising is easy, calling is hard. Wide open + narrower-than-sim call = a giant raise-fold slice → the rejam opens up to nearly any-two (roughly two to three times *(calibrate)*); EVERY position behind — the 20bb HJ included — widens its rejam hard. **Rule:** build your rejam width against the leader not from his opening percentage but from the question "have I SEEN a light call?". Opens wide + no light call ever seen → open the rejam range dramatically (suited Ax/Kx, broadway, all pairs); one light call seen → back to the sim, standard. Band: 12.1-EK's license applies — below ~25bb; above it the tree shifts to the non-all-in 3-bet, and the "gap" argument doesn't buy a wide jam. Opposite seat: as the leader, if you're widening your opens, widen your call vs the jam too; otherwise you open the gap yourself and finance the HJ's 27-point *(calibrate)* rejam.

### 12.4-EK-5 Shallow BvB, low premium — the SB folding a few points too much to the small 3-bet explodes the BB's bluff 3-bet; the exploits are hypersensitive, if you're not sure stay away from the extremes


Root error: opening ~30% in the SB and then folding J9o/74s/Q3s to the BB's small (3x) 3-bet on the "ICM, tighten up" reflex. Mechanism: at low premium (the SB's premium against a short BB is single-digit *(calibrate)*) the small 3-bet gives the SB a giant price; the theoretical SB continue is very wide — medium suited/offsuit connectors CALL, Q3s/QTo types jam, the raise-fold slice is small. When the SB's fold rises by two-three points (offsuit 9x + weak suiteds into the fold), the BB's 3-bet bluff slice multiplies — value stays the same, the bluffs go from single digits to forty *(calibrate)*. Meta-lesson: every exploit in this class flips between "too passive ↔ too aggressive" on a one-two point adjustment. If the signal isn't clear, stay close to theory; if it is, profit over loyalty to theory — playing balance while villain makes an open error is also a leak. **Rule:** in shallow BvB at low premium, as the SB keep your fold to the small 3-bet narrow (jam + call, raise-fold a minority — even a connected hand calls in a pair's stead); as the BB, watch the SB's fold to the small 3-bet — once you've seen one fold too many, open the bluff 3-bet hard, don't change the value.

### 12.5-EK A small premium turns the jam into a RAISE — at 10–15bb the jam-or-fold rote turns into a raise/limp/flat texture with premium


Root error: continuing at a short stack on "I was jam-or-fold in chipEV, so I am in ICM too." The jam risks the whole stack and pays the full premium; the min-raise risks two blinds and pays nearly zero premium. At the same time, opponents' call ranges vs the jam tighten with the premium — the jam earns less, the raise loses less. The result is visible even with a two-point premium: the early-position jam share drops, the min-raise share rises markedly *(calibrate)*; big pairs turn to the non-all-in 3-bet even at ~15bb; in BvB a small raise slice gets added to chipEV's limp/jam duo (the top + the weak high-card hands that can't jam go raise-fold); even a few flats open up at 10–12bb against the min-raise — the suited broadway that folds out no good hand but flops well, call instead of jam. The covered side knows this: in this band, the jam range narrowing is NOT the fold range widening — it's a shift to the raise. Rule: ≤15bb + premium ≥ a few points → add the non-all-in raise to the tree; narrow the jam to the "I can't fold it to a raise but I won't find a call for the jam" hands, write the top into raise-call and the weakest high-card hands into raise-fold *(boundaries: calibrate)*. 30.0's "4–12bb open = jam or fold" is the ICM-free baseline; 30.0-EK's covering-player exception opens up here for the covered player too *(mostly)*.

### 12.5-EK-2 Dead money scales jam width — a big ante opens the jam, a small ante/fewer players turns the jam back into a raise


Root error: the same jam range in every structure at the same stack + same premium. Mechanism: the jam's fold-EV is proportional to the pot's dead money; its risk is fixed at your stack. In a hyper/turbo the big ante (~1.25bb dead in the pot) opens the jam to ~29%; the same spot with a normal ante (~0.6bb) drops to ~21% *(calibrate)* — T9o/97s slides from pure jam to fold, A2o from jam to raise. The opposite extreme: on a mini-field bubble, small ante + both blinds premium-free against you → the covering 45bb's jam slice is ZERO, raise only (~40%; calibrate). And 4-handed, only 0.5bb extra in the pot → the price to call the jam worsens, the marginal call (A4o) drops. Rule: scale the jam range by three things — dead money (ante size + player count), villain's premium (high → jam), your own premium (low → jam). If all three are small, no jam — raise. 22.10's full-BB ante note is the blind-geometry face of this; it reads as the "ante column" of 12.5's jam card.

### 12.5-EK-3 RFI texture by the BB's DEFENSE width — if nobody defends, blocker-weighted (A5o opens, JTs folds); vs a wide-defending leader BB, suited-weighted (T8s opens, A8o folds); zero open-jam from EP; the offsuit Ax "U"


Root error: a single RFI chart at the FT; "suited connector is a good hand, I open." The mechanism, in three parts. (1) Texture: if the blinds behind are short/locked, a flop is almost never seen, the game is "raise → jam or fold"; an A/K blocker erases the jam combos of the players behind and reduces their bluffs → A5o/A7o/K9o open, JTs/T9s/Q8s fold. If behind sits a leader BB who covers you and defends wide, flops are seen often → suited/connected hands (T8s/Q8s/98s) open, A8o/QTo/A5o fold. Same seat, two opposite ranges — the decider is the BB's defense width. (2) Open-jam from EP: with the premium in the middle (11–12% *(calibrate)*) and many covering stacks behind, the medium hands (KTs/AQs) can't jam — crushed when called; and if the middle can't jam, the top doesn't jam either (an AQ jam folds out AJ while the pairs pay — the min-raise does the same job and induces bluff-jams) → open-jam 0%, the range narrow and blocker-weighted (suited Ax, offsuit broadway, A9o; no 66/T9s). The open-jam comes back: you're the middle stack, the shorts carry premium, and the leader carries premium against you too ("they can't pay me wide") → 55+/suited broadway/KJo jam. (3) The offsuit Ax "U": AJ+ jams (occasionally gets paid by worse) and A5o- jams (folds out better like A6–A9); A8–ATo does NOT JAM — it doesn't fold out better and doesn't get paid by worse → raise: raise-call against the BB, raise-fold to the SB's jam. Rule: before opening the RFI, look behind: "will I see a flop?" No → blocker, suited connector folds; Yes → suited, medium offsuit Ax folds. The open-jam is licensed by the medium hands being able to jam; in offsuit Ax the middle raises, the extremes jam.

### 12.3-EK Read the Ladder as a CURVE, Not a Step — a Flat Ladder Plays for the Ladder, a Top-Steep Curve Plays a Stack That Reaches the Top for First


12.3 asks about a single jump's ratio to your stack; at the break, take one more step and compare CONSECUTIVE jump ratios. Two typical profiles: a flat ladder where each step grows by roughly 30% of the previous one (mostly online structures), and a curve that moves through the middle in 20–25% steps and then steepens to 45–55% on the last two steps (mostly live structures) *(ratios: read from the lobby, calibrate)*. On a flat ladder, every step is real money — ladder discipline is profitable; on a top-steep curve, for a stack that can genuinely reach the last two steps, sacrificing the small step to take a shot at first is usually correct, because the real money sits in the last two jumps. Two players at the same FT can correctly play two different styles — but the curve is the same for both; what selects the difference is WHERE the stack sits on the curve: on that same top-steep curve, if the next step is still huge relative to your stack, you're short and you still ladder (12.3: wait while someone shorter than you is at the table); the covering leader who reaches the top prices in first place.

**Rule:** At the FT break, ask two questions IN ORDER: (1) is the next jump big relative to my stack (12.3) — if yes, ladder regardless of the curve's shape; (2) if not, where does the curve steepen — if the steepening is at the top and your stack reaches it, price in first place, not the ladder.

### 12.6 The Postflop Money Book at FT Depth — the Top of the Range Shifts to CALL, Raising Is the Middle-Fragile Tier's Job; Scale the Raise to the Pot


B12 gives the preflop book; at 20–40bb FT depth, the postflop book also turns over. At deep stacks, check-raise value is built from the strongest hands; at low SPR, money already goes in over two streets and villain bets thin value all the way to the river → sets/the strongest hands mostly shift into the CALL book (trap), and check-raise material becomes strong-but-fragile (a good top-pair type): the slice one pair-class below you (middle pair, weak pair) turns passive on the turn, and raising is the only way to pry money out of it — hands better than you stack you regardless, that loss is fixed. As villain's bet shrinks (SPR effectively rises), the top of the range goes back to raising *(threshold: calibrate)*. And scale the raise to the POT, not as a multiple of the bet: against the 1bb micro c-bet that's in fashion at ICM tables, a "3x the bet" raise effectively gives away a free price — against a small bet, the raise has to be meaningful in pot terms. Pool mirror: the field plays exactly the opposite — it excitedly raises the top of its range, flats the middle — meaning at low SPR an INCOMING flop raise is value-heavy relative to balance → fold one notch tighter. *(The ICM premium stacks on top of these thresholds separately — B21; this entry gives the book's composition, not its threshold.)*

**Rule:** At short depth, the strongest hand doesn't have to raise — the money already goes in; raising is the job of fragile value prying money from the tier below it, and its sizing is set relative to the pot, not the bet.

## Chapter 13 — Multiway Pot Doctrine

*★ v5. B7 defines the root error as "misclassification in a bloated OR MULTIWAY pot" but the body had no multiway postflop rule. This chapter pays that debt. The Main (€5.3K) is rec-heavy: wide opens + lots of cold-calls = most pots are multiway. The texture you'll spend the longest time in across the 4 events.*

### 13.0 Core

> **Every additional player MULTIPLIES the price of a bluff, RAISES the bar for value, and INCREASES the value of the nuts.**

The reason is compound probability: as the number of opponents grows, the chance that someone has two pair+/a set multiplies. That's why a multiway bloated pot makes one pair an even clearer bluff-catcher than a HU bloated pot.

### 13.1 HU → 3+ way transition

| Situation | Heads-up | 3+ way |
|---|---|---|
| **C-bet frequency** | High | Collapses — only strong value + real nut-draws |
| **Top pair** | Value | Drops a class → check / pot control |
| **Overpair** | Big-pot candidate | Two streets + control; count the SPR (B11) |
| **Bluff** | Three criteria (B1) | Almost none — only nut-blocker semi-bluffs |
| **Nut FD** | Bet | Stays a bet (price is good, gets paid big when it completes) |
| **Non-nut FD / gutshot** | Depends | Reverts to check / dies |

### 13.2 Who am I afraid of

Not all the preflop callers — **the tightest range STILL STANDING on the flop.** A raise coming after someone's bet and a call in between = almost always the nuts; no continuing with one pair.

### 13.3 Bluffing multiway — the fourth criterion

B1's three criteria (blocker/connectivity/board ownership) were built for a single opponent. Multiway, the fourth variable: **number of opponents = number of doors the bluff must pass through.** Even a single station kills the bluff.

| Number of opponents | Bluff mode |
|---|---|
| **HU** | Three criteria (B1) |
| **3-way** | Only nut-blocker semi-bluffs |
| **4+ way** | None |

Added to B1.4's "who not to bluff" list: **Multiway pots (no matter who).**

### 13.4 Multiway PLO

Live PLO's default state is multiway (limp/multi-call norm). The rule "the second-best hand is expensive" becomes "the second-best hand loses your stack" multiway. A non-nut flush draw is a payoff machine multiway; a nut-ended wrap gains value (gets paid many ways when it completes). Play only for the NUTS. (Details in B15.)

**B0 c.14:** *Multiway, one pair is one class below what it is HU.*

*Root-error link: direct reinforcement. Fills the unwritten half of B7's heading; touches no existing range.*

---

### 13.0-EK When Multiway Money Piles In, the Bar Drops to Nuts + Redraw — the Flop Mostly Sees a Single Bet


On a board everyone connects with often, the multiway norm is this: the flop mostly sees a single full-size bet go in, and every aggressive action stacked on top of that sharply narrows the continuing range. Once two aggressions stack on top of each other (raise + jam, or raise + call + pressure), the bar to continue jumps from "strong hand" to "nuts + redraw": even the second nuts without a redraw is a borderline fold, dropping into the same raise-then-fold class as an overpair or a naked top two. This is the execution form of 13.0's "the value of the nuts increases" sentence — what gains value isn't you a class below, it's the actual nuts plus the redraw attached to it.

**Rule:** Multiway, after the second aggression the stack-off question isn't "how many points is my hand" but "is it the nuts, does it have a redraw" — if both answers are no, it doesn't matter which-numbered nuts it is.

### 13.1-EK In Multiway, Your SEAT Grants the License to Aggress — Not Your Hand


With the same hand, two opposite decisions come from a single axis: are you closing the action? Facing a bet (or even a bet+raise) with a player still to act behind you, even your strongest hands mostly just call — you see the turn cheap, carry the value of your position/information, and don't hand information to the player behind by splitting your range; even a continuing range heavy with two pair and open-enders reverts entirely to calling at this node. The player who closes the action does the exact opposite: he raises his best hands immediately, because folding out even one player meaningfully grows his equity, and there's little of the "wants to see the turn cheap" marginal hand left. Boundary case: on a highly connected board everyone hits often, even the nuts drops to roughly 70% equity, a set to 60% *(calibrate)* — there, even the player closing the action turns passive, waiting for the information the next card will bring.

**Rule:** Before reaching for a raise multiway, count your seat: if you're closing the action, play the top of your range fast; if there's a player still behind you, even your strongest hand mostly calls — the exception is a board so hyper-connected that even the nuts erodes.

### 13.1-EK-2 The Trap's Condition Is "the Remaining Opponent Is the Aggressor Who Will Bet in Your Place" — a Call in Between Ends the Trap


If you're left HEADS-UP against the aggressor with the weakest range, pure slowplay of the top of your range dominates: the aggressor stacks the money into your weak-looking range himself on the turn/river, and by raising you'd only chase off his bluffs. But the moment a caller gets in between, this math breaks — the aggressor can no longer bet thin value, the trap's payoff drops — and that same two pair/good top pair class reverts to fast-play. So multiway, the answer to "trap or fast-play" isn't in your hand, it's in the combination of players left at the table.

**Rule:** The trap only works at the node "the one remaining opponent = the aggressor who will bet" — the moment a caller mixes into the pot, play the top of your range fast.

### 13.1-EK-3 The Defense Bar Is Drawn by Out Count, Not by Class Name


13.1 gives the betting side; on the defense side, the bar looks at IMPROVEMENT outs. The HU reflex "you don't fold a pair to a small bet" breaks multiway: an underboard pocket pair is a 2-out hand — outside of hitting a set it can't get value on any turn, and every bluff has live equity against it; a 5-out bottom pair is clearly better than that, and a bottom pair with a backdoor continues comfortably. A gutshot building a straight from the bad end drops too: multiway, completing a non-nut straight doesn't make you money, it costs you your stack — combos holding the top end are plentiful in opponents' ranges.

**Rule:** In multiway defense, weigh the hand by out count + whether those outs are to the NUTS; a 2-out pocket pair ranks below a 5-out bottom pair.

### 13.2-EK The Limit of the "Raise After a Call in Between = Nuts" Rule: Gutshot-Heavy Boards


13.2's read of "raise = nuts" is strongest on textures where the raiser has NO natural semi-bluff material. On a static board — three-gap type, rich in gutshots but with no open-ender — the weakest range's squeeze check-raise is forced to carry bluffs: value combos (a handful of sets + suited two pair) are so few that the range can't be built from nuts alone, it's forced to take in low-frequency bluffs from gutshots and bottom pair+backdoor. That's why on a board like this, good top pair isn't theoretically folded — you're in the defense zone against the raiser's polarized-but-bluff-containing range. The pool reality is a separate layer: if the opponent isn't finding these bluffs, 13.2's fold comes back as the exploit.

**Rule:** The "nuts" stamp on a raise is pressed by looking at the board's bluff stock: on a board rich in natural semi-bluffs, theoretically defend good top pair; against a pool that can't find bluffs, fold.

### 13.2-EK-2 The Continuation Bar Is Set by RANGE + Seat, Not by the Hand — the Flatter's Top Pair Isn't the Opener's Top Pair


Facing the identical check-raise with the identical hand, there are two different correct plays: thanks to the umbrella of his strong range (plenty of overpairs/sets) and his position, the opener carries his marginal hands comfortably; the flatter, holding the weakest continuing range, folds that very same top pair as a borderline decision — the hand is the same, the range and the seat are not. In a weak range, a medium-strength hand is punished twice over: it has no umbrella, and the raiser knows it — a barrel comes at the capped flatter noticeably more often than at the strong opener *(calibrate)*. The attacking side obeys the same law: after a multiway check-raise, the one you fire at is the capped flatter, not the strong range still left behind.

**Rule:** Answer "can I continue" with your range, not your hand: in a range with no umbrella every class drops one notch — and aim your barrel at the umbrella-less range too.

### 13.2-EK-3 The Blocker Filter Against a Multiway Check-Raise: a Hand Holding the Raiser's Bluff Class Falls to a Fold


In choosing whether to continue, raw hand-strength ranking misleads; the real filter is WHICH of the raiser's range your hand removes combos from. Middle pocket pairs (the TT–88-type underboard kind) are the worst class: they block the raiser's gutshot bluffs, almost never resolve into the nuts, and even when they turn a set they're frequently dominated — mostly a clean fold. Bottom pair + a low backdoor is a similar trap: that combo IS the raiser's OWN bluff class — you're blocking his bluffs while also being dominated when you run into the same hand, and the backdoor flush isn't the nuts either. The remaining continues are the ends that CAN resolve into the nuts: top-kicker pair, and the NUTS version of the backdoor draw — a backdoor flush's value is measured not by its mere existence but by whether it's the nuts against the raiser's pair+backdoor cluster.

**Rule:** Filter your hand against a check-raise not by "how many points" but by "whose range am I removing combos from, and is it the nuts when it completes"; hands carrying the raiser's own bluff class fold.

### 13.2-EK-4 Correct Your Pot Odds When a Player Is Still Behind You


Multiway, don't read your price against a bet from the pot alone — read it against the raise frequency of the player still to act behind you: if that frequency is roughly p, your real call price is "price ÷ (1−p)" — if the raise frequency is roughly a third, a 25-unit call effectively costs ~37, because when the raise comes you'll burn that money and fold. Test sentence: "Does this continue win net against this same bet HU — and by enough to recover the extra I'm overpaying?" Against a range betting into two players, most borderline-looking calls flip to a fold once you apply this correction. If you know the player behind you doesn't raise (pools love the overcall), the borderline hands come back — most of all the ones carrying outs *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** With a player behind you who can still raise, divide your call price by (1 − raise frequency); set your bar above the HU threshold.

### 13.3-EK Anchor multiway check-raise on VALUE COUNT — manage the bluff by frequency, not combo


The number of value combos in your hand sets the ceiling on the multiway check-raise range: if the value share is a small slice of the range, your total raise frequency is chained to a multiple of that share; how plentiful the gutshot is in the range doesn't concern you *(value share and multiplier: calibrate)*. This is the theory layer of the 3-way check-RAISE node; 13.3's "only nut-blocker semi-bluffs / no 4+ way bluffs" table still holds against bluff-lacking pools and 4+ way pots. At that node the correct construction isn't "pick the best bluff combo" — it's drawing thin, low-frequency slices from MANY classes (gutshots, bottom pair + backdoor, second pair + kicker-connectivity); bottom pair, counterintuitively, raises at close to the same frequency as gutshots.
