If you draw your entire bluff range from a single class (gutshots only), you pay two costs: your range becomes readable, and when that class reaches the river, either it has all given up or you have no bluffs left.

**Rule:** Value count governs frequency, variety governs the combo: in a 3-way check-raise node, build your raise-bluffs in thin slices from many classes rather than one natural class — against 4+ way pots and bluff-lacking pools, 13.3's table still holds.

### 13.5 Who carries the 3-way balance — find the tension point, exploit from there


On a flop with three ranges, balance is built along a single axis: the aggressor's bet frequency and the SECOND-strongest range's check-raise frequency constrain each other; the weakest range isn't a player in this game — its strategy is simple, passive, and unavoidable. The exploit map that follows is clean: if the opener c-bets too much, the answer comes from the second-strongest seat (turn its mixed combos into pure raises), not from the weakest seat — there's no lever to turn there. And if you're the opener, what you should fear isn't the caller, it's the range playing police: if its raise is too low, add your marginals to your bet range; if it's too high, narrow your bet range down to hands that hold up to a raise.

**Rule:** On a multiway flop, first ask "who's carrying the balance" — both the deviation and the fix come from those two seats; the weakest range is just a spectator.

### 13.5-EK On a static board, continue = raise; there's no such thing as a medium hand left


In a 3-way pot the equity anchor is 33%; 50% equity isn't a coin-flip like it is HU — it's a very strong hand. The consequence: on a static board that has seen a bet + a call, the opener's range splits in two — hands strong enough to comfortably stand a raise, and nothing; since there's no medium hand left to crawl to showdown, nearly all continues become raises *(mostly — calibrate)*. This is the general principle: if your range has no medium hand left crawling toward showdown, turning your continues into aggression becomes correct. The limit: a board with a flush draw compresses equities — the bluff ratio a made hand can carry drops, draw-heavy continues multiply → the calling range comes back, and the "all-raise" simplicity breaks down.

**Rule:** On a static multiway node, the continue is mostly a raise; as the board turns more dynamic, the calling range comes back.

### 13.6 Multiway transparency freedom


HU, you need to check occasionally with your strong hands so your checking range doesn't stay undefended; multiway, that need drops away. With two opponents, at every node there's SOMEONE who might be holding strength back — even if you play every hand along its most natural line, no one can attack on the assumption "neither of them has a strong hand"; your opponents' mere presence protects you. Fire your value bets, control your medium hands, and save your range-protection checks for HU pots.

**Rule:** Multiway, do what's natural — the deception budget is for HU; here the other players protect your range for you.

### 13.7 Multiway lead is a tax levied on the weakest range — and de facto collusion with the opener


On a dynamic middling board, the coldcaller's lead isn't a "the board hit me" read; its real job is to stop the weakest range from seeing a free card and realizing cheaply. Part of this tax flows to the opener too: once you lead, your checking range weakens, and the opener — seeing two checks — becomes able to bet far more often; the leader and the opener squeeze the third player together. Two byproducts: (1) the set you slow-play with a check wins with a check-raise over the c-bet that your lead brought back to life; (2) in a 3-way pot, a skipped lead isn't automatically your gain — the EV you leave on the table by not leading goes almost entirely to the WEAKEST range (free realization); the opener doesn't profit from it either, since he also loses his licensed c-bet.

**Rule:** The compass for a multiway lead isn't "whose board is it?" but "who's going to tax the weakest range's free card?" — and in a 3-way pot, the EV left behind by not leading mostly ends up addressed to the weakest range, not automatically to you.

### 13.8 Turn-lead map after paying off the raiser: overcards are the CALLER's card


If you called a flop check-raise, the roles on the turn distribute counterintuitively: the raiser's range is weighted toward board-pairs and gutshots, while your range is loaded with broadway top-pairs — that's why A and K turns are YOUR cards, and a lead opens up OOP into the raiser *(frequency: calibrate)*; on low turns and turns that connect with the board, the lead dies and you revert to a pure check. The reflex of "a scary overcard arrived, switch to check-fold mode" works exactly backwards here: that card hasn't improved a single one of the raiser's semi-bluffs. This is the mirror image of the against-the-c-bettor map in 19.12 (where mid-low cards belong to the leader) — change the node, and the map flips too.

**Rule:** Against a check-raiser, look for your turn lead on overcards, forget it on connecting cards: it's not the rank but the composition difference between the two ranges that tells you whose card it is.

### 13.9 A fold is a card


An uncapped defender forced into passivity on a multiway flop gets repriced the moment one of the players folds: the situation has changed — you're HU now, your range is uncapped, and your obligation to stay passive is gone. Even without waiting for a good turn, a small protection-value lead opens up: it collects instant folds from your opponent's air mass and cuts the cost of the free card. On some cards, even a geometric-sized lead is playable with top-pair value: if the opponent's range is air-heavy, the sizing that sets up a river jam is marginal but real *(calibrate)*; the pool almost never does this, and it's hard to defend against when someone does.

**Rule:** Read a player's fold like a good turn card — closed action hands you back your right to lead.

### 13.10 Offsuit combo monopoly — bomb lead on the straight-completing turn


Preflop, only the defender plays offsuit connectors; the other players either 3-bet those hands or folded them. As flop action tightens the ranges, this monopoly grows: on the turn that completes the straight, a huge slice of the defender's range collapses onto that single hand class, the straight *(share: calibrate)*. Don't check that card, bomb it: the other players' ranges are showdown-bound — no one bets in your place, and a check becomes a free card, not a trap. The count works in reverse too: straight cards you share with opponents' suited combos (the ones you don't monopolize) don't give you the same lead.

**Rule:** Once the straight in the defender's offsuit monopoly completes, the lead is mandatory — multiway, no one else will grow that pot for you.

### 13.10-EK Read the turn by counting combos — flop suit and kicker-connection shape your range


Your multiway defending range doesn't distribute the way you'd assume. One: offsuit hands only continue when the kicker connects to a board card, while some of the suited top-pairs went to a preflop 3-bet — that's why second pair can arrive on the turn MORE crowded than top pair; this count tells you which board-pair actually helps you. Two: if you're only continuing a pair class on the condition of a backdoor flush draw, that suit's pairing turn kills your own trips combos — on a rainbow flop, trips are plentiful on the board-pair turn, while on a two-tone flop the same card arrives nearly empty *(calibrate)*. The rote rule "always a small lead OOP on a board-pair" breaks here as a result; against an opponent who leads every board-pair by that same rote rule, don't over-fold — open up your semi-bluff raises instead.

**Rule:** It's not textural rote memory but the count of your own continuing combos that tells you which turn helps whom.

*Root-error link: direct reinforcement. Fills the unwritten half of B7's heading; touches no existing range.*

### 13.2-EK-5 In Multiway, Carry the Size History to the River

Multiway pots repeat far less often than HU ones — you run into the same three-way node very rarely across a career, and the pool has studied these nodes just as little; the result is sizes that stay more unbalanced and honest than in HU, closer to real human behavior. That's why in multiway you should trust not the size on a single street but the ENTIRE size sequence the hand has SHOWN SO FAR — if the same opponent bets big on the flop and drops to tiny on the turn with the same hand, that's usually not two separate classes but a SINGLE line's shift in intent (protection is done, now it's pot control/thin value or giving up). Before a big river decision, recall every size from the start of the hand one by one — don't tunnel-vision into "call or fold" by looking only at the last card.

**Rule:** Because the pool has studied multiway so little, sizes stay unbalanced and honest — before the river decision, recall the hand's ENTIRE size sequence so far; don't read a single street's size, or a size shift within one hand, as a class distinction.

## Chapter 14 — The 40–70bb Bridge Band

*★ v5. B4 gives tables for 100–150bb, B5 for 25–30bb; the space between was left to B4.7's one-line character notes. Main Day 2+ and the mid-phase of all the High Rollers are played mostly at 40–70bb — the band you'll have the most hands in. Question Bank 13 and 34 ask about this band; this chapter expands B4.7's lines into a table.*

### 14.0 The band's character

The completion of B0 c.7 ("stack mode before every hand") between the two extremes. In this band:

- 3-bets increasingly approach COMMIT (not at 60bb; full commit at 40bb).
- The flat window narrows but isn't zero as in B5 — a narrow flat survives IP and in the BB.
- Offsuit broadways and dominate-able hands drop relative to B4; playability comes first.

### 14.1 3-bet framework — derive DIRECTIONALLY from B4

*We are not writing a new combo list; we give the direction in which B4's CALIBRATED ranges tighten in this band. Exact thresholds in drills (calibrate).*

| Band | VALUE 3-bet | BLUFF 3-bet | Continue vs jam | Flat exception |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **60–70bb** | B4 VALUE ≈ same | B4.7 direction: more polarized — narrow bluffs to the best blocker+playability hands *(calibrate)* | Value that can continue vs a 4-bet | IP: narrow; BB: narrow |
| **40–60bb** | B4 VALUE − weak ends | Nearly cut — live, nobody folds | APPROACHES commit; full commit below 40bb (B4.7) | Almost none |

**Rule:** Below ≈60bb the "3-bet then fold" structure starts to weaken and ends at 40bb — exact threshold in drills *(calibrate)*. A hand you 3-bet must be able to continue vs a 4-bet/jam — if it can't, flat (IP/BB) or fold.

### 14.2 60bb → 40bb transition markers

As you drop through the band, the first to leave the table: 3-bet bluffs with suited connectors, speculative flats with small pairs, offsuit broadway cold-calls. B0 c.7's warning (repeated in B4.7) becomes concrete here: **"Playing a 130bb range at 45bb is the most expensive habit."**

*Root-error link: completes the mode-before-range spine (B0 c.7) between the two extreme bands; wrong-mode ranges are the number-one source of bloated-pot production.*

---

### 14.1-EK The postflop threat of a 3-bet in the bridge band


In this band, the 3-bet's approach to commit doesn't stay preflop: in a ~40bb 3-bet pot the flop SPR is ≲2, a flop call is mostly a de facto stack-off, and the flop-bet-plus-turn-jam threat can leave the caller's draws indifferent. At 100bb this threat doesn't disappear, it's just delayed: the turn jam becomes an overbet (turn SPR ≳1.5), and the caller can carry a draw on the flop without committing. The same dynamic is why a high board (J-T-x type) gets a big size at 40bb and a pot size at 100bb; on a three-broadway board, though, the caller's dense two-pair/straight share (a thin nut advantage) pushes the size toward small at every depth *(calibrate)* (see B32.3, B32.5). The hand you 3-bet must be able to carry the postflop jam/check tree.

### 14.1-EK-2 The 40bb squeeze-jam: WHICH range does the blocker cut — and the 4-bet against a polar squeeze


The EV of a squeeze-jam comes from two sources: hands folded out, plus equity when called. An **A/K blocker** thins the calling range (AA/AK/AQ/KK) → fold equity rises, and when called, suited Ax is live — it works well. A **J/T blocker** removes hands the opponents were ALREADY going to fold (JTs/QJs/KJs) → the caller's share of the remaining range grows, fold equity drops; when called, AK/AQ/JJ+ dominates. The same ace produces two opposite verdicts: suited low Ax jams, offsuit AJ is borderline/mixed — the A-blocker pushes toward jam, the J-blocker holds it back; it depends on position and the opener's range *(calibrate)*. The backbone is medium-high pairs plus AK/AQ, the bluff side is suited low Ax; A/K-high suited broadway (AKs/AQs/KQs) is in the jam, J/T-carrying suited broadway (KJs/QJs/JTs) is mostly call/fold rather than jam in this band; suited low Kx is borderline *(calibrate)*. Test: "does my card remove combos from the opponent's CALL range or his FOLD range?" If it removes from the call range, jam.

**As the opener facing a polar squeeze:** at 40bb the squeezer gets good odds on a jam relative to the money already in (roughly close to 2:1 — work it out with your own size) → he pays wide on price: 99+/AQ/AJs/KQs type *(calibrate)*; against that calling range, TT–JJ is NOT a favorite, roughly a coinflip or slightly under *(calibrate)*; what mostly makes the jam +EV isn't being ahead when called, it's the fold of the polar squeeze's bluff half plus the dead money already in the pot — borderline, calibrate. "Only better hands than mine pay, the bluffs I beat fold" is 100bb logic — don't carry it into this band. The call is mostly for medium pairs (88–99 type) and borderline TT *(calibrate)* — when the squeeze came from the blinds (you're IP on the squeezer) and the sandwiched caller's overcall odds are low; since the sandwiched caller is still behind you, the call never closes the action in any scenario, and if you're OOP against a late-position squeeze, the jam/fold weighting increases. Suited Ax with an A-blocker, and AK, jam. Small pairs are mixed.

**Sizing:** the sandwiched caller closes the action, but at 40bb the squeeze size is already big relative to the stack and caps the range; a call leaves an SPR≈1-2 multiway pot → it mostly collapses into fold/jam, flat is rare → you don't need to size up to deny him a price; a medium size relative to the opener *(calibrate)*. B4.2's "every cold-caller +1 size" rule is for 100bb+, and loosens at 40bb.

### 14.1-EK-4 The squeeze-jam is priced by the BB's STACK and by depth, not by the hand (30–45bb; loose opener + wide flatter)


14.1-EK gave the blocker test; the second axis is the stack behind. HJ opens wide (loose opener; *lines: calibrate*), BTN flats wide, you're SB:

- **Does the BB cover you?** If the BB doesn't cover you, the jam range widens noticeably; if it does, it narrows sharply *(thresholds: calibrate)* — speculative suited Ax/low connectors lose EV against a covering BB, only the premium core stays profitable under every condition *(boundary hands: calibrate)*. Against a non-covering stack, speculatives survive; against a covering one, they die (the jam-side of the 12.4 asymmetry).
- **Is everyone deep?** As depth increases, the jam range narrows; callers being squeezed into medium pairs plus the strongest broadway partly offsets the narrowing — not as wide as at 30bb, but still playable *(calibrate)*.
- **Material:** a suited-broadway backbone (QTs/KTs/KJs/T9s), a suited Ax/Kx body — K7s's K reduces AK/KK call combos; a small pair is no worse than 77.
- **Gambler brake:** if the opponent is calling with JTs/QTs, the line narrows fast; attack the loosest one — even against a nit flatter, jam frequency stays higher than expected *(calibrate)*.

**Boundary with 14.1-EK:** there, standard-opener-plus-blocker analysis takes J/T-carrying suited broadway out of the jam; here, a loose opener plus a wide flatter (dead money large, callers relatively tight) makes them the backbone — a difference in conditions, not a contradiction *(borderline — calibrate)*.

### 14.1-EK-3 Two extensions of the blocker test: a 60bb jam into a capped cold-caller (T9s) — and a pair has no blocker in a reshove


**Capped cold-caller.** In the pool, the CO 3-bets its premiums at 100% (4.6-EK) → its calling range settles into 22–99 + suited broadway; it only pays a 60bb jam with 99/AQs and up → a T9s/J9s/JTs jam from the SB is +EV *(calibrate)*. T9s is the best candidate: it blocks the paying 99, and it unblocks the dominated KQ/KJ/QJ/Axs folds — the T also cutting JTs/QTs/KTs folds is a small minus, but the net is still a plus. The blocker rule flips here relative to 14.1-EK: a J/T blocker holds you back — if it leaves the dominating broadway in the paying range; it pushes you forward — if the paying range is nothing but pairs. Which range a card removes combos from depends on the cold-caller's composition. Two conditions: the window closes fast once the cold-caller starts slow-playing part of its premiums — the threshold frequency *(calibrate)*; the window stays open if the opener also folds its non-premium hands to the jam *(continued: calibrate)*, and the jam loses value if it pays wide. Against a tight opener plus a tight cold-caller, K6s/K5s is better than K8s (the 8x is in their range). The BB jams wider after a raise-plus-call-plus-SB-overcall — the overcall is dead money.

**In a reshove, a medium pair ≈ a small pair.** A tight EP opener (77+/AJs+/AK) pays a reshove with TT+/AK/AQs; against that range 99 and 55 carry the same equity — 55 removes no combo from the raise-fold hands (77/88/AJs), 99 only removes the opener's own folded 99 (a small minus). A5s, on the other hand, blocks AK/AA, and is live when called → it ranks above the pairs *(ordering: calibrate)*. The reshove range isn't pair-heavy, it's blocker-heavy: 99/TT are borderline, 55–88 mostly fold.

### 14.1-EK-5 The 4-bet's OOP-punishing value has a depth threshold

At deep stacks (100bb+), the value of trapping an OOP opponent in a 4-bet pot is realized postflop — the opponent enters a big pot out of position, gets pressured for a street or two at SPR≈1.5–2, and you realize your equity through genuine maneuvering. As you drop into the bridge band, this mechanism fades: because SPR shrinks, the opponent answers the 4-bet not with postflop maneuvering but directly with a jam from a top range like JJ+/AK — you're forced into a call/fold decision, and the postflop edge you were chasing never comes into play. So while the rationale "the opponent can't withstand this OOP" is a genuine source of profit at 100bb, in the bridge band and below the same rationale is empty. The value range doesn't NARROW in this bridge, it WIDENS — as the commit threshold approaches, QQ (and, heading toward 40bb, JJ/AQs) also enters commit-value (see 14.1-EK-2) *(calibrate)*; what remains as the rationale is this widened value itself against the opponent's wide jam range like JJ+/AK, plus a small slice of blocker bluffs (see 14.1-EK).

**Rule:** If you're 4-betting on the OOP-postflop-punish rationale, first ask whether the depth allows it — if the opponent turns directly to a jam instead of postflop (bridge band and below), that rationale is empty; what remains is a value range that widens as depth drops, plus a small slice of blocker bluffs.

## Chapter 16 — The Mental Spine: Bustout/Re-entry, Tilt, Autopsy, Series Plan

*★ v5. The book was 100% technical until now. But August 21–29 is an endurance race (SHR + PLO + 8-day Main + HR) with event overlaps. However good the technical doctrine, there wasn't a single line managing the decision-state (fatigue, tilt, re-entry pressure) that must execute it. Short CARD format — not a textbook.*

### 16.1 Bustout + re-entry decision card

The series' most expensive single decision isn't a hand: it's the re-entry decision made in the 5 minutes after busting. Auto re-entry while tilted = the bankroll-scale version of the root error (treating a single bullet as value in a bloated "series investment").

**Mandatory wait:** Bustout → 20 min *(default)* away from the table → fill in the below → then decide.

| Question | Yes | No |
|---|---|---|
| Was the elimination a bad DECISION? (not a bad outcome) | Ask 16.3's TWO QUESTIONS now — full autopsy at day's end | Variance — you may continue |
| Which Day 1 flight / event does the next bullet eat? | Check the calendar (16.4) | Free |
| Is total series exposure within the pre-set limit? | Continue | STOP |
| Is my physical state (sleep/hour) green? | Continue | STOP — enter tomorrow |

**Max bullets per event** *(calibrated 2026-08-10)*: SHR: **1** · PLO: **2** · Main: **2** · HR: **2** — total max exposure ~€181K (single-bullet base €140.6K). No re-entry at the SHR €100K: a second bullet there is +€100K on its own, outside discipline.

### 16.2 Tilt card

The root error is rarely committed in cold blood; it typically happens in the hands following a trigger *(calibrate — mark the duration from the B7 cases)*. While tilted, memorized sentences don't get read — first the tilt must be NOTICED.

| Trigger *(all 3 apply to Emre — 2026-08-10)* | Tell | Immediate move |
|---|---|---|
| Bad beat / big pot loss | Chasing with worse hands | Deep breath |
| Long card-dead → action hunger | Faster play / snap-action | 1-orbit VPIP lock |
| Bluff shown / ego | Reaching for the phone | Drink water / stand up |

*Emre tells (2026-08-10) — cross-cutting, no single dominant trigger: **faster / snap play**, **reaching for the phone**, **chasing with worse hands**. Any ONE of the three appearing is the alarm — don't stop to diagnose which trigger fired; notice the tell, then run that row's move. The three map above by best fit, but any tell can come from any trigger.*

The trigger rows are back-filled with references from your three elimination cases (B7) — no generic tilt list is written.

**B0 c.15:** *A decision made on tilt comes not from a range but from a wound — label the wound first.*

### 16.3 Autopsy rule

| Step | Rule |
|---|---|
| **When** | NOT the moment the hand ends — after the day ends. At the table, only note the hand. *Exception: bustout+re-entry — only the two questions immediately (16.1), the written autopsy still at day's end* |
| **The two questions** | (1) With what I knew at decision time, what was the book's rule? (2) Did I follow the rule? |
| **Followed + lost** | Into the case log as "right decision, bad outcome" — **THE RANGE DOES NOT CHANGE** |

This filter PROTECTS calibration: at the SHR, correct jams will be lost often; without the filter you'd wreck B4-B5's careful tables mid-tournament. Warning: don't let the "it was correct, it was variance" label become a self-absolution door — valid only if you FOLLOWED the rule. Example: the TT jam in B5.5 (Question 23).

### 16.4 Series plan and overlap pre-decisions

| Date | Event / Day | Estimated finish | Sleep target | Next-day risk |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Aug 21 | SHR Day 1 | *(fill in)* | *(fill in)* | Main 1A on the 22nd |
| Aug 22 | SHR D2 · PLO HR D1 · Main 1A | triple overlap | — | If SHR is alive, Main 1A burns |
| Aug 23 | SHR Final · PLO D2 · Main 1B | overlap | — | Main entry CLOSES today — last bullet |
| Aug 24–26 | Main grind | *(fill in)* | *(fill in)* | — |
| Aug 27–29 | HR €10.3K · Main D5–Final | overlap | — | If deep in the Main, don't enter HR fresh |

**Overlap pre-decisions** *(NO fixed priority — decide by current stack/ICM; rule below)*:

**Decision rule:** Of the two overlapping events, stay in the ONE where your stack is deeper and you're closer to the payout/ICM advantage; sacrifice the other. The determinant isn't buy-in size but your CURRENT expectation of winning (stack depth × field × payout proximity) — the event-scale version of the mode-first spine (c.7).

- If I reach SHR Day 2 vs a Main flight → if my SHR stack is above average, stay in the SHR and leave the Main to the last bullet (1B, Aug 23); if the SHR is short, switch to Main 1A.
- If the HR starts while I'm deep in the Main → if I'm above average and near the money in the Main, stay in the Main and skip the HR; if the Main is short, enter the HR fresh.

**Break routine:** 3 items every break — (1) walk, (2) eat/water, (3) look at the Quick Reference. NO social media on the phone.

*Root-error link: fatigue and tilt are the frequency MULTIPLIER of the root error — the day-plan-scale parallel of B4's "reduce the frequency preflop" logic (prevent the error on the calendar, not inside the hand).*

---

### 16.2-EK The fourth trigger: positive arousal (euphoria tilt)


B16.2's three triggers all come from negative emotion; but deep-run/FT EUPHORIA produces tilt too — broken sleep plus a hot run of cards plus a rising image turns into over-aggression and ends in a punt.

| Trigger | Tell | Immediate move |
|---|---|---|
| **Euphoria / deep-run excitement** | Frequencies stay elevated across the board plus the feeling that "every spot plays now" | Slow your decision speed and return to baseline (default) frequencies; drop one notch at a marginal 4-bet/5-bet node |

**A table-dominating, attention-grabbing talkative opponent (B23 appendix):** if such an opponent breaks your flow, the real leak is loss of concentration — the tell: decisions get shallow, your preflop reads go stale. Root-error link: with attention scattered, range discipline slides into emotion/autopilot. Exploit: build your own return-to-flow ritual (earplugs/breathing); since everyone at the table is out of flow, reads get easier and his overly-wide chip range is open to attack. CALL the clock (passivity against a time-abuser is a direct EV loss); the longer a hand runs, the staler your preflop read gets — refresh it consciously at the start of each street.

### 16.5 The five-hand rule after a big loss


Autopsy #4's (AQs bust, B7 Case 8) real finding was timing: TWO MINUTES after an 88 hand that lost roughly 34K chips, the session saw its first preflop call-off with 70% of the stack. There was no comparable spot in the eighty hands before it. The same pattern shows up in the cash series too: three big pots in six minutes, a few thousand dollars. Same mechanism — decision quality drops right after a big loss, and the drop lands in the most expensive hand.

**Rule:** for the 5 hands after losing a big pot: preflop all-in only with a premium (QQ+, AK); any hand that gets 4-bet folds to a 5-bet. "Big pot" in a tournament = 25% of the stack, in cash = 100bb. This is the 16.2 tilt card made concrete as a hand-counter: it doesn't wait for you to feel the tilt, it engages automatically after a loss — cutting off the story-based "make it back" decision (B25) before it's born.

### 16.6 Big-spot preparation: plan the week backward from EV, a one-hour protocol before Day 3 / the FT


A tournament's EV doesn't spread evenly across the hours: the last two tables of a deep run, plus the FT, pack most of the series' EV into a handful of late-night hours. Three columns for 16.4's series plan: **the most expensive time window → the sleep window** (the night before the critical day, counting backward from finish time to go to bed late / wake up late — "morning" freshness at 03:00 *(calibrate to your own chronotype)*) **→ pre-night logistics ✔** (food/water/clothes ready, social calendar moved OUT of the series in advance). The working material is the 20–25 hands flagged during the session (9.3).

**A one-hour protocol** (before Day 3 / the last 2–3 tables / the FT, written out):
1. Write the lobby down as stack and bb; interpret the distribution — very short plus small intermediate steps = a fast start → tight for the first 30–60 minutes; a deep distribution → close to chipEV.
2. Two lines per opponent: how I see him plus **how he sees me** (I showed a bluff → he sees me as bluff-prone, bluff-catches me lighter → less bluff, thinner value against him; he knows I know ICM → he sells pressure). This is the most expensive line.
3. Weight your study time by P(a big pot with this opponent): hours for the big stacks, minutes for the short ones.
4. Build hypothetical ranges from the showdowns you've seen, and work out reshove thresholds in advance across a few stack scenarios.
5. Read the payout ladder FOR THE OPPONENT: the size of a step relative to his bankroll tells you who's about to freeze up (12.3).

### 16.7 Variance literacy and an exposure plan — 16.1's limit derives from here


A series' result isn't a skill measure; unplanned full exposure isn't a plan either. Three lines before a series:

1. **Investment goal → total limit.** "Steady flow" wants low variance (small fields, high volume, partial sale of action); "the big hit" wants high variance (big fields, full exposure). The bankroll version of ICM: your life stack-role picks your strategy. Estimate ROI pessimistically — plan with half your estimate.
2. **Count the variance** (in a variance calculator, with your own parameters): in an ~800-runner field at 10% ROI, 5,000 tournaments a year → a losing year ~20%, a losing month ~40%+, a losing session ~70%; 20% ROI drops the losing-year odds to ~2% but barely changes the losing-session odds *(representative — calibrate)*. For someone who wants stability: adjust volume/field size, not edge.
3. **Slice per event.** At ~10x ABI buy-ins, 200 tournaments a year, even at 20% ROI there's a non-trivial chance *(representative: ~⅓ — calibrate)* of wiping out the small-buy-in profit. A ticket at 20% ROI is worth ~1.2x its face value → selling half of it at a 1.1 mark-up halves the swing (the standard deviation) and creates a premium; a swap is mostly bad. Steep payouts plus a big field = the highest variance → partial exposure.

The autopsy (16.3) asks "did I follow the rule" — even at 20% ROI, a series ending in a loss is MOSTLY normal; the outcome doesn't change the calibration, a rule violation does. Going broke has a floor: with a proven track record, capital gets found.

*Root-error link: fatigue and tilt are the FREQUENCY MULTIPLIER of the root error — the day-plan-scale parallel of B4's "lower the frequency preflop" logic (prevent the error on the calendar, not inside the hand).*

### 16.3-EK A third category in the autopsy — the concept was right, the combo was wrong

B16.3's autopsy pair — (1) what was the rule at decision time, (2) did I follow the rule — needs a third distinction when you later verify against a sim an exploit idea you brought from the table: was the concept wrong, or was the concept right but the combo selection wrong? Example: if the read "there's a bluff-lead at this node" is correct, but a different slice of the range should have bluffed instead of the specific combo in your hand — that's a combo error, and as long as you're sliding among bluff candidates it's small and doesn't call for abandoning the idea in a panic; but if you turned a hand with showdown value into a bluff, or bluffed with a combo that blocks villain's fold range, the loss can be as big as a concept error. If the concept itself was wrong (there was no bluff at that node at all), the loss is bigger and calls for a real correction. In the autopsy, classify the concept first, then the combo selection — don't give the two the same weight.

**Rule:** In an autopsy against a sim, separate "was it the concept or the combo" — with the right concept, a combo sliding among bluff candidates is a small correction; if you bluffed a combo that blocks showdown value or blocks villain's fold, the loss can be as big as a concept error.

## Quick Reference

*The page to check on your phone during breaks.*

### Decision order

0. Is ICM on in this event? (Chapter 12) — if on, tighten jam/call ranges
1. What's my stack mode? (Chapter 3) — below 30bb go to Chapter 5 (**Chapter 15 in PLO** — no jam)
2. What's my position and villain's position? (Chapter 4; if 40–70bb, Chapter 14)
3. How many ways is the pot? If 3+, go to the Multiway card (Chapter 13)
4. Is this hand a 3-bet, a flat, or a fold?
5. If I see a flop, am I comfortable making one pair? (Count the SPR — Chapter 11)
6. Am I staying OOP?

### Sizes

| Move | Size |
|---|---|
| **IP 3-bet** | 3–3.5× |
| **OOP 3-bet** | 4× |
| **Per coldcaller** | +1× |
| **Squeeze** | 4.5–5× |
| **4-bet IP / OOP** | 2.2× / 2.5× |

### 25–30bb card

| Situation | What you do |
|---|---|
| **Open size** | 2–2.2× |
| **3-bet** | None — JAM. TT+/AQs+/AKo vs early pos.; 88+/ATs+/AQo+/KQs vs CO-BTN |
| **Jam at the chip leader** | 77+, A9s+, AJo+, KQs (widened) |
| **Call vs jam** | 99+, AJs+, AQo+ |
| **Flat** | NONE — from no position |
| **Auto fold** | All suited connectors, suited gappers, KJo/QJo/JTo |

### Postflop sizes (Chapter 11)

| Situation | Direction |
|---|---|
| **Turn second barrel** | Do I have a river plan? Leave a check-fold pot for a bad river *(size calibrate)* |
| **River value vs rec** | He pays → BET even if thin; thin hand small, strong hand size up (11.3) |
| **River value vs reg** | Thin / check-call |
| **Villain overbet** | Read polarized: one pair → fold direction |
| **Overbet (mine)** | ONLY nuts + deep + villain capped — one pair NEVER |

### ICM / Final Table card (Chapter 12)

| Situation | What you do |
|---|---|
| **Is the event's ICM on** | SHR/HR early · Main late (12.0) |
| **Near the money** | JAM ≈ stays, CALL tightens — fold hard when covered |
| **My FT role** | Short: ladder · Middle: most fragile, patience · Covering: crush |
| **Bubble hunting** | Locked middle stack = most profitable target |
| **<15bb + everyone locked** | WIDEN, don't tighten |

### Multiway card (Chapter 13)

| Situation | 3+ way |
|---|---|
| **C-bet** | Frequency collapses — strong value + nut-draws |
| **One pair** | One class down → approaches bluff-catcher |
| **Bluff** | 3-way: nut-blocker only · 4+: none |
| **Non-nut draw** | Reverts to check / dies |

### Tilt card (Chapter 16)

| Trigger | Immediate move |
|---|---|
| Bad beat / big loss | Deep breath |
| Card-dead → action hunger | 1-orbit VPIP lock |
| Bluff shown / ego | Drink water / stand up |

### Red flags — stop and think

- I'm making a big decision with one pair in a bloated pot → classify it as a bluff-catcher.
- I'm considering a river jam → which hand weaker than mine pays? No answer, no jam.
- Villain is all-in and I'm counting chops → chops don't jam.
- I'm at 45bb but flatting a small pair → mode error.
- I'm 3-bet bluffing from OOP → usually a mistake.
- I'm bloating a pot with bare AA in PLO → treat it as one pair.
- I'm at 28bb and entering a pot with a suited connector → Chapter 5, auto fold.
- I'm firing the second barrel on the turn with no river plan → Chapter 11, don't bloat the pot.
- I'm thinking HU-style c-bet/value in a 3+ way pot → Chapter 13, one class down.
- I'm calling a jam near the money with AJs+ → Chapter 12, calls tighten (covered?).
- I can't remember my last 2 decisions / it's 01:00+ and a marginal spot → fatigue, Chapter 16.
- I busted and I'm firing a re-entry within 5 minutes → Chapter 16, wait 20 min.

---

*Poker Pocket Book v5 — EPT Barcelona Edition · August 2026*

## Chapter 17 — SPECIAL MODULE: WSOP Online Main Event Day 2

> WSOP Online #28 — $5,000 MAIN EVENT, $25M GTD · Day 2 game plan and range guide. Muzun · 784,748 chips (56 BB) · Restart: 21 September 2026, 20:00. This is a tournament-specific module to study after EPT Barcelona (1–20 September); it adapts the general doctrine (Chapters 0–16) to this exact structure.

### 17.1 Tournament profile and reality

This is a **freezeout** with **no re-entry**. Day 1 flights run daily through 21 September; Day 2 starts with an estimated 600–900 players. **The money is not locked yet** — you sit down at the restart as if it were a tournament before the bubble.

| Item | Value | Item | Value |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Stack | 784,748 (56 BB) | Blinds | 7,000/14,000 (1,750) |
| Format | 8-handed, freezeout | Level length | 25 min (fast!) |
| Prize pool | $25,000,000+ | First place | ~$5,000,000 |
| Deal | Banned (incl. FT) | FT | 9 players, next day |

**Critical structural fact:** at 25-minute levels blinds rise ~2.4× per hour. 56 BB drops to 25 BB after three hours of passive play. Passivity here is not "safe" — it is **slow elimination**.

### 17.2 Core philosophy: not tight, selectively aggressive

**We will not sit and wait for premiums.** The right profile rests on a dual split:

- **Active in small pots:** positional opens, blind steals, c-bet pressure, 3-bet pressure on short/medium stacks. This is the main chip source — pots won without going to showdown.
- **Disciplined in big pots:** the whole stack goes in only with a clear plan + a strong hand combo. Never risk your stack with one pair (AA included) in a bloated pot — the root-error rule applies here too.

In short: **wide in hand count, narrow in stack risk.**

### 17.3 Opening ranges (8-handed, ~50 BB, 2.1–2.3x open)

Online opponents defend blinds more accurately, so early position is a touch tighter and late position is standard. With antes, stealing is still very profitable.

| Position | Range (~%) | Hands |
| --- | --- | --- |
| UTG / UTG+1 | 13–15% | 77+, ATs+, KJs+, QJs, JTs, T9s, AJo+, KQo |
| LJ (MP) | 17% | 66+, A9s+, A5s, KTs+, QTs+, J9s+, T9s, 98s, ATo+, KJo+ |
| HJ | 20% | 55+, A8s+, A4s–A5s, K9s+, Q9s+, J9s+, T8s+, 98s, 87s, ATo+, KJo+, QJo |
| CO | 26% | 44+, A2s+, K8s+, Q9s+, J8s+, T8s+, 97s+, 87s, 76s, A9o+, KTo+, QTo+, JTo |
| BTN | 40–44% | 22+, all suited aces, K5s+, Q7s+, J7s+, T7s+, 96s+, 86s+, 75s+, 65s, 54s, A4o+, K9o+, Q9o+, J9o+, T9o |
| SB | 35% (raise-only) | Slightly tighter than BTN; no limp, open 3x |

- **Table adjust:** if BB is a tight player (fold 60%+), widen BTN/CO by 10%. If a 3-bettor reg is on your left, tighten CO, keep BTN.
- **If the stack drops:** below 35 BB this table no longer applies — go to the stack modes in 17.8.

### 17.4 3-Bet ranges

**Sizing:** in position (IP) ~3x the open, out of position (OOP) ~4x. At 56 BB deep a 3-bet pot already sees half the stack — so your 3-bet range is polarized, but the bluff side wants "good blocker + playability."

| Scenario | Value | Bluff / Semi-bluff |
| --- | --- | --- |
| vs EP open (IP) | QQ+, AK | A5s–A4s, KQs (low freq) |
| vs MP/HJ open (IP) | TT+, AQs+, AKo | A5s–A3s, KJs, QJs, 76s–65s (mixed) |
| vs CO/BTN open | 99+, AJs+, KQs, AQo+ | A5s–A2s, K9s–KTs, QTs, J9s, T9s, 87s |
| SB vs BTN open | TT+, AQ+ value; wide bluff | A2s–A5s, K9s+, Q9s+, suited connectors — no flat from SB, 3-bet or fold |
| BB vs BTN/SB | 99+, AQ+ | A5s-type + KTs/QTs/JTs; defend (call) with the rest |

- **Whom to 3-bet:** 30–50 BB medium stacks (they feel bubble pressure most) and reg opening too much.
- **Whom NOT to BLUFF 3-bet:** big stacks that cover you (as the bubble nears) and sub-20 BB — their only reply is a jam, and your bluff 3-bet burns. **A value 3-bet is free** (QQ+/AK 3-bet); continue vs the cover's jam only with KK+, and 3-bet-fold QQ (see 17.10 ICM thresholds). What's forbidden is the bluff 3-bet, not value.

### 17.5 3-Bet sizes (IP and OOP)

Playing OOP is a disadvantage, so the OOP 3-bet is bigger (raises the call price, lowers SPR). IP, position already creates pressure, so a smaller size is enough.

| Scenario | Size | Example (vs 2.2x open) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| IP 3-bet (BTN vs CO) | 3.0–3.3x the open | 2.2 BB → 7 BB |
| IP 3-bet (vs EP) | ~3x the open | 2.3 BB → 7 BB |
| OOP 3-bet (SB vs BTN) | 3.8–4.2x | 2.2 BB → 8.5–9 BB |
| OOP 3-bet (BB vs BTN/SB) | 3.6–4x | 2.2 BB → 8–8.5 BB |
| Squeeze IP (open + caller) | 4x + 1 open per caller | 2.2 + 1 call → ~11 BB |
| Squeeze OOP | 5x + 1 open per caller | 2.2 + 1 call → ~13 BB |
| 20–25 BB | 3-bet ≈ jam / small-commit (~2.5x, no fold) | finish the plan preflop |

**Don't size by hand:** same size with AA and with A5s. A sizing tell is more expensive online than live.

### 17.6 4-Bet and play vs a 3-bet

**Sizing:** 4-bet = ~2.2–2.4x the 3-bet (IP), OOP ~2.5x. At 56 BB the 4-bet is ~20–22 BB = the commit threshold; a stack that continues past the 4-bet can't fold.

| Scenario | Action | Hands |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Faced 3-bet on your open (IP) | 4-bet value | KK+, (AK jam/4-bet mixed); QQ depends on villain |
| Faced 3-bet on your open (IP) | 4-bet bluff | A5s–A4s (ace blocker), low freq, vs aggressive 3-bettors |
| Faced 3-bet on your open (IP) | Call | QQ–99, AQs, AJs, KQs, JJ–TT, suited broadway |
| Faced 3-bet on your open (IP) | Fold | A9o-type offsuit edges, low suited gappers |
| Faced 3-bet (OOP) | Tighter continue | KK+ 4-bet; QQ/AK mixed; JJ–TT, AQs call; rest fold |
| Facing a 4-bet | Jam / call | KK+ always; QQ/AK per villain profile (fold QQ vs nit, jam vs aggressor) |

**Golden rule:** every hand you 4-bet at 56 BB must have a pre-decided plan against a 5-bet jam. There is no "4-bet and see" mode.

### 17.7 Fold discipline

- **Preflop:** vs a 3-bet, offsuit broadways (KQo, AJo) to the muck. If the 3-bet came from a stack that covers you, you're one tier tighter.
- **Flop:** in a multiway pot don't continue with one pair vs a raise. If your c-bet got check-raised and you hold less than overpair + backdoor on a wet board, folding is on the table.
- **Turn:** a big turn barrel + a board that completes a four-straight/flush ends one pair. "The pot got big, I'm committed" is the root error itself — pot size is not a commit reason.
- **River:** overpair + bad river → check-call a small bet, check-fold a big bet. Before jamming, the only question: "Which hand worse than mine pays?" If the answer is empty, the jam has no value — check.
- **ICM folds:** on the bubble and at big ladder jumps, even a chip-EV marginally profitable call is a fold. A flip vs an equal stack = last resort, never a plan.

### 17.8 Stack modes

| Mode | Stack | Play |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mode A — Standard | 45 BB+ | Full ranges; postflop room; suited connectors + small pairs at full value |
| Mode B — Squeezed middle | 30–45 BB | Opens hold, flat calls narrow (set-mine math breaks); commit threshold near, bluff 3-bet down, value up |
| Mode C — Pressure | 20–30 BB | Open 15–20%, 2.1x. 3-bet is mostly jam/small-commit. A5s–A2s becomes jam-3bet ammo |
| Mode D — Jam/Fold | 12–20 BB | Some opens are direct jams (esp. SB/BTN). If you opened, jam or fold vs a 3-bet. Near Nash; ICM tightens jams slightly |
| Mode E — Short | <12 BB | Pure jam/fold; first-in edge; isolate-jam over limps |

### 17.9 Phase plan and ICM

- **Phase 1 (bubble far):** Mode A. Keep the stack above 50 BB, steal from the table's passives, no needless wars with big stacks.
- **Phase 2 (bubble nearing):** your most profitable phase. 15–25 BB stacks freeze up: 3-bet their opens, open into their blinds, float their c-bets. The only brake: stacks that cover you (one tier tighter). **On the bubble vs a covering stack, even QQ may not be a 4-bet-call.**
- **Phase 3 (money hits):** stay tight for 3–4 hands (shorts kamikaze-jam, meet them with premiums), then return to normal.
- **Phase 4 (deep ITM / FT):** ladder jumps are huge: $227K → $346K → $631K → $1M+. ICM overrides chip-EV. **Crush the shorts, dodge the equals, premiums only vs the bigs.** FT is 9-handed, deal banned. Going to the FT short beats not going at all; at the last 2 tables survival is the priority.

### 17.10 Root-error guardrails

- **One pair in a bloated pot = alarm.** AA included. If the pot passed 40 BB and you still hold one pair, the default is pot control + bluff-catcher play, not a stack race.
- **AA river jam:** if no worse hand pays, the jam has no value — check.
- **The check-raise all-in urge** (the KTo lesson): the correct line is check-call then re-evaluate.
- **Chop mechanics:** re-read the board on the river (the A4s case).
- **Tilt protocol:** after a bad hand, 2 hands auto-fold (premiums excepted).

### 17.11 Coach notes — blind spots in the plan

- **BTN 40% open is a live assumption.** Online vs a reg, tighten to 33–35%, muck the offsuit trash (Q9o, J9o); vs weak players keep 40%+.
- **Medium-stack 3-bet pressure is a thin exploit.** Limit bluff 3-bets to blocker hands (A5s, K9s). The real danger: your 3-bet gets called and you enter a bloated pot with an overpair on the flop.
- **"Escaping" cover is not a strategy.** Narrow but resolute defense; every hand vs a covering stack has a street-by-street plan written in advance.
- **Transition errors:** 56→35 flat-call habit (below 40 BB go 3-bet-or-fold); 35→20 the commit threshold slides forward (commit is decided preflop with an SPR plan); ~20 BB panic-jam/freeze (memorize the Mode C/D thresholds — at 20 BB A5s is resteal-jam ammo, ATo still min-raises from the BTN).
- **ICM thresholds:** QQ vs a covering stack's 4-bet jam has ~40% equity vs {KK+, AK} + a bubble ICM premium of 6–10% → required ~48–50% → **FOLD**; vs an aggressive reg (TT+, AQ+) ~54% → call. AK: fold to a covering stack's tight jam, always call a ≤20 BB jam. Practical: on the bubble, for-your-whole-stack vs a cover the range is **KK+**. When you cover, it flips (open into 15–25 BB blinds up to 60%).
- **Online substitutes:** min-3-bet = value-heavy; river overbet = polarized (bluff-catcher math); 1/3 flop = a range bet, no info. Timing is a weak signal. Note system: a 5-second tag every showdown. 14-hour protocol: stand up every 55-min break; cut caffeine after hour 8.

### 17.12 Socratic trap set (S-T1–S-T5)

Each scenario has a "feeling" pulling you into the mistake. Write a street-by-street plan; autopsy after.

1. **S-T1.** 52 BB, you open AA from CO, BTN reg (48 BB) 3-bets, you 4-bet, call. Pot ~44 BB, SPR ~1.3. Flop T♠ 9♠ 8♣. C-bet 1/3, villain RAISES. Feeling: "AA + SPR 1.3 = already committed." Action?
2. **S-T2.** 34 BB, KK in the BB. HJ (38 BB, tight-passive) opens, you 3-bet, call. Flop Q-J-T rainbow, you bet, call. Turn 9♦. Feeling: "take back the initiative with a check-raise all-in." What do you do, and why?
3. **S-T3.** 25 to the bubble, 41 BB. Chip leader (140 BB) opens the BTN for the 3rd time in a row. QQ in the BB. 3-bet or call? A jam comes over your 3-bet — is there a call?
4. **S-T4.** Deep ITM, 3-bet pot, IP, TT. Board 8-6-2r, villain check-calls. Turn 8, checks. River A, villain POT DONKS. Feeling: "I had an overpair, the A is a scare card." Decision?
5. **S-T5.** 47 BB, BB vs BTN single-raised pot, you hold A9o, board A-9-4 two hearts. Check-raise, call. Turn 6♥ (flush completes). You bet, RAISE comes. "I have two pair, not one pair, I'm safe"... are you? Decision and reasoning.

### 17.13 Drill set — 10 questions (specific to this tournament)

When you say "prep me" during restart week, we'll work from this set. The answer key is kept separate; decide for yourself first.

1. **S1.** 56 BB, Phase 1. You open A9s from HJ, the 48 BB reg on the BTN 3-bets. Your action?
2. **S2.** You've dropped to 38 BB (Mode B). Should you open 66 from CO? If you do, what's your plan vs the BB's 3-bet?
3. **S3.** 40 to the bubble. A 130 BB chip leader sits in the BB, you hold KQo on the BTN. Open?
4. **S4.** Phase 2, you have 60 BB. The 22 BB in the SB opens, you're in the BB with A5s. Most profitable line?
5. **S5.** 3-bet pot, OOP, you have QQ. Flop 9-7-5 two spades, villain pot-bets. Plan (flop/turn/river)?
6. **S6.** Money's in, first hand. A 9 BB UTG jam, you're HJ with ATo, 3 big stacks behind you. Call?
7. **S7.** Turn overpair KK, board T-8-4-J two-flush completed, villain 3/4-pot second barrel. What's your continue criterion?
8. **S8.** Mode D (17 BB), A7o from the BTN. Jam, open-fold, or fold? Why?
9. **S9.** Deep ITM, 3 tables left. An equal stack (45 BB) opens CO, you have AQo in the SB. Your 3-bet size and plan vs a 4-bet?
10. **S10.** River AA, pot bloated, board 2-4-5-9-6. Villain checks. Is there a bet? What size, targeting which hand?

### 17.14 Prep calendar

| Period | Focus | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 17–29 August | EPT Barcelona | Don't think about this tournament; all focus on the live series |
| 1–10 September | 2 drills/week | ICM/bubble-weighted + stack-mode transitions |
| 11–18 September | 3 drills/week | Derivatives of this set; mistakes return 1–2 days later in a different form |
| 19–20 September | Light review | Range tables + guardrails; no new concepts |
| 21 September 20:00 | RESTART | Table draw analysis when posted; sit rested — it can run 14+ hours |


---

### 17.3-EK "SB raise-only" is this tournament's context condition, not BvB doctrine


17.3's row "SB 35% (raise-only), no limp, open 3x" is specific to this ~50bb online field: if the BB isos limps rarely/passively, the trap (limp-reraise) share drops but the value of a cheap flop for marginal hands rises — this regime doesn't justify raise-only; if the BB isos/jams limps at a high rate, weak limps get cut but the premium's limp-reraise share grows — that field leaves EV on the table with raise-only too. Raise-only is an acceptable simplification only if the BB clearly over-folds to a raise (the raise's fold equity exceeds the limp's cheap-flop value, *calibrate*) or the player can't manage the limp tree. At the same depth in general BvB, this sits at the boundary of the deep/medium layer, and the SB's limp share varies with which engine is running (SPR trap / polarized iso / jam) — the premium's limp-reraise slice included. Pick which regime is running by the BB's iso/jam behavior, not by the table label (see B33.1, B33.7).

### 17.11-EK Timing online: the doctrine that updates B17.11 — the PRIMARY channel, read together with sizing


**Doctrine update (a revision of B17.11):** B17.11 says "timing is a weak signal"; that stays true for LIVE (physical tells are richer there). But ONLINE has no physical tells — the only real-time data you have is timing plus sizing. That's why, in the online module, timing isn't a "weak signal" — read as a CHAIN with sizing, it's the PRIMARY opponent-read channel (commit to a repeating pattern, not a single data point):

- **Fast check-call plus fast check** = a medium/capped-range tendency → attack (turn/river barrel).
- **Big-money river snap-jam** = a pre-planned, polarized line; the spontaneous-bluff rate is low → TIGHTEN your bluff-catch (lean-fold), don't go to a hero call.
- **Snap-call** = an admission of "raise isn't in my menu" → grow your OOP thin-value size without fear.
- **Long tank plus a small/medium bet** = weakness/decision difficulty → add light bluff pressure.

> **Live, timing is a weak signal (B17.11 stands as written there); ONLINE, the timing-plus-sizing chain is the main weapon of opponent reading. In WSOP Online Day 2 this chain is the primary channel — play the repeating pattern, not a single tell.**

### 17.11-EK-2 General forms of the coaching notes: "KK+ on the bubble" → 12.2-EK-2; "60% open" → 12.4-EK; Mode D's direct jam → 12.1-EK-3


17.11's three lines were written specifically for this tournament; the general doctrine later went into B12/B21 — cross-reference:

- **"KK+ for the whole stack against a cover on the bubble"** — the FT/medium-stack generalization is 12.2-EK-2: the COVERED medium BB's value 3-bet is effectively KK+ (AK borderline), while QQ–TT/AQ isn't a fold but a PREMIUM FLAT — a flat that protects the range; the bluff slice is almost closed. For a COVERING BB it's the opposite: the value top is narrow, the bluff comes from the offsuit bottom.
- **"Open up to 60% into a 15–25bb BB while you're the one covering"** — the sizing-and-material layer is 12.4-EK: against a short BB whose response is size-inelastic (playing strict jam-or-fold), the size stays small (~min-raise *(calibrate)*) — a bigger size doesn't increase the fold rate, it just costs you more when you get jammed on; 2.5–3x only starts to matter once the BB can actually CALL a min-raise on price, cutting off the cheap call (12.2-EK-5). The material is chosen by how the players behind respond (blocker offsuit vs. suited). Pressure on locked-in medium stacks, though, is sold through FREQUENCY, not size (21.7-EK) — the size of a wide open can stay small *(calibrate)*. 23.14's tension — "the pool's BB over-jams in the short band" — applies exactly as written.
- **17.8 Mode D's "part of the open goes straight to jam"** — at ≥20bb, if the players behind cover you, the open-jam range closes and only raise-call plus raise-fold remain (12.1-EK-3); a direct jam belongs only to a covering / big-ante context.

The B17 module itself doesn't change; this appendix only gives an address.

*(Note: since B17 is the WSOP Online module, this appendix belongs there; it doesn't change the B0-B16 live doctrine.)*

### 17.4-EK-2 The offsuit bluff 3-bet floor maps to the lowest value pair — depth gate first: at 56bb, 17.4's bluff stays SUITED


**Depth gate (this first):** this floor rule applies only to nodes where the offsuit bluff 3-bet is genuinely in play — the band where the response to a shallow non-all-in 3-bet collapses to jam-or-fold, playability resets to zero and only the blocker counts (B31.13, roughly 25bb — HU-derived, but the mechanism is depth: the response collapsing to jam-or-fold), and the ICM seat of the covering BB (B12.2-EK-2: "bluff from the LOWEST offsuit"). 17.4's own node (~56bb) is NOT that band: 17.4 explicitly defines the bluff side as "good blocker + **playability**," and the table's bluff column is suited start to finish (A5s–A2s, K9s–KTs, QTs, J9s, T9s, 87s). The direction is fixed in the book: suited when deep, the same ranks' offsuit when shallow (B31.13; same direction in B4.7/B14.2 — as you drop out of the band, the first thing the table drops is the suited-connector 3-bet bluff). In the band in between, offsuit trash does NOT step in — the bluff slice narrows instead: B14.1's 40–60bb row says "nearly cut" for the bluff 3-bet, and 17.8 Mode B also says "bluff 3-bet drops, value rises." If the rule is applied to 17.4 literally, the lowest value pair is **99** vs CO/BTN and **TT** vs MP/HJ, so the floor would fall to 9x/Tx and K9o/Q9o/J9o bluff 3-bets would become legitimate — but 17.11 raises even the BTN **OPEN** off the same offsuit trash (Q9o, J9o). So at 56bb this entry doesn't select bluff material — it only gives an address.

When you ARE inside the band (the shallow jam-or-fold node / the covering BB seat), the construction anchor: find the lowest value 3-bet pair in your range — offsuit trash stops at that card's rank. If value ends at JJ, the offsuit bluff ends at Jx; T9o/T8o don't make the list. On top of that, the ladder principle: use Ace-combos slightly more often than King-combos, King-combos slightly more often than Queen-combos — because the value side (AA/AK/AQs) is already Ace-heavy, so when the bluff side leans on the same cards, villain's 4-bet value never finds a "clean" combo *(exception: in the covering BB seat, the A-blocker is marginal — the A cuts both the fold range and the jam range, B12.2-EK-2)*. It won't hold exactly, but it's the one anchor memorizable at the table *(deviations: calibrate)*.

**Rule:** The offsuit bluff 3-bet's lower bound is the rank of the lowest value pair; distribute with the Ace > King > Queen ladder *(roughly; in the covering BB seat the A-blocker is marginal)* — but ONLY in the band where the offsuit bluff is actually in play (the shallow jam-or-fold 3-bet node / the covering BB in ICM). At ~56bb, 17.4's bluff side stays suited: the floor doesn't drop to 99/TT, and K9o/Q9o/J9o don't open.

### 17.4-EK-3 3-bet bluff — block the live players still behind you too, plus freedom to simplify


At a full table (with many players still to act behind you), the sole yardstick for choosing a bluff 3-bet isn't the opener's fold — the hand you pick also blocking the players behind's likely 4-bet/jam menu (hands like KK, AK) multiplies its value; look not just for "a good blocker" but for "a blocker that blocks multiple future actors." Second point: trying to memorize hyper-sensitive combo-level mixes (which single suited-Ax combo is fully pure and which isn't) demands disproportionate effort for a marginal gain — picking a few clean combos and playing them pure gets you almost the same result. One exception: if you're playing the same opponents repeatedly, or you're on record (stream/final table), keep the frequency tighter.

**Rule:** In an early-position bluff 3-bet, widen the blocker choice with the question "how many live players are behind, and am I blocking their 4-bet menu too?"; simplifying combo-level precision is cheap — except vs a repeat opponent or on record.

### 17.7-EK The bluff's mirror test — if the fold list is short, there's no bluff


17.7 tests river value with a single question ("which hand weaker than mine pays?"); the bluff's mirror question is missing from the book: before a big river bluff/jam, write out loud the list of "hands BETTER than mine that WILL FOLD." If the list amounts to only one or two hand classes (e.g. just a handful of QJ/QT-type combos), pass on the bluff — it means there's no fold mass to buy with the risk. Side note — the side that's missing is the CANDIDATE side, not the fold side: a low-preflop-aggression (nit) range doesn't naturally bring air/busted candidates to the river, so its bluff is forced to come from "higher up" in the range (expensive — you have to convert a hand with showdown value into a bluff). The fold-list, meanwhile, does NOT shrink with a nit image — it grows instead: a big river bet from a known-tight player gets credit, and villain folds good hands too *(calibrate)* — the same direction already exists in the book on villain's side, a range that defended very tight preflop is prone to over-folding on the river (B29.5's rationale for the delayed stab). In your normal range, though, there's a better natural candidate on nearly every runout — if you can't find a candidate, that's a message about your range, not your line.

**Rule:** If the value test is "who pays with a bad hand," the bluff test is "who folds a good hand" — if the fold-list doesn't exceed one or two hand classes, pass on the bluff.

### 17.10-EK The narrative guardrail — "accumulate without confrontation" and "double up or bust" are two faces of the same mistake


Both narratives mortgage decision quality in advance. The "I'm staying out of big confrontations and accumulating" narrative pushes you to the passive side on every close decision — but you don't get to choose whether the next hand turns into a giant pot; the right play is sometimes a big bluff, sometimes a big call, and a player who sits down with that narrative is closed to both. The "I'm at 20bb, I need to double up" narrative, meanwhile, makes you stop taking your stack seriously — tournaments are won a few bb at a time, and a player with one foot out the door doesn't play 15–30bb with precision. The third trap is a reference point: neither your starting stack nor your peak stack is a scoreboard; whatever stack you have, the game is that stack's best decision.

**Rule:** Play the hand, not the narrative: a tournament day is ~hundreds of decisions, and only the one in front of you is yours — the stack is a decision table, not a scoreboard.

### 17.13-EK The personal-deviation compass — break a borderline decision against your own habit


In drills, log the DIRECTION of your mistakes, not their shape: do you always skip the 3-bet bluff, always over-jam, always give up a street too early? For every decision that stays borderline at the table, the compass is a single question: "which direction do I typically miss in this spot?" — and break the tie AGAINST that direction. On decisions whose EV is already close, this files down a systematic leak at zero cost; since regs who know you have already priced in your deviation, occasionally playing against it on purpose also refreshes your image.

**Rule:** On borderline decisions, break the tie against your own known deviation.

### 17.11-EK-3 Manage your own timing — a snap action gives away free information


17.11-EK gave you how to READ villain's timing+sizing chain; the other side of the coin is closing your own chain. A snap-check is an admission of "I have no value, my raise menu is empty"; a half-pause then check reads as "he thought about it and gave up" — take a fixed tempo even when the decision is obvious, and spend that time replaying the hand from the start (street sizes, villain's tempos). Give your easy folds the same tempo too; if tempo only stretches on hard hands, the hard hands give themselves away.

**Rule:** Let every action have one tempo — information leaks through tempo VARIANCE before it leaks through frequency and sizing.

### 17.11-EK-4 Bubble anxiety is the bill for an off-table mistake — don't pay it at the table


If you notice that a min-cash on the bubble would genuinely relieve you, the mistake isn't at the table, it's in the registration: you played too big, or held too much of your own action — this is a professionalism problem, not a mental-game problem, and it doesn't get solved mid-hand. Protocol: note the signal, set it aside, return to the single decision in front of you; fix the buy-in/stake policy AFTER the tournament. The same frame applies to the fear of "looking stupid": if you're cutting an aggressive line you believe is correct just because people are watching, that's a signal you don't know the spot well enough — not a reason to fold at the table, but an item for your study list once you're home.

**Rule:** Anxiety is a signal, not a decision input: note it, park it, return to the highest-EV play; cut its root outside the tournament.

### 17.15 Eve of the FT — not a sleep expectation, but sleep hygiene + mental exit


On the night before a final table, the expectation "I must get perfect sleep" generates anxiety on its own and usually doesn't hold up — focus on hygiene instead of the expectation: put the screen down, no stimulants/heavy alcohol late, and instead of forcing sleep, pick an activity that will get your mind OUT of the tournament (a familiar/already-watched show, an audiobook, chatting with someone who doesn't know poker, a short walk without electronics). The goal is first to stop thinking about the tournament, then to let sleep come without forcing it.

**Rule:** Don't target sleep — target hygiene and mental exit; the expectation of "perfect sleep" is itself the source of anxiety.

### 17.15-EK Eve of the FT — a blanket "I'm busy" boundary for people close to you, set up in advance


Responding one-by-one and instantly to well-wishing messages in the hours before/during a final table is a waste of time and attention; don't spend your prep and rest time on it. Set up a single blanket message/expectation that applies to everyone (close or distant) — "I'm prepping for the final table right now, I'll get back to you after" — and turn this into a habit IN ADVANCE, before your first final table; once your circle learns the rhythm once, you won't even need to offer a justification.

**Rule:** In the final-table window (prep + play), answer messages as a batch and with delay — set the boundary in advance, so you don't need to explain yourself in the moment.

### 17.15-EK-2 No new concepts on the eve of the FT — the one exception: a rare stack distribution specific to your table


Trying to learn a new concept, a new c-bet size, a new BvB idea in the hours/night before a final table doesn't make you better in that moment — it only adds noise and anxiety; the goal is to execute your existing skill at its best, not to improve yourself that night. One exception: if you know you're genuinely going to face a rare/unusual stack distribution at tomorrow's table (e.g. two big stacks massively covering the rest of the table, or 1-2 extremely short stacks at the table with the rest very deep) — reviewing that specific, known distribution a few times in advance is worthwhile, because you'll walk straight into that exact spot in the first few orbits.

**Rule:** On the eve of the FT, general study is forbidden — do only execution refresh (light, familiar repetition); the only exception is a rare stack-distribution spot you're certain to face the next day.

### 17.15-EK-3 Camera/nerves — don't suppress, reframe as excitement, everyone's in the same boat


Final-table pressure, the camera, and a rising pulse are a normal reaction; trying to suppress it, or the expectation "I shouldn't be affected at all," makes the reaction snowball. Reframe it: frame it as something exciting, not as fear — and remember that even experienced players feel the same thing, and that this is not a sign of inexperience.

**Rule:** Don't suppress your FT pulse, relabel it as "excitement"; everyone feels it, it's not a sign of weakness.

*(Note: since B17 is the WSOP Online module, this addendum belongs there; it does not change the live doctrine of B0-B16.)*

## Chapter 18 — Flop C-bet: Frequency, Board Texture, Size

*★ v6. The street where the root error is BORN. B4.2 gave preflop sizing, B11 gave turn/river execution, B13 gave multiway; but the flop c-bet decision itself — which board, at what frequency, at what size — was scattered (only the 17.11 seed "1/3 flop = range-bet" existed). This chapter opens that seed. The flop c-bet is where the seed of the bloated pot is planted: a wrong c-bet → a pot you inflated yourself → a bluff-catcher on turn/river (B11). The right c-bet cuts the error on the flop.*

### 18.0 The one-sentence thesis

> **In a rec-heavy field the default c-bet frequency is BELOW GTO. A c-bet is fired for a REASON — value or real fold equity — not as an automatic "range-bet" reflex.**

A GTO solver c-bets at high frequency on many boards because a balanced opponent folds. In the Main/EPT field the opponent is a station — fold equity drops. C-betting air into a station burns money; worse, once called you enter a pot you inflated yourself as a bluff-catcher — the root error. Compass: **do they pay (value), or do they actually fold (fold equity)?** If both are "no," check.

### 18.1 Two reasons — why a c-bet is fired

| Reason | When it holds | In the rec field |
|---|---|---|
| **Value** | A weaker hand will pay | Strong — the station pays, even thin value is collected |
| **Fold equity** | A better hand folds / a draw gives up the call | WEAK — the station doesn't fold |

In the rec field the c-bet's center of gravity shifts toward value. Save the "for fold equity" c-bet only for genuinely foldable opponents (an aggressive reg, a dry board, a tight BB).

### 18.2 Board texture → frequency and size

You're the preflop raiser (PFR), IP. "Range advantage" decides whom the board favors: a high-card dry board favors your opening range; a low-connected board favors the BB who called.

| Board type | Example | Range advantage | Frequency | Size |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Dry, high-card, disconnected** | A-7-2 r, K-8-3 r, Q-9-4 r | Yours | High — range-bet | **1/3** |
| **Dry paired** | 8-8-3 r, K-K-4 | Yours | High (cheap fold eq) | **1/3** |
| **Middling, one draw** | J-9-6 r, T-8-5 one-tone | Mixed | Selective | 1/3–1/2 |
| **Wet, connected** | 9-8-7, T-9-6 two-tone, 7-6-5 | NOT yours | Low, polarized | **2/3** (value+nut-draw), rest check |
| **Monotone** | K-8-3 one-tone | Ambiguous | Low, careful | small / check |
| **Low-connected (BB's board)** | 7-6-4, 6-5-3 | BB's | Check-leaning | most hands check |

> **Dry board = small size, wide range. Wet board = big size, narrow range. The board sets the size, not your hand.**

### 18.3 Which hand c-bets — four classes

| Hand class | Dry board | Wet board |
|---|---|---|
| **Value (top pair+, overpair, set, two pair)** | Bet (1/3, thin value included) | Bet (2/3, the hand you WANT to bloat with) |
| **Real/nut draw (nut FD, OESD+overcard)** | Bet (semi-bluff, 1/3) | Bet (2/3, paid heavily when it completes) |
| **Air + backdoor/blocker** | Small range-bet (part of the range) | GIVE UP — check-fold; the board favored villain |
| **Marginal showdown (2nd pair, weak top pair)** | Small bet if paid, else check-back | Check-back — pot control |

Barreling air into a wet board = the flop seed of the root error. If the board favors villain's range, your air has no fold equity; check everything except thin value and real draws.

### 18.4 Sizing logic — 1/3, 2/3, check-back

- **1/3 (small):** dry-board range-bet; cheap fold equity + thin value; **preserves the check-fold luxury on the river** (B11: every time you grow the pot your buying power for that luxury shrinks — this B11 rule starts on the flop).
- **2/3+ (big):** wet-board polarized; only the hands you WANT to bloat with — strong value + nut-draws.
- **Check-back:** marginal showdown value; wet/multiway board; pot control + a free card.

> **Size = the price of your river plan. If you fired 2/3 on the flop you've narrowed the SPR for the turn second barrel and the river; don't walk that road with air (B11.1).**

### 18.5 IP vs OOP

| Situation | IP (position yours) | OOP (position villain's) |
|---|---|---|
| **C-bet freedom** | Freer — control + realize equity | More selective |
| **Preferred line** | Range-bet dry, polarize wet | Prefer check-call / check-raise; OOP range-betting air is a TRAP |
| **Root-error risk** | Low | High — an OOP bloated pot is the worst bluff-catcher spot |

An OOP c-bet cuts value against a station but doesn't shut the door on an aggressor; so check-call with marginal hands and check-raise with strong ones is sturdier. C-betting air OOP drops you into a bloated pot out of position — the root error itself.

### 18.6 The multiway exception

In a 3+ way pot this chapter is suspended: **flop c-bet frequency COLLAPSES** (B13.1). No range-betting multiway; only strong value + a real nut-draw. Detail in Chapter 13.

### 18.7 Calibration

*(calibrate: split your last 20 hands into three board textures — dry/middling/wet — and derive your own c-bet frequency in each. At a rec-heavy table target ~10–15% below the GTO baseline; fold equity is low.)*

### 18.8 Cheat card

| Board | Frequency | Size | What happens to air |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Dry high/paired** | High | 1/3 | part of the range-bet |
| **Middling one-draw** | Selective | 1/3–1/2 | give up most |
| **Wet connected** | Low polarized | 2/3 | check-fold |
| **Multiway (any board)** | Collapses | value/nut | none |

### 18.9 Drill — flop c-bet (4 questions)

1. **F1.** IP, single-raised pot, board A-7-2 rainbow. What do you do with your whole range, what size, why?
2. **F2.** IP, 3-bet pot, KK, board 9-8-7 two-tone. C-bet or check? Size and rationale (count the SPR — B11).
3. **F3.** OOP (3-bet from the SB, BTN calls), AK, board Q-6-3 rainbow — you caught nothing. Line?
4. **F4.** 3-way pot, IP, top pair good kicker, board J-9-4 two-tone. How do frequency and size change (B13)?

*Root-error tie-in: this chapter disciplines the street where the error is BORN. B4 lowers the error's frequency preflop; B18 cuts the seed of the bloated pot with the flop c-bet decision; B11 delivers the execution once the error moment arrives. The three are one chain.*


---

### 18.0-EK Two layers: theory-baseline first, THEN the rec deviation


B18.0 says "automatic range-bet is forbidden" — true, but read narrowly it sounds like it also forbids the solver-approved single-size simplification. The two don't conflict — they're separate layers:

1. **Layer 1 (theory-baseline):** on solver-approved dry boards, a full-range single small size is a SIMPLIFICATION — easy to learn, error-free to execute, small exploit surface. Build this and verify it against total money: if you collapse to one small size, raise the frequency so the total money you put in (frequency × size) stays close to what the solver puts in.
2. **Layer 2 (rec-exploit deviation):** B18.0's "below-GTO frequency" is the deviation added ON TOP of this baseline — you cut air c-bets because fold equity is low.

> **What's forbidden isn't the solver-approved baseline, it's REFLEX range-betting without looking at the board. In the rec field the default is NEVER automatic range-bet — build the baseline with a single size, then per B18.7 pull the frequency 10-15% BELOW that. Conflating the two layers makes it feel like the two rules cancel each other out.**

### 18.0-EK-2 The 3-bet-pot exception: the caller over-folds to a small bet


"Fold equity is low in the rec field, the station doesn't fold" is the rule for a single-raised pot. In a 3-bet pot theory calls for wide defense against a small bet (cheap price); but on the felt, once the caller's narrow, medium-strength range misses the flop it MOSTLY over-folds *(calibrate)* — that's a population deviation, not theory. The theoretical justification for the air-c-bet restriction loosening in a 3-bet pot is range advantage + low SPR (at low SPR even a small bet builds stack-off pressure, and because the caller punishing with a raise means committing, that gets hard); the conditions for a full-range small bet are in B32.3 (see B32.0, B32.3).

### 18.0-EK-3 A small bet into the BOTTOM of a wide range: frequency climbs ABOVE GTO


18.0 says "in the rec field frequency is below GTO"; 18.0-EK-2 added that the caller over-folds to a small bet in a 3-bet pot. The single-raised pot has the same deviation, and it bounds 18.0. The station's stickiness lives in pairs and Ax, not at the bottom of the range. The bottom of a wide defending range — suited K9/Q9/J9 without a backdoor, off-suit-of-the-flush suited trash, offsuit hands like KJo/QTo — should in theory continue often against a small bet; the pool doesn't, it folds *(calibrate)*. Once the bottom folds, the "bet or check" gap for every hand that was already close (backdoor air, weak Ax, a medium pocket pair) widens the bet's EV → small-bet frequency climbs above the theoretical mix, close to a range-bet on a low/middling DISCONNECTED (dry) board. Exception: a connected low board — there the wide range doesn't miss the board, it connects (connectors, small pairs) → 18.0 holds, checking rises.

Triple condition: (a) villain's range is WIDE — a blind defense in a single-raised pot (BB's flat against a BTN/CO open, an SB-vs-BB blind war); a 3-bet pot isn't this item's subject, that's 18.0-EK-2; (b) the board missed villain's mass (low/middling DISCONNECTED, low monotone, paired); (c) the size is SMALL. Miss any of the three and 18.0 is back in force: the bottom of a tight EP-vs-BTN flatting range is pairs and suited Ax — nothing there folds.

> **As the range widens, small-bet frequency climbs above theory; as it narrows, it falls below. 18.0's "below GTO" is correct against a narrow/structured range and a dynamic board; it's wrong against the bottom of a wide range on a static/paired/monotone board.**

### 18.1-EK Build order: value picks the size — and the protection paradox


Build the range in the same order every time: first which hands are value, then the size THAT value wants, then coverage, and bluffs LAST — bluffs ride the size value picked, the size never picks itself from bluffs. This order also filters out the third, false reason people try to smuggle into 18.1's two-reason table: "betting for protection." If you bet every hand that CAN bet for protection, the range left unprotected is the check range — that's where the root error is born.

### 18.1-EK-2 The limit of "protection is a false reason" + the low-pairs compass


The protection paradox holds at high SPR (single-raised pot, commitment far away); the nut advantage isn't the axis of that limit — in a shallow 3-bet pot, SPR draws the limit. In the fragile overpair mass where the 3-bettor has the RANGE (overpair) advantage but the nut is usually with the CALLER (set/two pair) — 77-JJ-type on a low, disconnected board — protection-value is a real reason: not because of a nut advantage, but because the money goes in before it melts away at shallow SPR; its name is "value taken before it melts" (see B32.4). What grows the size isn't fragility but SPR: this mass is merged, at 100bb the size shrinks and the frequency grows; shallow, the size comes from geometric/commit sizing *(calibrate)*. This is also where "value picks the size" applies to the 3-bet-pot case: the low pairs set the CEILING of the size, not the floor — if the low pairs want to bet, go linear-small; if they don't, go polar-big and check the low pairs; the compass picks the size, range advantage picks the frequency (see B32.2).

### 18.2-EK Two blind spots in the size heuristic


B18.2 is a solid starting rule but works backward in two spots:

1. **Causation: the size is set by what VALUE needs, not by the board.** "The board sets the size" is a proxy — what actually decides is (a) how many streets the value range needs to carry across and (b) who HOLDS the nut advantage; board texture just represents those. Blind spot: the table reads size off board texture alone and doesn't see whose range the board favors, or position. The "dry → 1/3" row holds in a tight EP range (every overpair at full weight) — but in a node where that same dry texture plays in the BB's favor (a wide BB-defense range), that small bet is an over-c-bet; checking should rise (the remaining bets polarize, the size doesn't shrink). The table can't tell the two cases apart because it only looks at "dry or wet." Ask "how many streets does my value need, and whose favor is the board" first, and derive the size from that.

2. **Rainbow = the home of the big size (in wide-range pots).** The BIGGEST (overbet/geometric) c-bet sizes cluster on RAINBOW/static boards, not on flush-draw boards — most players assume the opposite (the "wet → 2/3" default still holds on dynamic textures; the family that shifts to a static board is the overbet class). In wide-range pots (HU, blind wars, BB defense) when the overpair/best-Tx advantage is large, a large-polar size on a dry rainbow board is correct; the "dry = 1/3" reflex leaves equity-edge on the table in these pots.

> **On a dry board, small size STAYS the default — but the reason isn't "the board is dry," it's "the advantage is spread across the whole range and not concentrated in the nut"; when the nut advantage is large AND villain is capped, a dry board is the home of the giant (overbet) size.**

### 18.2-EK-2 The A-high and monotone rows in a 3-bet pot


B18.2's table is for a single-raised pot. A-high isn't one bucket in a 3-bet pot: a disconnected A-high goes full-range small; A + a second broadway splits into big/small by the low-pairs compass; a wheel-connected A-high sees the check share rise and small-bet frequency fall at 100bb *(calibrate)* — the caller's wheel/set share gets priced in at depth (see B32.3). The monotone row ("ambiguous / small-check") opens into three buckets in a 3-bet pot (3-bettor OOP), and the divider is NUT-flush ownership: A + two low monotone is check-weighted / small mixed *(calibrate)*; a no-A disconnected middling monotone, where the 3-bettor holds a share of A♠x nut-flush/nut-FD → mostly small; where the caller's density of structured non-nut flushes plus straight/combo-draws rises (a T-9-8-type connected middling board; the 3-bettor still holds the A♠x nut-FD share) → check-weighted, a small poke at the margin; on a K-high monotone the 3-bettor keeps the nut-FD/nut-flush share, and the caller's non-nut-flush density plus the overpair's loss of value again keeps it check-weighted *(calibrate)* (see B32.3).

### 18.2-EK-3 Depth ↔ size: the direction depends on the texture


18.2's table sizes are read for 60bb+ / IP-vs-BB; once depth changes, the size moves in OPPOSITE directions depending on texture. The mechanism is villain's raising capacity.

- **Dry/paired (8-8-3 r type):** deep, BB's continuation is raise-weighted (even backdoor trash raises) → small bet + high check.
