As it gets shallower, raise-fold candidates shrink, and the raises that remain mostly turn into jams → a medium/big size is mostly safe, and you collect thin value from weak Ax/Kx *(calibrate)*. The table's "1/3" is for deep.
- **Wet/connected (9-8-6 r type):** deep, even a half-pot-ish polar bet that eats a check-raise mostly wants a turn jam that's overbet-the-pot — rare. At 40bb the same size + check-raise → turn SPR≈1 → BB jams open-ended straights/overcard-draws, and you fold your equity hands → SHRINK the size so the check-raise also comes in small, so the turn brings a one-third bet instead of a jam. The table's "2/3 polar" can work backward at the 30-60bb baseline; small at 40bb *(calibrate)*.

The test sentence has two conditions: *"Is villain's equity-carrying raise FREQUENT — and does a jam follow on the turn after the raise?"* If both are yes, lower the flop size (wet texture); if the raise candidate is already scarce (dry/paired, shallow), the test doesn't trigger (18.4's "size = the price of the river plan" row is read against depth).

> **Mostly: dry/paired → size GROWS as it gets shallower; wet/connected → size SHRINKS as it gets shallower. Don't compress both into a single "dry small / wet big" row.**

### 18.2-EK-4 The home of the big size is narrowly defined: top card below Q + BB has no flopped straight


18.2-EK point 2 bounded "rainbow = home of the big size" to wide-range pots; the boundary isn't pot type, it's the board, and it opens in BTN/CO-vs-BB too *(calibrate)*. Two conditions: (1) the top card is BELOW Q *(calibrate; the boundary moves between J and T)* (T-7-3 / J-8-4 / 9-6-2 r type) → the QQ-AA overpair mass is at full weight but fragile, and top pair doesn't carry three streets either → both want the money now. On a K-high board (K-8-4 type) the only overpair is AA; on Q-high it's AA-KK — the overpair mass is thin, top pair carries three streets → small range-bet stays. (2) BB can't flop a straight on this board; on T-9-7, offsuit straights live in BB's range → the size doesn't grow, checking grows.

Cleaning up the second mechanism: a big flop bet folds out two-overcard hands like Q-J / K-J → on a J turn your J-8-type bluff becomes top pair AND villain's J's have thinned out; a small flop bet pushes the range toward checking on that same turn. That's why the big-size bluff isn't a two-overcard broadway, it's a single overcard + backdoor (A-5s / K-7s type): K-Q wants a call from Q-8 → small size; A-5s needs to fold out A-8 / Q-8 → big size. On the small size, keep Q-T / J-T as top pair so it can continue when it eats a raise.

> **Big size = "top card < Q *(calibrate; the boundary moves between J and T)*, BB has no flopped straight"; give your bluffs from single-overcard+backdoor. In the pool, the size that gets picked is the smallest one that still folds out the target class *(calibrate)*.**

### 18.2-EK-5 On a low board, texture doesn't pick the size, MASS does: rainbow big, two-tone small


18.2 calls a low-connected board "BB's board → check"; that's for IP against BB. When you're the OOP PFR and the caller is BTN (a narrower, more structured range than BB's), the size is picked by asking: **which of villain's masses is already folding, and which one wants a price for its indifference?**

- **Two-toned low (6-4-2 FD type):** the caller's non-flush suited hands and backdoor-less offsuit hands fold to any size → small size; frequency, though, DROPS relative to rainbow — the FD mass raises the caller's equity, so what's "wide" is only the distribution of the hands you're betting *(calibrate)*. A big bet buys a free fold at a high price.
- **Rainbow low (6-4-2 r type):** almost every one of the caller's hands carries a backdoor FD plus two overcards; a small bet makes none of them indifferent → go pot-size-ish *(calibrate)*. The most expensive blind spot of the "dry → 1/3" reflex; the second reason behind 18.2-EK-4.
- **The target is villain's most crowded offsuit-overcard block:** the target is the weakest combos in that block; the wider that block is and the closer to indifference, the bigger the size — if the Ax block is crowded, go pot-size-ish; on an Ax-light texture where the Kx/Qx block is the target, a smaller size (2/3) is enough *(calibrate)*.
- **Top pair being in your range raises FREQUENCY, not size:** on a 7-x-x board, HJ/CO holds 7x (77, 76s, 87s, 97s, A7s) → frequency rises; on a 4-x-x board (4-3-2 type) 4x is almost absent (44, 54s, aside from A4s) → it collapses.
- **Check-raise doesn't eat value:** on a low rainbow board, putting your overpair into check doesn't cost value — BTN's medium pairs stab into that check; collect the value via check-raise (x/r) instead *(calibrate)*.

> **Big mass that auto-folds → small size; big mass of backdoors/gutshots → pot-size. A low rainbow board is MOSTLY the home of the big size; a low two-tone board is mostly the home of the small one *(calibrate)*.**

### 18.2-EK-6 On a middling board, connectivity flips the size: 9-8-7 small-and-often, 9-5-2 big-and-rare


18.2's "wet connected (9-8-7) → 2/3 polar" row is for IP-vs-BB and rests on the assumption that the board favors BB's wide range. In a structured-range node where the nut is SHARED (OOP PFR vs a BTN flat; in a 3-bet pot, B32.3's "fully connected → check/small" row), the split isn't "is it wet," it's **"is the nut shared?"**

- **Middling-connected (9-8-7 / T-9-8 type):** sets, straights, two pair exist in both ranges; the overpair itself doesn't want a big pot, its highest-EV play is a small bet *(calibrate)*. Any hand with a connection to the board (a gutshot broadway, a backdoor overcard, a pair, an overpair) goes small — villain folds little, but you cash in a razor-thin equity edge. A hand with no connection checks-folds. Drop to 8-7-6 and the offsuit broadways lose their connection → frequency drops too, even AA drifts to check.
- **Middling-disconnected (9-5-2 / 9-4-2 / T-5-2 type):** overpairs are nut-like, villain's continuation is overcards + backdoors → big bet or check; nothing in between. The size is whichever one folds out the backdoor-less offsuit broadways (KQo/KJo/AJo type) *(calibrate)*; the bluff is a Tx/8x-type wrapping the 9x.

> **Small edge + shared nut = small bet, high frequency. Big edge + concentrated nut in your hand = big bet, low frequency. The reflex is the reverse of this; getting it backward on a connected middling board turns your own overpair into your own bluff-catcher (B11).**

### 18.2-EK-7 Three-broadway / A + two broadway (single-raised): a small size folds out nothing


18.2's "dry high → 1/3" row is for a single-broadway board like A-7-2 / K-8-3. On a three-broadway board like K-Q-J FD-type, BB's continuation is Q-x/J-x pairs and T-x/A-x draws (on KQJ, T-x is an open-ended straight draw, and ALL of A-x has a gutshot); none of that folds to a small bet. The right size is the one that pushes Q-x to indifference — pot-size-ish; the 2/3 simplification loses no EV *(calibrate)*. Against a pot-size bet, T-x (the OESD) continues at the margin, kicker-less Q-x is marginal, A-x (the gutshot) is marginal; the first block that folds is offsuit 8-x/7-x (9-x continues with a gutshot to T). A-Q-J reads differently: A-x is top pair (it doesn't fold), K-x is a gutshot rather than a pair, T-x is only a gutshot (folds), the first block that folds is 9-x/8-x.

If villain over-calls this board, change your TURN plan, not the flop size: go pot-size on the turn with most of your range — K-x becomes indifferent, the rest of the Q-x/T-x/gutshots fold; villain's excess flop calls turn a profit on the turn. Bluff construction: a middling pocket pair like 6-6/7-7 is a bad bluff (it doesn't fold better hands, doesn't get calls from worse); low hands that still have two streets left to run (5-4s/6-4s type) are better — BB's first-folding block is offsuit 8-x/7-x, and these don't block it. At 40bb one "big" tier drops out (BB starts fast-playing Q-T/combo draws) but small still doesn't open up; at 15bb checking rises (on KQJ, K-x is the nut and jams; see 18.10).

> **On a single-raised high-broadway board there's no small size; the one size that leaves Q-x indifferent, then pot on the turn. This doesn't contradict B32.5's "three-broadway is SMALL at every depth" row — in a 3-bet pot the nut advantage is thin; in a single-raised pot BB isn't capped on the nut edge (nut-straight T9/KT combos are plentiful even in BB), but its continuation is dense with pairs/draws that don't fold to small → bet frequency drops, size grows.**

### 18.2-EK-8 On an A-high board, the second card picks the size


Don't dump every A-high board into the "A-7-2 → 1/3" bucket; the question is **"is there anything NEXT TO BB's bottom pair?"**

- **Wheel-A (A-4-2 / A-3-2 type):** BB's bottom pair is a pair-plus-gutshot, and wheel straights and 2-2/3-3 sets live in BB's range → a big bet doesn't fold out bottom pair, and BB turns straights/pairs and flips its range into a bluff on the turn, realizing its equity at a high rate → small size, target the auto-folds.
- **Middling-A (A-9-4 / A-8-5 type):** there's nothing next to BB's bottom pair — but bottom pair is already indifferent to a small bet → mostly small/full-range bet; a big size enters the mix when IP's KK-TT/AQ+ density is high and BB's crowd of 9x/8x middle pairs is pronounced *(calibrate)*.
- **A-K-x:** big size, but K-x and Q-Q/J-J-type low pairs CHECK — if they bet, only a better hand pays, and whatever folds was already behind. The big size belongs to A-x plus J-T/Q-T-type gutshot bluffs (weak-kicker K-x and Q-x fold on the flop; K-Q/K-J only fold under turn-river pressure). A-K's share of check-backs isn't a trap, it's range arithmetic — it doesn't block the draws that would turn into bluffs, and it catches them on a check-check river.

The turn is counterintuitive: after a big flop bet gets called, go SMALL on the turn — the target is thin value from middle pairs and weak A-x plus cheap pressure on K/Q-high; "I bet big, so I keep betting big" isn't automatic, and continuing big on a blank turn is also in the mix *(calibrate; B11.1)*.

> **On A-high, the second card asks the question: is there anything next to BB's bottom pair? Yes (wheel/connection) → small and rarer; no (middling-A) → small full-range bet; second card is a broadway (A-K / A-Q / A-J) → big. The 3-bet-pot version is 18.2-EK-2 / B32.3.**

### 18.2-EK-9 Monotone flop: one small size; frequency is picked by "did the board burn villain's flush?"


18.2 treats "Monotone → ambiguous, small/check" as one bucket; 18.2-EK-2 opened that bucket by nut-flush ownership in a 3-bet pot. In a single-raised pot there are two dividers:

- **Size (IP vs BB):** the flush card splits BB's response — non-flush overcards/gutshots/weak pairs fold, flush-card holders and non-flush top pair mostly call. There's no need for a big size (the folders fold to small too), and deep nobody wants to blow up the pot with AA/a set (second-nut is frequent) → a single small size, close to full-range; the size is picked as "the smallest size that folds the non-flush air/weak pair" *(calibrate)*. The pool's BB folds its non-flush hand more than theory calls for → frequency climbs above GTO (18.0-EK-3).
- **Frequency (OOP PFR vs BTN):** the rule is counting — how many of villain's high suited combos (broadway + Ax-suited) did the board itself burn? If most turned into pairs, the flush count is low → high frequency, small bet, few raises (K-T-8 monotone: KT/K8/T8-suited are now pairs); if none burned (a low monotone) → check-weighted *(calibrate)*. Counting alone isn't enough: low suited connectors don't burn, and on a connected monotone their weight rises as both flushes AND straights/two-pair → a connected middling monotone is the most check-heavy end; bet and it's still small, and a flush-card-holding AK/AQ enters the mix *(calibrate)*.

Depth: as it gets deeper, BB starts fast-playing its high flush card → your check share rises; at a short stack, non-flush A-x also drifts to check *(thresholds: calibrate)*. The turn (fourth flush card / brick) is in B11.1.

> **On a monotone flop the first question isn't "who has the nut flush" (that's the 3-bet pot's question), it's "did the board burn villain's high suited hands (broadway + Ax)?" — burned it, bet; didn't, check; the size is small-and-single either way.**

### 18.2-EK-10 Trips board: villain's nut is diluted by the board itself


18.2's "dry paired → 1/3" row is for a pair board; on a trips board the mechanism changes: the board has erased three kickers itself — every matching kicker makes a full house, villain's full-house share is low, and because of kicker dilution the range advantage doesn't turn into a nut advantage *(calibrate)*. The size is picked by asking, "is the defender's non-folding mass Ax, or broadway?"

- **IP, against BB:** strong hands and overpairs want small; air that wants to fold out overcards wants bigger, but the EV gap between the two sizes is negligible → mostly a single medium-small size. Only open a separate size family if the big side is genuinely bigger; if it just wants "a bit more," shrink the size, don't open the family (18.4-EK). A high trips board (K-K-K type) is a better range-bet home than a low trips board: villain's Kx combos (including AK/KQ/KJ) got burned by the board; Ax is the non-folding top-kicker layer, and the fold-rate increase lives in the lower kickers and disconnected combos *(calibrate)*. In the rec field, medium-kicker/suited-connected hands fold to a small bet more than theory says → full-range small.
- **OOP, against a tight BTN flatting range, low trips (7-7-7 type):** BTN's mass is offsuit broadway; Ax doesn't fold → the target is making everything but Ax indifferent → a size that leans big (around 2/3, *calibrate*). On high trips it's small again: the board blocks one leg of villain's broadway mass by itself, and the target layer already folds to small. In this node OOP's EV is usually higher than IP's — a rare reversal in a single-raised pot *(calibrate)*.

The defending side (the kicker-liveness threshold) is in B26.1.

> **On a trips board the size mostly shrinks and frequency rises. The one exception is a tight-range OOP low-trips spot — there, a big-leaning size that makes villain's offsuit-broadway mass indifferent *(calibrate)*.**

### 18.2-EK-11 On an A-high board, OOP as the OPENER (single-raised, ~30bb): you have the Ax advantage but not the realization — A-low checks, A+broadway full-range small, A-middle+FD 2/3


Three seats, three answers — 18.2 is IP PFR, 32.3 is the 3-bettor, this item is OOP opener vs IP caller *(frequencies: calibrate)*. The reflex "A-high, the opener's board → bet" confuses the seat.

- **A-low (A-5-2 / A-6-2 r):** the opener's non-Ax range is dead — KTo/QJo/JTo have neither a pair nor a draw; the caller's pocket-pair-heavy range connects 2 through 6, and 44/55/66 don't fold to a bet. Few hands want to bet, few hands fold to it → check-weighted (roughly two-thirds). Villain's stab into your check, and the answer to it, is in 19.4-EK-2.
- **A + broadway (A-J-6 / A-T-8):** everything flips — the opener's whole range picks up a pair or a gutshot, the caller's pocket-pair-heavy range dies (77's EV is near zero) → close to full-range small bet.
- **A-middle + FD (A-8-6 FD):** the size climbs to 2/3 — a small bet always keeps backdoor-FD combos (Q9♣/J9♣ type) in, 2/3 pushes them to indifference; on a rainbow A-6-2 those combos already fold, so small is enough. If the middle card pairs the caller's suited block (8x on A-8-6), the size grows; A-7-6 is more passive.

> **The question isn't "do I have the A?" it's "what is my non-Ax range doing on this board?"**

### 18.2-EK-12 A dense range doesn't fold to a small bet on a dry board


18.2-EK-4's rule "rainbow → pot-size, two-tone → small" was for low boards; the engine is the same, and if villain's range is dense (pockets + suited, no auto-folding trash) it carries over to Q/J/T-high too — a covered tight opener vs a covering wide-flatting SB in an ICM spot, or a covering wide opener vs a covered tight-flatting SB, both produce this range shape:

- **Rainbow/dry (Q-7-3 r, J-3-3, J-6-2, T-7-2, Q-6-4 r):** a small bet makes nobody indifferent — every suited hand has a backdoor, every pocket pair is a pair. Half-pot or 3/4 pot, or check; no small size *(calibrate)*. Even a paired J-J-7 is no exception.
- **Flush-drawy (Q-7-4 FD, Q-J-2, 7-6-5 FD):** non-flush combos and backdoor-less offsuit hands fold to any size → small is enough, the range is wide.
- **The test:** "Who does the small bet fold out?" Only trash → grow the size or check. If the target is the backdoor-suited mass, only a half pot brings them to indifference.

> **A dense range has no auto-fold on a dry board; the small bet is aimless there. Rainbow/dry → half-pot-or-check, flush-drawy → small. It's not the board's height that picks the size, it's villain's folding mass.**

### 18.3-EK The thin-value threshold is derived from villain's range — look at the distribution inside the class, not its name


The thin-value call isn't a feeling, it's a rewind: write down the continuing percentage your size forces, accept that you need to beat the better half of the range that ends up calling, and roll that back into a turn threshold *(calibrate)*. And count WITHIN the class: since typical ranges play AXs with every kicker, the top end of the flush class in villain's range is combinatorially crowded — A-high combos clearly outnumber the low flushes — so even if villain "pays with every flush," you may still not beat the average. This is where the root error is born: you look at the class by name and bet, when you should have looked at the distribution inside the class and checked.

### 18.3-EK-2 If villain's continuation is a raise, the bet is "anti-protection": on a high paired board, raw-equity hands check back


18.3's "dry board → small range-bet with air" column assumes villain's continuation is CALL-weighted. On a high-card paired board like K-K-x/Q-Q-x/K-5-5, BB's natural call is scarce (few hands connect with the board, the rest are either trash or trips) → the continuing range is weighted toward RAISE *(calibrate)*. Bet a medium hand small into a raise-weighted node and both reasons die at once: there's no thin value (nothing worse than you is calling — it's either folding or raising), and there's no protection (once you eat the raise, you're the one folding the best hand). Betting a medium hand here is putting chips in and getting them stolen.

When the continuation is raise, the bet POLARIZES: value that can continue against a raise, plus air that gives up cheaply once it eats a raise — a zero-showdown-value hand gets frequent folds with a small bet, and if it faces a raise it only loses the small bet. The only thing cut is the medium/raw-equity hand: Q-J/A-high/a low pocket pair goes to check-back, realizing on the turn by calling villain's small bet. It scales with position: BB keeps its check-raise tight against an EP opener, and is ready to stack off with top pair against a BTN opener → the check share of a low pair/medium hand rises against BTN. Against a tight check-raiser, DON'T call a raise with your pocket pairs — paying off the nit is this node's classic leak. 18.2's "K-K-4 → 1/3" row gives the right size; this item tells you which hand the frequency gets cut from.

> **The reason for the bet is derived from the SHAPE of villain's continuing range; if the continuation is a raise, the bet polarizes — value that can continue against a raise plus air that gives up cheap, medium hand checks back. A low paired board (3-3-2 type) and an A-paired board (A-Q-Q type — BB's Ax is the top-pair call block) have a call block, and range-bet holds there — "paired" isn't a single bucket.**

### 18.3-EK-3 On a wet middling board, the bet's license isn't the kicker, it's SIDE EQUITY


18.3's "marginal showdown → small bet if it gets paid" row is for a dry board. On a wet middling board (8-6-5 FD type), the "top pair = bet" reflex bets K-8/Q-8 and, once it eats a check-raise, is comfortable neither folding nor calling — it's a two-street bluff-catcher. The right divider isn't hand strength, it's **"can I continue against a raise"**: top pair + a straight draw (OESD/gutshot) like 7-8/9-8 calls a raise (the turn's middle cards improve it, high cards don't improve villain) → bet; Q-8/K-8 has nothing to do against a check-raise → check-back, a free turn. The same filter applies to high cards: air like A-K/K-Q gets called/raised by the hands it beats and folds out the hands it was already ahead of → the bet has no bluff value, check.

The overpair flips depending on depth: at 100bb, AA/KK is dead to value and marginal to bluffs in the bet-raise node; 9-9/T-T wants protection and blocks value (9-9 also carries a gutshot to 7 plus a 9x blocker) — against a check-raise it's just as marginal as AA/KK, and the difference isn't comfort continuing, it's the need for protection → the medium pocket pair bets, the biggest pair carries more of the trap/check share *(calibrate)*; at 40bb and below, AA/KK is willing to stack off, so it goes back to betting (18.2-EK-3).

> **On a wet middling board, build the betting range from "what continues if a raise comes"; it's not top pair's kicker that decides, it's its extra equity. If villain is aggressive, check the weak draws and bet the nut-FD/strong top pair; if villain is passive, add the weak draws to the betting range too.**

### 18.3-EK-4 On a low board, the bluff candidate isn't "the best air," it's "the air that folds out better air"


On a low board like 5-4-2 r, c-betting K-Q/K-J/A-J as "two strong overcards" only folds hands that were already trash — A-high and better K-x continue → a bet that doesn't fold the better hand and doesn't get paid by the worse one. Middling-high cards like K-6/K-7/Q-8, on the other hand, bet: they fold out BETTER air than themselves (K-6/K-7 folds K-9/K-T, Q-8 folds Q-J/Q-T), carry live equity against the board, and lightly block villain's continuing range with their low card. The pyramid is built upside down: the strongest air realizes by checking (turning into value when a pair/A arrives on the turn), the middling air bets *(mostly — calibrate)*.

A pool note: BB 3-bets hands like K-Qo/K-Jo against a late open less than balance calls for → these hands are more common in BB's range than theory says → the block folded out by the middling-high-card bluff is bigger than theory says *(calibrate)*. This is the flop-c-bet version of B1's "bluff whom, with which hand" question, and runs the same direction as B1.5's "pick from the bottom" principle.

> **On a low board, pick your c-bet bluff by asking "which better air am I folding out?"; if the answer is "none," check with your strongest overcards.**

### 18.4-EK The hidden cost of adding a size family


Adding a size family isn't free: once you move your strongest hands to the overbet line, the small bet loses its protection — villain opens up thin value and bluff-raises into it, and thin-value EV leaks out of that line. This is the hidden benefit of the single-size simplification: because every class sits on the same line, a raise is deterred. If you're going to open the overbet family from 18.2-EK, open it with its cost in mind *(calibrate)* — the polar EV you gain must outweigh the protection you're cutting from the small bet.

### 18.4-EK-2 A big polar bet freezes the action — if villain isn't raising it, drop polar and grow the size


18.4's "2/3+ only the hands you want to bloat the pot with" row has two faces. Against a big polar bet, villain's reopen is unprofitable: a raise runs into the nut, a bluff raise runs into an equity-carrying bluff → villain gets squeezed into call/fold, slowing down even with top pair. Same for a tight IP caller: in theory its raising range against a big c-bet is nearly empty (a draw carries enough equity to call, a set/two pair plays slow against the barrel) → a raise into a big bet is value-heavy, continuing with an overpair is marginal *(calibrate)*; against a small bet the B19.11 logic returns.

Once medium hands come in too (88/A-J-type added to "big range-bet"), the range stops being polar → villain's best response is to cut calling sharply and multiply raising (mostly); the twin of 18.4-EK's "size-family cost." The attacking face: if villain NEVER raises the big bet (most of the pool), the reason for checking back 88/A-J disappears — their check was out of fear of a raise, and against a calling range their bets are higher EV → bet everything; AA/KK, meanwhile, grows its size — if villain won't put the money in for you, put it in yourself, and the overbet class opens up *(calibrate; same direction as B23.11)*.

> **A big size stays polar (medium hands check); if villain isn't raising it, drop polar, bet everything, and scale the size up with the nuts (medium hands go small/medium). A raise into a big c-bet is mostly value.**

### 18.5-EK The limit of the OOP range-bet trap: the 3-bettor in a 3-bet pot


The "OOP range-betting air is a TRAP" row is for a PFR OOP in a single-raised pot. In a 3-bet pot, the range-advantaged OOP 3-bettor fires a full-range small bet on a disconnected A-high — that's not a trap there, it's the baseline; on an A-less disconnected middling monotone board it's MOSTLY a small bet too (as connectivity rises and the caller's flush density, non-nut included, grows, the check share grows — the nut-flush share mostly stays with you via Axs — *calibrate*). The mirror side also holds: on a low-connected board (7-6-5) the OOP 3-bettor mostly checks (some overpairs still bet), and because the IP caller has the nut advantage, its stab frequency rises (see B32.5).

### 18.5-EK-2 The hidden variable of the OOP c-bet: when the caller is BTN, who's the opener?


18.5's table calls OOP "more selective" in a single row; 18.2-EK asks "whose range does the board favor" but doesn't count the opener's position. Against BTN, when you're OOP PFR, this variable flips the frequency:

- **BTN's flatting range isn't BB's.** Around 30bb, BTN almost never finds a 3-bet bluff against an EP open; it flats pairs, suited broadway, AJo-type hands — narrow and structured. There's no "8-2s, J-3o" mass → the "range-bet into a wide-weak range" logic doesn't apply (18.0-EK-3's condition (a) drops out). B5.2's "no flat" is YOUR prescription; this item is for when villain flats.
- **EP opener:** a tight high-card range is ahead in equity against this flatting range, and despite being OOP the realization loss is small *(calibrate)* → frequent c-betting on high/broadway boards, 2/3 when needed; on low/middling boards, see 18.2-EK-5/6.
- **CO opener:** a wide range, still behind in equity against a wider but still structured flatting range; OOP realization drops noticeably *(calibrate)*. The license splits by board: on a dry A/K-high board the nut advantage is still CO's (BTN's premiums got 3-bet away) → frequent small c-bet; on a connected broadway/middling board (K-Q-J/Q-J-T/J-T-9 type) BTN's flatting range connects better → check-weighted — building a good checking range and playing check-call/check-raise beats a c-bet doing a tight-range impression. On low boards both are check-weighted — CO's 7x/6x claws back a little frequency there.

> **The OOP c-bet's license is granted, before the board, by the question "is my opening range stronger than BTN's flatting range?" EP: mostly yes. CO: mostly no → check-weighted — but even behind in equity, the nut advantage grants a license on a dry high board; on a connected board keep the range in the check column.**

### 18.5-EK-3 What picks the OOP c-bet frequency isn't the board, it's the DUST ratio in your own range — EP range-bets, CO checks


18.5's "OOP range-betting air is a TRAP" row holds for a CO/BTN opener; it lifts for EP under the condition of a "dust-free range." 18.5-EK-2 asked about the opener's position; this is its mechanism *(ratios: calibrate)*. 30bb single-raised, OOP opener, BTN caller, a high board like K-J-8/K-T-7:

- **EP:** there's no dust in the range — even 66/77 lives off a backdoor, everything else connects to the board; BTN's calling range against EP doesn't carry KTo/QTo/JTo (that went to a 3-bet or a fold), and is dense with suited Ax + low pocket pairs → it folds noticeably to a small bet → EP goes full-range 1/3, realization climbs noticeably. Overpairs stack chips with T-9-x at 2/3. Range-bet's home is a dry high board (K-8-3/A-J-4 type); on a connected board like K-T-9/Q-J-9, BTN's range connects hard too (hooks, suited connectors, sets/two pair) → even EP mixes in checks, splitting the size.
- **CO:** the range is full of dust — A7o/A5o, 65s/54s, K4s/Q6s; BTN's range against CO carries offsuit broadway at full frequency → it folds less to the same bet, and the middle layer calls → CO is weighted heavily toward CHECK, keeping the range in the check column. It checks the overpair too: BTN stabs frequently into a weak range, and the overpair collects its EV from x/r instead.

Test sentence: *"What fraction of my range is dust — a hand that doesn't want to bet, and doesn't mind folding?"* If the dust ratio is high, the range can't carry its own weak hands, and frequency collapses. This is the range-composition face of 18.2-EK's "dry board favors BB → check" row.

### 18.10 Shallow band (15-25bb) IP c-bet: bet only with a hand that can continue into a jam


This chapter's tables are for 30bb+. At 15-25bb, BB's answer is a flood of jams (the pool jams Qx, draws, pair+draw into both a small bet and a medium one; theory keeps most of it calling) → your betting license comes down to "can I continue into a jam." B3's jam/fold doctrine is settled for the opener at ≤12bb; at 15bb a BTN min-raise tree still lives *(calibrate)*. This is the IP mirror of B19.1-EK and B30.7.

**Checking frequency moves in two directions; the direction is set by how the middle layer stands against villain's jamming range.**
- **Low board (5-4-2 r type):** even offsuit A-x calls a jam (two overcards + backdoor against a pair ≈ marginal) → A-x, which checks deep, starts betting shallow → check DROPS.
- **Middling/high dynamic board (T-8-3/9-8-6/K-Q-J FD type):** BB jams its top pair and its draws; your Q-x/A-high/weak draw wants neither to call nor to fold a jam → check RISES; a board that's a range-bet at 40bb turns into a half-check at 15bb *(calibrate)*.

**Three buckets on a dynamic board (Q-7-6 two-tone type):** hands that don't mind a bet-fold (backdoor air, a weak pair) go small; bet-call hands (top pair, a strong draw) go medium; A-high/a low pocket pair/JJ-type low-overpair check back — AK/AJ eating a jam after a small bet makes calling clearly minus-EV, and bet-fold is expensive too. Top pair's size SHRINKS: villain puts the money in; if villain is flatting Qx instead, the big size comes back — villain's raise frequency picks the size. Draw-vs-value flips: deep, bet the draw and slow-play top pair; shallow, the opposite — top pair is "the nut," fast-play it; if the draw can't call a jam, check it. The pool finds a jam more often than theory says, even shallow → push the check share up one more notch *(calibrate)*.

> **Shallow, bet only with a hand that can continue into a jam. If the middle layer is marginal-ahead of villain's jamming range (low board), check drops; if it's behind (dynamic middling/high board), check rises. This triad doesn't carry over to 30bb+ — there, 18.3 stands as written.**

### 18.6-EK The limit of the multiway-collapse rule: against two CAPPED blinds, the opener approaches a range-bet on a dry high board


18.6's "c-bet collapses multiway" rule is at its strictest against coldcallers whose range is uncapped. If two BLINDS have flatted against the opener, the picture changes: since both of their best hands went to a preflop 3-bet, their flat ranges are capped at the top. On an A-high dry board they DO find the ace — 4.8 puts "all Ax" into the BB's defense, and with two blinds in it isn't rare for one of them to hold an Ax — but there's NO TOP to it: AA/AK/AQ went to a preflop 3-bet, the remaining Ax has a weak kicker and can't carry a second barrel (4.10: "on an A-high flop, low-kicker Ax is mostly a small-bet/check and pot-control hand"). The target here, therefore, isn't a fold but a PRICE: the opener fires at a very high frequency with a small size here despite being 3-way *(calibrate)* — and the turn plan is built not on the assumption "no ace" but on "there's an ace, no kicker" (see the turn row of 18.2-EK-8). As the board gets more connected (connected broadway/middling textures), the collapse rule returns — and it also returns if a fourth player enters: being capped doesn't cancel out the number of opponents (13.3). The second surprise of this same node: at a low SPR, sets aren't forced to bet — since two streets are enough and both opponents are weak, sets mix in some checking.

**Rule:** While the number of opponents stays FIXED (3-way), what triggers the collapse is whether villains' ranges are capped: two capped blinds + a dry high board = small size, high frequency (they have the aces, not the kickers — they'll pay one street); uncapped coldcallers = collapse. The number remains the primary variable (13.3): 4+ way, the collapse returns even if villains are capped.

### 18.6-EK-2 The third reason for a multiway c-bet — no free card for the weakest range


18.6's "frequency collapses" rule stays correct for the bluff end; but when the pot is 3-WAY, the board favors the opener, and there is a SINGLE wide overcaller in the pot (the weakest range), the frequency still collapses — what changes is the SHAPE: a polar bet is an HU luxury, and the COLLAPSED betting range at this node is not polar but merged — the merged range's lower bound is not "medium strength" but **hands that beat the weak range's CALL range**; medium strength below that stays in 13.1 and checks. The boundary is this tight because cheaply realizing the weakest range's equity is a real cost (18.1-EK's "protection is a false reason" rule isn't unlimited — 18.1-EK-2 draws that boundary with SPR; the denial here is the multiway face of that same boundary, not a false one). The two aggressors are effectively dividing labor: if you check, IP bets in your place — this lowers the cost of your check and pulls the betting frequency in two opposite directions; the net result is a merged middle path, neither a range-bet nor a full check. The scope ends here: if a fourth player enters, 13.1 returns with no exception — top pair drops a class, check/pot control; 4+ way, the probability of the bet getting called eats up the denial gain, and the division of labor also falls apart. The bluff end is still dead: zero-equity air doesn't get bet multiway (13.3).

**Rule:** Multiway, the reasons for a c-bet grow to three — value, fold equity, and denying the weakest range a free card. The frequency collapses per 13.1 regardless; 3-way plus a single wide overcaller changes the SHAPE — the collapsed range is merged, not polar, with its lower bound being "a hand that beats the weak range's CALL range." 4+ way, 13.1 returns with no exception: medium strength checks too. Air is off the table regardless.

### 18.3-EK-5 A second filter for picking the bet/bluff combo — block villain's check-raise material

When picking a value/bluff combo, don't just ask "does this not block villain's folds" — also ask whether the card blocks villain's most natural CHECK-RAISE material (the card that completes the flush, a strong combo draw). Between two candidates that look equivalent (say, two different overcard+backdoor combos), the combo carrying the card that feeds villain's check-raise is the safer bet — it cuts raise risk; the other produces the same fold but stays more exposed to a raise.

**Rule:** The "doesn't block villain's folds" test isn't enough on its own; between two equivalent bluff candidates, the combo that blocks villain's check-raise material takes priority.

### 18.4-EK-3 Sizing also depends on your own reading edge — keep wet value+nut-draw outside this calibration

Calibrate flop sizing not only against villain's range but also against your own postflop reading/adaptation edge — but this calibration doesn't touch 18.4's wet-board polarized (2/3+) home: that size already exists TO bloat strong value + nut-draws, and your reading edge doesn't override that call — sizing down sells value cheap and hands a live draw a cheap card. The home of this calibration is the dry/static board and the marginal-showdown class: there, if your reading edge on villain is strong, stay on the small side to keep SPR alive instead of the solver size — that lets you use the edge on the remaining streets, and in this class a big size's payoff is low anyway. If you have no read history but villain is weak (recreational, makes frequent mistakes on later streets), keep SPR high the same way in that same dry/marginal class — your postflop edge is at its biggest against exactly this kind of villain, and sizing down early leaves the EV from their mistakes on the table. Only against an unfamiliar STRONG villain/field (neither a reading nor a skill edge) is it safer to apply the solver size (dry board 1/3, wet polarized 2/3+) verbatim and simplify, because your decisions aren't leaning on fine-grained reads on later streets anyway.

**Rule:** On a wet board, keep value + nut-draws at the solver size (2/3+) — your reading edge doesn't change that call. On a dry/static board and in the marginal-showdown class, if your edge rests on reads or villain is weak, size down and keep SPR high; only against an unfamiliar strong villain apply the solver size (dry 1/3, wet 2/3+) verbatim.

## Chapter 19 — Initiative Lines: Who Bets When the Lead Is Unclear

*★ v6. B18 gave the "lead is yours" case of the flop c-bet. This chapter gathers the six lines where the lead is UNCLEAR: donk, delayed c-bet, probe, stab, turn float, river blocker-bet. All of them are the advanced-aggression zone the book deliberately keeps light; so all are given with a NARROW frame, a shared diagnostic, and a hard "when NOT." Correct initiative ends the pot cheaply (anti-bloat); wrong initiative is a new door into the root error.*

### 19.0 The shared diagnostic

All six lines come down to one question: with the lead unclear, do I take the pot, or realize my hand for free?

> **In the rec field the default = NO invented aggression. Initiative is taken only if (a) someone showed weakness AND (b) the board favors your range AND (c) realizing your hand with a check isn't more profitable. If all three aren't there: check.**

Each line's "when NOT" column matters more than its "when." The shared trap: shoving a marginal made hand (one you should take to showdown with a check) into a bet in the name of initiative — that turns float/probe/stab into the root error.

### 19.1 Donk bet — OOP, leading into the PFR

Donk: OOP, betting out into the preflop raiser instead of checking. It breaks the "the PFR bets" convention.

> **Default: do NOT donk — checking to the PFR is standard. A donk usually telegraphs and inflates a pot OOP (root-error terrain).**

| Variable | Donk OPEN | Donk CLOSED (→ check) |
|---|---|---|
| **Board** | Hits YOUR range: low, connected, low-paired (7-6-4, 5-5-2 you defended in the BB) | High-card board (the PFR's range) |
| **Villain** | Check-folds when it misses the board | A station that never folds to a donk |
| **My hand** | Two-pair/set/straight edge + small size | One pair (a big donk = the root error) |
| **Player count** | HU | Multiway |

**Logic:** a low-connected board hits your BB-defense range far more than the PFR's high-card range; a small donk denies the PFR a free check-back and charges its equity. On a high board a donk is a bet into the PFR's turf — don't.

### 19.2 Delayed c-bet — PFR checks flop, bets turn

Delayed c-bet: you're the PFR, you checked the flop back (pot control / gave up / trap), then bet the turn. This is the DISCIPLINED opposite of B18's automatic c-bet reflex: you skipped the wide flop c-bet and bet the turn only when a REASON appears.

| Bet the turn — reason | Don't bet |
|---|---|
| You improved on the turn (pair/draw) | No reason — don't turn a flop give-up into a turn bloat |
| Villain checked a second time (weakness) | Villain leads/check-raises the turn a lot |
| The turn card favored your range | No equity + no fold equity |

With a marginal made hand: check flop → thin value bet the turn (if checked to). Small size.

### 19.3 Probe bet — OOP bet after the flop checks through

Probe: the flop went check-check (you're OOP, the PFR checked back = showed weakness, range capped). On the turn (or river) you bet.

> **If the PFR gave up the flop, most strong hands would have bet it → its range is capped. On the turn, take the initiative into that capped range with an OOP bet.**

| Probe OPEN | CLOSED (→ check) |
|---|---|
| PFR's check-back is weakness (rec norm) | PFR check-backs strong to trap (rare, tricky reg) |
| The turn card favored your range | Bad turn, missed your range |
| Your hand has no showdown value (a bet is its best use) | Marginal made hand → realize it with a check-call |

Size: small-medium. This is the answer to "flop check-check → can you steal turn/river": yes, against a capped range.

### 19.4 Stab — IP, take the pot when it's checked to you

Stab: you're IP, everyone checks to you (flop or turn). Take the pot nobody wants with a small bet. The most fundamental, lowest-risk initiative tool — if it's checked to you, everyone already showed weakness.

| Stab OPEN | CLOSED |
|---|---|
| HU, checked to you, any equity/fold equity | Multiway (someone's trapping) |
| A board the checkers missed | A board that hits the checkers' range |
| Small size | Check-raise-happy villain / an SD-value hand wants the free showdown |

### 19.5 Turn float — positional delayed aggression

Float: call the flop c-bet in position, then take the pot when the villain checks the turn. The goal isn't showdown, it's a delayed bluff.

| Variable | Float | Don't float |
|---|---|---|
| **Position** | IP (clean takeaway) | OOP — never |
| **Villain** | C-bets the flop wide and gives up the turn | Fires the second barrel (station/aggressive reg) |
| **My hand** | Backdoor draw / overcard / blocker — with a plan | Pure air, no plan |
| **Board** | A dry-ish flop the villain range-bet | Wet — the villain barrels |
| **Player count** | HU | Multiway |

**Execution:** if the turn is checked to you, bet and take it; if the villain fires a second barrel, GIVE UP.

> **A float ends on the turn: you either bet and take it or give up. Drifting on and paying isn't a float, it's the root error.**

### 19.6 River blocker-bet — the exception, not the default

Blocker-bet: OOP on the river, a SMALL bet with a thin bluff-catcher — deny the villain's big polarized bet, buy a cheap showdown.

> **WARNING: the default for this spot is 11.2 — check-call / check-fold. The blocker-bet is a narrow deviation. Against a station check-call is better; a station doesn't "discount" to a small bet, it still pays you with value, and if it raises you're sunk.**

| Variable | Window OPEN | CLOSED (→ 11.2) |
|---|---|---|
| **Villain** | Capped + aggressive/bluffy | Station |
| **My hand** | Thin bluff-catcher (beats bluffs, loses to value) | Clearly strong or clearly trash |
| **River** | Dry/static, range well-defined | Wet/dynamic, villain uncapped |
| **Size** | Small (~20–33%) | A big blocker is a contradiction |

### 19.7 Calibration

*(calibrate: each line hinges on a villain label. Donk & probe & float: open them against villains whose "show weakness then give up" rate is high. Blocker-bet: only against a capped+aggressive labeled villain, NEVER a station. Stab & delayed c-bet: low risk, free HU. No label → default to the passive line / 11.2 on all.)*

### 19.8 Cheat card

| Line | Who showed weakness | Open | Close (default) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Donk** | — (board edge) | Your board + small | High board / station / multiway |
| **Delayed c-bet** | PFR gave up the flop (you) | A reason on the turn + small | No reason / a villain who leads |
| **Probe** | PFR checked the flop back | Bet the turn into the capped range | PFR trap / bad turn / SD value |
| **Stab** | Everyone checked | Take it with a small bet | Multiway / check-raise / SD value |
| **Turn float** | Villain checked the turn | IP + a plan | OOP / barrel / multiway |
| **River blocker** | Villain would bet if checked to | Capped+aggressive + thin BC + small | Station / uncapped → 11.2 |

### 19.9 Drill (6 questions)

1. **F5.** You defended the BB, flop 7-6-4 two-tone, you're not the PFR. Donk or check? Why does the board matter?
2. **F6.** IP PFR, flop A-9-4, you have KJ, you checked back. Turn K. Bet? What's it called, which reason column?
3. **F7.** OOP, flop check-check, on the turn the PFR is still capped, you hold QJ (turned a gutshot). Do you steal, and what size?
4. **F8.** IP, everyone checked the flop, the turn is checked to you again, you hold ace-high. Stab or check?
5. **F9.** IP, dry flop, villain c-bets, you have a backdoor flush + overcard. Float; state your turn plan separately for a check and for a second barrel?
6. **F10.** OOP river, thin bluff-catcher, villain an aggressive reg + dry/capped board. Blocker or check-call? What changes if the villain is a station?

*Root-error tie-in: all six lines are initiative tools — used right they end the pot cheaply or punish a capped range (anti-bloat). Used wrong they all lead to the same door: inflating with a marginal hand in the name of initiative and staying a bluff-catcher. Guard: who showed weakness + whose board is it + should I realize my hand instead. If all three aren't there, check.*


---

### 19.1-EK Shallow exception (≤15bb): SMALL donk on a high board, JAM on a low board


The 19.1 default is for 30bb+. At ≤15bb in the BB (the villain opened deeper), two things change:

- **On a high-card DRY board there IS a donk — not a jam.** On a K-9-4 rainbow type board your top pair isn't fragile; it wants VALUE, not protection. The problem is IP's answer: if the villain, shallow, is check-back-heavy with second pair, bottom pair, low pockets and A-high, that is the layer that will pay your K-x, and when you check it sees a free turn — a jam folds that layer out and only what beats you stays; a small-medium lead keeps the layer in. If the villain range-bets small when shallow, the lead is unnecessary: check-call / check-jam *(calibrate)*. Combos: lead the weak-kicker K-x that does NOT block the paying layer (9-x, middle pockets, A-high); check-trap the K-9/K-4 types that block that layer *(calibrate)*. On a connected K-Q-9 the need for protection returns — this line does not carry over there.
- **On low/middle boards the lead is mostly JAM or CHECK,** the small lead borderline *(calibrate)* — the mechanism is in 30.7.

19.1's "a big donk with one pair = the root error" line stands unchanged at 30bb+; shallow, on low/middle boards, jamming middle pair (borderline bottom pair) is the antithesis of the root error. The stack threshold is written out explicitly so it doesn't leak into 30–60bb.

### 19.1-EK-2 The 20–30bb band: on low-connected boards the lead approaches range-width — the size is picked by the second card's future


The 19.1 default is for 30bb+ and high boards; 19.1-EK settled ≤15bb into jam/check. The band in between — below the baseline — carries its own rule:

- **Board:** 7-high and below, three low cards close together (6-5-4, 7-6-5, 7-6-4). The BB range is a mass of straights/two pair/sets/low pairs; EP/HJ's is offsuit broadways + pocket pairs. At 8/9-high the frequency melts fast; at A/K-high it is near zero *(calibrate)*.
- **How much:** at 20bb, close to the full range, mostly at small size (top pair combos split off to big/jam per the table); at 30bb roughly two-thirds; at 60bb under half *(calibrate)*. The lead's money comes not from the villain's folds but from his continuing at a bad price; the frequency is pushed down most by IP's overcard-Ax bluff-raise — the pool doesn't make that raise, so the curve flattens. If you take a raise: at 20bb pair+gutshot combos (and better) continue — bare gutshots and weak one pair are folded to the raise; at 30bb weak top pair is bet-fold.
- **Size:** 19.1's "a big donk with one pair = the root error" stands at 30bb+; here top pair splits in two *(calibrate)*:

| Top pair | Size | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Pair + second card working (7-6, 8-6, 6-3) | Big, borderline jam | If it improves the action dies or it loses to a straight — the money is NOW |
| Pair + high kicker (K-6, Q-6) | Small | Keep KQ/KJ in, let it pair on the turn — cooler money |

Against the BTN the size grows (the offsuit 9x/Tx/weak Ax block). At 60bb the table closes: 7x is a "three streets small-medium" hand.

### 19.1-EK-3 The lead got called, turn: card height picks the size in REVERSE — river geometry is the real selector (20–30bb)


The target is always the same: IP's offsuit broadway block that continued on the flop. The size follows what that block did on the turn:

- **High turn (Q/K/J):** part of the block paired (the paying class); the rest (unpaired broadways; e.g. KT/JT on a Q turn) folds even to a small bet → SMALL is efficient. The pool goes "scary card, range-check" — you bet at more than half FREQUENCY (at small size) *(calibrate)*.
- **Low/pairing turn (6/4/2):** the block didn't pair but continues vs the small size with its overcards → BIG, around two-thirds *(calibrate)*. Bluffs: low cards that don't block the block (9-2/5-2 types); a bluff carrying a K/Q blocks his folds, one carrying a T cuts KT/JT — not a candidate either.
- If you fired a big lead on the flop, the block already dropped out → bet even on a high turn.

**River geometry:** low turn + big bet → SPR ~1, on nearly every river a pot-jam with most of your 7x/6x, mostly a single size. High turn + small bet → many medium hands are carried to the river, the value-jam class melts, the size splits into three. The turn size is chosen by "how many value-jams will I have on the river?". The 11.1 table chooses by your hand's class, this entry by the villain's block — after a lead-call, read both.

**Boundary:** small-small-jam works shallow; at ~50bb+ milking the villain's overfold costs you the big river bet → deep, grow the flop/turn by geometry *(calibrate)*. The same rule carries over to the IP c-bet.

### 19.1-EK-4 The second trigger: the villain's c-bet RELUCTANCE — on a dynamic middle board the overpair is the most frequent lead hand


19.1 ties the lead to board ownership. An independent second trigger: if the texture discourages most of IP's range from betting (middle-connected two-tone 8-7-5 / 9-7-5: when Ax/KQ/KJ bets it folds out little and is open to a check-raise; weak pairs and middling Qx don't want to bet), the cost of a check is a free turn — for him and for you. The question is not "is the board mine" but "does the villain want to bet on this texture".

- **Range:** nearly full. The overpair is the MOST FREQUENT lead hand *(calibrate)* — carrying it to a check-call is its weakest use: the villain check-backs and realizes his 9x/5x for free. Top pair a bit bigger for protection, weak pairs small, T9/J9 with their overcards' equity. This is not a nut-advantage claim, it is the villain not wanting to bet (the 19.12 distinction is preserved).
- **Turn (overcard K):** continue at ~40% size *(calibrate)*; bet the overpair mostly — since the K strengthens IP's KQ/KJ continues, part of it slides to check-call — 5x/7x is pulled back to check → the range polarizes. IP's A-high is a fold even in theory — the pool calls it, that's your value.
- **River (neutral-bad Q):** overpair CHECK — if you unblock the villain's bluff candidates, leave him to bluff (11.1-EK-2); the value-jam is with Kx/straights.
- **IP lesson:** on a dynamic board, calling the turn with A-high against bet-bet is bad — no bluff door on the river, and on an A river the leader checks anyway.

A shallow-mid stack (~25–35bb) example; deeper the mechanism is the same, the frequency drops *(calibrate)*.

### 19.2-EK "Second check = weakness" only vs a villain WHO LEADS — the check of a player with no lead range is his entire range (check-back even with AA)


19.3 says "if the PFR check-backed the flop, he's capped" — correct, he would have bet his strong hands on the flop. The same logic does not carry automatically to OOP's turn check: most of the pool has NO turn lead range. The "Villain checked a second time" cell of the 19.2 table is conditional on this.

- **A reg who leads** (one who plays 19.12): on K-7-3-7 he leads with 7x/strong Kx/straight draws → his check is capped → 19.2: thin value + bluff.
- **The pool with no lead range:** his check is his whole range — 7x, Kx, 54/65 semi-bluffs, QT/T8 whiffs. PFR IP's answer is a near-full-range CHECK-BACK, AA included *(calibrate)*. Why: (a) the small flop bet already folded out the offsuit broadway block, the remaining range is dense on this board; (b) betting with 9x/QJ takes a check-raise from 7x AND from semi-bluffs; (c) the check-back's EV is born on the river — QT/65 bluffs, weak Kx block-bets → you raise. On 9-7-3-7 some betting opens up (TT/JJ-type middle pocket pairs are the target).

**Exploit:** the pool IP makes thin bets + too many bluffs on this turn → on a middle-card-pairing turn, open up the OOP check-raise and the river bluff volume. This is the mechanism behind 11.1's "board paired → overpair check" row.

### 19.3-EK Probe size: the class the turn gave YOU + the villain's c-bet frequency choose it — "small-medium" is only for the wide-weak check-back


**Buckets** *(calibrate)*:

| What the turn gave you | Example | Size |
|---|---|---|
| A mass of medium value (all the offsuit Qx is yours; on 6-6-3-Q many times more than trips) | 6-6-3-Q, 8-5-2-Q | Small merged (~one-third): A/K-high pays, it doesn't open you up to a raise; grows on the river |
| In between: the overcard block is the target | 7-6-4-9 | Half pot — "what size does AQ fold to?" |
| Nuts + air (value ~nutted; bluff candidates plentiful: offsuit Ax, 5x, gutshots, FD) | 7-6-4-4, T-9-3-6, K-Q-5-2 | Big polar 1.2–1.5×: the middle layer that won't fold to small folds to big (on 7644/T936 the target is AK/AQ/pocket pairs; on KQ52 A-high + pocket pairs — AK/AQ is a pair there) |
| The nuts passed to him | 7-6-4-A | Range check |

The high-SPR relative of 26.6: there the big bucket jams, here it overbets. If the river matches his range (K-J-7-3-T), come down to 2/3-pot.

**The villain's c-bet frequency.** An IP who doesn't know range-betting bets the first/second layer and checks the third (A-high, K-high, underpairs, weak Jx) with "what business does a bet have here" → a low c-bet frequency is not a trap but a "third-layer check-back"; that layer folds to big and pays small → the probe goes BIG and close to full range *(calibrate)*. The rare check-back of a high-frequency reg is a different animal → back to the table. 23.10's over-bluff warning is for IP barrels; this entry is the OOP probe — but growing the probe without the low-c-bet tag is exactly that over-bluff.

### 19.3-EK-2 River probe (x/c flop, x/x turn): the strong class 2× pot, jam borderline — one size vs a tight range, block-bet + 2× vs a wide one


19.3's "(or river)" parenthesis. BB vs EP 60bb, small c-bet on 6-5-3 called, turn T checks through, river 6 (SPR ~4–5). IP's turn sizes at this node are pot and above (polar) → his check-back arrives without overpairs, without strong Tx, without sets; what remains is A-high catchers (AK/AQ/AJ), 77–99, a few weak Tx. Weak but not empty — a "pays according to price" range.

- **Size:** for 6x trips / 7-4 / boats, **2× pot** *(calibrate)*: Tx and the pairs pay, good A-high catchers (A5/A3) call. **Jam (5× pot) borderline:** A5/A3 folds to it and so does 88, only 6x pays — no extra gain, and catchers are lost. 11.2's "overbet" row does not cover 5× pot; from the catcher's side, a good catcher calls 2× and folds to 5×. Bluffs at 2×: candidates that block 6x (the A6 kicker) are at the TOP of the list — they reduce the villain's calls; K7-suited-type 7x is also good (it blocks the 77 catcher); those that block the A-highs the villain folds are at the bottom of the list.
- **Villain position:** one size vs EP (no one pays thin value, the block-bet has no target); two sizes vs CO — a small block-bet with 3x/5x/Tx + 2× with 6x/74/boats including weak 6x *(calibrate)*.
- **Footnote:** IP fires big polar bets on the turn → leave pairs in your check-call range to defend blank rivers. Pool hypothesis: at this node the pool over-calls → lean on value, throttle the bluffs *(verify at showdown)*.

### 19.4-EK A 3-bet-pot row for the stab table


In a 3-bet pot there are two stab lines. (1) Caller IP, low board (7-6-5 / 6-5-4), the 3-bettor checked: the small stab is near full-range — the 3-bettor's broadways are nothing here; the boundary question is "does the 3-bettor's overcard/broadway segment pick up a straight draw?" (8-7-6 / 9-8-7, and the two-top-cards-consecutive 9-8-2 / 8-7-3 types — on 9-8-2 JT/QJ picks up a draw, on 8-7-3 T9/JT does — yes → selective; truly disconnected 9-5-2 / 8-4-2 types no → free rein; *calibrate*). (2) On a high-card connected (K-Q-x type) board, a SMALL stab against the OOP check is inefficient — it doesn't fold out most of the check range (pocket pairs, broadways with gutshots), only the already-weakest hands drop; around half pot + low suited-connected / gutshot bluffs that don't carry an A (low Ax blocks the folding A-high block — bottom of the list) (see B32.5). The table's "small size" cell does not apply to the second line *(calibrate)*.

### 19.4-EK-3 The IP stab's second street in a 3-bet pot: flop small, turn VERY small — the jam is not part of this line


19.4-EK and 32.5 gave the flop stab; the line doesn't end there. At the node where the OOP 3-bettor checks a middle-low board he should be range-betting (9-6-4 type) *(calibrate)*:

- **Flop small stab** — the target is the medium-weak hands that skipped the range-bet (KJ/KT/A5 types, AJ/AQ that sees no value). OOP's answer splits in two: pairs and overpairs x/r; A-high (AK/AQ/AJ, KQ with backdoors) check-call.
- **Turn brick, checked again → VERY small (near-min) bet** *(size: calibrate)*. The check-call block is A-high; it folds to the small bet partially in theory, largely in the pool. The "SPR ~1, jam is natural" reflex folds out the same block at a higher price and grows the risk against pairs that stayed in check-call instead of going for the x/r.
- **Took an x/r on the flop → a pair, fold.** The villain's x/r range is pair-heavy; the stab's pairless bluffs (QJ/T8s/87s-type backdoor+gutter) end here, and T9/86s-type thin value/protection stabs become bet-fold too.
- **Depth:** works at 40bb, more profitable deeper — after the small turn bet, fold equity still remains on the river.

The OOP side (with which 3-bet range to small range-bet instead of checking) is in 32.5-EK-2.

### 19.4-EK-2 IP full-frequency stab vs the OOP check on A-low boards — OOP's answer is a thin VALUE x/r, not bluff-catch (single-raised, 30bb)


The pool is more passive than theory in both seats, and each passivity opens its own exploit. On A-5-2 / A-6-2 rainbow the OOP opener checked (his Ax-less range is dead — the c-bet face is in B18):

- **IP (caller):** don't check back with "it's his board". In theory OOP's check range folds to a small stab about ~25–30% *(calibrate)*; the pool folds everything of the J9/Q9/K8s/98s type → 40%+ *(calibrate)*. Every mixed bluff candidate (J9s/T8s/K9s/Q9s) goes to full frequency; the table's "small size" cell applies here as is.
- **OOP (opener):** don't keep AQ/AJ in call against the stab to "protect my bluff-catch range". Theory keeps the x/r narrow because it wants to protect the weak calls (J9/Q9); in the pool those calls don't exist anyway, and IP's stab carries less bluff and more equity than theory → the highest-EV answer is a THIN value x/r with AQ/AJ/AK/sets, not widening the bluff-catch.
- **A-K-x rainbow:** the pool over-folds its low pockets (22–99) → QT/JT, which don't block them, bluff at full frequency; bet Kx less — the bad hands are folding anyway, the thin value/protection EV drops (and your K blocks the villain's paying Kx).

Candidate row for the 23.8 cheat sheet: "A-low OOP-check node".

### 19.10 Initiative lines under ICM — when the lead OPENS and when it CLOSES


B19 kept the lead/donk narrow in chipEV (default: don't fire it). ICM adjusts this line not symmetrically but in two directions along the **WHO covers whom** axis — and this is the OOP complement of B21.0's "when covered, come down one notch" rule, not a contradiction of it.

First fix the direction of the risk premium: the side carrying the premium is the one in the pot who **CAN BE ELIMINATED (the COVERED player)** — he folds even chipEV-profitable stack-offs. The big stack that **COVERS** you cannot be eliminated, carries ~no premium, and calls off close to chipEV. Two lines are born from this asymmetry:

- **If YOU are COVERING (your premium is low, the villain short and scared) → the lead/donk OPENS.** The player you cover cannot happily stack off his overpair/TPTK (his premium pushes him off it); that passivity of his hands you an aggression license. On a low-connected board the small donk is correct more often than in chipEV (it prices your equity, cuts off the villain's risk-free realization); your check-raise's fold equity rises too, because the villain has narrowed his call-off.
- **If YOU are COVERED (your premium is high) → the lead/donk CLOSES (B21.0).** Since you cannot take the stack-off risk yourself, you can't carry the barrel wide: lead frequency and size NARROW; the check-raise range squeezes from thin/linear value (AJ, middle pair → check-call/pot-control) down to nutted value + the strongest draws.

> **The aggression license belongs to the COVERING player, not the covered one. If you cover, the lead/check-raise WIDENS (the villain can't stack off); if you are covered, come DOWN one notch per B21.0.**

Boundary: this holds only while ICM is ON (B12.0) and the board hits your range; on high-card boards and against an ICM-blind station, the B19.1 default (do not donk) stands. *(calibrate: lead frequency scales with the risk-premium notch — B20.6.)*

### 19.10-EK The covering SB's lead map: three regimes, and the lead is part of the flat's license


19.10 gave the direction: if you cover, the lead opens. Here is the map — a ~zero-premium big-stack SB, wide flat; a covered tight opener, premium high. The total lead share approaches 40% *(calibrate)*; play without the lead and the bottom half of the flat range loses. **The lead is the wide flat's license, not its ornament.**

| Board | Regime | Mechanism |
|---|---|---|
| **A-low rainbow** (A-4-3) | Half-pot polar, medium frequency | Low sets/two pair are yours, the opener has none; bluffs are the low cards; the opener folds middle pockets + broadways |
| **A-low fd** | More frequent, medium size | The high-equity draws are yours |
| **A+broadway** | No lead (A-T-x light) | The opener's range is too strong |
| **Monotone** (A-Q-3) | Half pot, high frequency | The board burned the opener's suited broadway flushes; the flush count is yours; naked Ax folds |
| **K-high** | None (mono, K-5-4 excepted) | The opener's board |
| **Q-low** (Q-3-2) | Big polar + **out-cleaning** | 33/22 value; the A7s/K8s bluff folds out better Ax/Kx, your overcard outs come alive |
| **J-low / J-middle** | J-4-3 big; J-8-7 small-frequent | Sets are yours / target the unpaired high card with wide value |
| **T-high** | Disconnected check; connected small range-lead; T-9-8 fd big | Nut advantage grows the size |
| **Low unpaired** | Small range-lead | Pair advantage; the opener folds little but every turn continues small |
| **Paired / dry 9-4-2 r** | Check | Value is small / the 9 is in the opener's range |

- **After the turn:** low card small range-bet, high card polar-big; a board-pairing low turn checks.
- **The map is a function of the flat range:** near-bubble CO-vs-SB the Q-high lead is ZERO (no offsuit Qx in the flat), J-high 70% *(calibrate)* (AJo is there), K-low small-frequent linear. Know on which boards your own flat range carries sets/top pair; the map comes from there.
- **Pool:** the opener folds the K9s/A5s-type theoretical calls → pull the lead frequency above theory.
- **Mirror:** if the SB isn't leading on a board where he should, the opener range-check-backs.

> **The covering SB's lead has three regimes: A-low / Q-low / J-low big-polar (low sets + out-cleaning), low and connected middle boards small range-lead, A+broadway / K-high none. A wide flat without the lead loses — the lead IS the flat's license.**

### 19.11 The flop-3-bet node (when your c-bet gets raised)


The book has never treated the flop c-bet → raise → **3-bet** node; it carries money in both directions:

- **On offense:** flop-raising the villain's c-bet is cheap because the pool almost never finds the flop-3-bet — you see the turn without getting 3-bet, your bluffs aren't torn away from their equity (the raise realizes it cheaply); your raise frequency can run several times above theory *(calibrate)*.
- **On defense:** when your own c-bet gets raised you too must have a flop-3-bet range, or you take the same exploit yourself. The range: strong value + low-frequency low-equity bluffs; offsuit hands carry 12 combos, so they pull weight even at low frequency — full composition *(calibrate)*.

> **When your c-bet gets raised, the "call or fold" pair is incomplete — the flop-3-bet is the third option: because the pool almost NEVER fires it, both raising the villain's c-bet and building your own flop-3-bet range for when you get raised are profitable. But the flop-3-bet that RARELY does come from the pool is extremely value-heavy → overfold against it.**

### 19.11-EK The node's two boundaries: the IP raise vs a big c-bet mostly does NOT exist in theory (if it comes, it's value) — small size + flop-3-bet vs the raise-happy villain


19.11's "the raise is cheap" line is against the villain's SMALL bet; two extremes bound it *(calibrate)*.

**Reopening against a big polar c-bet is unprofitable.** A tight IP caller's raise range against a big bet is nearly empty in theory — it mostly collapses into call: his draws carry enough equity to call and don't want to get jammed on; his sets/two pair slowplay against the barrel; he has no hand to raise-fold. The raise opens only against the small bet (pair+gutshot, Ax with backdoors, AA/KK fast). → A raise coming in over your big c-bet is value-dense, continuing with an overpair is borderline; against one over a small bet, 26.2 returns. The turn/river continuation is in 11.1-EK-3 and 26.4-EK; the offensive face in 18.4-EK-2.

**The raise-happy villain (a B23 tag):** against one who raises small bets like mad, the answer is not "bet big" — GIVE him the node he will raise: carry AA/QQ/AQ at small size, let the villain build the pot, then flop-3-bet/jam (≤40bb standard; deeper non-all-in, same direction). The diagnosis splits in two: (a) if he raises thin value too (Q-9-type top pair) as well as gutshots → low pockets/A-high want neither bet-fold nor bet-call → checking rises, betting is small-only; (b) if he raises only bluffs/gutshots and keeps thin value in call → full-range small bet + 3-bet jam with pairs/gutshots/top pair — he can't find a hand forced into raise-call. Turn — profile (a): having spent his thin value on the flop raise, his call range is capped on the turn → big turn bet with most of your range; in profile (b) the call range contains thin/strong value — don't assume capped. The inverse profile of 19.11's "the pool doesn't find the flop-3-bet": against this villain, YOU find the flop-3-bet.

### 19.12 Turn lead (AFTER a flop bet-call): the TYPE of advantage picks the size


The difference from the 19.3 probe: on the flop the villain BET, you called, and on the turn you're considering an OOP lead. **Equity advantage** and **nut advantage** want different sizes.

You called a big bet on the flop → the bottom of your range folded, the villain still carries his bluffs. A middle-low turn (the zone where the BB range concentrates) adds new pairs/draws to your range full of middle-low cards (trips/two pair if the board pairs), not to his overcard bluffs → equity slides to you *(calibrate)*; but the nuts are HIS: his overpairs/sets sit intact in his range, while PART of your strongest hands went to the flop x/r — your range is partly capped, his is not. A big bet is wrong — his air folds, what pays beats you. The tool: a VERY small lead *(calibrate)*, a WIDE range (middle pair, weak top pair, improved draws). Test: if the villain said "I never fold to a small bet", are you happy? Yes → small lead. If he said "let's get all-in with everything"? No → no big bet.

**Your lead range = what you did NOT raise on the flop.** Big flop bet → call-heavy defense → a wide range with its class intact → wide room for the small-wide lead. Small flop bet → you raised a lot (top pair, straight draws, bottom-pair protection) → a filtered range → the lead narrows; even when the straight completes it mostly gives no lead, since most of the straight draws went to the flop raise *(calibrate)*. The remaining lead spot: the class you didn't raise but that turns into the NUTS on the turn — typically the board's bottom/middle card pairing → bigger size, narrower range *(calibrate)*. If neither exists, check. Exploit: the pool folds its overcard junk to the small lead, rarely raises.

### 19.12-EK The nudge is not a line but the meeting of three conditions — the 3-bet pot's caller, the SB flat and multiway nudge too


19.12 gave the mechanism with a BB-vs-late-position example. Three conditions; every node that meets them is a nudge home:

1. **Your range is "strong-weak"** — lots of middle pairs, lots of draws, little air.
2. **The villain's range holds the ends** — both more air and more nuts → equity is yours, the nuts are his → small size.
3. **The villain's turn-raise impulse is near zero** — the lead has no cost.

| Home | Why | Lead |
|---|---|---|
| BB vs CO/BTN, flop bet-call | Classic (19.12) | ~10–15% |
| 3-bet pot, OOP caller (T-4-2 / K-Q-x, x/c vs a small c-bet, blank turn) | The 3-bettor's overpair doesn't need the raise, his AK/KQ can't fold anyone out with a raise; his check-back is a mass of A/K-high | ~20% *(calibrate)*: payment from A/K-high, cuts off Ax's big size; if a raise comes, cheap information |
| SB flat → x/c vs BTN range-bet → blank turn | Your range is pockets+pairs+draws; his K/A-high check-backs are plentiful | Small: folds out the whiffed KJ |
| Multiway, x/c vs the range-bettor, flush-heavy range | The check-call holds flushes/pairs/sets, no air | Small; bluff-raise risk zero |

The pool's reaction is shared: passive and honest — call with AT/KJ, raise only with the nuts; the solver's mixed lead approaches full range once the villain's raise is constrained *(calibrate)*. Bad river after the nudge: 11.1-EK-2.

### 19.13 IP vs the lead: read the size, then play against the BOTTOM of his range


19.12 gave the side FIRING the lead; the side TAKING the lead is three nodes and opens with a single question: what does the lead's size say?

1. **Small lead (~10–15%) = like a check.** 40bb CO vs BB, big c-bet on T-6-4 called, turn 5, BB small-leads: equity his, nuts yours. In the solver, IP's answer to this lead overlaps combo-for-combo with his answer to a check *(calibrate)*: the QJ/KQs you would barrel vs a check → raise; the K-high/weak 6x you would check back → call; fold a few pieces of pure junk. The pool IP gets dazed — cuts the raise, folds broadways, plays medium hands passively because "it could be a straight". Most of the leader's hands are one pair or a draw: play against the bottom of it, not the top.
2. **One-third lead = a real bet.** A correctly playing BB fires the one-third polar (straights/strong Tx/two pair/selected bluffs) → offsuit broadways and weak A-high turn into folds *(calibrate)*. Playing "like a check" here is the root error.
3. **One who fires one-third with his WHOLE range = a jam node.** A villain who has learned the lead but not the size puts 4x/5x/weak Tx/draws into the one-third too → JAM with all Tx, 7x+overcard, A8 types — he can't bet-fold pair+draw at this SPR; QJ/AQ medium hands fold. Note the tag.

**The same law when you fire the lead:** the range-lead's size must be genuinely small; one-third is the polar lead's size.

### 19.14 IP vs the probe: the check-back is a raise node, not a cap — no raise vs the big polar, ~15–20% raise vs the small-medium


You check-backed the flop, OOP fired a turn probe. The pool IP, afraid that "I'm capped and he knows it", almost never raises. The node splits in two by the probe's SIZE (the mirror of 19.3-EK):

- **Big polar probe (pot+ / 1.2–1.5×) → raise ~0–5%.** On 7-6-4-4, T-9-3-6, K-Q-5-2 the villain built the pot himself with nuts+air; the AA in your check-back doesn't beat his value (4x/7x, Kx), and no raise is needed to pick off his bluff → call/fold *(calibrate)*.
- **Small-medium probe → raise with ~15–20% of your check-back range** *(calibrate)*: the villain didn't blow up the pot with his thin value AND you hold hands that beat it.

| Board | Value raise | Bluff raise |
|---|---|---|
| 7-6-4-9 (half) | A9 100%, K9 50%, Q9 25%; T8 | KT/QT/JT: overcard+gutshot, clean outs |
| T-9-3-Q (small) | Two pair over Qx, strong Qx | Kx: blocks KQ/KJ, straight outs |
| 6-6-3-Q (small) | Two pair/trips — NARROW (his AA/KK is waiting for your raise) | 8-7 borderline |
| 7-6-4-A (small) | Strong Ax, two pair | 5x/8x/J5 that didn't enter a bet-fold on the flop |

Raise size small (~2.5× the bet); the frequency ladder is 100%/50%/25% — keep the order. The OOP face: he doesn't raise the small probe with the nuts, he wants your bluff-raise → fire the small probe weighted toward the bulk of your range.

### 19.2-EK-3 Grow the size against a capped range

19.2's "small size with a marginal made hand" rule is for thin value; if the villain's continuing range is narrower than expected and squeezed into medium strength (e.g., if you know the pool's top pair always bets, the villain's flop check — not a check-BACK; in this node the villain is the first checker — already means it's stripped of the top-strength slice), the delayed c-bet carrying a value+bluff mix should size up toward near-pot rather than half-pot *(calibrate)* — a small size prices everyone into a cheap call, while the targeted size is the point where the villain feels "I sometimes have to pay, but not with every hand." Condition: your own check-back range must NOT be capped — you need to be carrying a share of hands that improved on the turn or were held back to trap, otherwise the big size stays open to a raise. If the range keeps narrowing all the way to the river and your own hand also converges toward nut-or-air (polar), the same logic legitimizes a genuine overbet bluff too — the opposite of the small size.

**Rule:** If the villain's continuing range is narrower than expected and squeezed into medium strength (and your own range isn't capped), don't shrink the size — grow it: a small size gives everyone a cheap call; if the range keeps narrowing to the river and your own hand is polarized too, an overbet is legitimate.

### 19.2-EK-4 A good villain's back-to-back double check

19.2-EK reads a reg's SINGLE flop check-back as "capped"; if a good/thinking villain checks the flop AND the turn back to back (and the board texture gave no new reason to bet), this double-check range is capped — no strong hands remain — and it carries plenty of GIVE-UP air: real players, even good ones, load checked ranges with more air than theory (the give-up reflex outweighs balance, on the high side, not the low). Against this cluster a big/polarized river bet gets folds often — the range is stripped of top strength AND heavy with air; small-medium pairs, weak Ax, weak broadway-high-card types that "have showdown value but don't trust a barrel" are also in the cluster, but these mostly CALL the bet — the over-bluff's edge comes not from them but from the piled-up air (a gutshot-carrying broadway is a separate cluster: it's already nearly showdown-worthless by the turn, and on the river it resolves into either value or air — it doesn't belong to this medium-strength cluster). The signal has to come from the villain's ACTUAL behavior: if you've SEEN the villain fold the river after a double-check, over-bluff; don't raise the frequency just because he's tagged "good player" — a known good reg is the villain MOST likely to defend/hero-call the river, the exploit's trigger is the villain's leak, not his label. If the signal isn't clear (a pool-type villain, no read), fall back to 19.2-EK's general pool read.

**Rule:** If you've SEEN the villain fold the river after a double-check, over-bluff with a big/polarized size; don't raise the frequency just because he's a "good player" — with no read, fall back to the general pool rule.

### 19.3-EK-3 At the probe node with the nuts, the default is still bet — induction is a read-based exception only, on a dry turn

After a flop check-check, when you hold a genuine nut hand on the turn (a set, two pair) the default line is still BET: as 19.3 established, the PFR's flop check-back leaves him capped, and a capped range pays off a lot against the nuts. Checking a second time ("induction") is a read-based EXCEPTION riding on top of that default — not the general rule. Only if IP's stab frequency against a second check is genuinely high (if, in the pool, IP starts assuming you're capped after seeing two checks and begins betting even his medium value freely) does a second check + turn check-raise/jam extract more than a direct bet would pull in on its own — the villain puts the money in himself. Without that read, stay on the direct bet; IP's "medium value" is already the set of hands that would call a probe, and the check only turns into a real gain with this specific read. The mechanism is the same as 32.6-EK-2's "not protection but induction" principle, only the node differs: there it's about the IP 3-bettor in a 3-bet pot, here it's about OOP at a single-raised probe node. A texture filter is required too: induction is only considered on a dry/static turn — checking the nuts on a draw-heavy (wet) turn, if IP checks back, hands out a free card and lets his flush/straight draw realize its equity; at this node checking the nuts is the most expensive mistake mode, and on a wet turn the direct bet (protection + value) stays the line.

**Rule:** If you hold the nuts at the probe node, the default is still a direct bet (a capped range pays it off); a second check + check-raise/jam is ONLY for a dry/static turn AND when you've SEEN that IP's stab frequency against a second check is high — with no read, or on a wet turn, bet directly.

### 19.15 Bet-check-bet: the bluff blocker targets the survivor, not the board's nuts

On the flop-bet + turn-check + river-bet line (bet-check-bet), the turn check caps YOUR own range — if you were holding the board's TRUE nuts (e.g., the nut straight), you'd have bet the turn; not betting means it isn't in your hand. But the bluff blocker's target is the VILLAIN's calling range: what strips the nuts out of that range isn't your check, it's the VILLAIN also checking the turn back once you've checked — on a static/dry board this usually doesn't zero out the nut frequency, but it does cut it sharply (balanced ranges check back part of the nuts for protection/trapping purposes).
