On the river, pick the bluff blocker not against the board's absolute top, but against the real top layer that SURVIVES the villain's turn check-back (a specific pair/kicker); blocking the board's nuts is hunting a hand whose weight has already dropped.

**Rule:** On a bet-check-bet river, pick your bluff blocker against "the top layer still weighted after the villain's turn check-back," not against the board's absolute nut card.

### 19.15-EK On a bet-check-bet river, size is the bluff's story test

On this line, the real bluff mostly comes from a hand that checked the turn to take a free card and has NO showdown value (a busted draw, a whiffed overcard) — this hand already burned one street of money on the flop, checks the turn to reach the river for free, then tries a second barrel. A weak pair WITH showdown value usually checks the river too and goes to showdown; turning a made hand into a bluff and taking on raise risk is an exceptional choice. To be credible, such a bluff still has to REPRESENT a strong hand — that's why it's fired big. A small or "lazy" size (the default button pressed without thinking) at this node is a VALUE tell more than a bluff one; a value hand uses the default button, not a carefully chosen size that fits the story.

**Rule:** On a bet-check-bet river, a big size keeps open the possibility of a showdown-less double-bluff from air; a small/lazy size, lean toward value.

## Chapter 20 — Risk Premium: The Number Behind ICM

*★ v6. B12 gave ICM QUALITATIVELY ("tighten when covered", "on the bubble even QQ can fold"). This chapter gives that discipline its NUMBER: the risk premium. B12 tells you "what to do"; B20 measures "why and how much." A hand that's enough in chipEV isn't enough under ICM — the gap is the hidden price of every fold.*

### 20.0 Thesis

> **ICM's price is a number: the risk premium — the extra equity ICM adds on top of what chipEV requires. "Tighten when covered" = "add the risk premium to the equity you need."**

The book has given ICM by feel and direction so far. But "how much do I tighten?" has a numeric answer, estimable at the table in tiers.

### 20.1 Two definitions

| Concept | Definition | In chipEV |
|---|---|---|
| **Bubble factor** | Cost of losing chips ÷ value of gaining them | 1.0 |
| **Risk premium** | Extra required equity ICM adds to the chipEV threshold | 0 |

As the bubble factor rises above 1.0 (bubble, FT ladder, a villain who covers you) the risk premium grows. Both say the same thing: under ICM, losing chips costs more than gaining them.

### 20.2 Worked example — see the premium

- **chipEV (cash-like):** to call a jam, pot odds of 37.5% → **37.5% equity** is enough.
- **The same call on the bubble** (a loss = bust): required equity rises to ~**47%** *(representative — the exact number is 20.6 / calibrate)* → **risk premium ~9–10%.**

Result: a hand that's a comfortable chipEV call — say A9s (in B12.4 it was a wide call when NOT covered) — turns into a **fold** on the bubble against someone who COVERS you; at the highest premium even QQ gets marginal. This is the numeric example behind B12.4 and 17.10.

### 20.3 When the premium grows

| Factor | Premium |
|---|---|
| **A payout jump is near** (bubble, FT rung) | Grows |
| **The villain COVERS you** (you bust if you lose) | **Highest** |
| **The villain does NOT cover you** (short jam, you don't zero out) | Small/negligible → **call wide** |
| **Few players, shallow money** | Sharpens |

This table is B12.4's cover/not-cover asymmetry itself: **the asymmetry IS the risk premium.** Against someone who covers you the premium is high (tighten); against someone who doesn't the premium is ~0 (widen).

### 20.4 How to use it at the table

1. How much equity did chipEV require? (pot odds)
2. Add the risk premium on top (covered? bubble? → high).
3. Does my hand clear this ICM threshold? If not, fold.

> **JAMMING is less affected by the premium — you have fold equity. That's why your jam range STAYS wide and your call range NARROWS (the B17 asymmetry). The risk premium is the numeric basis of that asymmetry.**

### 20.5 The two-way leak (root-error tie-in)

- **Mistaking not-covered for covered** = adding a premium that isn't there = **over-folding** (the A9s/KTo leak in the B12.4 drill).
- **Mistaking covered for not-covered** = skipping the premium = **over-calling → bust.**

### 20.6 Calibration

*(calibrate: the exact risk premium comes from ICMIZER/HRC — by stack distribution, payout, players left. In the app this slot fills from the solver pipeline's ICM mode; at the table estimate "high/medium/low premium" in three tiers and confirm the exact number at the break.)*

### 20.7 Cheat card

| Situation | Premium | Action |
|---|---|---|
| **chipEV / money far off** | ~0 | Normal pot odds |
| **Non-covering short jam** | ~0 / negative | Call wide |
| **Bubble, neutral** | Medium | Add to the threshold, trim marginals |
| **Covering villain + bubble/FT** | High | Tighten hard; even QQ can be marginal |

### 20.8 Drill (3 questions)

1. **R1.** A call needs 37.5% in chipEV; on the bubble a stack that covers you jams. How does your threshold change — is A9s a call?
2. **R2.** Same bubble, but the jammer does NOT cover you (you're deeper). What happens to the premium, how does your range change?
3. **R3.** At the FT you're the chip leader, a short stack jams into you. What's your premium (you stay leader even if you lose)? Does your call range widen or narrow?

*Root-error tie-in: the risk premium is the numeric engine of the "am I covered?" leak (B12.4). B12 gives the direction, B20 measures the price. The two are the qualitative and quantitative halves of ICM discipline.*


---

### 20.3-EK The premium is never zero — it's small from the very first hand and multiplies with the number of starting stacks you hold


Root error: "Money's far off, premium is zero, I'm playing chipEV." Because the prize pool isn't paid to a single person, every elimination bumps up the stack-dollar value of everyone still alive by a notch; when you eliminate someone, a slice of that dead stack's value gets distributed to everyone at your table and every other table — a double-up never fully doubles your stack-dollars. That gap is the premium, and it exists even on the tournament's first hand: roughly one point per starting stack *(calibrate)*. The premium scales with the stack you're risking: a player holding two starting stacks carries close to double the premium against single-stackers, and against a stack as big as his own it's as if he's risking four stacks — even early in the phase, two big stacks colliding produces a medium-tier premium *(roughly around five points; calibrate)*, while single-stackers are still around one point. Rule: early in the phase the premium is small but not zero; the real alertness point is when two big stacks face off — there, set your stack-off threshold a notch above chipEV, and stay close to chipEV against single-stackers. The 20.7 card's "chipEV / money far off ~0" row should be read as "small, growing with stack count."

### 20.3-EK-2 The third driver of the premium is the payout structure — a FLAT payout raises the premium, a top-heavy one lowers it; the distance from bubble to FT determines the depth of the dip


Root error: reading the premium only from "is the bubble close + am I covered." Same stack distribution, same players remaining: as the ratio of the lowest paid spot to first place grows (a flat ladder), surviving is worth more dollars relative to winning, and the premium rises; as first and third pull apart (top-heavy), it approaches chipEV — the difference can run several points *(calibrate)*. A second consequence puts a boundary on 22.1's "bubble bursts → dip" line: the depth of the dip scales with the distance between the bubble and the FT — when the bubble bursts with hundreds of players left, the premium drops to its starting level; when it bursts at fifteen players left, it barely drops at all because the FT payouts are right behind. Rule: before you sit down, read two numbers off the structure sheet — (1) lowest-paid spot ÷ first place (flatness), (2) bubble→FT distance. Flat + small field = the premium never dips, 22.2's "gas" phase effectively doesn't exist; top-heavy + big field = a genuine chipEV window after the bubble.

### 20.3-EK-3 The SHAPE of the payout curve doubles the premium — same stacks, linear payout → double the premium


Root error: reading the premium only from the stack map and "is the money close," without looking at the shape of the payout table. Mechanism: same stack distribution, same seat, same hand — if first place takes a large share of the remaining pool (top-heavy), every rung is small, the price of busting is low, the premium is low; if the pool is spread across the rungs (linear — third place takes more than half of first), every elimination is big money, and the premium roughly doubles *(calibrate)*. The same table is two different tournaments. A second thread: the premium DROPS as the table shortens — fewer opponents means fewer rungs left to climb, and at HU the premium is zero *(tier: calibrate)*; the premium a single short stack creates 3-handed is noticeably smaller than the one the same short stack creates 6-handed. Rule: **the premium estimate's first two inputs are the payout curve and the number of players left; the stack map comes ON TOP of these two.** Don't say "FT = high premium" without checking the lobby: top-heavy payout + 3-handed = the premium is medium, linear payout + 7-handed = it's the ceiling — most of the time.

### 20.3-EK-4 A live structure lowers the premium through two channels — a top-heavy payout (paradox) and the full-BB ante


Root error: "live = big money = more ICM." The reverse. Mechanism (1) — the payout curve: the risk premium comes from the relative SIZE of the money jumps. On a top-heavy curve (first takes more than double what third takes *(calibrate)*), the lower rungs are a small slice of the total pool → each rung's value drops → the premium drops. On a linear curve (first takes less than double what third takes) the rungs carry near-equal weight → the premium is at its ceiling. Same stack, same spot: going from linear to top-heavy, the covered medium stack's premium drops noticeably (several points *(calibrate)*), while the leader's — already low — barely moves → the asymmetry CLOSES: the leader's jam range narrows, the covered player's call range widens (medium pair+ → small-medium pair + suited broadway → even wider tiers *(calibrate)*); the covered medium stack's 3-bet value ceiling drops from "AA only" to KK/AK, the size grows, the bluff slice opens up — 12.2-EK-2's "KK+" row should be read against the payout curve. Measure the curve on a break: the fifth/first and third/first ratios — no formula, just a habit. Mechanism (2) — the full-BB ante: at a short table (5-6 players), a full ante grows the pot by roughly a third relative to a fractional ante → SB's open-jam slice widens by several notches (offsuit Ax, broadway, pairs), and the limp slice NARROWS — once the strong hands go to jam, there's nothing left to protect the limp; a jam is born from nothing in a spot that had none. Once a low premium combines with a full ante, limping comes back: a limp-raise license with strong hands → the weak limp widens too, total folding drops noticeably *(calibrate)*. **Rule:** live, with the same stack, play a notch LOOSER than your online habit — wide call, wide jam, wide 3-bet value; but read the curve from the lobby first, not every live structure is top-heavy. B12.0's "steep payout" phrase is correct if it's describing the gap between rungs, backward if it's describing top-heaviness.

### 20.4-EK The premium works with the PROPORTION of your stack you're risking in the pot — RFI barely changes, the response to a 3-bet changes hard


Root error: "My premium is 5%, so I should shrink every range of mine by 5%." The premium is a number that applies when your whole stack is on the table; a two-blind opening risks only a small slice of your stack, and the premium barely bites at all — opening ranges barely differ from chipEV outside of one or two combos. Defending against a 3-bet puts a tenth of your stack on the table: at the same premium, folding rises noticeably, flatting roughly halves. A jam or a call against a jam pays the full premium. This is a second mechanism, distinct from and complementing 20.4's "jam is less affected by the premium — fold equity" line: there, fold equity protects the jam; here, the investment ratio picks the node. Rule: apply the premium to the depth of the decision tree, not to the strength of your hand — opening ≈ chipEV, response to a 3-bet ≈ half the premium, all-in ≈ the full premium; the node that should shift the most at the table in the early/mid phase is the response to a 3-bet, not the opening *(tiers: calibrate)*.

### 20.6-EK Estimating the premium in three steps — stack count × phase coefficient, then a distribution and paid-percentage correction


Root error: filling in 20.6's "estimate in three tiers" with pure feel at the table. There's a mechanical ladder: (1) How many starting stacks are you holding × a phase coefficient — roughly one point per stack early in the tournament, around two points as late registration closes, multiplying as the bubble approaches *(calibrate)*; this number is against players who cover you, and roughly half of it against a stack half your size. (2) A distribution correction: if chips have pooled into a single wave and the other tables are full of short stacks, your double-up is nearly a lock for the money → run noticeably above the premium estimate *(roughly double for a medium stack; calibrate)*; if the distribution is flat (even the leader holds only a few stacks), stay below the premium estimate. (3) Paid percentage: if a tenth of the field cashes, the percentage of players remaining will mislead you — even with a third of the field left, the bubble is far off and the premium is low; if a fifth of the field cashes, the premium is high at that same percentage remaining. Rule: say these three steps out loud before every ICM decision, then confirm with a calculator on the break — as your margin of error shrinks, 20.4's threshold sum (pot odds + premium) becomes usable at the table.

### 20.6-EK-2 The premium is estimated at the table — the starting-stack rule and four correctors; the rule breaks down post-bubble


Root error: saying "premium high/medium/low" without being able to estimate HOW MUCH; or assuming "I'm holding 6 starting stacks, so the premium is fixed." Mechanism: as the bubble approaches, the premium scales roughly linearly with your stack's size measured in STARTING STACKS — a small percentage per starting stack early in the phase, closer to double that as the bubble nears *(coefficients: calibrate)*; 6 starting stacks + money nearby ≈ a medium-high premium. Four correctors: (1) **Min-cash size** — a min-cash at 2x+ the buy-in pushes the premium up, one at 1-1.5x pushes it down. (2) **Payout curve** — a linear payout (min-cash ~15% of first, 5th place half of first) pushes the premium noticeably up, a top-heavy one (first takes a large share of the pool, 5th place under a third of first) pushes it down; with the same starting-stack count, the premium can swing by a third between the two structures *(calibrate)*. (3) **A sudden rung** — if FT-type jumps begin right after the min-cash, even a small min-cash pulls the premium up to bubble level; read "the two rungs after min-cash" off the lobby. (4) **Cover margin** — the premium against you drops with how deep the villain covers you: someone covering you very deep carries a noticeably lower premium than someone covering you only slightly, at the same blinds. Post-bubble, in a giant field, the rule BREAKS DOWN: 40 starting stacks doesn't produce a 40% premium; there, the measure becomes "distance to the next big jump + stack relative to average + the stack distribution at the table." Rule: do this at the table, not on the break — count your starting stacks, multiply by the phase coefficient, adjust with the four correctors, add it to 20.4's threshold. Then measure your hand against a "realistic" jam range; look not at the flattering side (what you dominate) but at the slice that dominates you — if the required equity climbs to 58%, ATo can't get there against any realistic range, and the argument is over. This method ties 20.6's "estimate in three tiers" to an actual number; the 20.2 example doesn't change.

### 20.1-EK Turn the bubble factor into a threshold with one division


BF = $EV lost if you lose ÷ $EV gained if you win. Required raw equity ≈ BF/(BF+1): BF 1.5 → roughly 60%, BF 2 → roughly 67%; the risk premium is the portion above 50%. BF is mostly 1-point-something; above 2 shows up only in extreme distributions. At the table, estimate BF in tiers, convert it to a threshold with one division, then adjust DOWN for pot odds and dead money — calling a jam is never 1:1 in real life, the blind and ante are always already in.

**Rule:** Required equity ≈ BF/(BF+1); convert first, then discount for dead money.

### 20.4-EK-2 Calculate a 3-way all-in as side pot + main pot, separately


In a 3-way all-in decision from third position, look at two separate pots, not one: in the side pot you're usually close to a flip — but a flip is neutral in chipEV, NEGATIVE in ICM (it costs you $EV whenever the premium is > 0, and it can bust you fast), so add the flip's premium cost ON TOP of the main-pot threshold; the real decision is the required equity for the MAIN pot — divide the slice you put into the main pot (= up to the shortest stack) by the main pot in full, not by your total call. Also, in a multiway all-in the risk premium runs a notch HIGHER than the heads-up calculation: for the same payoff you're entering a bigger, more loss-prone pot, and diminishing returns apply twice.

**Rule:** if the table gives you 30-60 seconds to decide, split the side pot out as "neutral in chipEV, negative in ICM" (add its premium to the threshold), calculate the required equity for the main pot as your main-pot slice ÷ the main pot, then sum across the two live ranges.

### 20.4-EK-3 The same opening percentage can come from two opposite forces at different absolute depths


At a shallow and a deep final table with the same stack ratios (everyone's share relative to average is the same), the opening PERCENTAGE can come out surprisingly similar but doesn't come from the same mechanism. Shallow, the players behind CAN'T flat (widens the opening) but you get called cheaply, their thresholds are low (narrows); deep, the players behind CAN flat (narrows) but their thresholds are high, they play you passively (widens) — they cancel each other out in the percentage.

**Rule:** when two outcomes give the same percentage, don't call it "the same spot"; check absolute depth separately and adjust hand selection to it (see 12.4-EK-3) — don't let the percentage staying the same mislead you.

### 20.4-EK-4 Two equal-chip lines are not equal under ICM


When the premium is POSITIVE (someone covers you), if two sizes produce the same chip gain, the smaller one is superior under ICM: the line that gets the same income with a smaller average pot gives less of a freeroll to the players not involved, and pays less of the diminishing return on every extra chip won. The bigger size is defensible only if it clearly wins more chips. The rule is NOT unconditional: when the premium drops to ~0/negative — a covering leader, a pot heading toward elimination (20.7's "Non-covering short jam → ~0 / negative" row) — this superiority disappears and 21.0/24.3's "if you're covering, go one notch UP / aggressive" direction takes over: there, of two lines equal in chipEV, the bigger/higher-variance one is defensible, even preferred *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** When the premium is positive (you're covered), of two sizes that look "equal" in chipEV, pick the one that risks fewer chips; when the premium is ~0/negative (a covering leader), the rule reverses — 20.7 and 21.0.

### 20.4-EK-5 The "2% bigger bet, 1% more folds" trade doesn't exist under ICM


In chipEV, sizing a bet a little bigger to pick up a bit MORE fold frequency can be a profitable trade — the gain line is the extra fold frequency, the cost line is the extra chips at risk when you get called. When your elimination risk is real, this trade is roughly never profitable, because every additional chip you put into the pot is more expensive than the one before it — the same nominal size is, effectively, a bigger bet under ICM. This is the logic behind the "big bet instead of jam" rule in 21.1-EK-2: even at very low SPR *(threshold: calibrate)* the largest non-all-in size is usually preferred, because the polar line's bluffs gain nothing from the stack's last few bb of chips, and value's gain doesn't pay for the rising price of chips either.

**Rule:** When elimination risk is real, weigh the last slice you add to a bet not by the fold-frequency gain, but by the chip's rising price tag.

### 20.4-EK-6 When the premium climbs a few points, the calling range closes in a block


Hand equities aren't a continuous ladder, they're buckets: against a wide jam range a huge block of hands sits at similar equity, and climbing a few points above the threshold is combinatorially very hard — the drops from AA to KK, KK to QQ are large, with nothing in between. That's why, once the premium threshold clears a bucket, the range doesn't close gradually but in a block, and the call charts of two similar-looking spots look "conceptually totally different" — they aren't; it's the same logic's bucket that changed.

**Rule:** If the premium threshold has crossed an equity bucket, don't weigh the borderline hands one by one — the block has dropped together.

### 20.6-EK-3 Convert the risk premium into a tangible unit


A raw risk-premium percentage means nothing at the table; there are two practical conversions. Pot-multiples: if a jam risks a certain multiple of the pot in chipEV, in a high-premium spot feel that same jam as REALLY risking more multiples of the pot — the jam's real cost is heavier than its nominal stack size. Cash-stake: pull a rough dollar equivalent of the average stack from the remaining player count and the prize pool (account for diminishing returns), and tie the EV difference to a unit that carries meaning at the table.

**Rule:** don't leave the premium percentage abstract — converting it to pot-multiples or cash-stake gives you a truer feel for the size of the mistake.

### 20.4-EK-7 Your own premium widens or narrows value, the opponent's premium picks the bluff

When you build a preflop 3-bet under ICM, two premiums do separate jobs, but neither ZEROES OUT the other. YOUR premium sets the width of your value range — which hands you can continue with into a 4-bet jam, since you're the one carrying the stack-off risk — and that same premium also has a CEILING: as it rises (you're covered, the bubble is close), because ICM's payout curve is concave, the cost of a bluff that doesn't hold up outweighs the chips won, and the first slice that gets trimmed is thin bluffs, not value. THE OPPONENT'S premium is the PRIMARY driver of the bluff-to-value ratio: the more often the opponent is forced to fold (the higher their RP), the bigger your bluff share gets. The root error "I'm the chip leader, my own premium is low, so I can bluff freely" is partly true — a low premium of your own really does raise your bluff ceiling — but the real engine is still the opponent's premium: with the same low premium of your own, against one opponent carrying a high RP and one carrying a low RP, your value range barely moves (your call threshold shifts only secondarily, because the composition of the opponent's jam range changes) while your bluff ratio should change substantially.

**Rule:** The primary driver of your bluff ratio is the opponent's premium, its ceiling is your own premium — your own premium sets the width of your value range and the bluff ceiling, the opponent's premium drives the bluff-to-value ratio.

### 20.6-EK-4 A table-computable ICM $EV shortcut

When there's no calculator on the break, there's a hand-doable way to rough out your ICM-EV: to the guaranteed min-cash, add your chip share (your chips ÷ the total chips held by ALL players still left in the tournament — at the final table this equals the total chips at the table) multiplied by the remaining prize pool (total prize pool − the sum of all min-cashes already locked in or about to lock in). This total comes out close to what an ICM calculator gives for stacks within roughly ±50% of the average stack; outside that band (a distinctly big or distinctly small stack) the deviation can exceed a single payout rung and the number stops being trustworthy. The formula only gives you the $EV LEVEL (the size of the rung), roughly — it does not give the call/shove threshold: the risk premium still comes from 20.6's three-tier estimate at the table, or from ICMIZER/HRC on the break.

**Rule:** When there's no calculator at the table, estimate your ICM-EV with min-cash + (chip share × remaining prize pool) — take chip share against ALL players still left in the tournament, and trust it only for stacks near the average; make the call/shove decision with 20.6's risk-premium estimate, not with this number.

### 20.6-EK-5 Carry blind pressure toward the bubble into ICM as a leak warning, not a mechanical input

A plain/basic ICM calculator only evaluates your STATIC chip count at that moment — it can't see how many chips the blind+ante will eat over the hands still to be played before the bubble bursts *(some ICM calculators, like HRC/ICMIZER, have a separate calculation mode that automatically models future blind/ante cost — run that if you have it, don't try to hand-correct the plain mode)*. Before you enter a decision with a below-average stack, ask: "if I win zero pots before the bubble, how many bb do I fall to?" — this is a CEILING scenario, not the real expected loss, because a short stack's whole job is stealing blinds and chipEV is roughly zero-sum (you pay the blind, you take it back with a steal/jam). Don't feed this ceiling number into the calculator as a mechanical input in place of raw chips — doing so systematically understates $EV and shifts the risk premium the wrong way; also, trimming only your own stack while leaving your opponents' as-is breaks ICM's relative-share math (blind+ante charges the same absolute cost to every player — those chips don't vanish from the table, they go to whoever wins the pot). Put the ceiling number next to the raw-chip calculation, and read the gap between the two as a "the real $EV is probably in between" warning — the gap grows with a shorter stack and with a big field that still has several orbits left before the bubble.

**Rule:** With a below-average stack, calculate ICM with raw chips, and set the "if I win zero pots" ceiling scenario beside it — don't make the ceiling a mechanical input, use the gap as a leak warning; calculate the blind+ante cost as SB+BB+ante per orbit × the estimated number of orbits left to the bubble, and don't confuse it with a cash-game unit like BB/100.

## Chapter 21 — Postflop Under ICM: Downgrade Every Aggressive Line One Notch

*★ v6. B18/B19 gave postflop initiative (c-bet, barrel, bluff, bluff-catch) in chipEV; B12/B20 gave ICM. This chapter is the intersection: how those same postflop lines shift under ICM pressure. The risk premium (B20) was shown at the preflop call/jam; here it carries onto flop-turn-river — because the premium re-accrues on every street.*

### 21.0 Thesis

> **Under ICM pressure — that is, against someone who COVERS you — downgrade every aggressive postflop line one notch: bet→check, raise→call, big→small, thin-value→check-back, hero-call→fold. If you're COVERING (you're the chip leader) the reverse: upgrade one notch — more barrels, more pressure. The direction is the sign of the cover map; the headline is the COVERED player's rule.**

B20 gave the risk premium as a number but showed it at the preflop call/jam. Postflop the premium re-accrues on every street: in a bloated pot the call-off is the most expensive action. This chapter carries the premium onto flop-turn-river.

### 21.1 Why the call-off is the most ICM-taxed action

Postflop the line that risks the most chips is a call/hero-call to a big bet or raise — where the pot bloats and a loss approaches a bust. The premium hits here hardest:

- **A bet/barrel has fold equity** → the premium punishes it less (the B20.4 asymmetry holds postflop too).
- **A call-off has no fold equity** → you tie all your chips to showdown, the full ICM tax lands.

Result: under ICM pressure your bluff-catching range NARROWS (fold the extra pair), but betting for value stays the same. The "one pair = a bluff-catcher" rule (B11.2) is even more fold-leaning under ICM.

### 21.2 Downgrade one notch — the rule table

| chipEV line | Under ICM pressure (when covered) |
|---|---|
| **Thin value bet** | Check-back (don't cut thin value) |
| **Second barrel bluff** | One barrel / give up |
| **Big size** | Small size |
| **Raise (value)** | Call (don't bloat the pot) |
| **Hero-call / bluff-catch** | Fold |
| **Check-raise bluff** | Flat call or fold |

Every row says the same thing: shrink the pot, don't tie chips to showdown, cut the marginals. The notch = the risk premium.

### 21.3 Two seats — the cover asymmetry

The direction isn't absolute; it depends on the sign of the cover map. The same spot gives two players opposite instructions:

| You | Premium | Postflop direction |
|---|---|---|
| **You're COVERED** (you bust if you lose) | High | Downgrade one notch — check, call, small, fold |
| **You're COVERING** (chip leader, you survive a loss) | Low/negative | Upgrade one notch — barrel, pressure, big |

This is the postflop face of the B20.3 table. The chip leader sells pressure (every pot is a bust risk for the opponent); the short/mid stack eats it (tightens). **The headline "downgrade one notch" is the COVERED player's rule; the COVERING player does the opposite.**

### 21.4 MDF collapses under ICM

Minimum defense frequency (MDF) is a **chipEV** concept — it holds in cash and in ICM-free MTT spots; but it collapses when a payout jump is near. Because losing chips costs more than gaining them, you fold BELOW the defense rate MDF names, blamelessly.

- **chipEV:** if the villain bets ⅔ pot, MDF ~60% → defend 60% of your range (else you're exploitable to bluffs).
- **A covered ICM spot:** your defense rate to the same bet can be far lower; over-folding here isn't an exploit, it's correct play.

So the "fold below MDF = leak" rule inverts under ICM: when a payout jump is near, folding below MDF is correct. The villain stealing with bluffs doesn't repay your ICM premium.

### 21.5 Protection-first + short-stack flop-jam

- **Protection-first:** under ICM you give up thin value (check-back), but betting to protect real equity stays the same. Thin ≠ fragile: thin = already ahead, indifferent to cards → check; fragile = ahead but the board threatens → bet (to deny a cheap card, not to tie yourself to showdown).
- **Short-stack flop-jam:** at shallow SPR (stack behind ≤ pot) there's no bluff-catch/float; either jam the flop or fold. A middling size ties chips and kills fold equity. When you're shallow under ICM the line collapses to two: jam or give up.

### 21.6 Calibration

*(calibrate: the size of the notch = the risk premium (B20.6); HRC/ICMIZER gives it by stack distribution/payout/players left. In the app this slot fills from the solver pipeline's ICM mode; at the table estimate "high/medium/low premium" in three tiers. MDF's exact collapse point also depends on payout proximity — confirm at the break.)*

### 21.7 Cheat card

| Spot | You're covered | You're covering (leader) |
|---|---|---|
| **Thin value** | Check-back | Bet (take the thin value) |
| **Marginal bluff-catch** | Fold | Call (you can eat the pressure) |
| **Barrel decision** | One barrel / give up | 2-3 barrels, pressure |
| **Size** | Small | Big / polarized |
| **Fragile value** | Bet (protection) | Bet (protection) |

The fragile-value row is a bet on both sides — protection is independent of the premium.

### 21.8 Drill (3 questions)

1. **P1.** Bubble, river against a villain who covers you, you hold a thin bluff-catcher, villain bets small. In chipEV it was a call. What do you do under ICM, and why?
2. **P2.** FT, you're the chip leader, a mid stack check-called the flop, the turn checks to you, you hold a good but non-nut hand. Which way is the notch, what do you do?
3. **P3.** In a spot where you're covered the villain bets ⅔ pot, MDF says ~60% but the payout jump is very near and your hand is on the defense borderline. Is folding a leak or correct?

*Root-error tie-in: this chapter fuses the B18/B19 postflop initiative with B12/B20 ICM. One guard: am I covered? If yes, downgrade every aggressive line one notch and cut the call-off hardest. If no / if I'm covering, upgrade one notch. The risk premium (B20) re-accrues on every postflop street; the most expensive action is a call-off in a bloated pot.*


---

### 21.0-EK The Cover Difference Under ICM Lives at the Flop C-Bet/Lead Node — Everything After Converges, the Difference Only Returns at the Stack-Committing Node (the ICM Mirror of 28.19)


Root error: reading "the premium accrues on every street" as shrinking every size and frequency again on the turn/river. Mechanism: the same range, only the cover direction differs — while IP is covered, the flop c-bet check-back roughly triples (one-tenth to one-third *(calibrate)*), and only the size gets smaller; while covering, it's a range-bet, sometimes half pot. The check-back material isn't thin/marginal, it's a "medium showdown hand that doesn't want to eat pressure": JJ-KK and Kx on A-high boards, QQ/JJ/TT and second pair on K-high. BB's response to the c-bet is nearly identical in both directions (fold about a third, check-raise about a tenth *(calibrate)*); the covering BB only opens the bluff-raise a notch wider — low premium wants less equity for a bluff. After the flop bet-call, the turn/river frequency, size, and continuing threshold are nearly identical in both directions — the ranges were adjusted on the flop, the rest is the standard tree. The difference only comes back at (a) the stack-committing node (jam or ¾ pot; jam or call with the K-high flush — 21.1-EK-2) and (b) the top of river thin value (the covered player checks AK, the covering player bets it).

**Rule:** Load the weight of the ICM postflop adjustment onto the flop c-bet/lead node; after the bet-call, play the standard tree, and only re-ask the cover question at the stack-committing node. Shrinking the turn/river "one more notch" as the covered player pays the range's already-paid premium a second time *(mostly)*.

### 21.1-EK Call-Off Material: When Value Is Narrow, the Blocker Outranks the Hand


21.1 declared the call-off the most ICM-taxed action; the remaining question is "with which hand?" When an ICM polar bet's value is narrow and well-defined (a set, two pair, a specific top-pair class), it's not the rank of the hand but the answer to three questions that decides:

1. **Am I blocking villain's value?** On an A-J-3 line, KJ is a better call than AK — it blocks JJ/AJ. Against a 6x pot bet on A-6-4, better than Ax; against a big lead on A-5-4, K5 is better than A9 (the 5 blocks three value classes, the A is a single combo).
2. **Am I blocking villain's bluffs?** KK is a better call than AT — it doesn't block bluffs, and it has a clean out. Against a 3x-pot flop jam, KT folds (it blocks the KQ/KJ bluffs), Q9/J9/QT/JT call *(calibrate)*.
3. **Do I have an out?** Against a big stab on T-7-3, the check-jam "bluff" is AK/AQ — two overcards, it blocks KT/KK, it doesn't block bluffs; Q3 (5 outs, blocks a bluff) folds. On 9-7-5, K5 folds, AT jams.

Mirror: run your own bluff through the same test — the "unblock" rule in 21.2-EK-4 is the bluff face of this.

> **Against an ICM polar bet, "the stronger hand is the better call" does not hold. When value is narrowly defined, the best call is the hand that blocks villain's value, doesn't block his bluffs, and carries an out — KJ > AK, 66 > Ax, Q9 > KT.**

### 21.1-EK-2 Deep and High Premium: The Stack-Off Threshold Is "Nut + Blocker" — Sets CALL, Top Set CHECKS, No K-High Flush Jam; the Covered Player's River Is a Big Bet, Not a Jam


Root error: applying the chipEV hand-strength playbook by rote (set = raise, K-high flush = jam, AK top pair = river value). Mechanism: deep (50bb+) and mutually high premium (ten to twenty points *(calibrate)*) → lost chips cost more than won chips; at the stack-committing node the value threshold moves toward the nuts. The gradations *(all calibrate)*: (a) against a big river bet (¾ pot), bottom/middle set CALLS, only top set/a straight raises; (b) top set mostly CHECKS back — it blocks villain's paying range (the top-pair combos), and when it does get paid it's running into a straight → a low two pair is better raise material than top set (it doesn't block the payer). A covering villain bluff-catches wider, so blocking matters less; it's sharpest when covered; (c) flush river: a K-high flush doesn't jam, it calls — only an A-high flush pays off a jam; a non-flush two pair doesn't bet; (d) even on a dry river, one pair (AK) checks back — "one pair worsens as money goes in," even on the cleanest runout; (e) the covered IP bets big instead of JAMMING value on the river: bluff-jams are very expensive when called, and a jamming range can't stand without bluff cover; a covering villain jams the same hand.

**Rule:** at high premium, before you ask about raw hand strength on the last street, ask "am I blocking the paying range, and what do I lose if I don't get paid?" — call with a set, raise/jam with the nuts plus a good blocker. This is the deep-band calibration of B21.2's "raise→call" row; it's the value-side face of B21.1's "call-off is the most taxed" thesis.

### 21.2-EK "Downgrade One Notch" Is the Rule for the MARGINAL Zone — Two Exceptions


The B21.2 table is a strong default, but in two situations downsizing is WRONG; both for the same reason: downgrading a notch is the rule for a thin/marginal range, not a strong-and-dense range.

1. **Nut-dense river:** even a covered short stack STAYS at big-size/overbet jam when its river value is concentrated on the nuts (full house + nut flush) — this is correct in theory. The tunnel vision of "shrink everything under ICM" is itself a leak here. Test: is this size's value range marginal, or nut-polarized? If nut-polarized, the notch doesn't apply.
2. **The first touch of light ICM:** right after late registration has just closed, with the money still far away, ICM's FIRST effect on postflop is pulling back thin value bets — because the remaining betting range gets stronger, size does NOT shrink, and can even grow. B21.2's downsizing row is for COVER pressure; under light ICM it's the wrong downsize.

> **Downgrading a notch trims the marginal, not the nuts. "I'm covered → shrink" only holds when the size's value range is marginal; sizing down with a nut-dense range leaves money on the table.**

### 21.2-EK-2 A-High Boards: Under ICM This Is Pot-Size Territory, Not Small-Size Territory


B21.2's "big → small" row is for a marginal range (21.2-EK). Under ICM, an A-high board falls outside that row, and the reason is range structure: under ICM everyone opens blocker-heavy, so on A-x-x villain's range is packed with random Ax and middling pocket pairs. A small bet folds nothing out of that mass; a pot-size bet immediately makes weak Ax indifferent and folds the pocket pairs outright *(calibrate)*.

- **Value is nut-polar:** a set, a low two pair, a slice of the strongest Ax. Middling Ax doesn't bet — it goes to check, it's already a bluff-catcher.
- **The bluff is the lowest cards:** the folding mass is broadway/middle pocket pairs → so the bluffs are the low connectors and low pocket pairs that don't block them. The A5-type "Ax with a gutshot" blocks your main bluff targets and isn't on the list.
- **The cover direction moves the frequency, not the size:** infrequent while covered, frequent while covering *(calibrate)*; in both seats it's pot or check.
- **Turn:** if you started polar, stay polar; on an A turn, GROW the size — the value combos have shrunk, and a bigger size lets in more bluffs. On a brick, a medium size around half-pot, at high frequency.
- **A range-dependent exception:** a paired A-low like A-6-6 — the covered IP has no suited 6x → check; the covering IP does → pot-size, value 6x. Same board, two strategies depending on the seat.
- **Where small size still lives:** paired A-high, monotone, three-broadway (A-K-J) — high frequency, small.

> **Under ICM, an A-high board is not small-bet territory: only a pot-size bet folds villain's Ax-plus-pocket-pair mass. Pot or check; middling Ax checks, the bluff is the lowest card. Size doesn't change with the seat, frequency does.**

### 21.2-EK-3 OOP Opener Against a Dense Caller: Check-First — a Single Combo Flips the Frequency


B18.5 calls the OOP c-bet "more selective"; under ICM a mid-stack opener against a dense flatting range isn't selective, it's nearly all check *(calibrate)*. The caller's range is pocket pairs plus suited broadways, including the premiums that didn't 3-bet; your range is blocker-heavy and wide. Your unpaired mass can't find a hand to bet with, and your paired mass doesn't want to bet.

- **The one spot that gets bet:** a paired A/K board (A-9-9, A-K-K) and A/K plus two low rainbow cards — small, infrequent. Skipping these too is fine.
- **Sensitivity:** A-K-8 bets, A-K-7 range-checks — the only difference is A8o being in the range. On the turn, on a card that carries the caller to a set/two pair (7/T/J), check even with 50%+ equity. You can't hold a difference this fine at the table → range-check.
- **The other side:** this is why IP stabs very frequently *(calibrate)*: A-high polar-big (21.2-EK-2), K-high small at half-frequency, Q-high and below range-stab.
- **The mirror rule:** if OOP is checking a board where theory says he should lead/bet often, IP range-checks back — the nuts are still in his range. "He showed weakness → stab" (19.4) doesn't apply here.

> **Against a dense caller, a mid-stack OOP opener is check-first under ICM. A bet/check EV gap that hangs on a single combo's existence can't be memorized — simplify to check, the cost is zero. If OOP doesn't bet where he should, IP doesn't bet either.**

### 21.2-EK-4 Bluff Material Under ICM: Realize the Draw, Bluff with Trash and the Lowest Cards


B1's bluff hierarchy is for chipEV. Under ICM, a polar bet's bluff has to pass two tests: does it sit comfortably as a bet-fold, does it sit comfortably as a bet-call? A draw passes neither — it can't fold to a check-jam, and it burns its equity when it does fold. So:

- **A draw realizes through checking.** An OESD/flush draw likes checking back on the turn; a flush draw that does bet is either happy with a bet-call (a K-high FD) or is total trash (62s). Middling draws (a Q-high FD, gutshot+bdfd) check.
- **Bluff 1 — zero-showdown backdoor trash.** Q3/J4/T3 plus a backdoor flush draw. Bet-fold doesn't hurt, and on the turn it can turn into an overpair or a backdoor draw.
- **Bluff 2 — the lowest cards.** On a broadway board (A-T-4, K-J-8) every hand villain folds carries a K/Q/J; 3-2/4-2/6-5 blocks none of them → the best bluff, and the best barrel on brick turns too. KQ/QJ/KJ gutshots are BAD bluffs here: they have showdown value and they block his folds.
- **On a turn that improves villain** (T/J), use the Q8/Q9 class instead of the lowest cards — it has equity and blocks his nuts.
- **The IP turn-stab rule** (villain called the flop, checks the turn): if you connected with something (a pair/draw) → check; with nothing → bet. The material swaps places when the card changes, the rule doesn't.

> **A polar bluff under ICM is zero-equity plus unblock: backdoor trash and the lowest cards. A draw likes neither bet-fold nor bet-call — realize it by checking. A broadway gutshot on a broadway board is not a bluff.**

### 21.2-EK-5 The Covered Player's Check-Raise: One Street of Protection, Then the Brakes


B21.2 says "raise → call, x/r bluff → call/fold"; B19.10 squeezes the covered player's x/r down to nut value. Both are about stack-off INTENT. While the covering IP stabs wide and polar (21.3-EK-3), the covered player's check-raise doesn't disappear, it changes shape:

- **What it's for:** protection plus one street of value. Against a small stab, KQ/KJ x/r (takes value from the pocket pairs); against a pot-size stab on A-9-3, AK/A9/99 and AQ/AJ/AT — against IP's polar range, AK and AQ are the same hand (its value is two pair-plus). Against SB's big lead on A-6-4, A9–AJ raises: reject the equity of 76/65/54 — if you were already dead, you were dead.
- **What comes after — the real difference:** after the x/r, the turn is check or ≤ half pot; no jam, no stack-off, SPR stays big. In chipEV, x/r is the start of a barrel; under ICM it's a one-street tool. After an x/r-call against a pot-size stab, OOP range-checks the turn, check-jamming or check-folding to a small bet.
- **Bluffs:** backdoor trash you didn't stab with (96 plus a backdoor flush draw, T6) and hands that are "not enough for a call but a shame to fold." If you stab every piece of backdoor trash, you have no x/r bluffs left — leave deliberate trash in your check range (the mirror of 31.3).
- **The flipped seat:** OOP big stack, IP covered → x/r against a pot-size stab is ZERO. There's no need for protection, and the covered IP already stabs tight. The covered player is the one who folds to the raise, the covering player calls it — counterintuitive, but consistent with the mechanism.

> **The covered player's check-raise is protection, not aggression: one street of value/protection against the covering player's wide stab, then the brakes. Barreling after an x/r is the chipEV reflex; under ICM it's a turn check or small, no stack-off.**

### 21.2-EK-6 The Threshold for Reopening the River: Beat the CALL Range, Not the Range


B21.2 sends thin value to check-back; on the river the scale is different. Under ICM a big river bet folds out villain's one-pair hands entirely — the premium pushes him to fold. So a bet only gets paid by the hand that beats you: a value-cut.

- **The threshold isn't equity, it's the call range.** On the A-Q-6-2-5 line, AK checks; even a weak two pair doesn't bet with ~78% equity *(calibrate)* — every hand that folds is one you already beat, every hand that pays is one that beats you.
- **IP bets TIGHTER than OOP.** IP has the check-back option; OOP's bet is also a deny. On the A-K-8 line, OOP value-bets AQ, IP doesn't jam AK (only AA/A8/K8 pay it off). B21.7's "if you're covering, take the thin value" row fits OOP and small size, not IP's river jam.
- **A flush completed, and you hold a flush:** bet small, stay protected — villain's flush count can outnumber yours.
- **SPR is not a license.** Even at 0.4 SPR, top pair Tx checks on a K river, K8/KT checks on a Q river *(calibrate)*. The "I'm already committed anyway" reflex doesn't exist under ICM.
- **The covered IP:** turn size drops (half pot, not pot), on the river one pair never goes big; small size only with the best Kx.

> **The hand that reopens the river must beat villain's CALL range, not his range. IP's threshold is one notch tighter than OOP's (it has the check-back option); even 75%+ equity isn't value with big size; a shallow SPR is not a license.**

### 21.2-EK-7 Turn Sizing: Low Card Is Linear-Small, High Card Is Polar-Big — Not Geometry


Under ICM, turn sizing is chosen street by street; the "¾-¾ geometric" plan is second in line here. The regime is set by how high the turn card is and how much protection top pair needs:

- **A low turn (below the board's top card):** small-linear. After a range-bet, hands with no pair or a middling pair — 88/99/A5/J2 — value-bet: villain's unpaired mass still folds, and you buy protection. Bluffs are wide: draws plus overcard air. Don't polarize when the premium is high and SPR is big.
- **A high turn (above the board / pairing the top card):** polar-big. The best Ax/Kx plus the lowest cards (21.2-EK-4); middling pairs check. Pot-size is legitimate — river SPR stays ~0.36, that's fine *(calibrate)*.
- **The protection yardstick:** on K-5-4, the K is hard to outdraw → the turn goes polar immediately; on T-7-4, the T is beaten by every broadway → small-linear on a low turn, polar on a high turn.
- **A board-pairing low turn** (a 2/3 on 7-3-2): check — value shrinks, bluffs don't develop. A low turn that develops the bluffs (a 9) bets.
- **The river after a small turn:** half pot, no jam; leave the jam to villain — if you fired a small turn, don't be the one opening the stack-off.

> **Under ICM, turn sizing turns on the card's height: a low card is linear-small (even unpaired hands are value), a high card is polar-big. Size honestly street by street; don't worry about river SPR. Small turn → river half pot, the jam is villain's job.**

### 21.2-EK-8 The Nut-Advantaged Leader at High Premium Goes Small-Small-Small — Nuts Aren't There to Bloat the Pot, They're There to PROTECT Thin Value


Root error: nut advantage plus I'm covering → "take his stack" with a big bet/overbet. Mechanism: at high premium the covered IP pulls his stack-off threshold toward the nuts; when a big size/jam comes, one pair, two pair, even a low set folds → the PAYOFF value of the nuts (a straight/top set) drops. At the same time the leader's range is loaded with thin value (6x, 8x, Kx, a low two pair) that gets paid off on every street with a small size (one-tenth to one-third of the pot *(calibrate)*). The proper job of the nuts is to protect the small-bet range: when IP raises a small bet with Kx/two pair/a set, reopen with a set/straight (jam). On the turn, once a slice of the nuts goes to a big size, IP finds the courage to raise; if only small size exists, IP stays raise-less — which is exactly what you want. Overbet is almost never used *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** when the nut slice is a MINORITY of the range and thin value is wide → small on all three streets, no overbet, reopen when a raise comes. Boundary: if the nut slice is the MAJORITY of the range, 21.2-EK applies (size stays big) — this note is the mirror boundary of 21.2-EK, not a contradiction: the question is the same one — "is this size's value range marginal or nut-polarized?" — and here the answer is "marginal-wide."

### 21.3-EK PKO ≠ ICM: The Covered Player's Discount Isn't Fear, It's a Loss of Fold Equity


B21.2/B21.7's rows are for ICM. Applying the same reflex when covered in a PKO (thin value → check-back, shrink the size) is wrong: the covered player has no positive risk premium in a PKO *(the ICM component is separate — calibrate)*. What makes him "passive" isn't fear of busting, it's that the covering villain across from him is paying wide for the BOUNTY. Against a villain who pays wide, bluffs die and value lives — thin value gets paid more; the covered player's value range is roughly the same in both directions, only his bluffing shrinks *(mostly)*.

**Rule:** when covered, downgrade the bluff/barrel notch, don't downgrade the value notch; price semi-bluffs by equity instead of expecting fold equity. "I'm covered → thin value check-back" is the ICM rule, not the bounty rule. At the final table when both apply together, calculate the two discounts separately: the ICM premium cuts thin value, the bounty discount cuts bluffs — don't collapse them into a single "downgrade one notch." See 28.20 for the covering side's flop, 28.21 for the covered IP's.

### 21.3-EK-2 The Covering BB Defends 90-100% — the Villain's Premium Is Your Realization; the Leader Uses the Size Lever on the River


Folding marginal suited/connected hands to a min-raise on the reflex of "ICM → tighten" in the deep BB is the rule for the wrong seat. Every postflop chip belongs to an opener who does NOT cover you and therefore carries bust risk → he can't fire a big flop size, an aggressive barrel, or a hero-call. The low-pair/backdoor hands that get crushed by big sizing in chipEV OVER-realize their equity here; on top of that, tools that are closed in chipEV, like a flop lead, open up. Result: defense narrows while covered, and widens while covering — deep it approaches 90-100% *(calibrate)*; the 3-bet value slice, though, stays narrow (12.2-EK-2). The second arm is the size lever: if villain is locked up by ICM (doomed to over-fold), the leader plays close to chipEV and, on the river, uses oversizes that look absurd in chipEV (multiples of the pot *(calibrate)*) — the slice he loses is small relative to his stack, but a bust for villain. B21.3's "one notch up" comes down to two concrete arms: preflop defense width plus the river size lever; the B21.7 cheat card is read through these two rows. Boundary: two big stacks against each other — the lever closes, the premium is mutual (22.4).

### 21.3-EK-3 The Covering IP's Stab: Frequency and Size Grow Together


21.3-EK-2 gave two arms of the covering side (preflop defense width, river size lever); the third arm is the flop stab. When the covered opener checks, the covering IP's stab isn't "small and infrequent" the way it is in chipEV:

- **Why:** the covered player's unpaired range — AK/AQ/KQ included — has to fold even to a small stab; the risk premium makes him indifferent. On J-high, T-high, and low boards, small-to-medium size wipes out a large slice of the covered range *(calibrate)* → a **range-stab.** The same IP, while covered, fires these boards at half the frequency and small.
- **Sizing map:** low/J/T-high → a single size, ⅓–40% pot, full range (solvers mix two sizes; merge them). K-high → half-pot, built around two pair-plus value; K-Q-x/K-J-x keep a check portion. A-high → pot-or-check (21.2-EK-2).
- **Turn barrel:** a medium size around ¾ pot, at close to half frequency; natural bluffs aren't enough → random broadways join the bluffing range too (the premium allows it). On a turn that improves villain (T/J), the bluff shifts from the lowest card to the Q8/Q9 class — equity plus it blocks villain's nuts.
- **The covered player's response:** the check-raise now EXISTS — see 21.2-EK-5 and 21.4-EK. Play the range-stab not because "folds rain down," but because "the unpaired mass folds, and pairs plus x/r remain."

> **The covering IP's stab is twice as frequent as chipEV and one size bigger: range-stab on low/J/T boards, half-pot on K-high, pot-or-check on A-high. The covered player's unpaired range can't pay for it.**

### 21.3-EK-4 The Covering BB's Flop Lead Map — NOT the Nut Advantage, the Second/Third-Pair Advantage; the Covered BB Doesn't Lead


Root error: thinking of the lead as a tool for "when I have the nuts," or never using it at all. Mechanism: the same range, but purely because the cover direction flips (covered BB → covering BB; a premium gap of a few points *(calibrate)*), BB's lead frequency goes from zero to roughly a fifth globally. Reason: at high premium, the covered IP can't raise the lead (he can't go in dead) → he's stuck calling wide; OOP's small bet goes unpunished. The map has two types: (a) low-connected (865/776/544-type) — nut advantage, close to a range lead, small; (b) the COUNTERINTUITIVE type: Q76/J54/T64/732/T75 — even in an equity DISADVANTAGE *(calibrate)*, BB has a lot of 6x/7x/4x, IP has none → a half-frequency small lead; the 7 and 6 lead as much as the Q. The bluff wants either a backdoor draw or genuine equity; total trash checks. Turn: a low card → continue, small; an A/K → range-check (this hits IP), IP bets big and polar, BB folds its 6x/7x. Covered BB: simplify the lead down to zero — in theory there's a low-frequency mix on three boards, but no hand leads more than it checks.

**Rule:** if you're covering, open up the lead with the question "who has the middle pairs?"; if you're covered, shut it down. This is the concrete flop tool for B21.3's "one notch up"; it's the map for 21.3-EK-2's "the flop lead opens up" line.

### 21.3-EK-5 The Covered Tight OOP Opener Almost Never C-Bets — Only on AK/KK-Advantaged Boards; the Covering IP Flats WIDE + Stabs Against a Tight Opener


Root error: "I opened tight from UTG → most boards favor me → c-bet." Mechanism: a deep covering IP flats very wide against a tight-and-covered opener (connected/gapper suited hands, JTo/QTo; above a fifth of hands *(calibrate)*) — he doesn't get squeezed (he covers the blinds), and villain is doomed to postflop check-fold → over-realization. The covered OOP's global c-bet is single digits *(calibrate)*, small size only; the exception boards are the ones where AK is the nuts — QJT, AKx, K8x-type (IP has 3-bet KK/AK, so the KK/AK advantage sits with OOP). On QJT, OOP small-c-bets the majority, IP folds a third and raises AK/sets. On A-K-x, pot-size polar (AK/A3s/33). Turn: if an A comes, range-check (IP's flop call is Ax-heavy); on a K plus a flush-draw turn, ALL of Ax jams — for the freeroll (if only suited Ax jammed, IP would fold and the freeroll is lost); on a flush-completing river: range-check, even AK check-folds (IP has a flush a sixth of the range, OOP has none *(calibrate)*). Message: the big stack's range — whether opener or caller — is wider and weaker; the covered player's range is high-card-dense.

**Rule:** as the covered tight OOP opener, check outside the boards where "what does the top of my range hold uniquely?"; as the covering IP, flat wide against a tight opener and stab into his check. 22.8-EK's "no OOP flat while covered" is correct FOR the covered player — the covering IP's wide flat isn't its opposite, it's the complement; 28.21 (the covered IP) is this note's mirror seat.

### 21.3-EK-6 The Leading OOP — Bluffs That Block the Top of the Covered Player's DENSE Range + POT Size on A-Low / Low-Paired Boards; K-High Boards Are Bad for the Leader, Small and Wide Value


Root error: choosing bluffs by equity (FD/OESD) as the leading OOP, and reading the board by rote position ("I'm in early position, K-high is my board"). Mechanism: under ICM the big stack's opening range is wide/weak (offsuit Ax, suited Kx, all pocket pairs); the covered caller is high-card-dense (KK/QQ/AK flat, only AA 3-bets). This texture flips the board map. A-plus-low (A42/A72), low-paired (332/544), Q42/T64 all work for the leader — even in an equity DISADVANTAGE *(calibrate)*, the set/A-low-two-pair advantage → POT-size polar (a quarter of the range *(calibrate)*): value is AA/set/A-low two pair; the bluff is the hand that blocks the TOP of the covered range — suited Kx (blocks AK; equity doesn't matter, even KTo goes pot), KQ/KJ on 33x (blocks KK/QQ, plus two overcards), small pocket pairs (folds out JJ/TT, and its set-out is clean). After the pot bet, the covered player's Ax becomes indifferent, A-highs fold on the turn, on a K river almost the whole range jams. A K-high board is BAD FOR THE LEADER (lots of offsuit Ax, little Kx): half-check the flop, small; the turn only goes small with WIDE value (Kx, QQ, JJ) — the covered player's flop call is pairs plus A-high, and it pays off a small turn bet ("who's paying a T turn with 88?"). On QJT the leader range-checks (the worst board — it's the covered player's nuts); A-plus-broadway is half-pot/check, second-pair Kx is multi-street bluff material (it blocks the K). Turn rule: if you fired big and polar on the flop, stay big-polar on a low turn, step down to small on a high turn (to value your new pairs); if you fired small on the flop, stay small and wide on the turn too.

**Rule:** as the leading OOP, two questions — "what's the top of the covered player's range, and what blocks it?" and "on which board does my trash range hold the nuts?" — this is range percentage, not a position label. This is the flop/turn arm to 21.3-EK-2's "river size lever"; it's the ICM twin of 28.22.

### 21.4-EK The Limit of the MDF Collapse: Against a Small Range-Bet, the Covered Player Folds Nothing


B21.4 says "folding below MDF is correct under ICM" — that's true against a big polar bet, and against a board that hits villain's range. The limit: if the covering player range-bets small on a board he should be checking (33.10's check-heavy boards; against a range dense with A-high/J-high/K-low), his range fills with trash while yours holds pairs and backdoors:

- **The answer:** fold ZERO, call ~80%, raise ~20% *(calibrate)*. Stay passive: he loses to himself with that bet. Don't invent a high check-raise frequency — where a raise does come, 13-20% is enough.
- **Raise material is backwards:** the version of a marginal continue WITHOUT a backdoor draw raises (J3/Q4 with no flush draw, 98 with no flush card), the version WITH a backdoor draw calls. Value: top pair; on J-high (J-4-3), AJ/KJ raise BIG — you have no set, and you need protection; the leader folds AQ/pocket pairs.
- **Turn:** the leader shows up with his whole range and checks a lot → stab with a hand that has nothing (21.2-EK-4), check if you connected with something. On the river, ¾ pot folds out half of IP *(calibrate)*.
- **Against a correctly-sized bet, this is normal:** if the bet comes on the right board with the right size (~40% on 8-3-2), the covered player still folds little (~15%) and raises little — the range is polar, and raising a medium hand into that is pointless.
- **Note:** this doesn't break 22.8-EK's "no OOP flat against the leader" — what's being discussed here is postflop with the dense range you've ALREADY flatted tight.

> **The MDF collapse is against a big polar bet. If the leader range-bets small on your board, the covered player folds nothing: call wide, raise rarely and with backdoor-less marginals, stab into his forced turn check. The fold reflex is a gift here.**

### 21.5-EK Fragile Value Isn't Always a Bet Under ICM: If the Villain Can Check-Raise, Wait for a Safe Turn


B21.5 says "fragile = bet (protection)"; that's for OOP's lead/c-bet and a dry board. While covered, a second cost kicks in on IP's stab: on a board that grows the pot around you, that kills you on many runouts, and that you sit worst against a check-raise on, a protection bet inflates your own pot.

- **A static high board (K-6-3 rainbow):** 66/AK/KQ stay strong for three streets → high-frequency small stab, almost every hand mixes bet/check, a backdoor flush draw doesn't matter.
- **A connected high board (K-T-9/Q-9-8):** even with HIGHER equity, the stab is RARER *(calibrate)*. Kx, KQ, even sets lose their equity on flush/J/Q runouts and can't stand up to an x/r → check the flop, bet a brick turn. There's almost no single "pure bet" hand.
- **On the turn, the lock hand checks:** TT/99 (a set, ~a lock) checks back on the turn — it blocks every hand that would call (Tx/9x), and the draws already bet on their own, so no protection is needed. KJ bets: Tx/9x pay it, and it wants protection from A5o. "The weak hand bets, the strong hand checks" isn't backwards here — it's correct.
- **Villain's side (OOP):** he only bets by equity (a JT with a backdoor flush draw bets, without one it checks); IP doesn't care about the flush. This asymmetry ties IP's "when do I check" question to the board, and OOP's to the hand.

> **When the risk premium is high, the license is durability, not equity. On a connected high board, a strong-but-fragile hand checks the flop and bets a safe turn; if the lock hand blocks the calls, it checks the turn too. B21.5's protection bet is for a static board.**

### 21.5-EK-2 Fragile-Strong Hand + Dynamic Board → the Covered Short Stack's BIG Flop/Turn Jam — the One Big Exception to "Downgrade One Notch"


Root error: as a covered short (~20bb) IP or a covered OOP opener, applying "premium is high, small bet/check" — the thin-value reflex — to a fragile-strong hand with AK/AQ on an A-high board. Mechanism: at high premium it isn't equity that's priced, it's equity REALIZATION. On an A-low-low plus flush-draw board, AQ is way ahead on the flop *(calibrate)*; on a flush/straight turn against villain's lead, with equity still over half, it FOLDS — realization collapses, because the paying range has shifted to the nuts and the premium forbids the call. The fix: put the money in NOW, while still ahead — a flop jam for several times the pot *(calibrate)*. Condition: villain's range must be loaded with pair-plus-flush-draw or pair-plus-straight-draw combos (A63ss/AJ3ss: bottom pair plus a FD; 985 rainbow: 9x/8x plus an OESD) — the jam gets paid by these, naked draws fold: both payoff and fold equity. If villain has no low-pair-plus-draw (an A-high FD board, AJ5), no jam, half pot instead. Material: value = AK/AQ/AJ WITHOUT a draw card (wants protection); bluff = trash that blocks villain's BEST Ax (JT/TT/99 on AJ3, KJ/QJ on A54) — not an FD, since an FD already lives on its own equity in a call. The worry that "he might have the straight" is wrong: if he does, you're already losing anyway; the jam makes villain pay for the runouts you'll go on to lose. The OOP version: a covered tight opener jams most of his range on a pair-plus-flush-draw turn after an A-K-x pot bet — because AK would be doomed to check-fold on half the deck.

**Rule:** a thin/static hand → downgrade the notch (21.2); a fragile-strong hand plus an abundance of pair-plus-draw combos in villain's range plus many bad runouts → notch UP, jam. Boundary: only for short-to-medium stacks (pre-bet SPR ≤ ~5 *(calibrate)*) and only on these board classes; on a low board like K54 where villain has the set/two-pair advantage, the opposite — check back at a high rate. Poker-sanity note: this doesn't violate the covered player's "both the bluff AND thin value get downgraded" rule — thin value gets downgraded, FRAGILE value doesn't (21.5 already separates them); this note writes out the extreme case of that distinction.

### 21.5-EK-3 The "Turning Into a Bluff-Catcher" Lead — If the Villain's Default Size Is Big/Overbet, Lead SMALL on the Turn/River with a Medium-Strong Hand, Not Check-Call


Root error: checking with two pair or a strong one pair as OOP and then facing the "call or fold" dilemma against a big bet. Mechanism: if IP's river size is polar-big (overbet *(calibrate)*), even two pair turns into a bluff-catcher after a check — turning a strong hand into a bluff-catcher burns value, and burns it twice at high premium (the call-off tax, 21.1). A small lead (one-tenth to one-third of the pot) collects payment from every one pair IP would check back with (7x, 9x-QQ, weak Kx) and blocks the big bet; if a raise comes, the nut slice (straight/set) reopens, two pair can fold. The bluff requirement is low — missed combo draws are enough. Where: Kx two pair on a K/T turn, Ax on an A river, A-highs on a brick 9-turn; widest for the covering BB, only the strong slice for the covered BB *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** "if I check, I'll eat a big bet and become a bluff-catcher" → small lead; "if I check, I'll face a small bet" → check-call. B21.2's "big→small" row is read here not for villain's size, but to protect your own hand; this is the OOP turn/river extension of B21.5's protection-first.

### 21.7-EK The Limit of the "Size: Big/Polarized" Row — Pressure on a Locked-Up Villain Is Sold Through FREQUENCY, Not SIZE


The "size big/polarized" cell in B21.7's leader column assumes villain reads price. A villain locked up by ICM doesn't read price: he folds his offsuit 8x/3x, his weak pairs, regardless of size → if the fold rate is size-independent, the cheapest size buys the same fold — a small c-bet, a small lead, a small ICM squeeze/3-bet (the leader-side face of 22.8-EK's "a small ICM 3-bet buys cheap fold equity" row). Pressure is sold by raising FREQUENCY, not size: open more hands, bet more often, each one small *(deviation: calibrate — at the margin)*. The exception runs two ways: (a) if a short BB in the jam-or-fold band is calling a min-raise by price, size GROWS to cut off that call (12.4-EK, 12.2-EK-5); (b) against a reg who isn't locked up and does read price, B21.7 applies as written — take value with a big size. Principle: against a rec/a locked-up villain, choose size with your HAND (whatever the hand wants, since villain isn't reading price); against a reg, choose it with your RANGE (27.12, 23.6-EK). B12.4 selects the locked-up target, this row selects its price.

### 21.1-EK-3 The bluff stock runs out on the early street — the catch decision looks back at the flop


The bluff-catch range is set by the villain's EARLY-street discipline as much as by combinatorics: a river bluff is born from the floats held on the flop. If the villain can't find the right floats on the flop (backdoor-equipped high cards, weak draws) and folds early, the bluff stock for river aggression is physically not there — even the catcher with the nicest blocker gets away with folding; against a type who plays sticky on the early streets the stock is full, the catch book stays open. ICM doubles this: while the call-off is already the most heavily taxed action (21.1), a hero-call against a stockless villain is premium-funded suicide. The same test works on the check-raise line too — the seat is flipped (the villain is the raiser, not the caller, side) but the read direction is the same: a type who holds bluffs on the x/r range (backdoor trash, protection raises) and check-raises the flop but almost never gives it up on the turn carries those bluffs forward to the turn as-is — his continuation is bluff-rich, the catch book stays open. The value read belongs to the type who gives up OFTEN on the turn: because his range concentrates, the continuation value he carries to the turn is purified.

**Rule:** Don't ask "does he have a bluff" on the river — ask what bluff raw material he HELD on the flop; the late aggression of a type who folds early (and gives up often after his x/r) is mostly value, while the one who never gives up anything carries bluffs.

### 21.2-EK-9 With no trash, the bluff comes from the TOP — the narrow-range exception


The rule "pick the bluff from trash and the lowest card" assumes there IS trash in your range. The range ICM narrows mostly consists of pairs + high cards only: there are no missed suited connectors, so bluff material necessarily comes from up top — A-high, even K-high, which would count as insanity in chips. The same narrowing promotes middle pairs to value and collapses the villain's bluff-catch threshold: hands like AQ/AJ with good removal drop to the borderline even against a small bet. This effect grows as passive streets go by, because both sides' top has thinned.

**Rule:** Pick the bluff from your range, not from your memorized list — with no trash slice, the bluff and the value come from the same neighborhood, and you have to find your fold from up top too.

### 21.3-EK-7 Folded micro stacks inflate EVERYONE's premium in the pot


The cover-asymmetry table misleads if it only looks at the two players in the hand: while 3 micro stacks sit on the rail waiting on the ladder, two big stacks close to each other carry a high premium in BOTH directions — a 1bb cover doesn't hand out a leader's whip. The result is cooperative passivity: the OOP opener reverts to near-global check on most boards *(calibrate)*, IP bets small, and both sides keep the pot small. Bonus: IP's small bets realize MORE than chipEV, because the check-raises coming back at them are both rarer and smaller.

**Rule:** Don't write only the players in the hand into the premium calculation — also write in the folded short stacks on the rail waiting for a payout jump; they are everyone's brake in the pot.

### 21.3-EK-8 The limp-check pot is ICM's main stage — don't write it off as a 3bb pot


The most frequent knot of high-premium BvB is the limp-check pot, and nobody fights hard enough for these pots. With a super-wide range you check plenty even on a high-equity board — on an A-high board, even with an Ax edge, you don't have a c-bet license because most of the deck is in your range; making the bet 1bb is already everyone's move. Three things make the difference: folding only your deepest trash against the tiny bet *(calibrate)*, finding a TINY check-raise with value, and picking the bluff-raise from high-low combos that touch the board plus fake draw cards — so you have a bluff when the draws arrive.

**Rule:** In a limp pot, a small bet is everyone's move; the edge is in wide continuing + a tiny check-raise.

### 21.3-EK-9 In a wide-vs-wide pot, the imbalance reads from the "raise accomplished nothing" class


In a limp-check pot, ranges are tracked by CLASS, not hand by hand: the middle offsuit class where iso-raising or raising accomplishes nothing (roughly offsuit 7x-9x and connectors like T9/J9 — only the better hands continue against a raise, the worse ones were folding already) piles up on the check line on both sides. If the board touches this class (something like 8-7-x), the pot plays passively and a small bet doesn't generate folds — if you're going to bet, pick a big size; if it doesn't touch, small sizes targeting the high-card class work.

**Rule:** In a limp pot, scan the board for "did it touch the middle offsuit class?" — if it did, a small bet isn't productive.

## Chapter 22 — ICM: When and Against Whom — Timeline + Laddering

*★ v6. B20 gave the risk premium's DEFINITION and direction (highest vs a coverer, ~0 vs a non-covering short; exact value calibrated/HRC). But the premium isn't constant: it opens and closes across the tournament (timeline) and which players it applies to shifts with the stack distribution (laddering). B22 gives when and against whom that premium changes.*

### 22.0 Thesis

> **The risk premium isn't a constant, it's a curve. In time: it peaks approaching the bubble, releases once the money is made, rises again approaching the FT, drops a notch after every pay jump. By person: high against a deep stack that covers you, ~0 against a lower stack that does NOT cover you. The person axis comes BEFORE the phase axis: the phase sets the premium's ceiling, the cover map sets whom it applies to.**

### 22.1 Timeline — the premium's four phases

Before ICM is felt (deep in the tournament, money far off) the premium is already ~0; the cycle starts at the bubble approach. Rough onset: when **players left ≈ ~3× the number of paid places**, ICM starts to be felt *(calibrate — depends on structure and payout curve)*.

1. **Bubble approach** — premium at the ceiling. The shorts' CALL range locks up (their JAM range STAYS — B17); big stacks (who cover everyone) sell the most pressure, mid stacks EAT the most. Call tightest against whoever covers you; the most aggressive stealing is sold by big stacks to the shorts they COVER.
2. **Bubble burst / early ITM** — the premium at its lowest within the cycle. Min-cash secured, the next jumps are small relative to stack value; in this window you play closest to chipEV. Gas: build chips.
3. **FT approach** — the premium rises again (2-3 tables left, then the FT bubble). Every elimination is big money. Second brake.
4. **Loosen after every jump** — when someone busts (a rung is passed) the premium drops a notch; as the table shortens and money locks in, gas again.

### 22.2 Phase table

| Phase | Premium (vs a coverer) | Mode |
|---|---|---|
| **Bubble approach** | Ceiling | Tight call (vs whoever covers you); aggressive steal (vs non-coverers) |
| **Bubble burst / early ITM** | Lowest in cycle | Gas — build |
| **FT approach** | High | Second brake — tighten |
| **After each elimination** | Drops a notch | Loosen briefly, then gas |

> **Precedence rule (B12.4): the phase premium applies ONLY to those who cover you. Against a non-covering short jam the premium is ~0 in every phase (B20) → call wide even on the bubble. The phase sets the ceiling, the cover map sets whom it applies to.**

### 22.3 What laddering is

Laddering is climbing a pay rung purely by surviving, when every elimination is a pay jump. It's a **mid-stack discipline**: for the leader the premium is low (free, sells pressure), the mid stack is covered (its ladder equity is highest). Two leaks:

- **Over-ladder:** nitting a healthy stack purely to ladder — you miss the chance to build and blind down.
- **Under-ladder:** ignoring the ladder and flipping needlessly with a mega that covers you — busting while covered.

### 22.4 Stack scan: whom to pressure, whom to respect

- **Attack a lower stack that does NOT cover you, not the leader.** A stack shorter than you doesn't cover you → pressure is cheap (premium ~0). The leader covers you → premium high, respect.
- **Short stack: its CALL narrows, its JAM STAYS (B17).** Being short isn't "shove everything" but it isn't nitting either: under cover pressure the thing that narrows hard is the CALL range; your unopened JAM range stays wide thanks to fold equity, tightening only a notch against coverers. "Don't enter the money by folding" (cutting the jam) is right only in an extreme spot: when another micro is about to bust (a direct ladder) *(calibrate)*.
- **An outlier sharpens ICM and creates asymmetry** (a mega crushes some, a micro makes the ladder clear). **In homogeneous stacks the asymmetry vanishes but the premium does not:** because everyone covers everyone, the conflict premium is mutually high → a homogeneous bubble table plays tight for everyone (the classic equal-stack "fold even AA" satellite spot).

### 22.5 The two-way leak table

| Leak | What you do | Why it's wrong | Fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Over-ladder** | Nit a healthy stack | Miss building, blind down | Gas where there's no premium |
| **Under-ladder** | Flip with a mega that covers you | Bust while covered | Read the cover map, add the premium |
| **Short-panic** | Inflate the jam range uncalibrated | Waste fold equity, bust | KEEP the jam, narrow the CALL (B17) |
| **Short-nit** | Fold a hand you should jam | Blind out, burn the ladder | Keep the jam range wide |

### 22.6 Day 2 / bubble application

Concrete: if you restart with ~12-15 left (e.g. WSOP Online Main Day 2 — online structures collapse the field to a few tables), players left are far below the paid places → you're squarely in the FT-approach phase, premium high. Against non-covering shorts/mids your ladder value is big and the premium ~0 (call/steal wide); against a leader who covers you the premium is at the ceiling (respect, tighten). Loosen a notch after each jump. Your steal target: players who do NOT cover you.

### 22.7 Calibration

*(calibrate: the onset threshold (~3× paid places), each phase's exact premium and the ladder boundary depend on structure/payout curve/players left; HRC/ICMIZER gives them. In the app this slot fills from the solver pipeline's ICM mode + tournament structure. At the table estimate phase + stack map in three tiers (ceiling/medium/low); re-read the cover map every hand.)*

### 22.8 Cheat card

| Signal | Read | Action |
|---|---|---|
| **Bubble near + covers you** | Premium ceiling | Tight call, respect |
| **Bubble near + non-covering short jam** | Premium ~0 | Call wide (B20) |
| **New ITM** | Premium dip | Gas, build |
| **FT approach** | Premium high | Tighten, protect ladder |
| **You as leader vs a lower stack** | Premium low | Sell pressure |

### 22.9 Drill (3 questions)

1. **T1.** The bubble just burst, you're a mid stack; which phase in the cycle is closest to chipEV and what do you do?
