2. **T2.** Bubble: a stack shorter than you (NOT covering you) jams into you; a leader who covers you is also at the table. What's your call threshold vs the short jam, and vs the leader?
3. **T3.** You're a short stack on the bubble. Does your jam range or your call range narrow; which one is "enter with your best hands" correct for?

*Root-error tie-in: B20 gave the premium's DEFINITION and direction; B22 gives how that premium changes in time (phase) and by person (stack map). The phase sets the ceiling, the cover map sets whom it applies to. Over-ladder and under-ladder are the time/stack faces of the cover-reading leak (B12.4); short-panic and short-nit are the two faces of the B17 jam/call split.*


---

### 22.1-EK-3 The bubble-factor curve: bubble peak → ITM collapse → START of the FT highest; the first phase is the leader's harvest window


22.1's four phases give the direction; the SHAPE of the curve adds three conclusions *(calibrate all of them)*. The premium peaks at the bubble, collapses ITM, and at the start of the FT climbs to the HIGHEST point of the cycle — higher even than the bubble: every rung is big relative to stack, everyone covers everyone.

1. **The harvest window.** In the FT's first phase (until the first elimination or the first short bustouts; its length depends on the table) everyone is tight → the leader steals with impunity; the window narrows with the first bust, and the premium drops a notch with every elimination (22.1 item 4). For the short/mid the same phase is the tightest phase — 22.2-EK as is.
2. **The ICM sim doesn't see future game.** As the leader, do your borderline widening with suited, connected, playable hands — not with offsuit broadways: of two hands the sim sees as equal, the one that realizes postflop wins (the leader version of 22.10).
3. **No squeeze in a passive field → wide flat.** If the table isn't finding the squeeze, the leader's IP flat goes unpunished (12.4-EK "freeze the table"); the reverse at a reg table that finds the squeeze.

The flip side of 28.6-EK's "I'm at the FT, tighten" reflex: the start of the FT is the leader's widest phase and the covered player's narrowest.

### 22.1-EK The bubble isn't one phase, it's a per-elimination staircase — the jammer cuts the bottom layer, the covering caller stays fixed; the leader's weapon is pressure, not the call


Both "23 left, 20 paid → I'm on the bubble" and "there are still 3 to go → chipEV" are wrong; the premium accrues per elimination. Three pieces: (1) The jammer (the covered player) — with every elimination approaching the pay line, the EV of marginal jams drops noticeably *(amount: calibrate)*; the top (AK/QQ) is barely affected, what gets erased is always the bottom layer (small-mid pairs, offsuit A-high, mid suited broadways) — the per-elimination reading of 22.1's phase premium; for a covering jammer the layer doesn't get erased this much. On the pure bubble that layer largely turns into a fold. (2) The covering caller — the call range of a stack that can still fold its way into the money even if it loses stays ALMOST unchanged as the bubble approaches (mid pair + A-high core *(calibrate)*; the coverer's premium is ~0 — B20); the lower bound wiggles by one pair, "sixes / KQs get in" mostly doesn't happen. (3) The leader on the pure bubble — the RELATIVELY widest call at the table still belongs to the stack that covers everyone (it makes the money even if it loses); but in plain ICM the required call equity rises SLIGHTLY as the bubble approaches, and since the jammer's range has narrowed too, even the leader's absolute threshold TIGHTENS a notch *(calibrate)* — the "on the pure bubble the leader opens up" mantra is wrong; the leader's bubble advantage isn't on the call side but on the pressure side, jamming/raising into stacks that can't call. Rule: update not how many players are left but "am I the one waiting for an elimination, or are they waiting for me" — if you're the jammer, cut the bottom layer with every elimination; if you're the covering caller, keep the range fixed (tighten a notch at the margin on the pure bubble); sell the pressure with the jam/raise, not with the call. The 22.2 table doesn't change.

### 22.1-EK-2 The soft-bubble test — "can I fold my way into the money with this stack?" and field size


Looking at the left/paid RATIO and playing the same bubble range in every field misses two things. (1) Field size: small-to-mid fields (up to a few hundred players) give a similar call range; once the field crosses a threshold *(calibrate)*, the pure-bubble call range tightens noticeably — across the remaining tables the volume of other people's mistakes grows, the edge premium (B24) thickens. A spot taken at the FT for a small stack-EV demands a gain that is large relative to stack *(calibrate)* on a giant-field bubble; that is 24.2-EK's field multiplier. (2) The soft bubble: ~20% away from the money, in a small field the call range is much wider than on the pure bubble (one notch below chipEV); in a big field the same percentage plays even WIDER — 15bb can't wait out that many eliminations by folding. The test: "can I fold my way into the money?" NO → the soft bubble is a gamble phase: arriving at the hard bubble with a stack (crushing the bubble with 30bb) is worth more than passing up small calls. YES → pass; the same opponent will open wide later and your rejam will be worth more (22.2-EK-2). Set the threshold with two questions: how many tables is the field, and does my stack reach the money by folding. The 12.0 table doesn't change; its "field character" column is read with this tightness.

### 22.1-EK-4 Field size FLIPS the stone-bubble asymmetry — in a giant field the coverer loosens, the short locks up


Root error: playing by the left/paid ratio ("64/63 = 10/9 = 489/488, all stone bubble"). Mechanism: same stacks, same SB-BB, three fields: **small field** (linear payouts, FT in sight) → both sides' premiums are high and SYMMETRIC (short ≈ coverer, both above double digits — *(calibrate)*); even the leader isn't at zero, carrying a few points because it can see the FT. **Mid-to-large field** → both premiums drop, the leader comes down to chipEV, the short carries the same premium against everyone (no rivalry). **Giant field** (dozens of tables) → the gap OPENS: the coverer's premium drops (even if it loses, someone busts at another table and it makes the money), the short's premium RISES (it folds its way into the money). Result: on a giant-field stone bubble the short SB's jam is cut in half and turns to limping; the covering BB's call WIDENS — A9o/K-high suited, a clear fold in a small field, turns into a call *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** on the stone bubble the first question is "how many tables are left": one or two tables → symmetric ceiling, the short jams any-two, the coverer calls very tight; dozens of tables → one-way pressure, the short cuts the jam, the coverer pays wide. The 12.0 "field character" column is read through this line.

### 22.2-EK The short stack: CALL-locked on the bubble, relatively free at the FT (there is NO inversion)


A common mistake: "the short stack's premium is higher than the mid's on the bubble." It is NOT. In ICM the raw premium ordering is the same in both phases: **short < mid.** The short's premium is low because a double changes the MOST for it (it escapes the min-cash and becomes a real stack); the mid stack carries the highest premium (a double adds little, a bust kills a nearly-certain cash — B3.2 is right, for the raw premium too). The real difference is **not in the ordering but in the short's POSTURE across the two phases**:

- **On the stone bubble:** the short's premium, though low, is >1 → even marginal calls that are profitable in chipEV turn negative; that's why the short genuinely "turns to stone" (the CALL lock, B22.1). The lock comes not from its premium being higher than the mid's, but from premium>1 + the min-cash lock.
- **At the FT:** the money is made, the min-cash lock is off → the short's premium loosens relatively, and it gambles more freely than the mid. But it's not "nothing to lose": if there's an even shorter stack behind it and a big ladder rung, the short's jam tightens too (B12.2 — be the first jammer, but watch the ladder).

> **"The mid is the most fragile on the bubble" is true both for the strategic squeeze and for the raw premium: the mid carries the highest premium. The short is lower-premium than the MID in both phases — what makes the difference is the min-cash lock on the bubble (the CALL narrows) and that lock lifting at the FT.**

### 22.2-EK-2 Premium ≠ the future-game value of the double — the arrival role: "who do I become if I win?"


22.2-EK says "for the short, a double changes the most" — true for the raw PREMIUM (the call threshold). The FUTURE-GAME value of the double is measured on a different scale: the role you arrive at. Going from 30bb to 60 dominates the table (covers everyone, becomes a bubble hunter); going from 20bb to 40 is still a covered mid stack. The math is two-layered: the ICM premium brakes the mid-deep more than the short (the covered player's premium is high, it has more $EV to lose); the future-game value of the double NARROWS that gap — and at ICM-insensitive tables it can flip it *(calibrate)*. The short gets squeezed toward the min-cash only when there are even shorter stacks at the table. The population error: the short shoves too much, and the mid-deep forgets to add the future-game value into the premium; the covering big stack, meanwhile, pays the short off with a fixed range (22.1-EK). Three applications: (1) 3-handed, the stack that becomes the LEADER when it doubles (whichever seat it's in) widens its call/rejam range against a shove noticeably compared to a stack that would still be covered after doubling — winning buys the pressure seat; (2) this value grows if you believe you play the big stack well, and shrinks if the opponents are ICM-insensitive (the B24 edge premium); (3) for the short, 8→16bb is a "living stack" (one more double = a shot at winning), 3→6 a "dead stack" — take the first double wide; a double after blinding out is useless. A fifth question for 24.2: "who do I become if I win?" (24.2-EK).

### 22.2-EK-3 The post-ITM "gas" phase is a BRAKE phase for the big stack when everyone is hitting the gas


22.2's "bubble burst → gas, build" line is not table-independent; it assumes the population is NOT also hitting the gas. The phase premium is low, true; but when half the field is under 20bb and everyone, knowing the same lesson, is busting off, the covering deep stack's highest EV is to wait for the COLLISION: a few players bust every hand, the next rung comes closer to you, and you ladder without risking your chips (22.11's ITM application). The brake is not "nit": every dealt hand in position and against a rec stays +EV (over-realize, 21.3-EK-2) — what gets cut is the marginal 3-bet/flip against aggressive regs. The test: "are there two-plus players at the table ready to eliminate each other in my place?" YES → brake, selective; NO (table passive/homogeneous) → 22.2's gas as is. Near the end of the day, a second layer: arriving deep on the next day collects for free the rungs produced by the field busting itself overnight. The phase table doesn't change; this line is the table-conditional exception to "gas" — for the short and mid stack the gas line stays exactly as it is.

### 22.2-EK-4 On the stone bubble the short stack's premium is tied not to bb but to the QUEUE — "how many players below me are waiting to bust?"


Root error: "I'm 10bb, nothing to lose → premium low," or the reverse, "I'm short → premium always highest." Mechanism: on the stone bubble the short stack's best-case scenario is the min-cash; if there are even shorter stacks in the field, it folds its way into the money → its premium climbs ABOVE the mid stack's (at the same table a 10bb stack carries a higher premium than a 30bb one; 7bb stacks one and a half times a 22bb — *(calibrate)*). For the mid stack the future value of a double is big, and bubbling is "embarrassing" but not a disaster → its premium stays low. Conversely, when nobody is BELOW the short stack (the field's shortest, facing a clear chip leader) its premium collapses: the same 8bb can carry 1% in one tournament and 10% in another *(calibrate)* — not the bb count, but the ratio to average + the number of bust candidates below it. In a mini field, with TWO shorts below it even the mid stack's premium jumps; with a single short it still has to fight.

**Rule:** the premium question isn't "how many bb am I" but "can I fold my way into the money, and how many bust candidates are below me." YES/many → premium at the ceiling: cut the jam, open the call only on equity against the range (12.2-EK-6). NO/zero → premium low, the double's value high, gamble freely. The 22.2 table doesn't change.

### 22.2-EK-5 The low post-bubble premium (3-7%) changes the TREE, not the opening chart — the open-jam disappears, the OOP call vs the 3-bet disappears


Root error: "ITM, premium low → straight chipEV" (jams and calls included), or the reverse, "3 tables left → tighten." Mechanism: three tables left, FT far off: premium 3-7%, the RFI range ~1% tighter than chipEV (only the bottom combos drop) — the opening chart is nearly identical. But the small premium erases two nodes: (1) **OPEN-JAM** — the 14bb HJ's chipEV jam drops from ~11% to ~2% *(calibrate)*: the min-raise does the same job (the players behind are passive because of the premium) without risking the whole stack; at 25bb effective, even with short blinds, the open-jam is zero → 3-bet-call/3-bet-fold; "a good hand induces": AQ as a 3-bet-call is clearly more profitable than the jam — it keeps the opponent's QTs/A5s jams in. (2) **THE OOP CALL VS A 3-BET** — even at a 5% premium the OOP call vs a 3-bet is nearly zero: pairs jam, the rest folds; only AJs/JTs are borderline calls. Calling a short's jam, by contrast, stays close to chipEV (the premium is symmetric, ~4% in both directions). The BB's rejam uses the same hands as chipEV; the differences are the widening of the 3-bet-call (JJ/QQ) and the bluff 3-bets being blocker-heavy (A2o/K3o/K5o). Blocker choice: shallow, HIGH cards (3-bet KJo/KQo/ATo: the opponent jams pairs + suited broadways — block those); deep/vs a narrow range, low suited Ax.

**Rule:** post-bubble "gas" = RFI and BB rejam at chipEV; open-jam → min-raise; OOP call → jam/fold. The 22.2 phase table doesn't change; 22.2-EK-3's "brake" is for the big stack, this line is tree selection for everyone.

### 22.2-EK-6 The premium is measured by your SHARE of total chips, not by the bb count — 20%+ premium with 5bb, ~3% with 8bb


Root error: the "I'm 5bb, nothing to lose, premium zero" reflex. Mechanism: the premium is the rung cost of busting; that cost is set by your stack's RATIO to the total chips at the table and by the existence of stacks shorter than you, not by its size in blinds. With two stacks shorter than you (2bb and 4bb), even a 5bb stack is in the "outlast them and take the rung" position → premium near the ceiling *(calibrate)*; the same 5bb, when everyone is 20bb+ and you're the shortest by a mile, premium ~0. The same law higher up: holding 8% of the total chips the premium is moderate; holding 18% (the covered second stack) it's much higher *(calibrate)* — as chips accumulate, there is more to lose.

**Rule:** not "how many bb am I" but "how much of the chips do I hold and who is shorter than me" — look at those two questions, forget the bb count. The 12.2 role matrix's "shorter than me YES/NO" column IS this law; the matrix doesn't change, this line is its numerical justification.

### 22.4-EK Direct rivalry — in a small field the premium varies by PERSON: for the short, low against a stack close to its own, high against the leader


Root error: putting every coverer in the same basket ("I'm covered → same premium"). Mechanism: in a giant field it doesn't matter whom you lose to or off whom you double — someone busts across hundreds of tables anyway, so the premium is equal against everyone. As the field shrinks (two-three tables, FT in sight), DIRECT rivals for the bubble emerge: the short stack's premium against a stack close to its own is a few points lower than against the chip leader *(calibrate)* — doubling off it makes IT the bubble candidate; doubling off the leader doesn't change the leader's life. The same engine makes two short stacks fight each other at nearly chipEV (single digits against each other, double digits against the bigs — *(calibrate)*).

**Rule:** in a small field target selection is two-layered — (1) 22.4: attack the non-coverer; (2) among the coverers pick the one CLOSEST to your own stack, the leader last. The other side of the mirror: if you're the mid stack, the shorts shove WIDER into you than into the leader — open your call book against a short you cover to match that width (12.1-EK-2), and keep expecting the leader to take shots at you. The time axis: post-bubble with 3 tables left the rivalry disappears (premium symmetric, cover difference small — 22.2-EK-5); pre-FT at 16-10 it opens up again.

### 22.4-EK-2 Two covering opponents don't give the same premium — what the double TURNS the opponent INTO sets the premium (the "satellite" table)


Root error: reading the premium on a single axis ("covers me → high, doesn't → low"). Mechanism: against two opponents who both cover you, your premium differs — according to what doubling off them does TO THEM. Doubling off a distant leader leaves him still the leader → earns you no rung → premium against him at the ceiling; doubling off a mid stack close to you puts him at the door → the rung is credited to you → same covered status, much lower premium *(calibrate)*. The extreme case is the "satellite" table: one runaway leader + a few similar shorts/mids → the shorts are at nearly chipEV against each other (premium ~zero, they have to bust each other — the only way to reach heads-up), at the ceiling against the leader; the leader, meanwhile, plays chipEV against everyone (nobody can bust him). The reverse is also true: with MANY shorts of similar size (3–4 stacks of 10bb), the premium against each other rises — everyone prefers to wait for someone else's bust.

**Rule:** a separate premium for each opponent: "if I double off this guy, who does HE become?" — if I'm putting him at the door, premium low; if nothing changes, ceiling. That's why in the same hand you play chipEV against the SB and turn to stone against the BB — same hand, three opponents, three premiums.

### 22.8-EK Two TEMPTING nodes — and why "NO FLAT" still holds


Two nodes look like invitations to flat/call wide; in both, the right answer is again the book's rule (B5.2 "NO FLAT", B22.4 "attack the NON-coverer"). Recognize the temptation, follow the rule:

1. **OOP against a wide-opening leader on the bubble (while covered):** "The leader is opening trash, I'll take a postflop edge with a cheap flat" is WRONG — OOP against someone who covers you is where your equity realization is at its WORST (you get barreled with impunity, condemned to overfold on every street). A weak opening range doesn't rescue this. The right play: **shove / click-raise / fold** — punish the leader with preflop pressure, don't give him the postflop. Your real pressure targets are the stacks that do NOT cover you (B22.4).
2. **Against a small-sized ICM 3-bet (while covered):** the small size doesn't sell you a "cheap call," it buys the opponent cheap fold equity. The small ICM 3-bet profits precisely from the covered player's postflop under-realization → the right exploit isn't a wide call, it's **jam-or-fold** (jam only with premium value — around QQ+ *(calibrate)*; fold the marginals).

> **"NO FLAT" is a rule that is size-independent and tied to the cover RELATIONSHIP: no flatting while covered — even if the size shrinks, even if the leader opens trash. When you see the temptation, cling to the rule TIGHTER.**

### 22.8-EK-2 Mid-vs-mid at the FT, ~30bb: the flat TRAP collapses — TT/99 not a flat, not a jam, FOLD


22.8-EK said "no flatting OOP with marginals"; this item gives the node where the IP flat collapses too, and adds a flat column to 12.1's "FT: tighten the jam two notches" line. In chipEV, half of the TT/99/AQs/KQs flat from CO/HJ against an open IS the trap: when a reshove comes from behind, a comfortable call. At the FT, with the mid stacks covering each other, the premium on calling a jam grows → TT/99 turns into a "flat that will fold to the reshove"; jamming, meanwhile, pays 22.11's collision premium and is a flip against an opening range that still has its top → the result is FOLD. The flat range shrinks to JJ/QQ/AQs/AKs/KQs *(thresholds: calibrate — depends on stack distribution)*; two options remain: "is it strong enough to call a reshove?" yes → flat/3-bet; no → fold. At ~50bb the same hand from a slightly later position turns back into a pure call — the reshove premium having dropped, the trap works again. Side effect: the narrow flat signals a "do-not-attack node" to the players behind. 5.2's "jam TT+ vs an EP open" is for ICM-off; 12.2-EK-3's "mid pairs live in the call book" is the UN-covered node — here the cover is mutual and the book closes.

### 22.10 Future game: blind geometry corrects ICM in two directions


ICM is a static one-hand model — it doesn't see who enters the blinds next hand. Two corrections make money at the table:

1. **If YOUR blind is approaching, WIDEN:** as a short stack, in the hand right before entering the blinds, jam noticeably wider than the ICM sim gives — the approaching forced blind-off kills the "wait" option. The full-BB ante (standard today both live and online) concentrates the erosion into a single hand and sharpens this widening (even from UTG).
2. **If a short OPPONENT'S blind is approaching, TIGHTEN:** if the short opponent enters the BB next hand, your jam-bluff and call ranges against him get tighter EVEN than ICM — wait out his forced erosion, climb the ladder for free by folding.

**Overcall hygiene (error asymmetry):** in a spot that looks EV-equal with a short's jam in front and players behind you — if you know FOR CERTAIN your answer to the subsequent all-in, call (zero loss + the information option); if you don't, jam yourself. A wrong jam is a small mistake, a wrong overcall a giant torch. Tiny stack differences flip this node → pick the robust line.

> **ICM is a one-hand snapshot; the table is a film. Widen as your own blind approaches, tighten as the short's blind approaches. And in an uncertain overcall: call if you're sure, jam if you're not — the wrong overcall is the most expensive FT mistake.**

*(calibrate: the widening/tightening thresholds depend on stack + distance to the blinds + payouts; verify with an ICM simulator that can model the future hand.)*

### 22.10-EK Deep (≥~22bb) stack vs a short's jam: default CALL, not rejam — overcall hygiene narrows; the SB is the one exception


Playing "jam or fold" with 25-35bb against a 6-12bb jam makes you fold hands like A9o/77 — wrong. The short's jam range is dense with weak Ax/small pairs/suited Kx; mid pairs and A-high dominate it → CALL is +EV. The REJAM, by contrast, risks your stack against the ranges behind; the players behind don't pay with small pairs and don't fold overpairs → when you get called, you're crushed. The call tree WIDENS the continuing range noticeably compared to a rejam-only tree *(roughly a fifth — calibrate)* and gives the option of releasing the bottom layer when a jam comes from behind: continue only with the strong slice. As fewer players remain behind (BTN), the call widens a notch. The one exception is the SB: if the BB behind is deep enough to be able to fold to a rejam (fold equity EXISTS), jam-only — a flat call hands the BB a cheap in-position overcall/squeeze; against a committed short BB, call and rejam are equivalent. A second layer: a deep rejam narrows the call ranges behind → the CO/BTN rejam widens a bit *(A9s/ATo — calibrate)*, but only while the players behind really do pay tight. **The 22.10 overcall line narrows:** at ≥~22bb *(calibrate)*, under uncertainty the default is CALL, with a tight continue against a jam from behind; "jam if you don't know" applies only in the SB, when the BB behind is deep enough to fold. The "a wrong overcall is a giant torch" rationale is preserved. The mirror rule: the short has JUST passed the blinds (UTG/early position) → its jam is tight → open wide against it; the short is in the CO/BTN heading into the blinds → it shoves with marginals → open TIGHTER with such a short behind you.

### 22.10-EK-2 A notch WIDER call vs the micro stack's jam — everyone's premium drops when it busts; but facing a jam + CALL, the shortest BB folds even TT/JJ


Root error: calling the micro's jam at the one-hand ICM threshold, or "I'm short, I pay with anything." The mechanism has two directions. (1) ICM is a one-hand snapshot; while a 4–5bb stack is at the table, your premium against the leader is high (you have to outlast it) and you're handcuffed in every decision. Once that micro busts, your premium drops (roughly a few points *(calibrate)*) and you become the coverer against the mid stacks → widen your call threshold vs the micro's jam by a notch (one or two combos, the K9o/86s class); the range the sim gives doesn't see that gain. Besides, your premium against the micro is already ~0 (you survive even if you lose) → the "the A is live, the K is dominated" distinction — A2o call, K9o fold — comes from the sim; the widening goes on top of that. (2) The flip side: you're the shortest stack in the BB, the BTN jammed wide and the SB CALLED — now you're up against two ranges, and when you fold the rung comes your way often (one of them will bust) → FOLD even TT/JJ *(calibrate)*; had the SB folded to the same jam, you'd call 45% *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** the micro jammed and there's no call behind → pay a notch wider (sending it out unlocks the handcuffs); if you've seen a jam + call and you're the shortest → the rung is free, fold even a mid pair. Same engine as 22.10's "tighten when the short's blind approaches" line, two new faces.

### 22.10-EK-3 As the micro approaches its forced blind, the covering jammer WIDENS — the waiting incentive belongs to the caller, the widening to the jammer


Root error: reading 22.10 one-way. "Tighten if the short opponent's blind is approaching" was written only for the CALLER; the jammer's seat was left empty. Mechanism: with a micro at the table that will be forced all-in within a hand or two, the mid stacks' calls narrow not just against the micro but against EVERYONE — the upside of the double is small (it still stays short, still folds every hand), the hidden EV of folding is big (the micro busts for free, the rung gets credited). With the calls narrowed, the coverer's jam widens: same opponent, same hand, a notch wider jam than the one-hand ICM snapshot gives — top and bottom go to the jam, the middle slice stays in the raise *(calibrate)*. The scaling rule: the size of the correction scales with the probability of a FORCED all-in over the next few hands. With a 2-4bb micro it's big (the mid stack's call range shortens by roughly a quarter *(calibrate)*); with everyone 12bb+ it's around a point, i.e. nothing; at a deep table the future-game correction is near zero, the one-hand snapshot suffices.

**Rule:** with a micro at the table, two questions — (1) am I the one waiting → narrow the call (22.10); (2) is the OPPONENT the one waiting → widen the jam into him, because his call has narrowed against me. Boundary: the widening lives in the marginal slice; future game never buys a giant deviation of the "fold KK" type — the spirit of 22.10 is preserved.

### 22.11 The collision effect: the hidden EV of folding exceeds the EV of opening


Context: FT/near the money, ICM on. "The open is profitable in chipEV → I open" doesn't price the probability that the players left behind you collide WITH EACH OTHER. If you enter the pot, the collision either doesn't happen (from late position the blinds mostly fold to you) or pulls you into it; if you fold, the players behind are left to each other — clearest in BvB: if the SB is short/aggressive it jams wide into the BB, the BB pays wide, someone busts — the rung is credited to you. The EV of folding ≈ P(collision behind) × P(bust) × the value of the next rung (from the BTN: P(SB jam) × P(BB call) × P(bust)); when the product is big, the marginal open's small EV falls below it. The decision changes only for marginal hands. The effect exists from every opening position, and its size scales with the NUMBER and profile of players left behind you: highest from early position (many players who can collide behind, many paths); from the BTN one path remains — the direct SB-BB war — but with a short/aggressive SB that one path is thick; from the SB, zero (you're alone against the BB, there's no third party to collide). Three questions: if I fold, do the players behind (in late position: the SB) shove? Is there a payer (the BB)? Is the rung big relative to my stack? If all three are yes, do NOT open the borderline hands *(thresholds: calibrate)*.

**The map is defense width, not short stacks.** A BB that defends wide and stacks off with a pair/draw on the flop collides with everyone → cut the marginal open in front of it; in front of a fold-fold-fold BB, open even the borderline hand (23.1: station = high, nit = low collision). The marginal 3-bet against the chip leader obeys the same map — with 4-5 players behind who see the same opportunity, leave the collision to them.

**The escape door:** at a collision table the 3-bettor's range is squeezed too → move the continue-vs-3-bet threshold up a notch *(at the margin)*. At a mid-short stack *(≈15–25bb, calibrate)*, when the raise is small enough to remain a small slice of the stack and there are two aggressive/short players behind you, with a strong-but-not-nut hand like AK: instead of the open-jam, a RAISE that buys the fold option — when one shoves and the other re-shoves, you fold and take the rung (12.4, 22.10). In the jam/fold band (≈≤12bb) this door is closed — shove AK or fold it.

### 22.2-EK-7 The Second Half of the Arrival Role: Who Do I Become If I Lose?


The mirror of the "who do I become if I win" test also works: if I lose this pot, whose stack do I fall BELOW at the table? If you risk half your stack and lose it but you're still many multiples above the micro stack, you can play close to chipEV despite that micro — even though colliding with it looks the most profitable, your relative position isn't shaken. But if the loss drops you among or below similarly sized stacks, size down: before the roles are settled, accumulating chips has low value — protecting relative position comes first. This is why a third stack's effect at the table isn't linear.

**Rule:** When sizing a bet, ask "what happens to my ranking if I lose?" — if the answer is "nothing changes," move toward chipEV; if "I drop," size down a notch.

### 22.2-EK-8 Post-Bubble, the Population Stays in the Old Mode for Hours


In the first hour or two after the bubble bursts *(duration depends on the field and level length — calibrate)*, most of the field is still playing on high-premium reflexes: some stay overly tight to call-off even with a value hand (folding a good hand unnecessarily), others keep selling bubble pressure (overly wide opens/aggression). The two modes call for OPPOSITE deviations: against the over-folding post-bubble nit, the side you widen is the fold-equity side (steal, bluff-jam, 3-bet bluff) — CUT your thin value, because it folds and the range that pays you is stronger (23.1: nit → steal his pots; "punish with value" in 23.15/23.8-EK is tied to a field that OVER-PAYS, not one that over-folds); against someone still selling pressure, the side you widen is call/3-bet. Diagnostic trap: a small 4-bet ("click" — the OPENER's small 4-bet against a 3-bet, see 12.2-EK-11) is NOT by itself a signal of "still in bubble mode"; in 12.2-EK-11 the click's material is value QQ+/AK + offsuit Ax bluffs, i.e. by construction it's a value-weighted polarized line, and its existence condition isn't a high premium but that villain is FINDING 5-bet bluff-jams — reading the click as "mode leftover, not value" gets you overcontinuing into real QQ+/AK.

**Rule:** use the first post-bubble hands to diagnose "who's still in the old mode" — against an overly tight folder, widen bluff/steal/jam (cut thin value); against someone still selling pressure, widen call/3-bet; don't count the click as evidence of mode, ask first "is this villain finding 5-bet bluff-jams?"

### 22.10-EK-4 What the Model Can't See — a Short Stack's Proximity to the Blind at Another Table


The ICM model counts all short stacks as equal regardless of position; in reality, a short stack at the same bb count that is CLOSE to the blind (whether at your own table or, in a multi-table view, at another table) has a higher probability of being forced all-in and busting within the next few hands. If you have this information (an online multi-table screen, or the next-to-act position at your own table), turning a marginal call into a fold increases your laddering value for free — a gain the model doesn't show.

**Rule:** if you can know how close a short stack at another table is to the blind, add that information as a third party to 22.10's blind-geometry adjustment — calculate the laddering value the model can't see yourself.

### 22.11-EK The Same Premium Ratio Gives a Different Collision Frequency at a Different ABSOLUTE Depth


Even if the stack RATIOS (and therefore the risk premiums) are nearly identical at two tables, if the table's ABSOLUTE average depth differs, the probability of a collision comes out different too. If the average depth is SHALLOW (everyone low bb) players get into each other more often, busts happen more often around the short stack — the hidden EV of folding rises. But this waiting isn't free: with the ratios fixed, your own bb count is also low at a shallow table, your forced blind erosion runs fastest, and your jam's fold equity dies quickest — meaning absolute depth gives you the collision FREQUENCY, your own bb count gives you the waiting BUDGET (22.10 item 1: your own approaching blind kills the "wait" option — you widen on that hand; 22.4: under cover pressure the CALL narrows but the JAM STAYS). If the average depth is DEEPER (same ratios, higher bb) the other stacks can fold each other more comfortably, and collisions become sparser — the short stack can feel comfortable thinking "there are a few orbits left, something will happen," but no one may collide and it quietly erodes into the blinds.

**Rule:** ask the two variables separately — the table's ABSOLUTE average depth gives you the collision FREQUENCY, "how many bb am I" gives you the waiting BUDGET. At a shallow table, fold and wait for a collision only as long as your jam still carries fold equity (widen per 22.10 on the hand where your own blind approaches); at a deep table, collisions are sparse, so you may need to create the hand yourself.

## Chapter 23 — Field Reading: Opponent Types and Exploits

*★ v6. So far the book has given a balanced/GTO-near default (B7-B21). But in a live field the real money comes not from balance but from the opponent's mistake. This chapter collects the exploit notes scattered through the book in one place: read the opponent's type, read their ICM-awareness, pick the direction to deviate from default.*

### 23.0 Thesis

> **GTO makes you unbeatable; exploit makes you profitable. At the table, three questions: (1) What type is this opponent? (2) Do they feel ICM? (3) Which way do they err — too loose, too tight, too passive? The deviation comes from these three reads. No read → return to default (balanced).**

### 23.1 Five opponent types

- **Nit (over-tight):** if they open/raise it's near the nuts. Exploit: respect their bet/raise (rarely a bluff), drop the bluff-catch, but steal their pots often (they fold).
- **Station (caller):** calls with everything, doesn't fold. Exploit: NEVER bluff; break strong value with a BIG size/overbet (they're size-insensitive, they pay with everything); only shrink the size on your thinnest value.
- **LAG (loose-aggressive):** opens a lot, barrels a lot. Exploit: call/bluff-catch more (their range is weak), fatten your value, don't fold to their bluffs.
- **TAG / reg:** balanced, thinking. Little to exploit; play balanced, they feel ICM, sell them pressure.
- **Whale / rec (fun):** unpredictable, pays big with big hands. Exploit: value-heavy, few thin bluffs, show your hand and get paid.

### 23.2 Taxonomy table

| Type | Their error | Exploit direction |
|---|---|---|
| **Nit** | Over-folds | Steal + respect their aggression |
| **Station** | Over-calls | No bluffs + size value up/overbet |
| **LAG** | Over-aggressive | Bluff-catch + fatten value |
| **TAG/reg** | Few errors | Balanced + sell pressure |
| **Whale** | Uncontrolled | Value-heavy, show and get paid |

### 23.3 Does the villain feel ICM?

SELLING ICM pressure works only if the opponent FEELS ICM. A reg tightens correctly on the bubble — you can sell them pressure. But a rec/whale doesn't know ICM; they won't fold even on the bubble. Against them: don't sell pressure (they won't fold) → take value. Steal/3-bet bluffs are wasted on an ICM-blind opponent; take wide value instead.

But careful — **your own risk premium (B17/B20/B21) holds even if the opponent is ICM-blind.** The deviation is "don't sell pressure → widen your value BETS", NOT loosening your call-off/stack-off while covered. Even against a whale, there's no light stack-off while covered on the bubble. Against a reg the reverse: they feel ICM → the steal/pressure premium works.

### 23.4 Pool-read deviation (showdown-first)

The majority of the pool goes to showdown too much (curiosity + not folding). This too is a read — at the pool level; it reverts to balance as the reg share rises (see 23.6). Deviation: lower your bluff frequency (they call); raise your thin value (they pay with worse). The single most profitable deviation in a low/mid-stakes rec pool *(calibrate — by pool)*.

### 23.5 Station playbook

- **No bluffs, zero** (a bluff is giving a station money).
- **Grind thin value:** bet hands you'd normally check.
- **Size:** strong value BIG/overbet (a station is size-insensitive, it won't squeeze the call); only shrink on your thinnest value.
- **Passive-river distinction:** a station's river RAISE ≈ nuts → fold your thin value. But a river LEAD is size-dependent: to a small lead a good top pair is usually a call (stations donk merged/weak); to a big/odd lead, tighten.

### 23.6 Against a reg / TAG

Balance is the shield here: a reg punishes imbalance. Play a balanced range, sell ICM pressure to THEM (they feel it), hide the thin exploits. As the reg share rises (higher stakes) exploit shrinks and GTO-near grows.

### 23.7 Calibration

*(calibrate: each type's exact deviation size (bluff %, value threshold, value size) depends on the pool and stakes. In the app the type tag + notes field carries this deviation; at the table tag it in three tiers (very loose/balanced/very tight). The solver gives the default, you add the exploit deviation.)*

### 23.8 Cheat card

| Read | Deviation from default |
|---|---|
| **Station** | Cut the bluffs, size value up/overbet |
| **Nit** | Steal, drop the hero-call |
| **LAG** | Open the bluff-catch, fatten value |
| **ICM-blind rec** | Don't sell pressure, take value (but your call-off threshold stays high while covered) |
| **Reg** | Return to balance, sell pressure |

### 23.9 Drill (3 questions)

1. **E1.** A station called every flop/turn, then took a SMALL lead on the river, you hold a good top pair. What type, what do you do — call or fold? What if they'd raised instead?
2. **E2.** Bubble, the opponent is a clear rec/whale who doesn't care about ICM. Steal-bluff or value? Does your call-off change if you're covered?
3. **E3.** A nit opened UTG, you hold a middling hand. What's the exploit direction — call or fold, and why do you steal their pots?

*Root-error tie-in: default (balance) protects you from losses; deviation (exploit) generates profit but backfires on a wrong read. Guard: type + ICM-awareness + error direction — if all three are clear, deviate; if not, stay balanced. Bluffing a station (and betting it small), and selling a rec pressure, are the classic faces of the field-reading leak.*


---

### 23.1-EK Mixed stacks against a whale's limp: three questions — the preflop execution of "Whale: value-heavy"


The reflex of "raise/jam no matter what against a whale" builds a linear-wide iso range and leaves it exposed to a jam/3-bet from the three players still behind. Answer the limp with three questions *(thresholds: calibrate)*:

1. **Does my stack fit a jam (≤20bb)?** A whale CALLS a raise with dominated hands, doesn't jam over it → RAISE with KTs/QTs/A9s–AQ (jamming would throw away the dominated call), JAM with 75s/85s-type hands — the limp mirror of 23.14's "playable hand calls, equity-denial hand jams" principle. SB 15bb: raise the top end, jam 77–TT and suited Ax more than in a BvB spot.
2. **How many players behind can still jam?** CO 18bb: iso 3× (not 3.5×) with TT+/AJs+ for value; the iso bluff is blocker offsuit hands (KTo/QTo/JTo), not suited connectors — if someone behind can jam, fold equity wants blockers. If BTN is over-calling, the blinds jam with T8s-type hands — over-calling is dead money.
3. **Am I deep against those behind me?** BTN ~50bb vs a limp + an 18bb iso: a 45bb jam has no upside → commit with a non-all-in 3-bet across your whole range (99 included); jamming into both the whale's rare trap and the 15–20bb stacks behind only loses.

**A tight whale (~12% raise):** at 18–20bb, fold 55–22/A2s; at BTN 40bb, fold K5s–K8s, even flat AKo (you don't 3-bet a tight range); BB's defense narrows hard — "defend every suited hand" is a leak here. This is the limp node of 33.6's mixed-stack logic.

### 23.6-EK At a reg-heavy final table, lower small-sized aggression, raise the check-call/check-raise share


23.6 says "return to balance"; at a reg-heavy table, balance also gives a DIRECTION. Everyone is sticky and raises/floats small bets, and raising a lead is frequent → the hidden EV of a small c-bet and a BB lead (villain's overfold) collapses: LOWER small-sized aggression (small c-bet, small lead, probe); raise the share of check-call and check-raise — realize against villain's aggression instead of licensing him to raise your small bet. This is the reverse face of 23.11's passive-pool triple: there you take your value big and early, here you wait for villain's bet and raise it *(calibrate)*. Sizing principle: against a rec pick the size with your hand, against a reg pick it with your range (27.12).

### 23.15 Against a field that doesn't know ICM, don't fight fire with fire — tighten, fast-play, punish with VALUE, not bluffs


Root error: if opponents don't see the early/mid-phase premium and are defending, squeezing, and stacking off at chipEV width, saying "I'll widen too." Their excess looseness lowers your own equity realization (squeezes from behind, wide stack-offs) → the correct response runs the opposite way: tighten your opening a notch; nearly close down flatting (you'll get squeezed); increase 3-betting, but with VALUE; drop the trap — jam big pairs directly instead of a non-all-in 3-bet, because there's no need to induce where wide stack-offs are already coming; cut the middling bluff-jams (medium suited broadway), they'll get called. Tighten against their 3-bet too: a chipEV 3-bet range is more LINEAR than a premium-weighted one (loaded with suited connectors, few offsuit blockers) → your medium pairs and suited hands realize poorly against it — flatting drops, 4-betting rises with linear value. Mirror: if villain folds too little to your 3-bet, linearize your 3-bet bluff (playable hands, not bottom-of-range Ax) and go back to flatting — a polar bluff finds no fold. Final layer: a chipEV-playing opponent's jam is STRONGER than an ICM jam (he's flatting his medium hands instead of jamming them) → when a jam comes in after a 3-bet, fold one notch tighter.

**Rule:** if the other side isn't paying the premium, don't pay it twice — fold more, call less, and go faster with a strong hand; the gain doesn't come from bluffing, it comes from collecting their excess looseness with value.

### 23.8-EK Against a spewy player under ICM, VPIP NARROWS


B23's "see a rec → take value" is correct, but it doesn't spell out the VPIP direction; under FT ICM the reflex can run backward: against a player spewing chips, the exploit is holding a good hand MORE OFTEN, not less —

- NARROW your VPIP slightly (don't loosen up and enter variance with him).
- Build the pot with good hands (he's going to give you his chips anyway).
- Keep the bluff side linear/playable, not polarized (your bluffs won't find a fold anyway).

> **A spewy opponent doesn't mean "play more hands"; it means "play more good hands, meet his spew with value." Loosening up and entering flips with him under ICM converts his leak into your leak.**

### 23.10 Node taxonomy: three lines, two opposite pool deviations, one field condition


Postflop aggression lines carry OPPOSITE deviations by pool; a single "passive line = overfold" rule wrongly generalizes all three at once:

| Line | Pool deviation | Correct response |
|---|---|---|
| **Cut-off aggression** (bet-then-check) | Under-bluff (reg) → over-fold | Bluff it / abandon the bluff-catch |
| **Delayed aggression** (check-back → turn+river barrel) | OVER-bluff (the pool learned the "check-back = capped" lesson and attacks that range) | Lean toward the catcher |
| **Rec-heavy field, any passive line** | Rec is STICKY on the river; he gives up his fold on the TURN | Move your bluff volume from the river to the TURN |

> **The moment the "check-back = capped" lesson is learned, attacking a check-back turns into OVER-bluffing. Don't confuse cut-off aggression (fold him out) with delayed aggression (catch him) — and in a rec-heavy Main, both of them have their fold window on the TURN, not the river.**

### 23.11 The value triad against a passive pool


A passive/call-heavy (non-folding) pool inverts several of B18's defaults:

1. **A small c-bet's hidden EV lives in villain's check-raise node** — in equilibrium, some of the continues to a small bet are raises *(calibrate; ~15-20%)*; a small bet outsources growing the pot to villain's raise. If the pool check-raises rarely + only when very strong → with a value hand (TPTK+), push the big size YOURSELF instead of a small bet.
2. **Delayed value collapses → take it on the turn** — a turn check-back's EV depends on villain putting money in for you on the river; the pool loves "calling with equity" on the turn and check-folding the river → with two-pair-type hands, take the turn bet NOW, front-load the money.
3. **The station/passive-pool double adjustment (B23.5)** — against a passive/call-heavy pool: WIDEN thin value (bad hands pay it off) AND NARROW bluff frequency (they don't fold). One read, two adjustments — forgetting one is a leak.

> **A passive pool breaks the "small bet is always safe" and "check-back the turn" habits: take your value yourself, big and early — because villain won't grow the pot for you.**

### 23.12 Three field-conditioned carve-outs (B18.2 · B3.2 · BvB)


- **B18.2's low-board rule reverses under ICM:** as money gets deeper, BB strips low cards from its range → a 7-5-3-type board now belongs to the opener, and a small c-bet opens it. "Low-connected = BB's board → check" only holds in chipEV; at ICM depth it's the opener's board.
- **B3.2's "big to your left → tighten" creates over-nitting in a soft field:** at low/mid stakes, the aggression the cover-to-your-left theory predicts doesn't SHOW UP; pre-emptive nitting before the evidence arrives just burns chips. The default is normal VPIP; tightening is a REACTION to observed aggression, not a pre-emptive measure.
- **BvB's "never fold" stickiness is conditioned on the position pair:** the established "never fold a pair/backdoor" doctrine in BvB is specific to the wide-range node; against a tight EP range, folding a weak third pair to a small bet on a medium board is disciplined. Without writing the two rules side by side, BvB stickiness produces over-calling against EP.

### 23.13 The pool's 3-bet gap: WIDEN late-position opens, SHUT DOWN the 4-bet-jam bluff


The blinds 3-bet late-position opens noticeably less than equilibrium *(rate: calibrate; jams close the gap in the shallow band, it opens up deep)* — what's missing is always the mixed combos, the "low frequency = zero" shortcut. Two mistakes assume villain also plays the solver's 3-bet frequency: opening a solver range from late position, and firing the solver's 4-bet-jam bluffs once you get 3-bet.

Three consequences: (1) Widen your BTN/CO open above the solver — the blinds aren't punishing it, and on top of that they overfold flop/turn, so marginal hands mostly collect EV postflop (as long as the pool deviation is large enough) (the mechanism behind B17.11's "BTN 40% open" and B4.1's live correction). (2) When you get 3-bet, the range is nut-weighted → a 4-bet-jam bluff's (an A5s type) fold equity collapses, and jamming a blocker-holding suited Ax loses heavily against equilibrium *(calibrate)*; calling a 3-bet with weak suited Kx/Qx is also bad. **Open wide, continue NARROW against a 3-bet** — that's where B4.5's "4-bet bluff very rarely" line comes from. (3) When YOU 3-bet, don't take the same shortcut — pin down your mixed combos per 27.12, don't zero them out.

Deviation note: this is a pool average; if you have a read on an aggressive 3-bettor, return to baseline. The gap narrows as stakes rise. 22.11's collision filter stacks on top of this widening when ICM is on.

### 23.14 BB's two band errors: too much JAM in the short band, too PASSIVE in the medium band


Root error: the "good hand = jam" habit from BB against a late-position open. There's a single principle: **a playable hand CALLS, a hand that wants to fold out equity JAMS.** In the short band *(roughly 15–25bb; band boundary: calibrate)*, jamming folds out only the hands you dominate and gets paid only by hands that beat you — strong suited broadway hands like ATs/AJs, KQs/KJs (AQs/AKs excepted — they mostly go value jam/3-bet since they get paid by worse) mostly prefer CALL instead: they keep the hands they dominate in the pot, continue to a small flop bet, and catch villain's bluff on a high-card runout → this realizes more equity than jamming does. Weak suited Ax and small pairs, on the other hand, mostly JAM: blocker + equity denial, live if called *(threshold: calibrate)*. At the low end of the band (≤~17bb, OOP low SPR), even ATs/AJs/KQs-type hands mostly drift toward jam too; the preference for call becomes clear from ~20bb on *(calibrate)*. In the medium band *(roughly 25–30bb)*, jamming risks the whole stack for a pot worth a few bb — the correct aggression is a small, non-all-in 3-bet; here a medium suited connector is a CALL hand, not an aggression hand (the class that best realizes its equity), and the material for a 3-bet is blocker-bearing hands like low suited Kx / offsuit broadway *(calibrate)*.

**Pool read (hypothesis — verify from showdowns, calibrate):** the pool's BB jams the high-card hands it should prefer to call in the short band (over-jams), and fails to find the non-all-in 3-bet in the medium band (over-passive). When you're in BB, widen your call range in the short band and your non-all-in 3-bet range in the medium band. There's no fixed rule against villain's BB jam: calibrate from the showdowns you've seen.

**Scope (B17.11):** "open up to 60% against a 15–25bb BB while you cover them" sits in tension with this read — if the pool's BB finds jam more often than call in the short band, a wide open gets jammed more often than expected; BB's fold frequency (your fold equity) doesn't change, what changes is the opener's shrinking chance of playing the flop in position and realizing equity, plus a rising raise-fold cost → price your marginal hands against raise-fold: narrow the hands you'll fold to a re-jam, widen with hands that can pay a re-jam off; shifting the width toward the medium (passive) band is safer, calibrate the short band from showdowns.

### 23.14-EK The engine behind "playable hand CALLS, equity-denial hand JAMS": the jam layer shifts one notch with the opener's position; the mass you dominate picks call vs. jam


Answering every open the same way from a 20bb BB with a single fixed jam list is the root error. A jam's EV comes from "the better hand I fold out" plus "the worse hand I get called by." If the opener is in an EARLY position, his range is tight and strong → K-Qo / A-To fold to a jam → high suited hands like suited T-x / J-x gain value by jamming; if the opener is in a LATE position, those same hands no longer fold → the jam layer drops one notch (suited 9-x, then 8-x), and the upper layer shifts to call *(notch: calibrate)*. The same engine resolves the "A-Js calls / A-7s jams" paradox: when A-Js jams, A-Q never folds and A-T never calls; when it calls, it dominates K-J / Q-J / weak A-x and continues to a small flop bet → calling wins more. A-7s dominates no mass; jamming folds out A-8/A-9/A-T → jam. The question isn't "is this a good hand?": it's **"does the mass I dominate pay off on a call, or on a jam?"** The pool is reluctant to jam suited broadway → a late open's risk of running into this jam is lower than theory, so the open widens one notch *(calibrate)*. 5.3's call table doesn't change; this entry adds a position notch to BB's jam list.

### 23.1-EK-2 Pair ladder against the whale — the value of aggression comes from dominated calls


The response of pairs to the passive whale's open is not one action but a ladder: mid-to-top pair (the 99 class) — a committing non-all-in 3-bet (jam only when the stack is ≤20bb and there's no room left for a raise-sized bet) — draws its value not from protection but from the whale's DOMINATED calls (they pay with 88/77; jamming throws away exactly those calls, 23.1-EK); low pair (22-66) — call, since making them fold from a flip earns little during the chip-accumulation phase, the real profit is postflop as that hand tells you its story; the AJs class — 3-bet-call, which dominates their continuing range. A finer point: against a rec who plays "I saw an ace → jam or fold," a mid-pair 3-bet also hands you command of A-high boards — they fast-play the boards without an ace, and on a board with an ace their hand is telegraphed.

**Rule:** If the whale pays the calls your aggression dominates, bet it — but keep the sizing NON-all-in, since a jam throws away exactly those dominated calls; if they don't pay, call with the low pair and listen postflop.

### 23.1-EK-3 Opens wide and stabs the flop too, but dissolves under pressure — the check-raise bluff exception to LAG


B23.1's LAG rule ("don't fold to their bluff, open up the bluff-catch") does not apply to every wide/aggressive-looking player. There's a subtype: opens wide preflop, stabs the flop often too — but dissolves when it meets YOUR counter-aggression (check-raise, 3-bet), and can't find the call. The tell: a wide open + a high c-bet frequency + low continuation against counter-aggression. Against this type the default "open up the bluff-catch against a LAG, don't bluff" rule reverses — a check-raise BLUFF with a marginal/medium-strength hand targets exactly their wide-but-foldable range. Don't label off one hand; watch their response to counter-aggression and decide.

**Rule:** Don't treat LAG as one type — "opens wide + stabs often" and "holds up under pressure" are separate axes; if they're weak on the second axis, add the check-raise bluff — don't apply the standard LAG rule here.

### 23.3-EK The half-ICM profile — chipEV preflop, locked-up postflop


23.3 asks "do they feel ICM?" as a binary; on the felt, the most profitable type is a third option: the player who feels the pressure HALF. Profile: as the money approaches, at 15-20bb they still limp-enter with a min-raise, see the flop, then lock up — defending the BB and check-folding, or opening and folding to a c-bet. The exploit is two-pronged: no respect for their preflop entry (their range is chipEV-wide), postflop pressure at full throttle — the money comes from their flop-fold; a 3-bet against their open also works, because the lock kicks in on raise-fold too. Its symmetry: if the short stack has correctly tightened (if their bubble open drops to the 10% range *(calibrate)*) do the reverse — their open is a situationally-tight nit range, apply B23.1's nit line: cut the 3-bet/light call, show respect.

**Rule:** Read ICM-feel street by street, not with one question — if you don't see the lock preflop but find it on the flop, move the pressure there; against a lock you do see preflop, move the respect for their range there too.

### 23.3-EK-2 Read the opponent's ICM feel before you sit down — the buy-in profile


The answer to "does villain feel ICM?" is usually ready before you ever see a hand. Results history: someone sitting down in a big tournament with a small-buy-in-heavy record locks up around a min-cash; someone whose record is always min-cash plays for cash — squeezes the bubble; a type with no min-cash at all, who always runs deep, doesn't care about the bubble. A high-roller chasing points/leaderboard plays chipEV at a low buy-in — you can't sell them ICM pressure. At the table: a player who stalls on the bubble or gets up and waits out the bubble away from the table labels themselves ICM-sensitive — note it, a pressure target for later phases. Verbal statements read in reverse: someone who SAYS "I don't care about the bubble" is usually the opposite; a vibe that leaks on its own is usually true.

**Rule:** Attach the ICM-feel label without waiting for a hand: results profile + bubble behavior + (read in reverse) verbal statements.

### 23.3-EK-3 Against an ICM-aware population that over-folds to c-bets — sometimes take the value with a CHECK


B23.3's rule "don't sell pressure to an ICM-blind opponent, take value" is for an opponent who doesn't FEEL ICM at all. There's a separate population: they do feel ICM (which is exactly why they already over-fold) but for that very reason they also give up too easily against a c-bet — in this case a straight value bet often just gets an instant fold and earns nothing. Against a felt like this, sometimes play your premium hand by PULLING BACK (check-back): the opponent may read the check as weakness/lack of initiative and put their own bluff/thin value on the table on the next street — a check invites an action a bet couldn't have gotten.

**Rule:** The rule "ICM-blind opponent → take value" is specific to one who doesn't feel ICM at all; against a population that feels ICM but over-folds to c-bets, sometimes wait with a check and let the opponent put in the bet for you.

### 23.4-EK Against a pool with weak bluff material — from EQUITY, not the blocker


23.4 lowered the bluff frequency; the SELECTION of the remaining bluffs also changes. A blocker bluff (a low-connectivity hand that minimizes card overlap with the opponent's folding range) draws its value from the opponent's disciplined fold; a showdown-first pool won't give you that fold → on the same line, pass the candidate with high equity instead: hands carrying real outs (a small pair, a live gutshot) are usually better than "clean" but outless combos, because your path to winning isn't making them fold, it's making your hand. Blocker selection comes back against a reg — fold discipline moves back to the front against an opponent you SEE it in *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** In a felt that doesn't fold, pick the bluff by "how many outs do I have if called," not by "who am I blocking" — the blocker bluff is a luxury of a fold-disciplined opponent.

### 23.4-EK-2 Rewind the hand at showdown — extract the sizing repertoire, write the outlier in bold


Showdown isn't one card's worth of information — it's the answer key for ALL streets of the hand: the moment the cards flip, rewind the hand from the start and match every sizing to the board type — are they using the same one-third-pot size on every texture (a one-size repertoire = they're sizing their memorized play, not their hand), or did it grow when the texture called for a big size (they're sizing their hand: the size-tell is live)? A small 3-bet's two opposite reads also split apart from here: an old-school/rec player keeps a premium hand small to keep it in, a thinking aggressor uses a small 3-bet to cheaply isolate a weak player to their right — which one it is is told by context, not by the size (from whom, to whom, is there an obvious target at the table). And the most valuable line: an observation that MATCHES the population tell is confirmation, an OUTLIER observation changes the category — if the guy you took for a quick-caller shows up with a war hero-call, the moment you see their first BB 3-bet bluff — move them to the "trickster/warrior" shelf on that single observation.

**Rule:** Rewind the hand at every showdown and match the sizings to the board; a single observation that breaks the population tell is worth more than ten observations that match it.

### 23.5-EK Inelasticity can also be a BOARD property, not the player's — "how much money would I want in the pot RIGHT NOW?" picks the size


23.5 says "station is size-insensitive → go big"; the same insensitivity is also born in a balanced opponent on certain boards. On a dry K-high board, the opponent's continuing range is composed of pairs and continues with roughly the same list against a small bet as against a big one *(calibrate)* → take the value BIG: get the money in now, while they can't fold — later the board changes or they stop, and the window closes. As the board grows more elastic (as middle cards/draws enter the mix and the continuing range becomes size-sensitive) go back to a small size. The table question: "for my hand's class, how much money would I magically want in the pot RIGHT NOW?" — if the answer is more than a small bet and the opponent is inelastic on that board, fire the big one.

**Rule:** The size is chosen by the opponent's fold curve, not by your hand's strength: if the continuing range is independent of size go big, if it melts with size go small — and this is sometimes a property of the board, not the player.

### 23.6-EK-2 A one-time "advanced" move isn't a single sample — it's the sign of a crossed threshold


When you see an opponent make an advanced/aggressive move of a kind you didn't expect (e.g. a rarely-seen bluff 3-bet from a short-to-medium stack), don't wave it off as "a single sample, doesn't say much." Moves like this usually aren't random — they're a sign of a crossed THRESHOLD — the player knows this move and will look for it again; permanently pull all your subsequent reads of them upward based on this information. The reverse also holds: if an opponent shows a hard fold or a disciplined line somewhere you didn't expect, permanently raise the likelihood that they're "studied" too.

**Rule:** A single advanced/disciplined move is not a random data point — it's proof the opponent has "seen" that door; update your read permanently from that moment on, don't ignore it because the sample is small.

### 23.7-EK Your first reaction is a calibration tool — "ridiculous" is working against you, "great" is your own leak


When you see an opponent's line, your first reaction generates data in both directions. If you're thinking "what a ridiculous bet" — stop: that line is most likely working exactly against people who think the way you do — the reflex of dismissal is the smell of an unstudied knot; ask about that knot on the break. Conversely, if you instantly fall in love with a creative line ("yes, everyone folds this here!") you're most likely overusing that line — and what you're not noticing is that this enthusiasm leaks out at the table as tempo and demeanor: the aggression of a player who keeps seeing the spot is, by itself, a tell for an attentive opponent. Neither reaction gets written into a decision — both get written into the study list.

**Rule:** If the opponent's line looks ridiculous to you, it's probably profitable against you; if your own line looks instantly perfect to you, you're probably overusing it — note both, and don't lean a table decision on either.

### 23.10-EK Range-bet learned, check-raise defense unlearned — open up the flop x/r bluff, brake on the turn


The typical profile of a mid/low felt has memorized the small range-c-bet (sometimes even on a board that isn't a range-bet spot) but has never studied that bet's continuation leg against a check-raise — they continue much narrower than they should against an x/r *(calibrate)*. The exploit has two parts and is deliberately UNBALANCED: widen the x/r bluff with draw/backdoor hands, and brake on the turn if a good card doesn't come (you got their fold on the flop, don't press further); keep the value x/r NARROW — this profile won't continue against your thin x/r with worse anyway, so x/r'ing a medium top pair is value-betting yourself. The turn leg of the same read: with x/r threat low on this felt, the worry "my hand is fragile, if I bet I'll get ripped off" also loosens — protection/draw bets can be fired more freely than their theoretical share *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** Against the automatic small bet — a wide x/r bluff plus a brake on the turn, a narrow x/r value — take your fold from the unlearned defense, don't wait for the payoff.

### 23.12-EK The vacated preflop slice determines board ownership — at a short stack, jams flip high boards too


23.12 gave you ICM erasing low cards; at short depth, JAMS do the same job, and the direction changes by board class. At ~20bb (the short band, 23.14), because the BB jams their small pairs and WEAK suited Ax preflop against a late open, and reserves AQs/AKs for a value jam/3-bet, their defense is stripped clean of these slices → an A-high board is overwhelmingly the OPENER'S: bet often and big; the BB's check-raise is almost shut off (their top of range left preflop), and their defense narrows down to holding their best remaining Ax (ATs/AJs — the class that stands on a call per 23.14) as a check-call *(calibrate)*. At ~30bb the same job is done not by a jam but by the vacated non-all-in 3-bet slice (the correct aggression in that band is a small 3-bet, not a jam) — same direction, thinner slice. The SB flat does the opposite: pairs go to a jam, good Ax to a 3-bet/jam — what's left in the flat concentrates on offsuit broadway/medium suited → broadway boards (the KQ8/AQJ type), despite appearances, belong to the FLATTER, while a board with three low cards belongs to the opener; against an EP open the same SB flat stays pair-heavy, so the table flips again (low boards belong to the flatter, high boards to the opener). The question at the table isn't the position label — it's "which slice did this seat vacate preflop at this depth?"

**Rule:** It's the vacated preflop slice, not the label, that tells you who owns the board — the board matching the vacated (jammed or 3-bet) hand class turns against whoever vacated that slice.

### 23.13-EK The squeeze double gap — the pool both under-squeezes and under-resists the squeeze


B23.13's 3-bet gap has a double version in the squeeze: the pool finds a squeeze noticeably less often than balance when they see an open+call, AND their defense when squeezed (especially a mid-depth rejam, with the suited broadway/pair class) is noticeably lacking versus balance *(ratios: calibrate)*. Both gaps work in your favor at once: play your squeezes and squeeze-jams at full frequency — missing a comfy suited broadway into a flat because "I'll play it postflop" is leaving this income on the table; and when you get squeezed, keep your rejam NARROW and value-heavy: the squeeze a squeeze-averse pool actually fires is effectively nut-heavy, and a rejam bluff has no fold equity (B23.13's "range is nut-heavy when 3-bet → narrow continuation" mechanism) — the discipline to protect isn't frequency, it's not missing your value rejam. The third consequence is indirect: if the BB's squeeze is lacking, your flat FROM the SB (against a BTN/CO open) widens — with no squeeze-happy BB behind you to punish it, you over-realize; the moment you see them squeeze, reel that widening back in.

**Rule:** When you see an open+call, let the default question be "is there a squeeze here?" not "is a flat comfy?" — in the pool's two-sided squeeze gap, every squeeze you pass on is money left behind; when you're the one squeezed, your rejam is narrow and value, not bluff.

### 23.16 The penalty for an over-c-bettor is stickiness, not a raise — the float line buys the river


A large slice of the pool range-bets nearly every board small in position, on the logic "I'm the opener/3-bettor"; this weakens their c-bet range below balance, but the penalty for it isn't inflating a check-raise — the same player also can't find the low-equity barrel on the turn. The correct line is FLOAT: continue sticky even off balance against the small bet (backdoor high cards, pair + backdoor), the turn gets checked often, and you pick up the river bluff and the thin value; on a dry board, deliberately put a few marginal hands into the check-call line, or you'll never have a river bluff in that line. Check-raise backfires in two ways: you run into their strong hands and fold their weak ones early — float, by contrast, hands them the job of announcing their hand to you first *(mostly)*. Boundary: the target is the ABC range-bettor; against a rec who won't fold on the river, cut the bluff arm and keep the thin-value arm (23.10); at a reg-heavy table the read shuts off, return to 23.6-EK.

**Rule:** The money against an opponent who range-bets and then gives up on the turn is in the float, not the check-raise — stick on the flop, collect the profit after their turn/river check.

### 23.17 Don't fix the opponent's mistake for them — aggression against someone keeping the pot small gives back the mistake's EV


Root reflex: "they bet tiny on a board where they're crushing → they're weak, crush them with a check-raise." But their mistake may not be weakness — it may be NOT growing the pot: on a board where their range is crushing, balance calls for a big bet; if they're firing tiny, they're letting you in cheap — if you check-raise, you grow the pot FOR their value, fixing their mistake for free. The correct answer is a wide but PASSIVE defense: take the price, keep your good hands in the check-call line too so you can withstand turn pressure; save the aggression for an opponent whose mistake is "overfolding" or "overbluffing." The compass for the deviation: whichever line COLLECTS the money the opponent's mistake left on the table — not the one that neutralizes the mistake.

**Rule:** Name the mistake before you deviate: if the opponent is keeping the pot small, stay passive and see it cheap; if they're overfolding or overattacking, open up the aggression.

### 23.18 Stickiness in a 3-bet pot depends on the board's intersection with the opponent's bluff texture


The rote rule "always continue with a pair against a small bet in a 3-bet pot" is board-blind. Write down the opponent's preflop bluff material — blind 3-bet bluffs are mostly Ax and high broadways *(calibrate)* — and on a board where their bluffs PAIR UP, tighten your defense hard: on an A-high AND a K-high board their "bluff" is now top pair (on a K-high board it also overlaps with their KK/AK value), and your medium pairs and weak backdoors lose value. On a board their bluffs genuinely miss (medium/low disconnected: 9-7-2, 8-6-3 type), those same pairs and backdoors continue sticky, and the float also widens — the air slice of their betting range has grown. The question is the same on every street: "did this card grow their value, pair their bluff, or multiply their air?"

**Rule:** Tie your defense threshold not to the board but to this question: did the opponent's preflop bluffs pair on this board — if they connected, tighten; if they missed, stick.

### 23.19 Half frequency → roughly half continue — a shortcut for scaling the exploit


If an opponent plays a given aggression node (check-raise, 3-bet, etc.) at roughly HALF the balanced frequency (the value part stays fixed while only the bluff/marginal part is missing), your continue (call) range against that node should drop to roughly HALF; if the opponent plays at roughly DOUBLE the balanced frequency, your continue range should roughly DOUBLE. Don't tie the multiplier to the fold rate: at nodes like 3-bet the balanced fold is already around 55-65%, so "double the fold" is undefined — the quantity that scales and is always executable is the continue/call rate (when the bluff slice is halved, the number of bluff-catchers that continue halves, while the fold rate runs into its ceiling). This ties the at-the-table uncertainty of "should I fold a bit more/less often" to a concrete multiplier when you can't compute exact percentages — the exact coefficient varies by population, but the direction and rough scale are a solid starting point.

**Rule:** Estimate the opponent's frequency at a node roughly relative to balance (half, or double); adjust your CONTINUE (call) rate in the same direction and by roughly the same scale — not the fold rate, which runs into a ceiling.

### 23.20 A fast, unthinking fold is a signal of systemic overfold at that node


If an opponent folds quickly without hesitation at a given node (e.g., BB defense against a min-raise), this isn't just a one-time decision for that hand — it's evidence that they play that node tight in general. When the same node comes up again (same opponent, similar spot), being more aggressive than what the solver says (opening smaller, iso-ing wider, c-betting more often) is usually profitable — a hesitant/long-thought fold does NOT carry the same signal, it's a decision for that hand alone.

**Rule:** Watch the opponent's decision SPEED and hesitation — a fast, unthinking fold is a node-leak, apply it generally; a slow/hesitant fold is specific to that hand alone, don't generalize.

### 23.21 Profile before you sit down — read the results list as a pattern, not a total


Before the opponent has even played their first hand, their result history (tournament CV, online client profile) gives you an aggression map — as long as you read the pattern, not the total. One giant score with nothing small around it = most likely a rec, that score is a lottery ticket, not a sample. Many min-cashes, no deep runs = a cash-focused profile that will always fall on the fold side at the margin — the customer most exposed to bubble pressure. Firsts in small fields = chip-accumulating aggression. A modest total spread over many years = mediocrity explained by volume. A high online-volume signal means "whatever their live appearance, they have solid fundamentals." These labels are hypotheses only until the first showdown — keep them ready to update.

**Rule:** In a result list, read the finishing pattern, not the total money: a pile of min-cashes is the passive's signature, small-field firsts are the aggressor's — all of it is a first hypothesis to be revised by showdown.

### 23.22 Dominant-aggressor dynamics — a crusher who reads you as passive won't trap you


Every matchup has an "aggressor hierarchy," and if the strong player reads you as passive, their strategy tilts accordingly: they don't expect you to put the money in, so against you they almost never check/slowplay a strong hand — their check is genuinely capped *(mostly)*, and against a raise on their thin value bet you can open up bet-call with discipline, reasoning "they wouldn't check something better than me." For the same reason their SB limp comes from the bottom of their range (their limp-reraise slice is thin; they're not pricing in that you'll iso wide) — attack their limp with a wider-than-normal iso, both with trash and with light value. The mirror: if you're the table's "crusher," the same law applies back to you — a passive opponent won't grow the pot in your place; take the thin value yourself, big and early, and throw out your bluff-induction plans.

**Rule:** If the opponent reads you as passive, their check is capped, their limp is from the bottom, and their value always comes from their own hand — open up thin-value bet-call and wide iso against the limp; if the roles are reversed, apply the same read to yourself.

### 23.23 The live tempo set — the tank pulse, the rehearsed line, the honesty window after a big pot


Three live reads, all three conditional on committing to a repeating pattern, not a single data point: (1) On a big river decision, deliberately extend the tank and watch the pulse/breathing — a value-holding opponent's tension fades within roughly half a minute *(calibrate)* (the excitement peak passes); a bluffer's tension lasts until the tank ends, and often grows, because the decision is still open. (2) A line/jab delivered eagerly, with a rehearsed feel, exactly at the decision moment mostly comes from the bluff side — a value-holding mind is busy planning the next street, not the show. (3) The moment a big pot ends, people are at their most honest: the post-mortem analysis of the hand that just played out ("it was AA or nothing") gives away the opponent's decision framework — against someone who thinks in two boxes, medium-value and in-between-bluff classes are invisible. Guardrail: don't try to apply these reads to someone more experienced than you — at that level your chance of getting caught is higher than your chance of reading correctly.

**Rule:** Tension that fades during the tank favors value, tension that persists/grows favors bluff *(commit to the pattern)*; a rehearsed line at the decision moment leans bluff, post-big-pot talk leans honest — and tell-reading doesn't work upward in the hierarchy.

### 23.24 Process the sizing tell with a hypothesis loop — a weird medium size is rarely the nuts


Live, a sizing-tell is processed with a hypothesis, not a single data point. Most players split their sizing without realizing it, but in two opposite directions: some grow with their value, some grow while thinking "fold your overcard" and play small with their real hand — only a SECOND example tells you the direction: if the same player has shown the nuts with a small size, the read locks in the REVERSE direction (for them small = strength, big = protection/bluff); if they've shown the nuts with a big size, the first direction is confirmed — either way, the split-sizing read starts printing money from that point on. The only thing that sends the read to autopilot-off is mixed, inconsistent examples: nuts and air at the same size. A reliable pool pattern on the river: a nutted hand either BLOCK-bets or FIRES BIG — a weird, medium-sized raise is the size at which the nuts is RAREST *(calibrate; a station's river raise is the exception, 23.5 stands)*.

**Rule:** A sizing read earns its DIRECTION on the second example (nuts with a small size = reverse direction — don't scrap the sizes), and only mixed examples send it to the scrap heap; until the direction is clear it's a hypothesis — and fear the nuts less from a weird medium river size, and more from a block bet or an overbet.
