### 23.25 The bubble-bully profile — the auto-opening leader folds to resistance


A sixth type: the bubble leader who opens every hand. Their opening isn't the mistake — their assumption is: "everyone's locked up, I'll bail if resistance comes" → they overfold to a 3-bet, and in practice they almost never find the theory's generous share of 4-bet bluffs *(calibrate)*. Once the evidence piles up (opening over and over + folding to the first resistance), the counter is a READ-gated exception to the book's default of "no bluff 3-bet while covering": a small, NON-all-in 3-bet — it risks a small slice of your stack, and collects without a showdown. Write the plan in advance: if a 4-bet comes, the default is to fold (their 4-bet is value-heavy); a 3-bet→5-bet only with evidence that they also fire the 4-bet on reflex.

**Rule:** The weapon against a bubble bully isn't a call, it's a pre-planned small 3-bet — don't fight their 4-bet without evidence.

### 23.26 A bullied rec explodes preflop — the social climate is a parameter of the exploit


An emotional rec who feels bullied has their explosion PREFLOP, not postflop: a postflop bluff takes nerve, a preflop jam is just a dice roll — and its material is predictable: pairs (they'll even jam 44), not offsuit broadways. In a stretch where you've been bullying them, size up your 3-bet by one notch with a TT-class hand: you're inviting the explosion that collects pocket pairs. They make their call decision not from their own equity but from "how much do I have left if I lose" — they pay if roughly half their stack remains *(threshold: calibrate)*. The social climate opens and closes the window: an opponent who's in pleasant conversation with you won't jam 44 ("I don't want to bust you") — if you want the explosion, don't make peace; if you want their marginals to stay calm, chat with them.

**Rule:** An emotional rec's bluff is the preflop jam, and its material is a pair — if you want the explosion, size up your 3-bet with your value; if you don't, soften the table.

### 23.27 Image is priced with a delay — the nit's curse and the harvest window


Your own image is also a read, and it's priced with a DELAY. A nit image (or genuinely overfolded past streets): your bluffs overperform for a while — turn your K-highs into pure bluffs on the river, and bluff even from the top layer that normally wouldn't bluff; the flip side is a curse: if your check-raise is only value + obvious draws, the moment the draw arrives you won't get paid and you'll take big folds — the antidote is putting the hard-to-find bluffs (backdoor "three-card" hands) into the repertoire IN ADVANCE, or your value will die. An aggressive/bully image works in reverse: when you shift down a gear, the opponent keeps reading your old image — close down your bluff mixes for a few orbits, your value gets paid extra. If you're undecided at a mixing node, let your last few orbits' image decide: if you looked aggressive, lean call/value; if you looked passive, lean bluff/raise.

**Rule:** The opponent plays your LAST image, not your current strategy — shift your bluff and catch frequencies to account for the image delay.

### 23.28 Labels belong to a phase — relabel everyone once the bubble bursts


A nit on the bubble might not be a real nit — it was a situational nit. The instant the bubble bursts, reset your labels and watch for transition signals: has their posture loosened, has how they look at their cards and their timing changed? The real prey are the ones "left behind": a player who still plays the bubble line once the premium has dropped (a small ICM 3-bet at a chipEV node, excessive respect for opens, a still-locked-up calling book) has missed the transition — go after them until the gap closes. The meta-edge is this: the premium regime changes many times over the course of a tournament; the money belongs to whoever switches regimes faster than everyone else.

**Rule:** At every phase change, update two lists — who was a situational nit, and who's still playing the old phase; the second list is your primary target.

### 23.29 The price of a marginal preflop continue is hidden in the postflop scenario


The chart's marginal defend assumes YOU will play the solver's postflop tree; two corrections are needed. (1) Your own capacity: if you're never going to fire the river with air, your marginal defends turn negative — either genuinely put the bluffs into your repertoire, or play tighter than the chart. (2) The opponent's barreling profile: against a type who keeps firing big, a marginal hand is resolved preflop with jam/fold (don't hand them the postflop tree); against a type who telegraphs their hand, bets small, single-barrels, the same hand turns into a call — the cheap street and clear information carry the realization. The test sentence has to be kept narrow — knowing you'll fold on a brick turn is not by itself disqualifying (in most profitable marginal defends, folding on a brick is already the CORRECT line; in B11.1's test "can I state my river plan NOW?", "call this size / fold that size" is a legitimate answer). The correct sentence is: "I can only continue with this hand if I hit the nuts — I'll fold on a brick AND on turns that HELP me; I have no bluff or bluff-catch plan on any runout" → that hand was a preflop fold.

**Rule:** The postflop movie prices a marginal continue, not the hand — your own bluff capacity plus the opponent's barreling profile; if the movie is bad, end the pot preflop or never enter it.

### 23.1-EK-4 Against a low raise-volume opponent, a river raise reads as an overstated claim


Opponent type (Nit/Station/LAG/TAG/Whale) is a horizontal axis; experience background is a separate, vertical axis that combines with any type. An opponent who's mostly played live, with low HU/online river-raise volume, reads a river check-raise not as a normal tactical move but as an overstated claim — this leaves money on the table in two directions: (1) they fold more than normal against your river raise; this inflates your BLUFF raise, not your thin-value raise — a folding range doesn't pay off thin value, so raise your bluff/leverage-raise frequency and take thin value with a value-bet/call instead of a raise; (2) their own river raise, for the same reason, is also almost always value — show heavy respect for a rare river raise from them, and keep your estimate of their bluff probability below standard. Tell: a low raise count, comments that betray HU/final-table inexperience *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** Read the experience axis separately from the type — a low river-raise-volume opponent over-folds to your raise (this inflates your bluff raise, not your thin value — take thin value with a bet/call), and their own rare raise is almost always value; price both at once.

### 23.1-EK-5 Pick the bluff candidate by what it's currently earning against the opponent's range


When picking bluff material, the standard question is "will this hand fold them out?"; the missing second question is: when this hand gets called, how much does it actually win against the range in front of me? Against a tight/nit opponent, if a medium-strength showdown hand is already winning very little against their calling range, the cost of holding that hand back as a bluff-catcher is low — turning it into a bluff is nearly free, because the real equity it's holding is already small. The same class of hand against a loose/sticky opponent, if it still carries real showdown value (their calling range is wide and weak), is expensive to turn into a bluff — you'd be giving up the money you'd win at showdown.

**Rule:** Pick the bluff candidate by "how much does it win at showdown against this opponent," not by "good hand / bad hand" — even a nominally-good hand can be a cheap bluff against a tight/nit opponent, because it's already winning little against their calling range.

### 23.10-EK-2 The uncut three streets — the pool's least-defended node


23.10's taxonomy separates cut-off aggression (bet-then-check, under-bluff→over-fold in a reg) from delayed aggression (check-back then two barrels, over-bluff); there's a third, separate node: three streets uncut from start to finish (bet flop-turn-river straight through). A reg-heavy pool almost always assumes value here and over-folds heavily — the mechanism: the pool projects its own flop strategy (no range-bet, bet only with strength) onto hero, assumes hero's flop bet is narrow/value, and so over-folds on the third barrel too *(calibrate)*. If you've built a range that can fire three uncut barrels (if you started correctly wide on the flop and turn), this is the least-defended node in a reg-heavy pool. 23.10's third row already gives the exception: in a rec-heavy field the fold window is on the TURN, not the river — firing this node at full frequency there is exactly the line the table forbids (stacking your bluffs onto the river); in a rec-heavy Main, shift the volume to the flop-turn and cut the third barrel heavily.

**Rule:** Three uncut streets (bet-bet-bet) is the least-defended node in a reg-heavy pool — a separate bucket from cut-off/delayed aggression, bluff it at full frequency. In a rec-heavy Main this node's fold window is the TURN: stack your volume on the flop-turn, cut the third barrel heavily on the river.

### 23.12-EK-2 Board ownership's second driver — respect, not depth


23.12-EK tied board ownership to the vacated preflop slice; the driver isn't depth/ICM alone — respect can vacate the same slice too, but the vacating has to work through the same mechanism: 23.12-EK's jam/3-bet examples all work through the hand LEAVING the range COMPLETELY (fold or all-in), not through merely avoiding a 3-bet and dropping into the call range — because a hand that isn't 3-bet, if it drops into the flat range, doesn't weaken that range, it ENRICHES it. This is the correct reflex to read in an opponent who sees themselves as weaker than you: to avoid entering a big pot OOP against you, they avoid not just 3-betting their pairs/medium suited hands preflop but also continuing against your open at all — a flat also opens the door to that big pot, so respect mostly pushes these hands to FOLD, not to call. Result: a low-connected board (the 7-5-3 type) you'd normally count as "the caller's property" is in fact YOURS if this slice has also been completely stripped from the opponent's remaining range — it's double-fed, both by the vacated slice and by the opponent's postflop reluctance to enter a big pot against you (23.22's "check = capped" mechanism). Its signal: the opponent 3-bets you narrower than normal AND calls you narrower than normal AND rarely continues aggressively on low boards — attack when all three show up together. This driver is SEPARATE from B18.2's ICM-depth driver (it can work even at chipEV) but doesn't replace it: B18.2 still gives the default (in chipEV, low-connected = the caller's board; at ICM depth, the opener's); once you see the respect signal, it cancels that default for that specific opponent — it doesn't change the general rule.

**Rule:** Don't tie board ownership only to depth/ICM gaps — an opponent who sees themselves as weaker than you can vacate this slice out of RESPECT by FOLDING it (not just by avoiding a 3-bet); once you see the triple of a narrow 3-bet + a narrow call + rare aggression on low boards, count the low board as yours even at chipEV — this doesn't replace B18.2's ICM-depth exception, it's an additional driver on top of it.

### 23.23-EK Manage your own tempo live too — entering the pot before your opponent says "I never considered raising"


17.11-EK-3 gave you closing your own chain (snap-check/snap-bet) online — the rule wants ONE tempo for ALL actions, not just for the call; live, the same logic applies at the same width. Reaching for chips at the same instant as, or before, your opponent finishes their bet — entering the pot ahead of your opponent — is a confession of "I never considered raising this hand," even when the decision is 100% clear; but fixing your tempo only on the call and leaving the fold and the raise fast doesn't close the leak, it just relocates it — this time a new timing tell is born: "pause = call, snap = fold/raise." This both leaks your current information and isn't a one-time thing: the same behavior (a fast verbal call, a fast chip motion — or a fast fold, a fast raise), once it repeats, turns into a permanent label and gets processed by the opponent through 23.6-EK-2's permanent-update logic.

**Rule:** No matter how clear the decision, take the same fixed pause before every action (call, raise, AND fold included) — entering the pot before/simultaneously with your opponent says "I never considered raising" and leaves a permanent read; pausing only on the call doesn't close the tell, it just relocates it.

### 23.24-EK The flop min-bet is its own class — not a smaller small c-bet, but the signature of a defensive intent


Live, on a wet/dynamic flop, an opponent's min-bet (by definition the minimum legal bet, ~1 BB — roughly 4% of the pot in a raised pot, but rising to 25-33% in a limped/multiway pot *(calibrate)*) shouldn't automatically be thrown into the same "small = weak" bucket as a standard small c-bet (25-40% *(calibrate)*) in a raised pot — as a hypothesis, they can carry different population signatures. A 25-40% *(calibrate)* c-bet mostly carries a MERGED range — protection + thin value + some air, a plan to continue a wide range cheaply with a cheap stab; a min-bet tells the opposite story — the opponent thinks their hand has showdown value but doesn't care about protection, their intent is to continue cheaply against your aggression, not to buy a fold. In a limped/multiway pot this distinction collapses: there, the min-bet already sits in the 25-33% band, falling right inside the small-c-bet range we just called a "separate class" — in that pot type it's player type, not size, that distinguishes. Even where the distinction holds in a raised pot, 23.24's own rule applies: this is a hypothesis, and the direction locks in only on the SECOND example — if the same player has shown the nuts with a min-bet, the direction flips (for them min = strength), and a mixed sample sends the read to the scrap heap. If the correct answer is that villain has been profiled as capped-but-sticky (won't fold), it's not hand-independent, it's hand-conditioned: fire the big raise with VALUE (there's no fold equity anyway, and a bluff raise against an opponent who won't fold is the worst leverage); flat your thin value/medium strength and carry the aggression yourself on later streets; use the bluff raise only against the sub-type that folds their min-bet to a raise.

**Rule:** In a raised pot, don't automatically throw a flop min-bet (~1 BB) and a 25-40% *(calibrate)* c-bet into the same weakness bucket — in a limped pot the min-bet is already 25-33%, and the distinction collapses there. The first min-bet is a hypothesis; the direction comes with the second example. If villain is capped-but-sticky, make the big raise with VALUE; use the bluff raise only on the sub-type that folds their min-bet to a raise, otherwise flat and carry the aggression to the next street.

### 23.30 The under-barreler is the same check-raise folder — a cascading trap


The player type that over-folds to a flop check-raise and the type that can't find a low-equity barrel on the turn/river are mostly the SAME person — both are two faces of coming apart against/while producing aggression, but they pay off through separate lines: a check-raise's real EV sits above theory because it's directly fed by the over-fold; the missing barrel, on the other hand, doesn't feed the check-raise, it feeds the PASSIVE/check-call line (the same mechanism 23.16 gives — if the opponent can't find a barrel on the turn, the money comes not from a check-raise but from a line where they stick on the flop and check/wait for the river, raising their equity realization). It also carries a cascading trap: if the opponent DIDN'T fold to that check-raise (continued with a call or a re-raise), it's a mistake to keep reading their remaining range with the label "this guy over-folds" — the weak part already left on the flop, and the remaining range is filtered and relatively strong. The turn leg here is subject to the same discipline 23.10-EK gives: the default is BRAKE — you already got their fold on the flop, don't press further. If the turn card gives equity or a new look, keep barreling, but narrow your value targets and re-pick your bluffs against the part of the filtered range that's still folding; if the card doesn't help, even though the opponent's general over-fold tendency doesn't reset on a single continue, the weak part at this node already left on the flop — there's no case left for pressing on.

**Rule:** An opponent who over-folds to a check-raise usually also under-barrels — a check-raise's rising EV comes from the over-fold, while the missing barrel feeds the passive/check-call line. Once they've continued against your check-raise, read their remaining range as filtered-strong; the default on the turn is BRAKE (the same discipline as 23.10-EK) — press on only when a good card/equity arrives, and if you do, narrow your value and pick your bluffs against the part of the range still folding.

### 23.31 The opponent-skill axis governs SPR


B23's three questions (type + ICM-feel + error direction) need a fourth axis: does the opponent play THIS HAND better than me? If the answer is yes, passing on a standard tool you'd otherwise mix (a combo like a 3-bet or check-raise that's supposed to be played at a certain frequency) just because "the opponent is skilled," and ducking into a high-SPR, multi-street passive line instead, is the WRONG direction — passing doesn't shrink SPR, it GROWS it and adds to the number of nodes left to come, which widens the field where the opponent's postflop skill edge gets to spread. The right direction is the opposite: apply the aggression tool you're mixing at mostly ITS FULL frequency, because aggression shrinks SPR, and as SPR shrinks the number of remaining nodes falls, and the field narrows. If the answer is no (the opponent is weak), do the opposite: GROW the number of postflop nodes — delay your aggression with multi-street passive lines like check-call, let the opponent bet into their own hand; a weak opponent usually gives their hand away on their own, with a size or a next move on some street, and a fast, simple line that ends quickly never produces that information. Check-raise is NOT on this list: a check-raise cuts the opponent's initiative and forces their weak hands to fold early (see 23.16) — that produces exactly the opposite of "let the opponent give away their own hand"; as an SPR-shrinking aggression tool it stays on the strong-opponent side only. If the opponent is UNKNOWN, don't treat them as weak by default: an unknown opponent could easily be better than you — play baseline, and only open up the line once a read of weakness is in hand. This adjustment is specific to THIS ONE HAND, not the field: running into one tough opponent at an otherwise soft table doesn't change your stance toward the rest of the table outside that hand — the next hand against a different opponent reverts to normal.

**Rule:** If the opponent is better than you, apply your mixed aggression mostly at full frequency (aggression shrinks SPR, nodes drop, the opponent's skill field narrows); if the opponent is weaker than you, extend the line (grow the node count with passive, multi-street lines like check-call, let them give away their own hand) — check-raise is not on this list, it's already an SPR-shrinking aggression tool. If the opponent is unknown, don't assume weak, play baseline. The adjustment applies only to the hand you're playing against that opponent, not to the field.

### 23.32 Table geometry is an EV asset


A borderline spot's EV alone doesn't make the decision; the stack distribution at the table is also an asset. If you're keeping a weak/exploitable player to your right (meaning you're sitting to their LEFT — you get to see their hands positionally, acting after them) or you cover them, PRESERVING that arrangement over the next few dozen hands is itself a source of EV — the total EV you'll extract from that player depends on the chips they hold and their relative position; risking your stack at a marginal-close spot and breaking that arrangement (entering a big flip and losing your cover, the weak player moving from your right to your left — i.e., you losing your positional edge) cuts off that accumulated EV. On a borderline decision (when "call or fold, jam or pass" isn't clear) add this to the question: if I win, how much does the table's geometry improve, and if I lose, how much does it break — if the answer is "I lose a lot," pass on the marginal spot.

**Rule:** On a marginal-close decision, weigh not just that hand's EV but also the ongoing edge the table's stack distribution provides (who you cover, who sits to your right — meaning you sit to their left and act after them); mostly pass on the marginal spot that risks this edge.

### 23.33 A tough table + a predicted break-up → buy patience


If your table is unusually tough AND, by field/structure math, is about to break up and disperse soon (consolidation, a falling table count), this alone is a decision: at borderline/near-indifferent points, take the low-variance side, and accept a small chip-EV cost. The logic differs from future-game's (22.10) stack-value axis — what you're buying here isn't a bigger stack, it's a WEAKER table: statistically, the table you'll sit at once this one breaks is easier than this one — so skip the marginal gamble against tough opponents right now. If there's no expectation of the table breaking (this is your last/fixed table), the item doesn't apply.

**Rule:** If both a tough table and an expectation of it breaking up soon are true at once, break the borderline decision toward low variance — the cost is a small amount of chip-EV, the payoff is sitting down at the next (probably weaker) table with a full stack.

## Chapter 24 — Edge Premium: When NOT to Gamble (and When You Must)

*★ v6. B20/B22 gave the risk premium — ICM's premium. This chapter adds a second premium: the edge premium (a skill premium). If you have an edge, avoid marginal variance; if you don't, take the ICM-adjusted +EV gamble. The risk premium weighs the loss, the edge premium weighs the later gain.*

### 24.0 Thesis

> **On a marginal gamble (a flip, thin +EV) two premiums are at work. Risk premium: if I lose, what do I lose under ICM? Edge premium: if I don't risk these chips, will my edge earn more later? If your edge is big (soft field, deep, slow structure) PASS the marginal flip. If you have no edge (tough field, shallow, fast structure) TAKE the ICM-adjusted +EV gamble — waiting doesn't help. Never -EV; the edge premium only filters +EV/marginal spots.**

### 24.1 What the edge premium is

In a soft field, if your skill edge is big you're making money automatically every hand. A marginal coin-flip turns that edge into variance: you lose 50% of the time and leave the table, whereas playing on your edge would have produced safer EV. That's why an edged player passes the marginal flip — a better spot comes later. The reverse is also true: if you have no edge (you're the underdog, e.g. against the world's best) waiting has no value; take every +EV spot (the +EV that remains after subtracting the risk premium, i.e. ICM-adjusted $EV), flips included — time is against you.

### 24.2 The four-question filter

Before a marginal gamble, four questions:

1. **Stack depth:** Are you deep? Deep = room to outplay = the edge realizes = PASS. Shallow = no room = TAKE.
2. **Structure speed:** Is the structure slow? Slow = the edge spreads over time = PASS. Fast/turbo = the edge erodes = TAKE.
3. **Your edge:** Is the field soft? Soft = big edge = PASS the marginal. Tough (underdog) = no edge = TAKE the +EV.
4. **Cover map (ICM):** Are you covered (B20/B22)? Yes = the risk premium also says PASS (exception: the shortest stack that covers no one has premium ~0). If you're covering = aggressive.

### 24.3 Filter table

| Question | Gamble PASS | Gamble TAKE |
|---|---|---|
| **Depth** | Deep | Shallow |
| **Structure** | Slow | Fast/turbo |
| **Edge** | Soft field | Tough (underdog) |
| **ICM** | You're covered | You're covering |

*(ICM exception: the shortest stack that covers no one has premium ~0 → "take" on that row.)*

### 24.4 How the two premiums interact

The edge premium and the risk premium usually push the same way: soft field + being covered = a marginal flip is a double PASS. But they can conflict, in a narrow spot: **you're a mid-short stack — the big stacks cover you (the risk premium says "pass") but you also cover the shorter stacks, you have no edge, and the structure is fast (the edge premium says "don't wait, you need chips").** Resolution: pick the best fold-equity spot (jam, not call; B17), avoid a passive flip, take the necessary +EV risk. Note: the shortest stack that covers no one has risk premium ~0 (B20) — there's no conflict there, both premiums say "take", the only lesson is the B17 spot selection. **Never take a -EV gamble, not even to survive.**

### 24.5 Your sidebar (Barcelona)

Concrete: in the €5.3K Main you have an edge (the field is wider/softer) → **PASS the marginal flips, play patiently** (your edge will produce safer EV later). In the €100K SHR you have little/no edge (the world's best) → the premium on waiting is low, don't shy away from marginal +EV spots (if they're still +$EV after subtracting ICM). The same hand, opposite decisions in the two tournaments: the difference is your edge.

### 24.6 Calibration

*(calibrate: how big your edge is — field, stakes, your history — isn't a number, estimate it in three tiers (big/medium/none). The depth and structure-speed thresholds depend on the tournament. The ICM layer combines with the B20/B22 premium.)*

### 24.7 Cheat card

| Situation | Decision |
|---|---|
| **Soft + deep + you're covered** | PASS the marginal flip (two premiums) |
| **Tough + shallow + fast** | TAKE the +EV (check the cover, $EV) |
| **No edge + survival** | Take the best fold-equity jam |
| **You have an edge but you're covering** | Selective aggression |

### 24.8 Drill (3 questions)

1. **G1.** €5K Main, deep stack, early; a marginal flip. Take or pass, and which two questions decide it?
2. **G2.** €100K SHR, the world's best, shallow-to-mid; the same marginal +EV flip. Does the decision change, and why?
3. **G3.** A mid-short stack: the big stacks cover you but you also cover the shorter stacks; no edge, fast structure. A passive call-flip or a fold-equity jam?

*Root-error tie-in: the risk premium (B20) weighs "the loss", the edge premium weighs "the future gain". Both usually say "pass the marginal" — but when you have no edge and survival is at stake, the value of waiting drops. Guard: do I have an edge + am I covered? If both yes, pass; if no edge + forced, take the best fold-equity spot. Never -EV.*


---

### 24.2-EK A Fifth Question: "If I Win, Who Do I Become?" — and the Field Multiplier


24.2's four questions weigh the PRICE of the gamble; two additional measures weigh the ARRIVAL point and the edge tier.

**5. Arrival role:** the same double gives two stacks different things — 30→60bb is a leader dominating the table, 20→40bb is still a covered medium stack (22.2-EK-2). If the answer to "who do I become if I win?" is "the player who covers everyone," the marginal gamble moves a notch toward TAKE; if it's "still medium," it moves toward PASS. This notch grows if you believe you play a big stack well, and shrinks if opponents are ICM-insensitive (if pressure can't be sold) — this is the arrival-state form of the edge premium.

**Field multiplier (feeds 24.6's calibration):** the edge tier thickens with field size. In a small field / at the FT, a spot with a few percent of $EV remaining AFTER subtracting ICM gets taken; on the bubble of a huge field, the same spot needs a noticeably bigger gain relative to stack size *(calibrate)*, because at the tables that remain, other players' error volume has grown — the pure-bubble call range tightens there accordingly (22.1-EK-2). Tough small field (SHR) ↔ soft huge field (Main) are opposite ends: 24.5's sidebar is read through this multiplier. The filter table doesn't change; these are two lines read as -EK.

### 24.1-EK When Your Edge Is Big, Choose the Low-Variance Line Postflop Too


The edge premium covers not just preflop flip decisions but also the choice between two postflop lines that are equal in EV (check-raise/barrel-off vs. float/call). In a soft field, with re-entry closed, or when your edge is big, if the two lines are theoretically equal in EV it's sensible to prefer the LOW-variance one (call/float) — this line earns you extra information (if villain checks back the turn, he mostly doesn't know how to fire a no-equity bluff, and he'll also make a mistaken fold/call on the river) and doesn't put you at risk of an early bust in a big pot. In a tough field, or without an edge, this distinction disappears — there, among lines equal in EV it doesn't matter, play balanced.

**Rule:** Soft field + big edge + two lines equal in EV: choose the information-gathering low-variance line, not the aggressive line that risks a big pot/early bust — the gain comes from exploiting villain's weakness on later streets, not from the first street itself.

## Chapter 25 — The Story in Your Head: Evidence or Narrative

*★ v6. B16 gave tilt and the autopsy (the end-of-day case log). This chapter places an in-the-moment filter in front of them: before a big bluff-catch/fold, "is this read based on evidence or on a story?" The bridge from B23's opponent reading (evidence) to B16's autopsy (result-independent grading).*

### 25.0 Thesis

> **Before a hero-call or a big fold, ask: is this read based on EVIDENCE (action sequence, sizing, timing, position, type — B23) or on the STORY in your head (emotion, momentum, ego)? If it's a story, return to default (B11.2). This is an in-hand version of the B16 tilt card: tilt wounds the emotion, the story distorts the decision.**

### 25.1 The "story in your head" test

Before a big bluff-catch or a thin fold, pause for a second. Which of two kinds is your reasoning:

- **Evidence:** the villain's action sequence (bet-bet-bet or check-call), sizings (polarized/blocky), timing, position, type (B23). Concrete, what you see at the table.
- **Story:** "I feel like he's bluffing me", "he just took a pot off me, now I'll catch him", "this hand always loses". Emotion and narrative.

If it's based on evidence, trust it — but evidence doesn't beat the cover: while covered, a hero-call is still a fold (B21.2). If it's based on a story, return to default: B11.2 (one pair = a bluff-catcher, respect the uncapped). A story drags you into both a needless hero-call and a needless fold; both are faces of the same root error (emotion > range).

### 25.2 The story ↔ tilt link

A story is usually a product of tilt: "he just took a pot, now I'll catch him" comes from a bad-beat/ego trigger (B16.2). The sequence is B16's: first NOTICE the symptom (B16.2 — speeding up, reaching for the phone, chasing → a physical move: deep breath / VPIP lock / drink water), then decide. While tilted you can't even run the "evidence or story" test reliably; label the wound first (the B16.2 card; the wound concept is B0 c.15), then look at the evidence.

### 25.3 Result-independent grading = the B16.3 autopsy, through the story lens

When the hand ends, grading lives in B16.3: at the **end of the day** (at the table just note the hand), two questions — what was the book's rule given what I knew at the moment, and did I follow it. This chapter adds one lens to that autopsy: **was my decision based on evidence or a story?**

- Right rule + bad result = a GOOD decision — **but only if you truly followed the rule/range (B16.3); "it was right, it was variance" is not a self-absolution door.**
- Winning on a story = a LEAK (the result rewarded you, the process was flawed; repeat it and you get burned).

B25 doesn't open a new autopsy layer; it adds the "evidence or story" question to the B16.3 autopsy.

### 25.4 Attention on the priciest decisions

A story-error isn't equally expensive everywhere. At the FT/under ICM a single decision is worth many buy-ins: in a bloated pot the call-off is the most expensive action (B21.1), the bluff-catch while covered (B21.2 → fold). Save your mental energy for there: the big bubble/FT pots, the call-offs while covered. On cheap decisions (small pot, money far off) don't chase perfection — play them by rote, bank the energy for the expensive spot.

### 25.5 Calibration

*(calibrate: the "evidence or story" threshold is personal — review your own past hero-calls in the B16.3 autopsy, which were evidence and which were story. The app's report card grades result-independently: did you pick the right concept, not the result.)*

### 25.6 Cheat card

| Situation | Right reflex |
|---|---|
| **Before a hero-call** | Evidence or story? If a story, return to default (B11.2) |
| **Before a big fold** | Same test — a story-driven fold is also a leak |
| **Tilt symptom** | A physical move first (B16.2), then decide |
| **End-of-day autopsy** | B16.3's two questions + "was it evidence or a story" |
| **Expensive spot (FT/covered)** | Save your energy; don't chase perfection on a cheap decision |

### 25.7 Drill (3 questions)

1. **M1.** A big bet on the river; you feel "this guy's a bluffer" but the action sequence (bet-bet-bet, polarized) fits value. Which reasoning do you trust, call or fold?
2. **M2.** You took a bad beat, hands are speeding up, you reached for your phone. On the next big hero-call decision, what do you do FIRST (B16.2), then which test?
3. **M3.** End-of-day autopsy: while COVERED you made a hero-call, caught the bluff, the pot's yours. Through the B21.2 + B16.3 + story lens, how do you grade the decision?

*Root-error tie-in: a story-based decision overrides the range discipline (B11.2/B12) with emotion — the decision-layer face of tilt (B16.2). Guard: evidence or story + did I follow the rule (B16.3). B25 places an in-hand filter in front of B16 and an autopsy lens behind it; not a new rule, a trigger for the existing discipline.*

## Chapter 26 — Facing the C-bet: Caller Discipline and Threshold Work

### 26.0 Core: defense is studied as THRESHOLD, not frequency


The correct question facing a c-bet is not "what percentage of my range should I defend" but "below which hand class do I fold." What you pull out of the sim isn't a frequency, it's three thresholds:

1. **Continue threshold** — the minimum calling hand (below this class, fold).
2. **Value-raise threshold** — the minimum check-raise-for-value hand.
3. **Bluff-raise candidates** — and after a few boards this step is SKIPPED ENTIRELY: bluff-raise qualifications (board ownership + blocker) recur, so you memorize them.

> **Read the continue and value-raise thresholds; apply the bluff-raise from memory after the first few boards. Study time is spent on the threshold line, not on combo selection.**

### 26.0-EK Why the threshold is robust — and how to score the drill accordingly


Here's the rationale behind 26.0's "threshold, not frequency" directive: run the range and the stack through the sim — combos swing between raise and call, while the continue line barely moves. As the size you're facing grows, the threshold tightens on pot-odds grounds *(calibrate)*, but raise-or-call is far noisier than that. The robust object to learn is the LINE, not the action-label. Score the B9 drill the same way: continuing on the correct side of the line = correct; fretting over missing the exact action spends your error budget on noise.

### 26.1 The threshold calibrates against YOUR OWN range composition


The rote rule "every pair calls, every draw continues" produces automatic over-defense on a connected board — and the cause isn't only that the opponent gets stronger, it's that YOUR OWN continuable combos multiply. As connectivity increases the threshold rises, because your own draw count increases; half of threshold calibration is the opponent, half is your own composition.

### 26.1-EK The continue threshold on a trips board: not the pair, but KICKER liveness + backdoors


On a 6-6-6 / Q-Q-Q type board, the "every pair calls" rule backfires: any kicker that pairs makes a boat, and the opponent's share of boats is low *(calibrate)* → so the threshold is drawn not by the pair but by the kicker's liveness + backdoors. A low suited hand carrying two overcards plus a backdoor flush draw (J2s type) is a better call than an offsuit broadway (JTo type): the opponent holds JTo's single live card more often, while J2s's low kicker arrives more often and, when it does, you're usually ahead. The concrete form of 26.1's "the threshold calibrates against your own composition" rule: on a trips board, continuable combos are counted by kickers, not pairs. Range condition: against a tight EP range the threshold hardens — even two overcards with a backdoor are borderline, suited trash mostly folds; against a wide BTN/BB range it's the reverse, low suiteds call wide. The attacking side (small size, high frequency; tight-range OOP low trips 2/3) is in 18.2-EK-10.

### 26.2 Check-raise candidates: look for a decisively-resolved runout


A check-raise builds a big pot; a big pot wants a decisively-resolved runout:

- DOMINATING draws (top-pair-plus + flush-draw types) stay in the call bucket — they realize without building an expensive pot.
- Marginal candidates that would fall into no-man's-land upon pairing aren't raised.
- On a disadvantaged board, if no hand is eager for a value-raise, shut the raise node down entirely (until the opponent notices and adjusts) — the defense-side face of B18's "throw the size out of the tree."

### 26.2-EK Three rules for the raise node: call scarcity, post-raise, blocker lock


**The call-scarcity law.** Don't misread the output on a board where you can't find a hand to call with: you'll both fold ABOVE your target (your defense stays below the MDF target) and raise wide — the two are the same law's two faces. When calls are naturally scarce, defense frequency is collected from the raise: push the definition of value down to medium pair classes, and add bluffs in proportion to that width *(calibrate the ratio)*. The reverse is law too: as your value-raise range narrows against a big size, your bluff-raise count narrows with it. Adding bluff-raises without looking at the value budget is the defense-side root error of over-bluffing.

**If you chose to raise, also study what comes AFTER the raise.** Play forward the node where your raise gets called: read the turn value threshold, then look at the check column — see that a meaningful share of your strongest classes sits there *(calibrate the share)*. The fear that "a wide raise range exposes my hand" dies in that column: if your check range is protected, you can continue against small bets with your pair classes and no one can punish you. If it isn't protected, fix the check range, not the raise range.

**The blocker lock-test.** No blocker rule gets memorized without being tested; there's one question: can the opponent's value core be purged of your key card? If the core structurally runs through that card, the rule is locked — memorize it and apply it. If it can be purged, its shelf life is short: lean on the class-percentage (26.4) instead of the blocker, and put your read on top of that.

### 26.2-EK-2 A 3-bet-pot example for the blocker lock-test: the side card


At 40bb effective (SPR ≲2 in a 3-bet pot, shallow), on J-T-x, against the 3-bettor's jam the caller's second pair (QT/KT) decides on its side card — but in two directions: a Q side card blocks QQ (value) and AQ/KQ (semi-bluff); a K side card blocks KK (value) and KQ/AK (semi-bluff); the two effects pull against each other. The blocker lock-test makes the call "borderline" here, not "comfortable"; determine which direction wins by counting combos, and recount when the 3-bettor's jam range changes *(calibrate)*. J8 doesn't belong to this group: it's top pair, the blocker effect of the 8 is negligible (it only touches rare 98-type 3-bet-bluff combos), and the decision comes from hand strength, not the side card. Check the lock-test against B26.2-EK's question, and check the texture against B32.3 (see B32.3).

### 26.2-EK-4 Three pool layers for the raise node: no call to a big range-bet · on a value-scarce board, polar-big x/r with bluff-raises from an UNBLOCKED card · a sticky small x/r against a draw-heavy turn bet


26.2 is the theory baseline; three opponent-labeled layers come alongside it *(calibrate all of them)*.

1. **No call to a big range-bet — raise or fold.** Reopening against a polar big bet is unprofitable (you run into the nuts); but if the opponent is also betting MEDIUM hands at the big size (88 / A-Q at showdown), the range isn't polar → convert the call range into a min-raise range: protection-raise with what has showdown value, equity-raise with bluffs; sizing ≈2× that of 26.5-EK-2. 19.11's "raising is cheap" is against a small bet; this label is what opens up raising against a big one (18.4-EK-2, 23.11).
2. **No slowplay when value is scarce.** On an A-T-2 type board, BB's value is scarce (the best is A-x, a bottom set, a bottom two pair) → there's no trapping luxury to feed the x/r range: value goes at full frequency, sized big, no thin x/r. Pick the bluff-raise from a hand that doesn't hold the opponent's BET-FOLD block (9-x/8-x/7-x) — T-2s is a better raise-bluff than T-6s. The pool's BB doesn't find this x/r; you x/r with every bottom pair. 26.5's "1/3 → x/r wide" cell here reads as "value-scarce → full-frequency polar."
3. **The sticky raise — only against a sticky profile.** When a second flush draw opens on the turn, the pool bets all its draws big and doesn't carry its top pair to the turn → the turn bet range is draw-heavy. Against this, a SMALL x/r: medium pair+draw, gutshot, non-nut flush draw pays the raise; a big raise/jam folds them out. The raise range is the dominating class (top pair, nut flush draw, board-connected medium pocket pair); no bluff x/r. Against a fold-prone opponent, 26.2 applies unchanged.

### 26.2-EK-3 The reason to raise is EV — the nuts CALL a big polar bet (not "protection"), the draw jams when the jam folds out what dominates it


**The nuts' call.** 80–100bb, OOP opener checks → IP fires a big polar bet (low pocket pairs + gutshot broadway bluffs + high value). AA's call-EV is higher than its raise-EV: calling lets 55/66/JT keep barreling on the turn too; raising folds them out and keeps only what beats you. "Range protection" is the RESULT, not the reason — 29.3's "set slowplay = range protection" sentence describes the result; the reason is EV. Call again against a big turn bet. The raise node belongs to combo draws (K♦9♦ type: it folds out Ax and the low pocket pairs). At a low river SPR, order your bluff-catching with the blocker lock-test (26.4): the A alone blocks AQ/AJ/A4 → call; KQ blocks both value and bluffs → borderline; QJ blocks a bluff (JT) → fold.

**The draw's jam (IP, 3-bet/squeeze pot, 100bb+ — outside the baseline).** On a J-9-7 flush-draw board the squeezer bets half pot: 8-6 suited (OESD+FD) is a pure CALL — none of the hands the jam folds out dominate it, the ones who pay are overpairs/better draws. KTss/QTss JAM — it folds out dominating pair+draw combos like KQ♥/KJ♥. The test: "does my jam fold out hands that were dominating me?" Yes → jam; no → call (EV is close). In a deep squeeze pot, AQ suited/A8 suited mostly fold to a half-pot bet — no over-defense. This is the IP mirror of 26.2's "dominating draws stay in the call bucket"; cross-reference 6.3.

### 26.3 Keep notes by the SIZE FACED, not by the board


Defenses against different c-bet sizes aren't comparable to each other; the pattern only emerges once boards facing the SAME size are placed side by side. Since the size faced on the flop determines the turn thresholds, notes are kept by LINE, not by street: "flop-1/3 → turn-2/3" is one box, "flop-2/3 → turn-jam" is another.

### 26.4 River defense: frequency-first, combo-second


The continue decision on the river has two axes; B11.2 gave only the price axis, the second was missing:

1. **Price axis (B11.2):** size → required continue %.
2. **Frequency axis (new):** match the required % to the class-percentages within your own range — "if top-pair+ is half my hand, call with all of it; if it's two-thirds, trim one class." Fit frequency to class-%; within-class selection is decided by the blocker rule (blocks value → call, blocks bluffs → fold), and only your read sits on top of that.

> **On the river, price tells you "how much do I need to defend," frequency tells you "from where in my hand do I cut." The missing half of B11.2 is the second one.**

### 26.4-EK Three bluff-catcher examples for the blocker rule: unblock catcher beats blocker catcher · K9 beats KK · on a monotone turn, AA/KK call, TT/JJ fold


26.4 said "blocks value → call, blocks bluffs → fold"; three node examples make it concrete *(calibrate)*.

1. **Against a river all-in on a high board (A-Q-J-x-x type):** K-Q is a better catcher than Q-T — the K blocks the opponent's K-x bluffs. A-x is better than both: it UNBLOCKS the opponent's T-x bluffs; the catcher is chosen by unblocking, not blocking. The attacking face is in 11.1-EK-3 (bluffing from K-x).
2. **On the river after a raise against a big c-bet (9-8-5 type, tight IP caller):** ranking by "high pocket pair" is wrong. KK blocks none of the opponent's bluffs and none of their value (the caller doesn't hold AK); K9/Q9 type blocks the medium-pair set and two-pair → K9 calls, KK folds. The value-blocking rule outranks the high pocket pair (19.11-EK).
3. **Against a turn polar bet on a monotone board (3-bet pot, you hold an overpair IP):** the opponent's bet is flush + set + straight-draw bluffs (65s/JT/QT type) → TT/JJ/QQ without a suit card fold, with a suit card call; AA/KK CALL — they don't block the opponent's bluff combos, TT/JJ does. "Overpair" isn't one class, it's a blocker class (32.3-EK-2).

Common lesson: the catcher is chosen not by the hand's rank but by whether it touches the opponent's bluff list; the blocker lock-test (26.2-EK) passes in all three cases — the opponent can't purge their value of that card.

### 26.6 Post-raise turn (x/r + call): locked nuts → DOWNBET, fluid nuts → JAM, medium size mostly absent


26.2-EK said "study the post-raise"; this is the mechanics of it. The 11.1 table is IP PFR's value turn; this section is the OOP x/r'er's turn. After flop x/r + call the SPR is low; the turn tree collapses to two sizes: SMALL (a small slice of the pot *(calibrate)*) or JAM. One question decides: **is my x/r range's nut class LOCKED on this turn?**

- **Locked** (hard for the river to change things AND the nuts is still yours: a straight completed, there's no flush draw, and your x/r range carries that straight — if you had OESD/gutshot semi-bluff x/r's on the flop; if not, the straight has shifted to the caller and you're not locked → weighted to check; a low card paired and your x/r held low pairs; on a low A-x-x board, a low turn that does NOT complete the wheel — if it completes it, the straight rule applies; if your x/r range doesn't carry wheel-draws, you're not locked) → nothing wants protection and nothing wants a jam → the ENTIRE range downbets: overcards, low pairs, one-card draws keep paying cheaply; thin value + bluffs go at the same size.
- **Fluid** (a second flush draw opened; a connected turn on a medium-connected board; top pair is fragile) → strong hands want to get the money in before the river changes things → jam weight increases; the semi-bluff jam comes from here too — but as the SPR drops, fold equity drops: at a low SPR the draw dominating you gets its price and mostly pays, the jam's target is to fold out weak pairs/overcards/weak draws and deny equity, and when called you still have outs; folding out the dominating draw is only possible when the SPR is high enough / there's enough fold equity *(borderline — calibrate)*.

The lock depends on the FLOP x/r composition: if you don't find protection-x/r's with the low card, you're not locked once the low card pairs. Adding low-pair/backdoor protection to the x/r range buys the turn lock. Locked → weighted to downbet, jam rare (as SPR rises a medium size opens up, calibrate); not locked → strong value + the best semi-bluffs jam, the middle checks *(class list: calibrate)*.

### 26.7 Read the turn card in three layers — and WHO the card locked


**Three layers:** (1) what the card completed; (2) whether a live draw still exists on the board; (3) whether the completed draw also exists in the opponent's range. A low turn completing a straight on a RAINBOW board is close to a pure downbet. If the same card leaves a live flush draw from the flop, it leaves the lock open: a straight/two pair/set loses to a flush on the river → the jam share visibly rises *(ratio: calibrate)*. Flush COMPLETED, your x/r range holds flushes, and the range that CALLED the x/r also had flush draws (calling with a flush draw on the flop is standard) → both sides are locked; a jam into a locked opponent is dead money when locked-vs-locked (only a flush pays) → the range reverts to downbet, jam to a very narrow class *(calibrate)*.

**If the card locked the opponent, the bet node closes.** Your BB x/r range is comparatively poor in A-x — strong A-x went to a 3-bet, medium A-x went to a flop call, the remaining A-x is mostly a gutshot/backdoor bluff; the opener's range is full of A-x/K-x. The Ace turn locks the OPPONENT's range, not yours: the layer that would pay a downbet now outranks you. Same on a turn that pairs the top card: the opener's top pair became trips, no one calls a small bet. On these turns the range mostly checks/controls; on an Ace turn, the narrow strong class (set/two pair) bets big for value over the A-x top pair — downbet only works if it's built MERGED (nuts + a medium hand wanting protection + a few bluffs), not with the narrow class alone *(calibrate)*; on a top-card pairing, the narrow class (boat/quads) bets big/jams for value over trips — downbet leaves value on the table there *(calibrate)*. A turn that pairs the low card is the reverse — the low pairs in your x/r become trips, low two pair becomes a boat, low set becomes quads, and the lock is yours: that class bets.

**The x/r's EV lives on the turn:** an x/r with no turn plan is the defense-side face of 11.0-EK's "unplanned bloating" — lower your x/r frequency on boards where you don't know the threshold. Exploit: the pool overfolds to downbets and doesn't find the turn raise → if you're torn between downbet and check, downbet; if the opponent doesn't find the raise, need picks the size — a hand wanting protection jams, a medium hand downbets.

### 26.5 Calibration + Cheat Sheet


*(calibrate: pull the three thresholds — continue / value-raise / bluff-raise — from the solver by board-size line; locate the caller (defending side) node in whatever solver you use.)*

| Size faced | Read | Note-keeping |
|---|---|---|
| **Flop 1/3 (range-bet)** | Wide continue threshold; check-raise FREQUENT/wide (opponent's range is wide-weak) | Calibrate with your own draw count |
| **Flop 2/3 (polarized)** | Narrow continue; check-raise narrow/polarized; value-raise threshold sharp | By line ("2/3 → turn ?") |
| **River (any)** | Price + frequency, two axes | Class-% matching |
| **Disadvantaged board** | Shut the raise node | A deliberate cost note |

### 26.5-EK Against a small bet on a monotone flop (3-bet pot)


You're facing the 3-bettor's small bet on a monotone flop in a 3-bet pot: the pair class CARRYING the flush-suit card is a call class; an overpair / top pair calls the small bet even without the flush-suit card (reassess when the fourth flush card comes on the turn); low/medium pairs and overcards without the flush-suit card mostly fold. Raise candidates are draw + blocker hands carrying the flop's high suited card (K/Q); a suited connector without the flush-suit card is NOT a raise — the 3-bettor's range carries a high SHARE of nut-flush/nut-flush-draws (not the total flush count, the nut ratio — the caller's range carries more flushes in most buckets) *(calibrate)*. First read which monotone bucket you're in (A + low / A-less disconnected medium / the caller's made flush+straight mass is large) — the 3-bettor's nut-flush share and the caller's made-hand mass vary by bucket (see B32.3).

### 26.5-EK-2 Check-raise size against a big polar c-bet is MIN-RAISE — the target is his AIR block; the instant the pool folds that block, x/r is pure profit


26.5's "flop 2/3 → x/r narrow/polarized" row is the theory (~9–13%); this EK adds the size + pool layer. A big polar bet range is two blocks: value (overpair/top pair — folds to no size) and air (K8s/Q5s/backdoor — folds to EVERY size). Growing the size doesn't buy anything from the value block, it just buys the air's fold at a higher price. At ≈50bb, CO vs BB, on a disconnected 9-7-4 / 8-6-3 type board, IP 75%: the solver picks ≈2× — the air folds at the cheapest price, and it also shuts down the 3-bet *(calibrate)*.

**Composition:** value = top-kicker top pair (A9/K9; a weak 9x is not x/r'd — there's no "worse hand to call" against a polar range); 9-7 two pair slowplays (their overpair will barrel itself on the turn); JTs is a nut-gutshot+overcard jam; low-pair protection (43s/76s) x/r's — the blocker selection is "a card that doesn't interact with the opponent's BET-FOLD range": 4/3 don't touch it, the J blocks their JT/QJ bet-fold → 43s beats J9.

**Exploit:** the pool folds offsuit K/Q-high and K6s/Q8s to the min x/r (theory ~33% fold → real world 45–50%) → with K2s/J6/55/K8 the x/r EV is far above the call → x/r the entire range *(deviation measure: calibrate)*. A 2.2× erases the "misclick" appearance. If the opponent splits their sizing in two, x/r the small size as well. Same direction as 19.11's "the pool doesn't find the flop-3-bet."

### 26.5-EK-3 A dry high board after an SB flat: check-raising the pool's 100% c-bet, bluffing with suited Ax (40bb, single-raised)


You flatted a late open from SB at 40bb (offsuit broadway, suited Kx/Qx, 77+, suited Ax — the body that doesn't go to a 3-bet; 4.2-EK). Flop is Q-6-2 / K-5-3 rainbow, IP fires a small c-bet. In theory everything is mixed — half of Qx raises, half of suited Ax raises; there's no memorizable rule *(calibrate)*. The pool deviates in two places and the two collide: (1) IP range-bets — equilibrium gives ~44% check on K-5-3; (2) IP overfolds to a check-raise — it folds 100% of weak Qx/Jx/suiteds without a backdoor. Fold equity is far above equilibrium.

- **Value:** Qx/Kx-plus (KQ, QJs, sets).
- **Bluff: suited Ax** — an overcard; a straight/flush draw around the 6/5 on the turn (K-7s can't turn a straight around the 6 → K-suited isn't a candidate); it doesn't block the Q-J-T-9-8-7 class the opponent would fold. The 7x/8x/Jx bet-calls that continue against the raise are rare in an SB flat, so their fold is clean.
- **Small pocket pair (44/55):** borderline raise since it can open a straight on the turn; 77–99 mostly call.

**Mirror (when you're IP):** on K-5-3, ~44% check — check back 9-7s, suited Ax, pocket 4s/2s; don't be the one range-betting on the assumption "SB flat = weak." If BB over-calls, return to 13. A fourth carve-out candidate for 23.12.

---

## Chapter 27 — Studying the Solver: Connecting the B9 Drill and the B16.3 Autopsy to the Solver

### 27.0 Base stance: the solver is a learning tool, not a source to copy


Feedback in poker is weak — you can bleed EV for years on a wrong assumption and never notice. This is why you don't carry the solver's output over without understanding WHY it did something (pull out the mechanism, not a memorized line). This is the solver-side of the B16.3 autopsy.

### 27.1 Study boards in BUCKETS, not one at a time


Build texture buckets instead of isolated boards (paired / monotone / connected / dry-high) and assign one strategy per bucket; only carve out the sub-class that genuinely behaves differently. This makes the B9 drill board-efficient — you don't rethink every board from scratch.

### 27.2 Bluff counting = plain arithmetic (do it with your own hand)


Count your value combos on the river, add bluffs up to the value:bluff ratio the size requires — this is arithmetic, not memorization. As the class that can be value-bet narrows (as only strong hands remain), the size grows; see this by counting it with your own hand, not from someone else's ready-made table.

### 27.3 TEST simplification with node-locking


Node-locking is a well-known solver feature: lock a size/action and measure the EV loss. If a simple simplification (a single size) loses little EV, it can be brought to the table; if it loses a lot, it can't. See the result in YOUR OWN run *(calibrate)* — don't take someone else's claim that "the range converges to a bucket" as fact.

### 27.3-EK Node-locking applied to a 3-bet pot


In a shallow-SPR 3-bet pot, "drop the middle sizes, reduce to jam/check" and at 100bb, "two sizes (small + big)" are concrete applications of this procedure: lock the middle size, measure the EV loss, bring it to the table if it's ~zero (see B32.1). Don't take someone else's claim of "zero EV loss" as fact.

### 27.4 Heuristic format: pattern + reason + BOUNDARY CONDITION


Write every rule you pull from the solver in three parts: (1) **pattern** — what's observed; (2) **reason** — the mechanism; (3) **boundary condition** — where it's INVALID.

> **A heuristic with no boundary condition written down gets over-generalized. Every line of solver-pipeline output carries these three parts.**

### 27.5 Self-check: the incentive sanity-check


At the table and in study alike: "what's the opponent's most profitable response to this?" If the answer is degenerate, the strategy is wrong. Test exploitability with the opponent's INCENTIVE, not with the memorized MDF.

### 27.6 Session engineering + the pre-game brain ban


- A short, time-boxed session (calibrate the duration for yourself), a SINGLE predefined goal per session, zero distractions (the chief enemy of the goalless "let me open the solver for an hour" method).
- Heavy solver work only on the morning of a day you're NOT playing; mental work before a session on a game day hurts performance (a line to B16.4). Low-intensity work (video/drill) goes after the session.

### 27.7 A third question for the autopsy (an addition to B16.3)


Matching your own combo to the solver's is misleading at a mixed node — your combo could be the outlier of its class. Add to B16.3's two questions: **"What was my CLASS doing?"** The lesson comes from the class level, not the single combo.

*Root-error link: this framework connects the B9 drill and your autopsy to the solver; a boundary-conditioned heuristic is the engine for preventing the root error at the study desk. The detailed reading-protocol will be derived from your own solver export (solver-pipeline).*

---

### 27.8 Four decision filters


- **EV-source tracking (B9/B27):** "the sim allows it" ≠ "you played well." The EV of equal-EV actions comes from WHICH opponent-response node — will that node actually occur in YOUR pool? If it doesn't occur, the line loses in practice (if the limp-reraise's EV comes from the opponent's 4-bet-jamming trash and the pool doesn't do that, the jam is superior). The missing link in the autopsy and the solver-pipeline.
- **The inevitability principle (B25 addendum):** when you run into a strange line, the first question — does the hand crushing me ALREADY have me stacked on every line? If yes, that branch DROPS from the decision, and the decision is made against the remaining range → often that means RAISE for equity denial (not a scared call). Unbalanced strange lines are equity-driven, not nut-driven. A table-side 10-second filter for B25's evidence-test.
- **3-bet size identity (B14/B12):** if the range has a meaningful flat, the 3-bet is polar → BIG; if the flat is closed off, it's the whole VPIP 3-betting → linear → SMALL. Once two short stacks appear, the strategy among the medium stacks shifts from linear-small to polar-big+flat. An identity rule for the fixed-multiplier table.
- **Stack-band protection (B24/B22):** if your edge lives in a specific depth band (being able to play flats-vs-open), even a speculative 2bb flat before a blind increase can drop you below that band → fold on a close decision and guarantee the band. Concrete mechanics for B24's edge-premium: the edge isn't abstract, it's a depth-band.

---

### 27.9 Reading the output: threshold first, check column first


On the first read of the solver output, don't read frequency — read the threshold. For every size, there's one question: what's the weakest hand that can be value-bet at this size; facing a bet: what's the weakest hand that doesn't fold? Mix percentages are equal-EV at equilibrium — they don't carry a memorized frequency, they carry a threshold; above the threshold is "may do it," and you calibrate the frequency later at the class level *(calibrate)*.

The order is also fixed for aggressor-flop output: start not from the bet column but from the CHECK column. Find the class that wants to check the most — if even that class mixes in some bet, check and bet are equal-EV for that class, and the remaining classes already prefer to bet: betting the whole range at a single small size doesn't lose EV on the flop, but it isn't free — a full-range bet also writes a debt to the turn (27.13); the exception is a turn range-bet, build your turn plan around that. Where the bet frequency drops off, tie the boundary to a single readable card feature — "bet if the lowest card is above this rank, check if below" *(calibrate the rank)*. The boundary being arbitrary isn't a flaw, it's a feature: boundary hands are already equal-EV and an arbitrary line is executable at the table.

> **A memorized frequency doesn't carry over; a threshold does. Start reading from the check column, tie the boundary to a single card feature.**

### 27.10 Range arithmetic: combo to percentage, per-street doubling, rewinding from the river


Three steps from combo to percentage: write the class's combo count, divide by 1326, multiply by the inverse of the opponent's range width (×5 for a 20% range). Recount as streets progress: if the weak half of the range keeps folding off and the strong class keeps surviving, that class's share roughly doubles per street *(calibrate)*. In a narrow range these percentages swing wildly board to board; in a wide range they're stable — spend your memorization budget on narrow-range nodes. Solver memorization only carries you on the board you actually saw; this arithmetic carries you on every board.

Start your study from the river and rewind: what gets value-bet on the river determines the turn range, and the turn determines the flop. The river threshold is arithmetic: against a pot-size bet, the opponent defends roughly half → so for a value bet you need to be ahead of MOST of your range; raise possibility and blocker share push the threshold up *(calibrate)*. The folding rule: every pot-bet call halves the range, and the share that beats you roughly DOUBLES — if you want two big streets of value, your share of being beaten on the turn needs to be small enough for that *(calibrate)*.

*Root-error link: this doubling is the arithmetic behind why one pair drops to a bluff-catcher in a bloated pot — a hand that doesn't count its per-street-doubling share of being beaten grows the pot itself.*

### 27.11 Memorize the RANGE, not the strategy — and the own-range impossibility test


Don't memorize the strategy; memorize the RANGE. For each texture family, ballpark and memorize the opponent range's shares of draws, sets, two pair *(calibrate: from your own sims)*; at the table, roughly halve the map on every call, dropping the classes that go to fold/raise. In narrow ranges the shares swing hard, in wide ranges little — keep a separate map for tight positions. 27.10's threshold arithmetic runs on this map; a threshold without a map is like a map without a compass.

Keep the same map for your own range too — the impossibility test (the arithmetic form of 27.5's incentive test): if what beats you is roughly half your range and you're firing a big size, your total bet frequency (with the bluff share) rests on the bulk of your range — the opponent's incentive to CALL with a worse hand dies (the "value" bets at the threshold get paid only by hands better than you, so they aren't value), and no such output exists at equilibrium. Rule: at a node where the opponent has a bet option, if you're firing a big size with more than half your range, your value threshold is too low — raise it *(calibrate)*.

### 27.12 Mixed strategy: mix = indifference = a free decision


If the solver mixes a hand, it's indifferent between the two actions; mixing is only legitimate when the EV difference is near zero. Table translation: a mixed hand is a FREE decision — fix it to one side; your read tells you which side, and if there's no read, the EV-source filter (27.8) does. Test your fixing with node-lock (27.3): lock the mix to one side, measure the EV loss *(calibrate)* — if it's small, it's brought to the table, that's the rule.

The gate to mixing is opponent quality: play pure against a rec who doesn't count your frequency, and take the exploit. Balance is only needed against a good opponent who's watching you — and there a rare dose is enough: occasionally show a strong hand on your passive line so the counter-adjustment door stays shut. Watch your tells live: if you only check your watch on a borderline hand, the watch announces the hand — if you need to mix, use a card-feature rule ("today's aggressive suit") and change the rule each day.

> **Mixing is only legitimate at equal-EV; an equal-EV decision is free. Fix it against a rec and take the exploit; save balance only for the one who's counting.**

### 27.12-EK Fixing must be NEUTRAL at the range scale — pulling a mix to one side at a 60bb 3-bet node opens the 4-bet-jam door


27.12's "mix = free decision, fix it" rule is for nodes where the fix doesn't change composition (a single postflop hand; a fix that's neutral at the range scale). The boundary: at a 60bb 3-bet node, if you pull the boundary combos (KJs/QJs/QTs/JTs/ATo/A9s) to 100% 3-bet, your 3-bet range's average strength drops while your call-off range (around AQ+/77+) stays fixed → BTN's 60bb 4-bet-jam becomes profitable with almost any two cards, and the 3-bet itself turns negative. The same composition isn't a problem at 100bb (a 4-bet-jam is expensive, BTN is stuck with a non-all-in 4-bet). Rule: at 60bb, either KEEP the boundary combos mixed, or preserve the total when fixing them — if you pull one to 3-bet, push another to flat/fold (a note to 14.1's 60–70bb row).

60bb is "deep in frequency, shallow in sizing": check frequency, realization, and c-bet frequency are close to 100bb; the sizing repertoire is closer to 40bb — half pot / 60% is common, pot-size only on a double-high-connected flush-draw board *(calibrate)*. Side effect: at 60bb the SB flat widens a bit with AJ/KJ/QJ type hands (a shield against BB's squeeze); at 100bb these hands shift to a value 3-bet *(borderline)*. On T-9-x, the 60bb flop jam class isn't AK/AQ but top pair (AT/KT/QT) + T8s, which unblocks broadway; AA/KK go smaller *(calibrate)*.

### 27.13 A street-based procedure: turn buckets and the simplification debt-ledger


Study the turn as an INCOMING CARD, not as a board (the street-form of 27.1): four buckets — flush-completing, blank, board-pairing, straight/overcard — plus at most ONE special card. Before carving out a card that looks like an outlier, test it: force it onto the bucket's single sizing and compare its check-frequency to the bucket's; if it converges, absorb it, and write the difference down as a deliberate cost *(calibrate the EV difference)*. And memorize this: the full-range luxury that exists on the flop is the exception on the turn — the default is a split range; outside the rare turns that sharply skew the range, the turn splits the range, and your study also starts by looking for the split range's threshold.

Simplification isn't free; the bill gets cut on the next street — write the debt down next to every shortcut. A texture you reduce to full-range check leaves a check range stronger than the solver's on the turn: you owe extra defense against a probe. A texture you reduce to full-range bet also writes debt: since your turn bet range is more diluted than the solver's, you need to tighten your barrel threshold; this debt is negligible only on textures where the solver was already betting nearly the full range anyway *(calibrate the frequency threshold)*. A simplification whose debt isn't written down becomes, two streets later, a leak whose source is forgotten — the study-desk version of the root error.

> **A full-range bet is the exception on the turn; and every simplification writes a debt to the next street — enter the debt in your ledger.**

### 27.14 The study workflow: the diagnostic ladder, the contrast pair, incubation, difficulty tuning


Stop staring at output you don't understand, get on the ladder: **One** — DERIVE the action from the strategy: which range is left exposed on which runout? **Two** — open the same line on a neighboring board: if the strategy holds, the mechanism is board-independent; if it changes, the reason is the advantage-gap between the two boards. **Three** — whose strength does this size leverage, whose gap does it target? **Four** — the incentive test (27.5).

When pulling a rule, don't look at a single node — build a minimal-contrast pair: change ONE variable (same board, different line / same line, different board / same size, different node), and read the delta. The "reason" isn't told to you by a node, it's told by the difference between two nodes; the other half of the contrast fills in 27.4's third line — the boundary condition.

Put a cap on a node you don't understand *(calibrate — in minutes)*; stop when the cap runs out. Stopping isn't giving up, it's incubation: come back a few days later, most locks open on their own. Read the gauge: confusion happens now and then; if it happens OFTEN, the problem isn't in the node, it's in the fundamentals — don't just stop studying, solve the node with a partner. Progress comes before perfection.

Tune the session goal to your skill level (an addition to 27.6): neither as easy as routine nor as hard as something seen for the first time — too easy produces drifting, too hard produces freezing. And the two-option rule: this work or nothing — sitting idle is allowed, there's no third option. Attention that can't find an escape door deepens.

### 27.15 Cheat Sheet


*(calibrate: session length, node-lock EV-loss threshold, mix frequencies, threshold percentages — all from your own run.)*

| Principle | Rule |
|---|---|
| **Stance** | Pull out the mechanism, don't copy; every rule = pattern + reason + BOUNDARY CONDITION — a boundary-less heuristic over-generalizes |
| **Bucket** | Study boards by texture-bucket (paired / monotone / connected / dry-high); ONE strategy per bucket, carve out only the sub-class that behaves differently |
| **Bluff counting** | Count value combos on the river, add bluffs up to the size's ratio — plain arithmetic, with your own hand; not from a ready-made table |
| **Node-lock** | TEST the simplification by EV loss *(calibrate)*: small loss → to the table, big loss → doesn't transfer; don't take someone else's claim as fact |
| **Self-check** | "What's the opponent's most profitable response to this?" — if the answer is degenerate, the strategy is wrong; test with incentive, not memorized MDF |
| **Session** | Short, time-boxed, a SINGLE predefined goal; heavy solver work before a session on a game day is BANNED — only on the morning of a day you're not playing |
| **Autopsy** | A single combo is misleading at a mixed node — third question: "What was my CLASS doing?" The lesson comes from the class level |
| **Reading** *(27.9)* | Read the threshold, not the frequency; on an aggressor flop start from the CHECK column, tie the boundary to a single card feature |
| **Arithmetic** *(27.10)* | Combo/1326 × inverse of range-width; the share of being beaten roughly doubles per street; rewind from the river |
| **Range map** *(27.11)* | Memorize the RANGE, not the strategy (draw/set/two-pair share per texture); firing big with more than half your range is not value |
| **Mixed** *(27.12)* | Mix = equal-EV = free decision → fix it against a rec + take the exploit; save balance only for the one who's counting; live, your watch is a tell |
| **Street** *(27.13)* | Full-range bet on the turn is the exception (study four buckets); every simplification writes a DEBT to the next street |
| **Workflow** *(27.14)* | Diagnostic ladder + minimal-contrast pair for what you don't understand; confusion budget/incubation; the two-option rule |

Four filters (27.8): **EV-source** ("allowed in the sim" ≠ good — does the opponent-response node the EV comes from exist in your pool?) · **Inevitability** (if the hand crushing me already has me stacked on every line, that branch drops → often means raise) · **3-bet identity** (polar-BIG if there's a flat, linear-SMALL if the flat is closed) · **Stack-band** (guarantee the depth-band your edge lives in by folding on a close decision).

### 27.2-EK On a multi-size river, pick the bluff candidate by the opponent's continuing range against the target size

27.2's bluff count is plain arithmetic for a single size; if you're using two different sizes on the river, each size produces a different CONTINUING (call) range in the opponent — against the big size the opponent mostly continues with a narrow/nutted range, against the small size with a wider/medium-strength range. When picking the bluff candidate, don't look at which value class your own hand carries — the opponent doesn't see your cards, and blocking your own value contributes nothing directly to EV. Instead look at whether the candidate blocks the opponent's call range AGAINST the target size while leaving the fold range unblocked: for the big size, write down the candidate that blocks the opponent's narrow/nutted calls; for the small size, the candidate that blocks the opponent's wide/medium-strength calls. Skip this match and the opponent's defense doesn't change (he can't see your combo selection, so he defends at the same frequency) — you pay the price: the bluff ends up blocking the opponent's folds instead of his calls at that size, fold equity drops, and the bluff doesn't do its job at the size it was aimed at.

**Rule:** In a multi-size river bluff, pick the blocker candidate by whether it blocks the opponent's call range against the target size while leaving the fold range unblocked — not by your own value composition.

### 27.9-EK Street addendum to the build order: the weight of the coverage step varies by street, zero on the river

18.1-EK gave the range build order: first value, then the size THAT value wants, then coverage, and bluffs last. This order stays fixed, but the WEIGHT of the coverage step isn't fixed across streets: on the flop coverage is the heaviest step because two more streets lie ahead, on the turn it lightens because only one street remains, and on the river coverage drops out ENTIRELY because no street remains — at that node all that's left is value, the bluffs that complete value, and protecting the check range you leave behind. Study time spent hunting for coverage on the river is wasted.

**Rule:** The range build order (18.1-EK) stays fixed; the weight of the coverage step is highest on the flop, lower on the turn, and zero on the river — skip the coverage step on the river.

## Chapter 28 — PKO / Bounty: Convert the Bounty to Chips, Protect Your Range

### 28.0 Core: convert the bounty to chips on EVERY decision


Recalculate the bounty's current chip-value on every single hand — do it in one hand and forget it in the next, and it's gone. In PKO, the cheapest way to raise your ROI is spending more time on preflop. A bounty converted to chips produces a **negative risk premium** against a player you cover (the inverse of B12): eliminating him earns you chips PLUS the bounty, so your call/aggression threshold against him DROPS.

> **In PKO, a player you cover carries a NEGATIVE premium for you: you play looser than chipEV to eliminate him. The mirror of ICM — there you price the cost of busting, here you price the gain of the bounty.**

### 28.1 The covering player widens VPIP — but don't take the solver's extreme flats


Bounty-aware solvers model the bounty as a premium and output flats far looser than the population would ever use (very weak suited hands that would normally never be flatted). Take the principle (widen) — don't take the specific loose combo. A live field doesn't reward that looseness.

### 28.1-EK The covering SB doesn't widen OOP, it TOUGHENS — flat becomes linear 3-bet


Root error: carrying 28.1's "the covering player widens VPIP" line over to OOP and, as the covering SB, flatting weak suited / offsuit Kx hands against the button. Mechanism: the button opens tighter against an SB that covers him (the covered-player reflex, 28.8); the equity of range-vs-range fringe suited hands drops, and at mid-deep stacks an OOP fringe flat doesn't stack anyone — the bounty wants "make a big hand and collect," and a fringe flat doesn't produce that. What changes: the flat share drops, the 3-bet share rises (calibrate); the 3-bet texture is LINEAR — chipEV's low-suited polar bluffs don't exist in PKO, because the button calls wide in position and bloating the pot with trash that folds to a raise doesn't hand you a bounty. Mirror: the covered SB in the same spot plays a limp-heavy stack and few 3-bets (28.25/28.26) — the two SBs' textures swap along the cover axis. The sizing note is independent of the bounty (B29/B32): at mid-depth, a big 3-bet plus a two-street plan; very deep, the OOP 3-bet shrinks — take the offsuit auto-folds first.

**Rule:** as the covering SB OOP, widen aggression, not VPIP — cut the fringe flat, open a linear 3-bet; the "I cover him → more hands" reflex is position-dependent.

### 28.2 Protect your wide flat/limp range with NUTS — don't be dead money


If there's someone behind you who can reopen/squeeze, put nuts like AA/KK/AK into your wide VPIP range so a squeeze can't punish you. This is the PKO exception to the B4.4 "3-bet or fold" reflex: you enter wide to hunt bounties, but the range still has to be protected.

### 28.2-EK Nut-protection is cover-conditional — flat AK if you cover the players behind you, 3-bet if they cover you


Root error: reading 28.2 as "always protect the wide flat with nuts," or as "3-bet AK against a wide-opening leader because I dominate him." Three parts. (1) A wide flat against a covering player who opens wide in position (calibrate) is correct — the issue isn't the bounty, it's range-vs-range; your flat against a wide opener is still the tight side.
