(2) That flat range is protected with AK/AQs/QQ+ ONLY IF you cover the blind behind you: a blind who sees a wide open plus a wide flat jams linearly; if his range has no strong hand it collapses toward a near-pure value-jam and you fold every time. If it does have strong hands, it's a dream spot: the blind jams, the opener folds, and you collect the bounty. If the players behind you cover YOU, there's no dream → AK reverts to a 3-bet, and the flat range narrows (calibrate). (3) 3-betting AK against a wide opener is not "pushing equity": his widening is suited trash, and it's the offsuit Ax you dominate that folds to your 3-bet — exactly the hands you don't want to run off. Flatting keeps that offsuit trash in the pot. Brake: even a nut-protected range mostly folds to two jams — no gambling with a suited connector just because "there are three bounties out there."

**Rule:** protect the wide flat with nuts (28.2), BUT the dream exists only if you cover the players behind you; if they cover you, move AK to a 3-bet.

### 28.3 If you cover, a LIMP-weighted VPIP


Limping risks fewer chips → play more hands → hunt more bounties. Raising only makes sense when it produces fold equity; with a very short/bountied stack behind you, a raise doesn't produce fold equity (very short stacks reopen far too often with a jam — calibrate), so limping dominates. In bounty-less ICM the leader's limp/iso is a separate engine — iso a mid-stack's limp with a suited connector, not offsuit trash (12.2-EK-4). The limp range is wide but protected (28.2). Band: mid-to-deep stack with a bountied short stack behind you; shallow HU-BvB is the opposite — jam, not limp (28.3-EK).

### 28.3-EK In shallow HU-BvB the bounty erases the limp-trap — the covering SB doesn't limp, it jams


Root error: carrying ICM's shallow BvB "limp-weighted" texture over to PKO — or reading 28.3 as "if you cover, limp at every depth." Mechanism: in ICM a shallow SB limps because the equity of the hands it would jam isn't enough, and it protects the top of its range with a limp-call-all-in trap. In PKO, for a covering SB the bounty closes that equity gap: against BB's standard calling range, raw equity plus the bounty pays for the jam → every hand "worth playing" goes to jam, and the trap slice becomes unnecessary. With no trap, the bottom of the range can't limp either (an unprotected limp folds to every iso) → the limp collapses, and the range is TIGHT but JAM-weighted: fewer hands than bounty-less, more all-ins (roughly the 12–20bb band, calibrate). Counter-intuitive, but the direction is clear. Two additions: (a) don't jam a dominating high-suited / Kx-Qx top — non-all-in raise instead, so BB's calls that you dominate stay in the pot (28.7); (b) the field, assuming "nobody jams a limp," widens from the bottom — that limp stack folds to a small iso (calibrate), it's free money. Band: 28.3's limp regime lives in the deeper, bountied-short-behind band; in shallow HU-BvB the opposite applies.

### 28.4 A very short stack whose "bounty ≈ its own stack" = an extreme isolation dynamic


Everyone builds their plan around isolating him. The short stack's POSITION determines your strategy:

- **If he's in the BB** (he closes the action, lowest chance of a reopen) → the players ahead of him can 3-bet more.
- **If he's SB/early position** (he can reopen with a jam) → the players ahead of him protect their flat range with nuts.

The isolation sizing depends on your own depth: if you're deep, "first-raise" big enough that the short stack can't reopen (deny the reopen); if you're shallow, min-raise (allow the short stack to reopen with a jam, and take it).

### 28.4-EK Mixed stacks behind → raise beats jam even when the jam is profitable


If everyone behind you is uniformly micro-short, fire the jam — everything except the very bottom of your range goes in (calibrate). But when short and mid stacks are MIXED behind you, don't jam just because "the jam is profitable"; raise instead. The shorts jam and you pay them off anyway; when a mid stack wakes up it folds its trash for free, and you play your strong hand postflop against it. Root error: memorising one number and never reading the stack distribution at the table.

**Rule:** the stack texture behind you — not your hand — picks the open type: jam behind a uniform micro-short field, raise when short and mid are mixed; you already pay off the shorts' jams, so don't let the mid stack fold its trash for free.

### 28.5 Mystery bounty = a preflop game / sandbox


Massive variance (a big roll is required), the population is clueless postflop. Main edge: a wide IP limp plus a protected range; people play the limp node badly. Bounty value CHANGES throughout the ITM run (it drops as the big bounties get pulled); the rough calculation = live bounty pool total ÷ remaining players.

### 28.6 Net premium: collapse the two premiums into one number


Root error: learning ICM and the bounty as two separate lessons and "feeling" them separately at the table. Keep one number: net premium = ICM premium − bounty discount; to measure the discount, convert your target's bounty to BB at the current exchange rate and compare it to the effective stack (calibrate). If the bounty has gotten close to the stack size, the net premium is deeply negative and you're in any-two territory; if the bounty has eroded to a few BB (calibrate), the hand is bounty-less ICM — the standard matrix applies unchanged. Read the sign correctly too: a negative premium is a CALLING premium, not a bluffing premium — the discount only exists when you're the covering player: there's no 3-bet-fold against a bountied short stack, because you always pay his jam at bounty-adjusted odds, but your bluff fold equity only drops against opponents who cover YOU. When you cover, do your widening linearly and with calls; save the bluff-jam for the bounty-less tournament.

### 28.6-EK The structural PKO discount: the premium drops for EVERYONE — deep at the FT the matrix approaches chipEV


28.6's bounty discount applies only to the covering player. There's a second, structural discount as well: part of the pool is a bounty pool and isn't distributed via ICM; a bust's total dollar cost still includes bounty equity, but the PREMIUM above chipEV arises only from the ICM'd share of the pool — since the bounty share is roughly chip-linear, it adds no premium → for the same stack distribution the premium drops noticeably compared to a bounty-less tournament — for the COVERED player too, even if he eliminates no one *(roughly by half; depends on the bounty share, calibrate)*. The direction holds: the covered player still pays a premium, just a smaller one; the covering player stacks both discounts on top of each other. Net premium = (ICM premium × structural multiplier) − bounty discount.

**Result at a deep PKO FT:** preflop range shapes mostly stay close to chipEV — including the 3-bet/flat/limp-raise texture *(shape and bb band: calibrate)*; there's no "I'm at the FT, tighten up" reflex. The deviation only opens up around a short, bountied stack; switch on your ICM reflex once your own bounty gets close to your stack. If a blind who covers you flats instead of 3-betting, widen your open — the reason the covered player is tight is 3-bet pressure.

**Future-game cuts both ways:** the solver doesn't count the covering gain after a double-up → at the margin, covering-player calls run a notch looser *(at the margin)*; but with a fat, bountied short stack at the table, pass on the marginal gamble that would extinguish your cover if you lose it (28.11) — "if I win, who do I cover; if I lose, who don't I cover any more; is there a bounty waiting?" (the fifth question to B24.2).

### 28.7 Cover direction chooses your preflop material


Root error: taking the same hand list to every all-in — the jamming range's texture flips depending on cover direction. If you're covered, your opponent will pay for your bounty regardless — linearize your jam: push the high card that dominates his wide calling range; throw away the suited connector that's buying a flip and the blocker-jam that folds out no one. If you're covering, the mirror flips: the short stack can't win your bounty, so he calls tight — he comes with high cards, and folds his good suited hands; now the suited connector is the one under the jam: the high cards in his tight calling range can't dominate a suited connector, so your equity stays live when called; the trash behind you, meanwhile, folds and leaves dead money — your jam collects exactly that dead money. On the 3-bet, tie the sizing not only to depth but also to cover direction: against an opener who covers you, a small sizing is free profit — go big; if you're covering, build small-linear (calibrate) — linear, but with the bottom end shifted: value drops down to include medium pairs, and the merge slice (KQ/AJ/suited Ax) goes to a flat instead of a 3-bet (28.8-EK). Composition wants direction too: never 3-bet a hand you'd fold to a jam — move suited aces to the call ledger, and build the 3-bet from hands you'll pay without hesitation once the short stack shoves.

### 28.8 The covered player's collapsed tree: either jam or call


Root error: the covered short stack saying "my risk premium is low, so I'll jam wide" — the direction is backwards. Your bounty lowers your opponents' calling threshold; once fold equity collapses, your jamming range should be even TIGHTER than at the bounty-less bubble (calibrate). The other blade of the scissors works in your favor: the ICM premium you pay when someone jams into you is below normal — your stack-off threshold is WIDE; the bounty takes the jam away from him, and gives you the call in return. The tree also collapses to two branches: as the covered player, a small 3-bet against a normally-wide-opening covering player is a donation, not a bluff (exception for an ultra-wide-opening leader: 28.8-EK) — nobody folds and leaves your bounty on the table — and setting a trap with a monster at a table where a squeeze can come from behind is the root error itself. Jam your top linearly, file the rest in the call ledger; the covered player doesn't have the luxury of a trap, he has a collection to make.

### 28.8-EK The covering player's 3-bet widens downward → the 4-bet jam threshold drops; exception: a small POLARIZED 3-bet against an ultra-wide leader


Two parts. **(A)** When you open as a bountied short stack and get 3-bet, reading it as "3-bet = QQ+/AK + a polar bluff" is wrong in PKO. A 3-bettor who covers you pulls his value down for the bounty: medium pairs (88/99) and the AJs class are now value 3-bets, ready to pay off your jam at bounty odds (calibrate); the KQ/AJ/suited Ax that gets merge-3-bet in bounty-less play slides to a flat instead. Result: a value-weighted but thin 3-bet range — JJ/TT is a 4-bet jam for you, not a folding hand; finding a call from 99 is part of the price. **(B)** 28.8's "a small 3-bet is a donation" applies to a covering player who opens at normal width. The donation requires the opponent to actually HOLD a hand that can pay for your bounty; a leader who enters with a large share of his opens (roughly 70%+, calibrate) auto-folds most of that range to a 3-bet, and his 4-bet jam is narrow and predictable. Jamming with a mid-good stack is also broken here: the gain is a few bb, the risk is your whole stack and your position. A small polarized 3-bet (~2x, calibrate) forces him into one of two errors — folding too much, or 4-bet-bluffing too much. Material: offsuit trash carrying an A-blocker that folds out (A7o/A5o type) — not a merge hand; fold when the jam comes. The requirement is threefold: an ultra-wide opener + a narrow 4-bet jam + a stack that makes the jam expensive.

### 28.9 The bounty isn't fixed: inflation, field, exchange rate


Root error: converting the bounty to chips once and carrying that figure fixed through the whole tournament. The bounty pool erodes slowly with the field; the same bounty is worth more chips in a late phase than in an early one (calibrate) — refresh the exchange rate on every major decision, because as the pool disperses, a chip's dollar value drops. Field size is the arbiter of the two premiums: in a huge field, late-stage, even the fattest bounty is a few BB (calibrate) — drop the term, play bounty-less ICM; in a small field the bounty is a serious fraction of the average stack — the net premium drops, and the bounty has a say. The threshold against a short stack's jam shifts too: the same bounty is bought at less risk, and the odds tilt in your favor — pull the calling threshold even lower than a flat-average calculation would say (calibrate). One check before you sit down: how many BB is the biggest bounty, and what fraction of the average stack is it?

### 28.10 The bubble paradox: your bounty erodes your own fold equity


Root error: the inference "my risk premium is low, so jam wide at the bubble." The very mechanism that lowers the premium — the bounty on your own head — widens your opponents' calling range; your fold equity erodes, and they pay you more than ever. The result is paradoxical but clear: a bountied short stack at the bubble jams TIGHTER than it would in a bounty-less tournament (calibrate). The marginal spot is justified only by the fact that after a double-up you'd cover extra players.

### 28.11 The quality of your cover: a position multiplier, a fragile status


Root error: treating cover as a scalar — counting how many players you cover. Cover is a product of position: a single covering player who is positioned behind you shrinks your opening range (calibrate); two covering players in the blinds barely shrink it at all, and you'll play the flop in position. The second quality test is thinness: if you cover the table by a hair, cover is a status you can lose — it evaporates on a single all-in and you drop to the side that pays the premium; don't ignore that just because the solver doesn't see future-game. In marginal cover, cut the thinnest jams and save full width for thick cover (calibrate); when bounties are shallow relative to the stacks, this whole debate is moot anyway.

### 28.11-EK Weight the loss of cover — it's a tiebreaker, not a decision


Root error: applying 28.11's "fragile cover" warning to every decision with a fixed weight — throwing away a clean +EV spot because "if I lose this call, I stop covering X," or, on the flip side, never counting the player you'd stop covering if you lost. Mechanism: the value of losing cover is multiplied by the actual probability of going stack-off with that player. If the bountied opponent is at a mid stack and the table is full, that all-in comes rarely → low weight; if the opponent has dropped into jamming range, the jam comes every orbit → high weight (calibrate). Second rule: the weight only operates on a BORDERLINE decision — on a near-equal-EV turn call/fold, "if I call I stop covering four players" tips it to a fold; at a clean +EV spot, losing cover is not a reason to fold. Procedure: (1) is the decision borderline? if not, forget about cover; (2) if it is, count the players you'd stop covering if you lost; (3) weight each one by the probability of going stack-off with them — short and bountied counts, deep doesn't. This is the calibration for the fifth question in 28.6-EK.

### 28.12 The cold-call gate and the handcuffed squeeze


Root error: starting the decision in a 3-bet pot from your own hand — look at the stacks first. The cold-call gate: don't enter unless you cover at least one of the opener or the 3-bettor; if you can't take anyone's bounty, the same hand is a fold, and if the gate is open, the hand speaks. On the flat decision, read the chain behind you: if the opener covers the likely squeezer behind you, the squeezer is handcuffed — no squeeze comes, you see the flop cheap, and your flat range widens (calibrate). Bluffing material comes from the same read: an opponent who covers you gives wide calls, no fold is coming — don't bluff with blocker-laden offsuit trash, bluff with suited-connected hands that turn into something on the flop; don't carry the polar-bluff reflex into the spot where fold equity is dead.

### 28.12-EK The re-jam gate: against the short stack's jam, the flatter between you — not the short stack — chooses your width


Root error: asking "I opened, someone flatted, the short jammed → is my hand good enough against the short stack?" — looking at the short stack. Your re-jam calls the flatter, not the short stack; the decision hangs on the cover relationship between you and the flatter. If the flatter covers you, he over-calls wide for your bounty (he won't let go of pairs or broadways) — your re-jam commits your whole stack against two ranges at once. If you cover the flatter, he calls tight or folds; your jam mostly stays HU against the short, and the short's bounty is already dead money. Flip the direction at the same node and the range changes end to end: if you're covered, the re-jam squeezes down to pairs plus suited broadways (calibrate) — low suited Ax is the worst material: the short stack's linear jam range is full of Ax, and the flatter's pairs crush it; a suited broadway flips against both a pair and an Ax. If you're covering, the re-jam approaches most of your opening range (calibrate) — if the flatter calls, you're also playing him at bounty odds.

**Rule:** when the short stack shoves, ask first "does the flatter cover me, or do I cover him?" — if you're covered, re-jam with pairs/suited broadways and fold suited Ax; if you're covering, re-jam wide, because the risk is the short stack's stack, not the flatter's.

### 28.13 The bounty is a dead ante: the limp regime


Root error: trying to "isolate" a short stack whose premium has grown to multiples of his own stack with a big raise — that bounty is a dead ante, and whatever sizing you choose, anyone holding half a decent hand gets a price. Change the regime instead: limp your entire playable range, embrace multiway pots, and pile onto the short stack in numbers when he shoves; as limps stack up behind you, widen your value-jam range (calibrate). There's a mirror threshold for your own bounty: while the premium is a small fraction of your stack, limping is fine; as the premium approaches your stack, limping just eats a jam every time — cut it; the moment the two are equal it's pure jam-or-fold (calibrate), and even in late position under cover, raising is a jam-magnet — limp or shove directly. One monotone band: as the premium approaches the stack, limping decreases and the line goes to jam-or-fold; if the premium clearly EXCEEDS the stack and the stack is also short, the same magnet flips to what you actually want and the line shifts to min-raise — the limp draws a small iso and flats stay cheap at 1bb, the min-raise forces the isolator into a jam and inflates the dead money left by folding flats; a raise-fold doesn't exist in either band, calibrate the transition boundary (28.23). If you're on the other side of the table, know the antidote: a limping range isn't trash, it's suited and sticky — a small iso folds out no one, bet big if you're going to punish it (calibrate); if opponents are betting a small iso into limps, the limp is free money for you — open up the frequency.

### 28.14 Multiway all-in: write the bounties into the pot first


Root error: building the odds in a multiway all-in from chips alone. Procedure: convert only the bounties of the jams YOU cover to BB at the current exchange rate — you can't win the bounty of a stack that covers you, it doesn't enter the calculation (calibrate); write the bounties you CAN win into the pot as dead money, and calculate the odds after that — the spot you thought was "pay-win" is actually "pay, plus win the bounties you're eligible for," and that difference moves medium suited hands from fold to call. The question in hand selection isn't "is my hand strong," it's "how many ranges am I against": against a single range, a high card pays off and you throw away the suited connector; against more than one range it flips — offsuit high cards and weak Ax get dominated constantly, while suited broadway/pairs and suited-connectors get to breathe — low suited Ax is the worst class (calibrate). One exception to memorize: if a giant covering jam lands on top of micro stacks, strip only the micros' RANGES out of the equity calculation (price your hand against the giant's range alone) — but still keep their chips and the bounties you can win on them as dead money in the pot; if you beat everyone, those bounties are yours. In the equity calculation, high card still wins.

### 28.14-EK The multiway all-in direction rule: every extra COVERED jam widens the over-call, an extra jam that covers you narrows it — the ICM discount stacks in sequence


Root error: the reflex "a third all-in came in, the spot got heavier, so I'll tighten up." The direction depends on the cover relationship: if the second and third shovers are shorter than you, your over-call range WIDENS — the short's equity threshold is low, his range is weak, and his bounty plus stack are dead money in the pot; this works in favor of 28.14's "how many ranges am I against" question (wide at the margin, calibrate). If that same jam covers you, the opposite: the over-call narrows noticeably — you'd be committing your stack to a range whose bounty you can't collect. The mirror for the player in the middle: if you re-jam over the short's jam, you put your bounty AND the short's bounty into a single pot against an opener behind you who covers you — the calling threshold collapses under two bounties, and your fold equity vanishes; as a covered mid-stack, stay on the call/fold axis instead of re-jamming (calibrate). Second layer, ICM: in the same spot, the first shover's range barely changes (he has fold equity), the covered re-jammer narrows a bit, and the over-caller narrows the most (calibrate) — even away from the money.

**Rule:** in a multiway all-in, count direction first (covered extra jam = widen, covering extra jam = narrow), then role: the jammer pays the least ICM, the over-caller pays the most — trim the chipEV range one notch on an over-call.

### 28.15 The micro stack's fold option flips your jamming right


Root error: the memorized line "there's a micro stack at the table, everyone's squeezed for the pay jump, so I'll jam." If the micro is a forced all-in, the pay jump is free for everyone and mid-stacks call you comfortably — cut the jam, narrow your range. If the micro can still fold (calibrate), the balance flips: an opponent who'd otherwise pay you is pushed toward calling tighter, because losing would hand the pay jump to the micro — but this "tighter call" isn't absolute; it only kicks in once the pay-jump/ICM risk exceeds your bounty premium. Since the player who covers you would also win your bounty, your right to jam is INVERSELY proportional to the size of your bounty — the bigger your bounty, the wider they call you. The first question in the decision tree is still not stack size, but the option: can the micro still fold?

### 28.16 The structure sheet prices the mystery


Root error: playing every mystery bounty tournament at the same aggression — the price of the envelope is set by the structure sheet, not by the draw. Read three items: when the phase starts, what fraction of the pool goes to bounties, and whether there's an overlay — the same buy-in can run average envelope value apart by multiples (calibrate); before the phase starts there's no envelope, and an elimination pays nothing — play the pre-phase portion like a normal tournament, and switch on bounty aggression at the phase gate. Then read the drawing regime: if envelopes are opened at the end, the expected bounty is fixed in DOLLARS until the finish, not in chips — as the pool gets paid out, a chip's dollar value drops, so refresh the exchange rate (28.24); if they're opened live, watch the board — as big envelopes get drawn the remaining average drops, as small ones get swept up it rises, and with no drawing at all it doesn't move from its spot. Late registration is the same price-reading exercise: in a normal bountied tournament you pay full price into an already-eroded pool — don't sit if the field has eroded past a certain point (calibrate); in a mystery, if the envelope phase hasn't started, the pool is intact and the penalty is largely erased.

### 28.17 Mystery ranges: who is carrying the odds


Root error: the memorized line "there's a bounty out there, so widen" — the side that widens is the side the odds have swung toward, not automatically you. The bounty doesn't hand you a rule, it hands you a question: whose bounty, and large relative to whose stack? If your own bounty has grown close to your own stack, your fold equity is dead and everyone calls you wide: narrow the jam, build it high-card-heavy — whatever the table odds say, there's no any-two jam. In a multi-way spot, simplify the equation the same way: find the one opponent whose money actually matters, reduce the spot to him, and price your range against him.

### 28.19 The bounty effect lives on the flop; on the turn/river it fades unless the stack is committed


Root error: reapplying the cover map on every street in a deep pot — cutting every barrel because you're covered, growing every barrel because you're covering. Mechanism: the covering player continues wider on the flop (chasing draws, hunting the nuts), so he arrives at the turn with more auto-folds already baked in; the covered player, despite his own reluctance to bluff, barrels at a normal frequency because that math of folds is already accounted for. The mirror also holds: the covering player's barreling appetite is high, but the covered player continued narrow on the flop, so his turn range is dense → there are no folds left to find. The two forces cancel each other out — but only on turn/river barrels where the post-bet SPR stays high (~2+, calibrate) and doesn't COMMIT the stack: there, bet frequency, sizing, and the bluff-catch threshold are nearly identical in both directions *(deviation of a few points — calibrate)*. In the book's 30–60bb baseline, a turn barrel after a flop bet-call mostly pulls the SPR down to around ~1 or below (about ~1.5 at 60bb) (calibrate) — meaning the turn bet is effectively the stack-committing node, and there is no fading there: the bounty keeps entering the pot odds, and its weight grows as the SPR drops — the covering player bluff-catches the covered player's jam a notch wider, the covered player trims his bluff-jam a notch, and keeps his value-jam standard or a notch thin (since he's paid wide) *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** load the entire PKO postflop adjustment onto the flop c-bet / flop-defense node (28.20–28.22); if the post-bet SPR stays high (a deep pot), play turn and river at your bounty-less standard — don't "reapply" the cover map there, it's already baked into the ranges. The remaining difference on turn/river lives at the stack-committing nodes (which, at 30–60bb, is mostly the turn after a flop bet-call): on a clean runout the covering player thins his value-jam a notch and pays the covered player's jam wide; the covered player trims his bluff-jam and keeps his value-jam standard or a notch thin *(calibrate)* — he folds to the covering player's jam because of the hand, not the bounty. There's no postflop bounty solver; these are boundary-condition principles, not solver rules.

### 28.19-EK Polarized river sizing in PKO: JAM or check — and shrink value against the covering player's collapsed river range


Root error: carrying a bounty-less tournament's polarized river texture (a big non-all-in + a blocked small bet + "leave a few bb behind") into PKO. Three mechanisms. (1) As the covered player on a polar river, a big sizing is mostly a JAM — your value (paid wide for the bounty, so it includes even a plain pair+) goes to jam, and your bluff has to go to jam too for fold equity; the pool in PKO can't sustain a call-vs-jam balance. Depth threshold: jam up to a mid stack, above that a big non-all-in (calibrate); the more the bounty matters, the higher the threshold. (2) The "leave the last few bb behind" trick gives a bluff-catcher a bad price in theory, but in PKO the opponent's bounty odds shift and the pool's response randomizes — jam instead; adjust your bluff frequency to the bounty, not the sizing (at the margin). (3) The covering player c-bets the flop wide for the bounty (28.20) → the range that arrives at the river via check-check is WEAKER than the bounty-less one; as the covered player, jam standard with your nuts, and one size smaller with strong-but-not-nut value — you get called wider from that weak range (calibrate).

**Rule:** covered player on a polar river → jam-or-check, no sizing games; only shrink value sizing against the covering player's collapsed river range.

### 28.20 The covering player's flop: IP is a range-bet (not a bluff), OOP continues for nut-potential


**IP:** a negative premium means "push your equity into the pot more" — check-backs shift to range-bets, small sizing shifts to medium, and to big sizing once the equity advantage is clear *(the steps: calibrate)*. This is NOT an increase in bluffing: the covered opponent's range has no bounty incentive, and folds normally; what's increasing is the range-bet frequency and thicker value — three streets of thin value with a hand that would be checked back bounty-less. The question isn't "check back or not," it's "which sizing"; medium sizing is the default, even on a dry high-card board *(calibrate)*. When a check-raise comes, the tree is bet-call/fold: the line that protects your wide high-card-plus-backdoor continues is a call, not a 3-bet — save the 3-bet for the nuts.

**OOP (BB):** the continuances you add aren't just a pair, they're lines that can turn into the nuts — a gutshot, a backdoor flush/straight, and the pair-less high cards that carry them. The bounty only gets collected if you make a hand that puts your opponent stack-off: fold less, check-raise more, jam more with strong hands/combo draws (a combo draw that gets check-called bounty-less goes to the stack here *(threshold: calibrate)*), and cut down on slowplaying. Filter: "can this become the nuts?" — tie the bounty to a hand with a draw, not to a bluff-catcher. See 28.21 for the covered side.

### 28.21 Covered IP: nut advantage crushes equity advantage — range-betting mostly disappears, accept the bet-fold


Root error: as the covered player, feeling your equity advantage because you opened tight, and firing a range-bet on a low-paired / low-connected board. The covering OOP player check-raises very wide on these boards — backdoor-carrying suited hands, two high cards plus a backdoor, medium pairs — because the road to the bounty runs through there; the covered IP player, against that check-raise, falls into bet-fold with most of his range, and folds even more to a narrower check-raise than usual *(the fold-rate gap: calibrate)*. Even with a large equity advantage, the betting frequency mostly DROPS.

**Rule:** as the covered IP player, ask "who has the nuts" before you ask about equity; if your opponent has the nuts, raise your check-back rate *(calibrate)*, keep your betting polarized, drop the small range-bet in most spots and keep it only at the margin *(calibrate)*; on a high/dry board (A/K-high, where the nut range is narrow and sits with you), bring the range-bet back, and on a medium/connected board keep the check-back. When a check-raise comes, accept the bet-fold — don't force an offsuit broadway into bet-call. What gets cut is the bluff, not the value (21.3-EK): lower the bluff volume, keep the value, and price semi-bluffs by equity. As the covering OOP player, open up your check-raise frequency on these same boards — know your target: the covered IP player folds, the covering IP player doesn't.

### 28.21-EK The covered player's bluff doesn't die, it changes address — not a hero-fold but an auto-fold


Root error: reading 21.3-EK/28.21 as "I'm covered → no bluffing." Mechanism: the covering player's wide payment for the bounty only works when he actually HAS a hand; without a hand, the bounty can't make him pay. If a very-wide-opening covering player fires a range-bet on a locked-down low-to-medium board (an A74-dry type), most of his range is an auto-fold — bounty motivation can't hold K9o against a check-raise; you're not looking for a hero-fold, you're getting an auto-fold. As the covered BB, you can build a wide small check-raise there (a bit above min-raise, calibrate); the material is trash that does NOT block the hands your opponent would fold (doesn't hold the board cards or Kx/Qx). The same line dies on a connected board (T96, 985 types) — the wide range continues there with a pair/draw, bounty motivation kicks in, and 28.21 applies exactly as written. Flip side: an opponent trying to isolate you to collect your bounty doesn't expect aggression from you — he reads a short stack's check-raise for real.

**Rule:** as the covered player, lower your bluff volume (21.3-EK) but don't zero it out; move the bluffs you keep to boards where your opponent's range does NOT have a hand — permission comes from board texture, not the cover map.

### 28.22 Range texture overrides cover direction — who bets where


Don't apply the memorized line "the covering player bets more" independent of the board. The cover adjustment reshapes ranges preflop: the covered player opens tight and high-card-heavy, the covering player opens wide and suited-connector-heavy; the covering player, as a deep caller, flats wide "like a BB." That texture can flip the cover direction on the flop:

- **A/K-high board:** favors the tight-covered range → the covered player range-bets small there; if his nut advantage is deep, lower the frequency and grow the sizing (polarize), and trim the bluff share because you're the covered player (the ICM/PKO cut) — it's your own risk position, not your bluff rate, that shrinks the polar sizing; the covering player's suited-connectors have bricked here → checking increases.
- **Low/medium-connected board:** the exact opposite: the covering player's wide range connects → range-bet, going all the way to a flop-jam with fragile pairs and backdoor-carrying high cards (folding out the short covered caller's high-card/suited-A trash); the tight-covered player is close to a check on the same board.
- **Stab:** the covering wide caller stabs polar-big, the covered dense caller stabs frequent-small *(calibrate)*.

**Rule:** two questions in order — (1) how did my range connect with this board, (2) cover direction. (1) is the main driver of frequency; (2) is a second-order adjustment to sizing and frequency — it trims the bluff share for the covered player, opens it for the covering player (PKO). "I cover him" doesn't grant a right to bet; your range does. If ranges shifted because of cover, texture dominates; if they stayed the same, the cover effect is clean (28.20).

### 28.23 If the bounty exceeds the stack, MIN-RAISE, not jam — and in the BB, pay a short stack's jam wide with Ax


**As the short stack (covered, bounty exceeds stack):** the reflex "everyone pays me → jam" makes the pot multiway — everyone re-jams/over-calls at bounty odds, and your equity gets split. A min-raise risks the same money — a raise-fold barely exists, you're folding to almost nothing — but it changes the chain: flats come from behind, someone jams, the flats fold; that money stays dead in the pot, and you're playing a bigger pot against fewer opponents. The range is mostly min-raise; the solver may leave a mixed slice at jam — pairs that hold up well in a multiway pot lean toward jam, Ax/high cards that want isolation lean toward min-raise *(which hand classes / the split: calibrate)*. Two parameters are needed TOGETHER: bounty > stack AND a short stack *(how many multiples / the upper band: calibrate)*. One monotone band (28.13): as the premium approaches the stack, limping decreases and the line goes to jam-or-fold; if the premium clearly EXCEEDS the stack and the stack is also short, the line shifts to min-raise — a raise-fold doesn't exist in either band, calibrate the transition boundary; if the stack isn't short, it's a normal open (a raise-fold exists), not a jam. This regime doesn't exist in bounty-less ICM: with no bounty odds, the multiway over-call problem doesn't arise, and jam (for fold equity) stays the default for a short stack; only the isolation dynamic is similar (the big stack isolates, the mid stack pulls back for fear of the pay jump). Same regime as 28.13's "a limp eats a jam → cut it; a raise is a jam-magnet" line: the limp draws a small iso and flats stay cheap at 1bb, the min-raise forces the isolator into a jam and inflates the dead money left by folding flats — the magnet here is exactly what you want; in the bounty > stack AND short band, this min-raise replaces 28.13's jam-or-fold line, and the EV difference is small but consistent *(calibrate)*. This is the one exception to 28.8's "either jam or call" tree for the covered player: in this band, the covered short stack's min-raise isn't a raise-fold, it's the chain-changing variant of the jam — the fold branch still doesn't exist; 28.8's "a small 3-bet is a donation" line stands unchanged (this is an OPEN).

**As the BB (while covering):** how wide you pay an Ax depends on the short stack's JAM TEXTURE — read the texture first. If the short is splitting his range as above (playable Ax has gone to min-raise, the jam is squeezed down to medium pairs plus select suited hands), a weak Ax is not dominated against that jam: against a pair, if your kicker is above the pair it's ~a flip, below it you're clearly behind *(calibrate)*; against a suited hand you're ahead; the bounty is added to the pot → pay the Ax wider than normal — heads-up, bounty odds overwhelmingly crush the kicker; splitting by kicker only applies when the pot is multiway (an over-caller behind you) or the bounty is close to the stack but small *(calibrate)*. Don't break the "jam without Ax" assumption without having SEEN it at showdown: if the short stack's jam hits every Ax (a narrow-but-Ax-inclusive jam), the standard defense holds exactly as is *(at the margin — solver

### 28.24 The envelope's chip price folds with the field — even if the dollar amount is fixed


Root error: figuring out the bounty's dollar value and assuming its chip value is fixed too. The pool shrinks as it gets paid out with every elimination, while the chips in play stay fixed → a chip's dollar value drops → a fixed-dollar bounty is worth more chips. The driver isn't the number of players left, it's the PERCENTAGE of the field remaining: every time the field halves, the envelope's chip-value jumps a notch (a single chip's dollar value drops) *(the notch thresholds and multipliers: calibrate)*; in a live drawing, big envelopes being drawn slows this down, and small ones being swept up speeds it up. In a big field, the same percentage is further from the FT → by the time you reach the FT, the envelope is worth more starting stacks *(calibrate)* — in starting-stack terms; in BB terms it's a separate question: if the blinds are climbing fast, 28.9's "a few BB" warning still holds *(calibrate)*.

**Procedure (on the break, four steps):** (1) bounty $ = remaining bounty pool ÷ remaining players (subtract already-drawn envelopes in a live drawing); (2) remaining total prize = remaining normal pool + remaining bounty pool; (3) chip $ = remaining total ÷ total chips in play; (4) bounty chips = (1) ÷ (3), divide by BB. Rough at the table: "what percentage of the field is left?" → read it off the starting-stack table you worked out ahead of time for your own structures *(calibrate)*. 28.16's "do the calculation once" is about DOLLARS; this is the mechanism behind 28.9's "more chips in the late phase" principle. The same effect exists in mystery PKO, just fainter as the bounty grows.

### 28.25 The limp pump: the covered player's limp-reraise isn't a trap, it's a pump


Root error: applying 28.8's "trapping is the root error" line to limps too — or carrying B33.5's population rule "narrow the value limp-reraise" over against an aggressive opponent who covers you. Mechanism: an aggressive opponent who covers you doesn't limp-behind your limp, he isolates — to stay heads-up, drop the SPR, and hunt your bounty; his iso range is linear and wide (suited hands, pairs, offsuit aces, broadways). Bounty motivation makes him CALL your big limp-reraise (a sizing that drops the SPR to one or two streets, calibrate) wide: medium suited connectors, the AJ/ATs class, medium pairs don't fold. Result: instead of a small multiway raise-call-call pot, you get a low-SPR heads-up pot against a range you dominate. The material is the top of your range — the TT+/AQ+ class, mostly as a limp-reraise; not "a slice of the premium hands" the way it is in chipEV (calibrate). The band is wide, roughly 15bb up to a deep stack (calibrate), independent of position. The trigger is mandatory: the opponent is LABELED aggressive and covers you. Against a calm covering player who limps behind, a limp is only a cheap flop, not a pump — if no iso comes, shut the line down. There's no bounty in B33's chipEV BvB (33.6); this is that layer's PKO arm.

**Rule:** as the covered player, play the top of your range against an aggressive covering player with a limp-reraise, not a raise — your bounty widens the opponent's iso and his call for you.

### 28.26 In the covered SB, B33's layers shift: the covering BB doesn't jam, he isolates


Root error: carrying B33.2/33.3's chipEV layers over unchanged once the bounty direction flips. Four shifts. (1) You don't 3-bet-jam a deep BB raise from an opponent who covers you — he flats in position, wanting to make a big hand and take your bounty; his 3-bet is nothing but the top of his range plus a trash bluff. Raise-fold fear drops → strong suited hands (the KQs/QJs/JTs class) mostly RAISE. (2) BB's iso widens for the bounty: the weak Ax that gets checked bounty-less now isolates → strong offsuit aces (AJ/AQ/AK) and pairs' limp-jam stop being B33.2's blocker semi-bluff and become semi-VALUE — it finds calls from the Ax hands you dominate; the limp-jam also protects the whole limp range from an iso. (3) The non-all-in limp-reraise slice is semi-value (the A9o/Q8s type): it gets calls from worse and doesn't fold out better — it's not B33.3's blocker-bluff cap. (4) A top-of-range (AA–JJ) raise is BIGGER than chipEV sizing (calibrate): BB pays wide in position, and your bounty is on the table — you set the price. Weak offsuit broadway is a limp-call; it doesn't push equity, it's not a raise.

**Rule:** as the covered SB, re-ask "who jams, who isolates" along the bounty axis — write strong suited hands to a raise, strong offsuit hands to a limp-jam, and the top of the range to a big raise.

### 28.18 Cheat sheet


*(calibrate: refresh the bounty→BB exchange rate on every major decision; all thresholds come from your own field.)*

| Situation | Rule |
|---|---|
| **Net premium** | One number: ICM premium − bounty discount; the discount applies only to the covering player. A negative premium is a CALLING premium, not a bluffing premium — do your widening linearly and with calls |
| **If you're covering** | The threshold DROPS: a limp-weighted wide VPIP, protect your range with NUTS (AA/KK/AK); against a short stack, a suited-connector jam is gold — a tight calling range can't dominate it |
| **If you're covered** | The tree collapses to two branches: jam or call. Jam TIGHT (fold equity is dead), stack-off threshold WIDE; a small 3-bet is a donation (exception: a small polarized 3-bet against an ultra-wide leader, 28.8-EK), a trap is the root error — but against an aggressive covering player, pump the top of your range with a limp-reraise (28.25) |
| **The bounty isn't fixed** | The same bounty is worth more chips in a late phase — refresh the exchange rate. Late phase in a huge field: bounty is a few BB → drop the term, play bounty-less ICM |
| **The bubble paradox** | Your bounty widens your opponents' calling range → fold equity erodes: a bountied short stack at the bubble jams TIGHTER than it would bounty-less *(calibrate)* |
| **Cover quality** | Cover × position: a single positioned coverer behind you shrinks your range, two coverers in the blinds don't. Hair's-breadth cover is fragile — cut the thin jams |
| **Cold-call gate** | Don't enter unless you cover the opener or the 3-bettor. If the opener covers the squeezer, the squeeze is handcuffed → the flat widens |
| **Dead-ante limp** | Don't isolate a short stack with a bloated bounty with a raise — everyone gets a price. Limp, embrace multiway, pile on in numbers when he jams; as your own premium approaches your stack, limping decreases, and once equal it's pure jam-or-fold; if the premium clearly EXCEEDS the stack and the stack is also short, the line shifts to min-raise (the magnet you actually want) — a raise-fold doesn't exist in either band, calibrate the transition boundary (28.13/28.23) |
| **Multiway all-in** | Write only the bounties YOU cover into the pot as dead money. Against multiple ranges, suited broadways/pairs and suited-connectors get to breathe, offsuit high cards and low suited Ax get suffocated; every extra COVERED jam widens the over-call, an extra covering jam narrows it (28.14-EK) |
| **The micro's fold option** | The first question isn't stack size, it's the option: can the micro still fold? If it's a forced all-in, the pay jump is free for everyone → cut the jam; your right to jam is INVERSELY proportional to your bounty |
| **Mystery** | The structure sheet prices the envelope (phase start / bounty share / overlay); play the pre-phase portion like a normal tournament. If your bounty has gotten close to your stack, there's no any-two jam — narrow it, high-card-heavy |

*Root-error link: PKO's wide VPIP INVITES the root error — entering wide and falling into a bloated pot with an unprotected range. Guard: enter wide, but (a) protect it with nuts, (b) postflop it's still "one pair = bluff-catcher."*

## Chapter 29 — Deep Stack and High-SPR: The Root Error's Most Expensive Band

### 29.0 Diversify — but flat is still widest (B4.7 holds)


At depth, the range doesn't WIDEN, it gets MIXED: you play the same hand across different lines (flat / 3-bet) and become unreadable on each line. B4.7 stays true — the flat range is still the widest; the "widening" happens MAINLY through strong hands moving from the 3-bet band into a mix; 200bb+ implied odds add some speculative hands (small pairs, suited connectors/gappers) to the flat to a limited extent *(calibrate)* — but this is not VPIP inflation. Which line each hand falls into (pure or mixed) *(calibrate)*; the total 3-bet range still stays value-weighted (per-hand mix is low frequency). B4's multiplier-based fixed 3-bet table collapses at this depth.

### 29.1 Putting medium pocket pairs in the 3-bet range — low-board coverage


When a low board comes in a deep 3-bet pot, if you have no pocket pair the board can't be played → the opponent strips you with a lead. Include medium pocket pairs in the 3-bet mix (which band *(calibrate)*); a preflop range-gap cascades into postflop: a nitty 3-bet → forced to check strong hands on a bad flop → gets scorched on later streets. **Preflop diversity is the load-bearing pillar.**

### 29.2 At high SPR, stack-off thresholds collapse


Top pair/overpair = pot-control/check, not stack-off; check-raises are polar (semi-bluff + nuts) → the pot explodes on the turn/river. Result: against a good opponent, IP **c-bets less** (don't put the middle of your range into a polar check-raise); against a weak opponent, **c-bet more** (they can't find deep check-raises).

> **The higher the SPR, the earlier one pair becomes a bluff-catcher (the root error). Don't force an overbet on thin value or in read-less spots — but with a nut advantage, geometric overbet is deep's PRIMARY weapon (getting 200bb in wants early and big sizing).**

### 29.3 Deep check-raise value comes from equity-driven hands


Pair+FD, combo draws — NOT thin one-pair (deep, you can't get three streets of value from it). Slow-playing sets = not a trick, it's range-protection because part of the range can't withstand turn pressure — but the protection is the RESULT, the reason is EV: if the hand that would pay the bet is gone and the opponent will bluff into a check, checking wins more (32.6-EK-2, 26.2-EK-3); against a station that won't bluff into a check, betting comes back.

### 29.4 Deep two-pair defense: what protects you isn't a pocket pair, it's a clean two-pair


Deep, what protects you isn't a bluff-catcher pocket pair, it's a two-pair that doesn't complete a straight / doesn't run into a cooler. On the turn/river, ask "is this two-pair clean?" — is it a disconnected structure that doesn't hand out cards *(calibrate)*.

### 29.5 Deep exploit: float → turn check-through → river stab


Call (float) a weak/nitty deep opponent's flop c-bet; when the turn goes check-check and the opponent defended very tight preflop (no medium-hand check-calls → prone to overfolding on the river), a small IP stab on the river (a delayed stab) over-performs against a poorly-constructed defense. *(calibrate — general principle; no specific hand is being copied.)*

### 29.6 Cheat sheet


*(calibrate: which hand is pure/mix on which line, the medium-pair band, the definition of "clean two-pair" — from your own solver.)*

| Topic | Rule |
|---|---|
| **Preflop** | The range doesn't WIDEN, it MIXES — flat is still widest; strong hands add to the flat/3-bet mix, implied-odds speculative hands add in a limited way *(calibrate)* |
| **3-bet construction** | Put medium pocket pairs in the mix — without low-board coverage, the opponent strips you with a lead; a preflop gap cascades into postflop |
| **One pair / sizing** | SPR>8: top pair/overpair = pot-control, NOT stack-off. Overbet only with a nut advantage — geometric overbet is deep's PRIMARY weapon |
| **C-bet** | LESS against a good opponent (don't put the middle of the range into a polar check-raise); MORE against a weak opponent (they can't find a deep check-raise) |
| **Check-raise** | From equity-driven hands: pair+FD, combo draw — not thin one-pair; set slow-play = range protection, not a trick |
| **Defense** | What protects you isn't a pocket pair, it's a CLEAN two-pair — turn/river question: "is this two-pair clean?" *(calibrate)* |
| **Exploit** | Against a nitty deep opponent: float the flop c-bet → turn check-check → small IP stab on the river (over-performs against overfolding) |

*Root-error link: deep play is the most expensive version of the root error — stacking off with one pair at high SPR commits the most chips to the most wrong place. Guard: at SPR>8, the default for top pair/overpair is pot-control.*

## Chapter 30 — Micro Stack 4-12bb: Jam/Fold Refinements and the Uneven-Table Overlay

### 30.0 Opening at 4-12bb = JAM or fold (no small-RFI)


At this depth there is no fine-sizing open chart — when you open, you either jam or you fold. Small pairs (22-66) are among the DEEPEST-living JAMs of this band *(calibrate the depth)*, they are not DEFENDED. Fold equity + live equity when called are both real; taking the jam for "polarized spew" is an MTT-reflex error.

### 30.0-EK "No small-RFI" is a covered-table assumption — if the players behind are shorter than you, at ~12bb raise-fold lives on the borderline


30.0's "4–12bb opening = jam or fold" rule was written under the assumption that the table covers you. If the players behind are markedly SHORT (you are the one covering), at ~12bb a small raise-fold and raising (not jamming) with the AJs/AQs class hold on at the borderline *(calibrate)*: the short player's jam is his entire stack and it meets a wide call (with your remaining stack you pay off most jams) → his fold equity is low → the rejam narrows, the small raise-fold gets room to breathe. If the players behind are deep, the reverse: the rejam costs them only ~12bb effective, the reshove widens — jam-or-fold hardens even further; the "a deep table prefers the call" zone at ~20bb (B33.6) closes here, there is no call option. The exception belongs to the top end of the band; at 4–8bb the doctrine stands as is. The covering player rejams comfortably, the covered player carries the premium — the same direction as B33.6's effective-stack mechanism.

### 30.0-EK-2 Footnote: under ICM, deep opponents may trim their rejams against a short opener somewhat because of the premium among themselves — borderline


30.0-EK says "if the players behind are deep, the reshove widens → jam-or-fold hardens"; solver-derived, it stands. Live intuition whispers the opposite: "I open at 15bb, the two aggressive deep stacks on my left can't rejam me comfortably — the risk premium between themselves squeezes their 3-bet-calls → I open wide; if everyone were 15bb the leverage would be theirs." Under ICM there is a share of this: the deep opponent risks the rejam not against you but against the other deep stack behind him (22.11's collision premium works for him too) → the rejam can come a tick narrower *(borderline — calibrate; observe, don't memorize)*. But in chipEV and when the antes are big, 30.0-EK outweighs it: the rejam costs them ~12bb effective, cheap. Rule: as the short opener at a deep table the default is jam-or-fold; try the small raise-fold only under the conditions ICM on + the players behind cover each other + you have SEEN that rejams are rare. The deep side's rule (call the short jam with ≥~22bb, not rejam) is in 22.10-EK; the 30.3 bridge is there.

### 30.1 Facing a raise or more: jam-or-fold, NOT flat


Fringe hands (Q9s/J9s/QTo-type middle-broadway/weak-suited borderline hands) at this depth are **jam-or-fold**, not CALL — at ~10-12bb the re-jam over a min-raise carries real fold equity; at 4-8bb the jam works on live equity + dead money, no fold equity is expected. Calling off a large part of the micro stack with fringe "to see a flop" is exactly the classic error the jam/fold doctrine prevents.

> **On a micro stack you are "committed to everything," but that does not mean "call to see a flop" — take your commitment as a JAM: fold equity + live equity work together; the flat-call gives up fold equity entirely, and realizes your live equity badly with a short stack.**

### 30.1-EK If fold equity is zero (villain is priced in), FLAT, not jam — a hand that needs to hit can't win without hitting; don't fold to one jam, fold to two


The root error: the "short SB, an open came → jam or fold" reflex at ~7bb. The mechanism: ~7bb SB, HJ opened: if you jam, the pot is ~11bb and villain pays ~5 → no hand folds, fold equity is ZERO (at 10bb it exists — no call there, jam). With no FE, jamming "needs-to-hit" hands like QJs/98s/suited K-x only runs a flip against the opener's range; the flat instead (a) buys the same equity cheaper, (b) pulls the BB into the pot with overcalls you dominate — when you hit, you win from both. The tree: jam = the top + pairs (live equity, no FE needed); flat = small pairs, suited connectors/Kx, as wide as the jam *(calibrate)*; if the BB jams alone, almost never fold (A7o borderline); if BB jams + HJ calls, everything but pairs folds (dead against two ranges). The same engine at ~5bb SB against an early-position open: JTs/Q9s flat (the opener never folds), the BB overcalls dominated, and when you hit you collect from both sides.

**Rule:** not "I'm committed → jam" but "can villain fold?" — if no, flat + the fold-to-two-jams rule; if yes (≥~10bb), 30.1 as written. This doesn't touch 30.6's BB rule — that seat closes the action.

### 30.2 In this band the BB defends wide against a min-raise — but defense = a jam/fold decision


With no deep reverse-implied penalty, the BB's price is good; but "defense" here too is read on the jam-or-fold axis (re-jam with suitable hands, fold the rest), not street-by-street flat-call postflop. *(calibrate — the threshold depends on sizing.)*

### 30.2-EK Short BB against a deep opener's min-raise — the price reflex is a trap: because the opener HAS a jam range, his min-raise is polar-strong


The root error: "I'm 10bb, the price against a min-raise is good, SPR is low, I defend wide." With a short BB across from him, the deep BTN's min-raise is not wide-merged as in chipEV: the opener has a jam range (it takes the middle block), and what remains for the min-raise is the top (big pairs, good Ax) + the strongest hands that can't be jammed — the premium sharpens this split. Against that range, seeing a flop with a middling-weak hand loses twice: you are dominated by his bluffs, and the one pair you make on the flop is dead to his overpairs. Result: the call slice is narrow *(calibrate)* — high-card hands that can make strong top pair and real suited connecteds; the rest is jam or fold. Jam material: every Ax (blocks the value, doesn't block the opener's raise-folds — a low kicker is an advantage here, a middle kicker blocks villain's raise-folds), all pairs, weak suited broadway (folds out the broadways that dominate it, flips against pairs), middle offsuit Kx (the KQ type is closer to trap/call because what it dominates is plentiful).

**Rule:** before defending against a min-raise, ask "does this opener have a jam range?" — if yes, read the min-raise as polar and switch off 30.2's "defends wide"; defense = narrow call + Ax-blocker jams.

### 30.3 Uneven stacks: the big-pot-against-a-single-range effect


Jam, one calls and the others fold → a big pot against a single range. Because the caller calls tighter under the threat of a reopen/squeeze behind, your jam range WIDENS; the effect grows as depth grows (the general ICM/multiway pressure logic). The deep side's situation — against a short jam, default CALL with ≥~22bb, not rejam; the SB the single exception — is in 22.10-EK.

### 30.4 No flat/trap in this band


Since the flat-call doesn't enter the tree at 4-12bb, the flat>3-bet trap doesn't exist either (trap/flat lines belong to 30bb+ deep play; B5's 25-30bb band is jam/fold too). Even with a jam-happy short stack behind you, the plan is jam-or-fold; flatting "so the bait gets swallowed" is the micro version of the root error (tying up chips without fold equity).

### 30.6 BB at ≤5bb facing a jam: almost any two cards CALL


The root error: folding to a jam from the BB at 5bb because "I don't have a hand" — especially in a fast structure. Three mechanisms stack on top of each other:

1. **Pot odds:** the BB + ante are already in the pot, your remaining stack is small; required equity = remaining ÷ (2×(remaining + BB) + SB + ante) — villain matches your total stack, the SB and ante are dead money; if the jammer is the SB, the SB's 0.5bb is inside the match, don't add it separately — the denominator becomes 2×(remaining + BB) + ante; compute it with your own hand.
2. **The jam range is mostly missing its top (borderline — a bonus, not a load-bearing leg):** while there are still players behind the jammer to be pushed off (a CO/BTN jam), a stack with the raise option *(roughly 12–20bb, calibrate)* mostly opens its strongest hands non-all-in (QQ+ *(calibrate)*); the jam range is small pairs + suited Ax + offsuit high cards + some suited Kx/Qx → small suited connectors/gappers and connected offsuits are rarely dominated and rarely run into a big pair; their equity stays live against small pairs *(calibrate)* — the top being opened non-all-in improves your price further at the margin. In an SB→BB or single-opponent jam the leg drops out: if you're already paying with everything, keeping his premium out of the jam costs him nothing, the range has its top; against a very short jammer (no raise option) it drops too — the decision rests on 1 and 3.
3. **Future game (22.10):** if you fold, the next SB + ante push you below 3bb, and from there even a double produces a dead stack; if you call and win you climb to a live stack. The win/loss ratio at the band's top end is roughly 2–2.5:1, at ~3.5bb and below it passes 3:1 *(depending on the ante structure, derive from leg 1's formula — calibrate)*; the future-game weight pushes it even higher, and the required equity even lower.

**Rule:** at ≤5bb in the BB the default against a jam is CALL; save the fold only for dead offsuit junk, and first ask "how many bb do I enter the next BB with?" In the band where your remaining stack's pot odds against a min-raise collapse — roughly 3–4bb *(calibrate)* — there is no fold, jam. One band up (~7bb) the call range narrows sharply *(the break point: calibrate)*. The pool pays far too tight in this band — know this on the jam side, don't copy it when in the BB. The numerical end of 30.2.

### 30.7 Postflop at ≤15bb in the BB (top of the band): on a low board the lead = JAM or CHECK


≤15bb, a deeper opponent opened, you flatted in the BB. The root error: carrying bottom/middle pair into check-call with the 40–60bb reflex. Shallow, the flop pot is so big relative to the stack that two things take over the math: PROTECTING your equity without giving a free card, and making villain's equity FOLD; even bottom pair mostly enters the stack-off class (around 10bb), borderline at 15bb. The jam's fold-equity source: bare overcards (roughly a quarter of the equity) and gutshots / weak one-overcard draws fold; high straight draws with overcards (ahead of most pairs) will pay — against them the jam's value is not the fold but denying the free card and realizing your equity instantly. If you have NO flop jam range, IP takes a free turn with every hand — shallow, being the first to put money in is worth more than position *(calibrate)*.

**The tree is at most three nodes: jam / small / check.** Against a BTN opener on a low-middle connected board: jam class = top/middle pair, straight draws with an overcard, gutshot+overcard. **The trap when shallow:** two pair, straights, sets, part of the strong top pairs sit in CHECK — the jam range is already full of fragile hands, and if you add the nuts your check range is left naked; the test: "is this hand afraid of a bad turn card?" — if it is, jam. **10→15bb:** the jam frequency drops somewhat, small/check-jam takes a share; the real change is the shift of class — on dynamic textures bottom pair moves to check, overcard draws to jam *(calibrate)*. **EP opener (narrow range):** an overpair-dense narrow range does NOT whiff a low board — the equity advantage stays with EP through the overpairs; the BB's advantage is the NUTS (two pair/straights/bottom set — EP doesn't have them). Overpairs don't fold (little fold equity) → not a small/frequent lead, CONTROL: the range mostly checks, the jam is saved only for the nut/near-nut class + the best straight draws (semi-bluff); a small lead only nut-weighted and rare *(calibrate)*. For high boards, 19.1-EK.

### 30.5 Cheat sheet


*(calibrate: the small-pair jam depth, the BB re-jam threshold — sizing-dependent.)*

| Situation | Rule |
|---|---|
| **Opening** | JAM or fold — NO small-RFI chart in this band |
| **Small pairs (22-66)** | Among the band's deepest-living JAMs *(calibrate)*; not DEFENDED |
| **Fringe vs raise+** | Jam-or-fold, NOT flat: at sufficient depth the re-jam carries real fold equity, very short the jam works on live equity + dead money *(band boundaries: calibrate)* |
| **BB defense** | Defend wide against a min-raise: at the shallow end the re-jam/fold axis; at the deep end (closing against the min-raise) a preflop flat is allowed — but don't flat and then play street by street *(calibrate)* |
| **Uneven table** | The reopen/squeeze threat behind squeezes the caller → your jam range WIDENS; the effect grows with depth |
| **Trap-flat** | NONE — even against a jam-happy short stack don't flat a premium as "bait": your jam range is already wide, the premium gets paid via the jam; the flat, if no jam comes, turns into a bloated/multiway pot (the micro version of the root error). The BB's action-closing flat against a min-raise is separate — that is defense |
| **The real leak** | Not too much stack-off but too much FOLDING + fringe flat-calls; the fringe-JAM is doctrine, not spew |

*Root-error link: in the micro band the danger is not too much stack-off but too much FOLDING (the jam/re-jam not wide enough) and flat-calling with fringe. The fringe-JAM is standard jam/fold doctrine, not a leak — the leak is the flat-call/limp lines.*

## Chapter 31 — BB-Ante Heads-Up and HU-vs-GTO

### 31.0 BTN 100% VPIP in BB-ante HU


Thanks to the dead ante, you're entering a ~3BB pot IP for half a BB — one of poker's best prices; no hand gets folded. Seeing your opponent fold the BTN is a tell in itself: it means he's pulled trash out of his range + declared his HU inexperience.

### 31.1 Depth-proportional limp/raise — above mid-shallow depth, the IP jam disappears


HU BTN strategy shifts with depth: the deeper you go, the bigger the raise share grows (very deep ≈ raise-only), the shallower you go, the more limp dominates *(calibrate)*. The critical surprise: above mid-shallow depth the IP jam disappears and limp takes over *(calibrate the threshold; roughly ~15-16bb)*; even at very shallow depth there's still a polar limp range — trap + trash *(calibrate the depth)*. Against a passive opponent, drop the balance: limp the trash, raise the good hands.

### 31.1-EK Position mirror: the "raise more as you get deeper" direction also holds for BvB SB — what mirrors is not the curve but position and mechanism


31.1's depth curve is for IP limp: an OOP opponent isolates the HU-BTN limp. In 8-max BvB, the SB limp is OOP, the iso comes from IP (the BB), and IP iso mostly grows with depth — the curve mostly doesn't invert in BvB, it stays in the same direction: at the deep end the SB also shrinks its limp share and shifts to raise-first, and as it gets shallower (roughly down to 12–15bb) limp gets heavier; below that, limp gives way to open-jam *(calibrate)*. The difference isn't whether limp-reraise exists, it's the level of the curve and the texture of the limp: the preflop cost of raise-fold is the same on both sides, the difference shows up in the branch where the raise gets called — because getting called out of position in a grown pot after an OOP raise, the SB limps more than the HU-BTN does at the same depth in most cases; the surplus is medium hands avoiding raise-fold, and at the deep end the SB's limp slice is wider and more merged than the HU-BTN's; the limp-reraise slice is preserved (part of premium + a few blockers) but its proportional share isn't large, it's borderline *(calibrate)* — on the deep branch limp doesn't drop to zero, the curve mostly flattens. B33.1's "U" is a content curve, not a frequency curve (the limp-reraise/limp-jam slice of the limp: premium trapping deep, blocker semi-bluff shallow). What changes isn't direction but mechanism and level: for IP it's "seeing a cheap flop," for OOP it's "avoiding the branch where the raise gets called + the SPR trap." This line is for HU; for BvB see B33.1 (the position-mirror box).

### 31.1-EK2 The trap test and the three buckets: limp's EV comes from "the hand that puts money into limp but wouldn't into raise"


The trap test: is the set of hands that attacks my limp different from the set that attacks my raise? The set doesn't change monotonically with depth — it disappears at mid depth, opens up at the shallow end, and closes again at the shallowest end *(calibrate)*. At the shallow end (roughly 15bb, *calibrate*) it's genuinely different: Aces/Kings already jam into a raise too (washed out), the real profit comes from whoever isolates non-all-in with two low cards — QQ gets paid at a small SPR. At mid depth (roughly 25bb, *calibrate*) the set disappears: hands that would call a raise check to a limp, whoever jams into a limp ≈ whoever jams into a raise → limp produces no EV, it forfeits the raise's calling-income (roughly ¾ gets called, *calibrate*). At very shallow depth (roughly 12–13bb, *calibrate*) the trap slice in a BTN limp is mostly close to zero — the BB's jam-into-limp range is narrower than its jam-into-raise range. Rule: if you can't name the hand that gives extra action to your limp, RAISE your highest-EV hand; medium broadways that "win less by raising" drop into the limp body *(which ones: calibrate)*.

Three buckets — 31.1's "limp more as it gets shallower" decline isn't distributed proportionally across every hand; what separates the buckets isn't the hand itself but the response the hand gives to the extra action that comes into a limp (B33.2's bucket construction): (a) two low offsuit cards (82o-type) are pure limp at every depth — raise-EV is near zero, and they dilute your high-card raises; (b) good suited / suited broadway (JTs-type) can't raise-fold, is weak for trapping → belongs to the limp-call body; (c) high-card offsuit (K5o-type) is the natural material for raise-fold — folding to a jam doesn't hurt, and it carries a blocker *(limits: calibrate)*. As it gets shallower only (c) drops into limp; as it gets deeper (c) climbs back out to raise-fold, while (a) and (b) stay put *(calibrate)*. If the opponent jams too much from the top, widen (b) and shrink (c); if passive, the reverse. The HU-IP version of the B33.2 bucket skeleton: (b) is wider than BvB-SB's, (c) is narrower.

### 31.2 The real cost of staying capped


The reason to leave part of your trips/nuts in check-call is NOT fear of over-bluffing: an opponent who knows you're capped grows both the VOLUME and the SIZE of his value (thin value opens up overbet/jam) + adds bluffs alongside that volume → EV stops flowing through both channels at once. An uncapped check-call closes both doors.

> **The penalty for staying capped isn't "I get bluffed," it's "the opponent both value-bets more often and bigger + adds bluffs on top." An uncapped range closes both doors at once.**

### 31.3 Constructing the check-back: the delayed-bluff requirement


In HU, flop check-backs are many times more frequent than in MTT; if you don't deliberately put zero-showdown candidates into your check-back range, you'll keep carrying A-high/one-pair to the river → your range becomes value-oriented, the opponent STOPS bluffing you (bluff appetite is a function of the shape of your range), and your own value finds no payoff. Choose the delayed bluff from hands that unblock the opponent's folds — in HU, bluff selection is mostly about unblocking rather than blocking (the range is wide, there's a lot of trash: T6o blocking KT is a small gain, blocking suited Tx folds is a bigger loss); a hidden trap: a middling card landing on the run-out (like a 9) can quietly give the opponent's A9/K9 check-back class a pair — cut your bluffing on those boards; but if you hold the 9 yourself, the opposite happens, those combos get blocked (the opponent's A9/K9 count shrinks), strengthening your bluff candidate.

### 31.4 No lead/donk in HU — the measure of asymmetry (B19.1's HU confirmation)


What gives rise to a lead in MTT is range ASYMMETRY; in HU both sides play the same wide range → a low/connected board is not "yours," a lead range can't be built by default. The main gate *(mostly)*: IP fired a small c-bet, you called wide, the turn paired the board or came low — asymmetry shifts to the BB at that node (31.4-EK); the same logic also holds on the river after calling a small turn bet wide. Preflop face: iso into a HU BTN limp ≠ the mirror of iso into a BvB SB-limp (positions are reversed); OOP iso is narrower on the linear slice, wider on the polar big-size slice and shallow (31.9, 31.12). B19's ICM-lead exceptions are ring-only; they don't carry over to HU.is not "yours," a lead range can't be built. Preflop face: iso into a HU BTN limp ≠ the mirror of iso into a BvB SB-limp (positions are reversed, OOP iso wants a stronger hand). B19's ICM-lead exceptions are ring-only; they don't carry over to HU.

### 31.4-EK The main gate to a lead: wide call vs. a small c-bet + a pairing/low turn


31.4's rationale is range symmetry; what breaks the symmetry is a SMALL c-bet + a wide call on the flop. On J-7-7 rainbow, BTN bets ~⅓, BB defends nearly all Q/K-high and every draw *(threshold: calibrate)* — the call range concentrates around one pair + weak A-high + draws. Turn J: BB's Jx becomes jacks-full (the very top), its 7x (the ones that didn't check-raise the flop) becomes sevens-full — below Jx but crushes IP's trash —, its low Ax is ahead of the betting range with board two pair + an A kicker; IP's range range-bet the flop, so it still carries 8-high trash. The asymmetry now sits with the BB → lead with: Jx, the remaining 7x, WEAK Ax (A6-type thin value/deny; A9/AT check more, *calibrate*), showdown-less draws. Had the flop c-bet been big, IP's range would have stayed polar/strong and BB's call range would have been capped (part of the 7x would already have check-raised the flop) → the turn lead's share mostly SHRINKS *(calibrate)*. The same mechanism works when the turn pairs a low card: a mix of small/big lead — don't do the big lead alone, the small flop bet also covers the top-card pair. IP's fold rate to a small turn lead needs to be very low *(calibrate)*; the pool over-folds even with two live cards. Gate CLOSED: if IP started polar/big and the turn is a medium/high card that builds IP's range, there's no lead; it opens on a low turn in a low-disconnected texture. Rule: a lead isn't the default in HU; B19's asymmetry gives rise to a lead, and in HU it's IP's small c-bet that produces the asymmetry.

### 31.5 Rainbow = home of the big size (B18.2 exception box — HU)


Big c-bet sizes cluster most on RAINBOW/static boards, not on flush-draw boards. In wide-range HU/blind-battle pots, if your overpair/best-Tx edge is large, a big-polar size on a dry board is correct — B18.2's "dry=1/3" rule of thumb leaves equity-edge on the table here. (The HU application of the same correction as B18.2-EK.)

### 31.5-EK Frequency of the big size and the small check-raise response


Don't turn 31.5's "rainbow = home of the big size" into a range-bet. In deep HU (roughly 100bb, *calibrate*), a big size on a low, disconnected, rainbow board is correct — except on connected and monotone textures *(calibrate)* — but the big size is for LESS THAN HALF the range (roughly 40% bet / 60% check, *calibrate*): medium overpairs, top pair with a good kicker, and overcard draws fire big, the rest check *(which combos: calibrate)*. The pool says "bet" for everything; range-bet + big size = failing to meet MDF against a check-raise with a range you've diluted (weak on average) in a pot you yourself bloated. The flip side: the response to a big polar c-bet isn't check-call or a 3x+ check-raise. The body of a polar big bet is little value + A LOT of air combos → a SMALL check-raise (roughly 2.2x the bet, *calibrate*) opens up a price against that stack that can't be defended; the pool's 3x+ polar raise puts in too much money, and it's mostly worse than a pure call. If you don't give your solver a small raise size, this node stays invisible (B27.3's size-restriction error). The shallow mirror (a shallow limp-check pot, *calibrate*): against IP's half-pot c-bet, a MIN check-raise with a weak hand of the overcard + backdoor type that targets the low-card air in the betting range — the betting range carries top/mid-card pairs + low-card air, and the min-raise targets exactly that air *(calibrate)*. Rule: big size = infrequent + wide check; small raise into a big polar bet; the raise size shrinks with the air proportion in the opponent's betting range.

### 31.6 The accelerated tell/meta cycle


Because VPIP is ~100%, tendencies surface in just a few hands; aggressive projection from a SINGLE data point is legitimate. Tells are read in three categories — timing (snap vs. tank), sizing (small/medium/big), line (which street is passive/aggressive on) — one read from each: flatting a pair without thinking at deep stack has declared pot-control (timing); an opponent who checks A-high thin value has an OOP range stronger than it should be — cut your own thin value (line); a snap-call that pushes you into the pot on the river is a confession that "raise isn't in my menu" — grow your OOP thin-value size (timing+sizing). Overriding rule: even an MTT elite is a relative novice in HU; but someone more experienced than you will hunt your own simplifications the same way.

### 31.7 Small pairs keep jamming deep + the ban on medium sizes


Against an open from the BB, small pairs remain jam candidates in HU at stack depths deeper than you'd expect — in the rejam-vs-open math they can stay +EV to a surprising depth *(anchor roughly ~40bb: which pair to which depth, calibrate on your own solver; 31.7-EK)*; but in a full strategy, above the anchor, call/non-all-in 3-bet is mostly higher EV than jam — jam dominates in the band below, and stays a borderline option above *(calibrate)*. Even so, the MTT-reflex confusion of "jam with a pair at this depth?" is a leak in the HU push/fold band. A common tell of a "skilled but not advanced" player is a reasonable-medium size (half-pot) in a polar spot: it should be either tiny (wide weak-value) or big/polar (nuts+bluff) — the middle kills both functions. HU river is the exception: the range is so wide that a single 2x-pot polar size isn't enough, thin value (a weak-kicker top-pair type on a paired board) wants a second, medium value size — "sacrifice thin value, play one size" is an MTT simplification that doesn't work in HU *(borderline, calibrate)*.the confusion of "jam with a pair at this depth?" is a leak in the HU push/fold band. A common tell of a "skilled but not advanced" player is a reasonable-medium size (half-pot): it should be either tiny (wide weak-value) or big/polar (nuts+bluff) — the middle kills both functions.

### 31.7-EK The purpose-of-the-jam test: pairs and offsuit Ax rotate with depth


Don't play the same hand the same way at every depth; every jam is priced by three questions — does it fold out a better hand, does what it folds out have equity, and what happens if it gets called. (1) An offsuit ATo/A9o 3-bet-jam at ~40bb "gets called by better, folds out nothing" → call or non-all-in instead; at ~30bb it goes back to jam, and live-equity hands like T8s/T9o get folded out *(calibrate)*. (2) Small pair vs. a BB raise: at the deep end (roughly 50bb) CALL (you don't want to eat a 4-bet-jam into your overpair), near the anchor (roughly 40bb) jam dominates — 31.7's anchor *(calibrate)*. (3) You face a ~50bb non-all-in 3-bet on the BTN: NO 4-bet-jam with a small pair — the range that pays is either an overpair (crushes you) or AK/AQ-type (coinflip); risking 50bb doesn't cover the fold-equity against either → flat; 4-bet-jam is for medium pairs, premium pairs + the strongest suited Ax go non-all-in *(limits: calibrate)*. (4) Facing a balanced polar non-all-in 3-bet at ~25bb (a mix of no-equity offsuit bluffs + medium-high pairs / good Ax): premium pairs slow-play — the polar range already puts the money in; the smallest pairs 4-bet-jam (no playability, has fold-equity); medium pairs go slow — the hand the jam folds out is the same, the difference is in the value of the call alternative: calling catches bluffs, and keeps the overpair on a low board; high pairs jam (equity denial) *(band limits: calibrate)*. In the pool condition of 5.2-EK (premium missing from the 3-bet range, 8-max BvB), medium pairs also revert to jam.
