(5) You face a ~40bb 3-bet-JAM: the jam range has no ceiling, TT+ is in the non-all-in 3-bet (B30.6's "jam has no ceiling" mechanism) → high-card suited, medium suited connectors, small pairs and the weak-suited-Ax class revert to call *(calibrate)*; the pool folds these → jam your jam candidates at full frequency when you're the BB. Rule: the justification for a jam isn't "my hand is strong," it's "the name of what I fold out."

### 31.9 The BB's iso into a limp is two sizes: linear small + polar big


In deep-mid HU (roughly 50–60bb, *calibrate*), the BTN's limp body is exactly "medium hands that comfortably call a small iso" (J7s/T5s, Q9o/K7o/A7o-type). A single small iso *(~3–4x, calibrate)* doesn't punish that body, it just builds the pot. A second, much bigger size *(calibrate)* folds out the body and opens two slices: (a) low-EV-for-check-back dip suited trash (62s/73s/92s) gets raised as a bluff — each one folds out the offsuit block of its own top card (73s folds out 7x offsuit; you'd have been dominated by the same hands in a check-back); (b) medium-premium hands that melt street by street (JJ/AQ/AK) get forced into the 88/99/AQ class jamming with the big size — it resolves preflop a hand that would lose value as cards come off. The small size stays linear: playable suited body, pairs, the strongest broadways — the "no problem if I get called" class. Dip trash can't be raised with a single small iso (its fold-equity isn't enough to fold out the body, the limp-callers who dominate you call and you pay), and JJ/AQ melts. The pool jams the big size more than theory and folds to it more than theory — both deviations favor the big size. Its mirror on the BTN: a J9s-type "weak for limp-reraise, strong for a trap" hand is a pure raise; a slice of premium gets limped for a limp-reraise against the big iso — the deep-mid application of 31.1's polar limp.

### 31.10 Raise-only opponent: the BB's any-two jam window


The condition for 100% VPIP in HU is limping; a BTN that never limps mostly either opens very wide (roughly 80%, *calibrate*) and calls a jam narrower than theory, or narrows its open and leaves the dip range free — but there's a third possibility too: open wide and call a jam at theoretical width (or even wider the more it sees) — the window only opens in the first case, so observation is essential. If you observe raise-only + a narrow call to jam, at 30–40bb jamming any two cards from the BB against a raise is mostly +EV *(verify on your own solver with a wide-open/narrow-call model)*. Protocol: (1) watch the first several buttons (31.6: a single data point is enough in HU; *calibrate the window*) — if there's no limp, assume raise-only. (2) Then jam into the raise — the whole range; the kill-switch isn't the limp body, it's the jam's call-eating rate: if the call rate crosses the threshold or the opponent widens against jam, drop the any-two, narrow the jam range *(threshold: calibrate)*, don't wait for a limp. (3) If the opponent also overfolds to non-all-in 3-bets, turn your lowest-EV jams (the T2s/J6o class) into non-all-in 3-bet bluffs. (4) Against an opponent who doesn't mix his raise (always raises JTs/98s), a non-all-in 3-bet is weak — it either gets called or eats a jam; if it's all-in, it folds out half his range. If the opponent settles into a "I got 3-bet → jam" reflex, better still: he loses more against a built strategy. The goal isn't to force him into limping; return to the chart once the limp body arrives. Measurement anchor: a plain call from the BB ≈ the zero point; price the jam against that, "better than fold" isn't enough. Limit: at 50bb+ the jam's risk/reward breaks down — a polar non-all-in 3-bet + a NARROW jam bucket (hands with good equity against the opponent's jam-call range but bad postflop playability: medium pairs, suited-Ax class; it shrinks as depth increases) + a call plan for the rest takes over *(calibrate)*.

### 31.11 A small limp-reraise from IP into a polar iso — the gap jam can't fill


At mid stack (roughly 30bb, *calibrate*) you limped, the BB isos; you have neither trap nor jam material → limp-fold or a reluctant limp-call. The technique is for the node where the BB's response to a limp is genuinely POLAR: the top (pairs/Ax/medium suited connectors) is in the iso-jam, the body of the non-all-in iso is 82s/T4s/Q8o-type trash, medium suited hands check (the mirror of B33.3). If the response is merged — if pairs/Ax/broadway also show up in the non-all-in iso — the premise fails and the technique doesn't work; then the node shifts to a shallower band where the iso really is jam-heavy *(calibrate)*. A limp-jam into a polar range is needlessly expensive: the trash body folds to a small reraise too, and the trap pays off against either size — the jam achieves the same fold while risking the whole stack; moreover, at this depth a limp-jam is already an overbet, it belongs to the shallow layer (B33.3). A SMALL non-all-in limp-reraise *(calibrate)*, on the other hand, folds out the trash body: the large majority of the iso doesn't want to continue OOP even against a small reraise. Material: the lowest-EV limp-calls (the K5o/T7o/97o class — the ones already continuing reluctantly); as a cover, the limped slice of premium (AA/KK/JJ) loses little EV, and the polar top still puts the money in. In most solutions this node isn't even an available option; it's structural against an opponent who can't find an OOP call. Dosage: overuse it and the BB turns its iso merged and jams on top of it — protect the cover and the indicator (has it started finding calls against your small reraise?). Its mirror (you're the BB, the pool limp-reraised early): suspicious — the pool mostly does this with a trap (B33.5's note also holds in HU); if you continue, do it with "not much can go wrong" hands (54s/66), not with dominatable suited Kx/offsuit Ax.

### 31.12 Shallow BB: Aces jam, Kings raise — the limp-call pot is Ace-less


At ~15bb HU *(calibrate)*, the BB's "good hand jams, medium hand checks" pairing against a limp is OOP passivity; the shallow OOP iso is WIDE, not narrow. (a) Card split: at this depth, Ax is either the BTN's open-jam/limp-jam or the BB's iso-jam — an Ace almost never makes it into a limp-call pot. Suited Kx of the KQs/KJs/KTs type, though, is a NON-ALL-IN iso and is enough for a raise-call; offsuit Kx of the K8o type is also an iso, but as a raise-FOLD *(calibrate)* — both dominate the Ace-less medium range that limp-calls postflop via the kicker, and the EV comes not from folding but from the limp-call pot. Result: in the iso range, suited Ax is scarce, Kx is plentiful; almost every offsuit hand does something *(calibrate)*. (b) The double cost of passivity: the BTN's dip limp realizes for free, your check line fills up with trash → in the limp pot, IP bets three streets. Its mirror (you're the BTN, you limped, an iso-jam came): the jam range is Ax/pair/blocker-heavy, Kx/broadway is non-all-in → medium suited connectors and weak-kicker suited Kx/Qx are limp-CALLs; a non-dominated connector is sometimes a better call than a dominated suited broadway (e.g. 98s vs QTs) *(calibrate)*. The pool limp-folds more than theory and, because it open-jams its Ax, its limp-call range is unprotected → a jam into a limp from the BB works better; on the BTN, pull your limp-call threshold below theory's. If the pool calls tight against jam, shift your strongest suited broadways from jam to raise, and shift medium suited connectors into jam — total jam frequency stays roughly constant, the content shifts *(which hands: calibrate)*.

### 31.13 The depth key to the 3-bet bluff: suited when deep, the same ranks offsuit when shallow


Don't pick your 3-bet bluff from the BB from the same hand at every depth. Deep (roughly 50bb) a 3-bet mostly GETS CALLED *(calibrate)* → playability and board coverage pay → the K2s/Q2s/J4s/64s class; drop low-frequency offsuit bluffs (J9o/K7o) — if you have edge it lowers variance, and the ROI loss is small. Shallow (roughly 25bb) the response to a non-all-in 3-bet is mostly either jam or fold (a small amount of call) → playability is near zero, only blocker + fold matter → the OFFSUIT of the same ranks (K2o–K4o, Q2o–Q4o, J2o–J6o, *calibrate*); suited hands are now too valuable as limp-call/flat to burn as bluffs. In the transition band (~40bb) the two mix. Second measure: the bluff folds out whoever it DOMINATES — 64s's 3-bet folds out 86o/K6o/Q4o; being dominated pair-vs-pair is the most expensive loss in HU, and these folds are the bluff's real income. In a 4-bet bluff too, K2/K3 is better than K7/Q8, but the reason is the kicker: the opponent's deep 3-bet bluffs are weighted toward medium-kicker Kx/Qx and 7-ish/8-ish suited connectors — K7/Q8 blocks exactly those bluffs (removing the hands you want to fold), while deuce combos are almost never in the bluff range → K2 doesn't touch the bluffs, it only blocks KK/AK. On the value side, ATo/KQo/KJo are high-EV 3-bet-FOLDs when deep: their EV doesn't come from calling a 4-bet-jam, it comes from the hands they dominate when called *(calibrate)*. B4's "3-bet-bluffing a suited connector at 45bb loses money" line is ring-only — in HU the same hand is the best bluff deep and rolls over to offsuit when shallow.

### 31.14 Open size = the opponent's response


There's no single open size in HU. The cost of the open is multiplied by 3-bet/jam frequency, its income by call frequency; the HU BB's call rate is much bigger than its 3-bet rate *(calibrate)* → the size's real job is to CHARGE the range that's trying to defend J4o, not to avoid a 3-bet. Direction is doctrine, not the numbers: bigger when deep, shrinking in the mid band, min once the jam threat starts — sizes and thresholds *(calibrate)*. To the extent you limp, your raise range is less exposed to a 3-bet → carry a slightly bigger size. Pool deviation shifts the size in two directions: in the mid band the pool jams LESS than theory (it doesn't find the weak-suited/blocker-heavy hands theory jams, *calibrate*) → a medium size survives; in the shallow band it jams MORE than theory ("I have a blocker, jam") → min. If the BB overfolds to the open (folding Q5o/low suited) → min-raise 100%, don't complicate. Same principle at every depth: if the response drifts toward "little call, lots of 3-bet," shrink; if it drifts toward "lots of call, little 3-bet," grow — and if you have postflop edge, a bigger size also grows your pot share. Deep-underdog note: the "narrow range + big size" idea doesn't substitute for choosing the size by the opponent's response (31.15).

### 31.15 Underdog and edge: you can be tight, you can't be passive


In deep HU (roughly 100bb), shrinking your 3-bet against a better opponent on the theory of "keep the pot small, wait until you get to a comfortable depth" is the wrong door. The 3-bet is a big slice of the HU range (roughly ¼ at 100bb, *calibrate*); pulling it back to a third has a big EV cost and leaves the BTN free to lean on you with 100% VPIP — you drop back to a "comfortable depth" where there's no edge to be had. Legitimate tightness: a bit more open-fold, a bit more check-fold. Not legitimate: selling off your aggression slice (3-bet, iso, barrel). If there's no expensive pot in the deep phase, there's no read either — B33.7's "tester" gets fired with a 3-bet in HU. The shallow-end mirror (roughly 6–8bb): the reflex of "I have a big edge, I'll catch it with a good hand" goes bankrupt — there's no street, there's no edge, the only edge left is the jam's fold-equity. In this band the pool calls a jam tighter than theory (folding J7o/86s/T6s, *calibrate*) → take every thin jam; from the BB, also don't fold the bottom 10% (Q2s/98s/J8o call at ~8bb, *calibrate*), or you hand the opponent an any-two jam — the 6–8bb extension of B30.6's ≤5bb rule. Common rule: whether deep or shallow, HU edge comes from aggression; passivity buys variance, not edge.

### 31.8 Cheat sheet


*(calibrate: limp/raise transition depths, small-pair jam threshold — from your own solver.)*

| Topic | Rule |
|---|---|
| **BTN preflop** | 100% VPIP — no hand folds; raise more as you go deeper, limp more as you go shallower, above ~15-16bb the IP jam disappears *(calibrate)*. The opponent's BTN fold is a tell in itself |
| **Against passive** | Drop the balance: limp the trash, raise the good hands |
| **Staying capped** | Dual-channel penalty: the opponent grows BOTH his value volume AND size + adds bluffs on top → keep part of your trips/nuts in check-call (stay uncapped) |
| **Check-back** | Deliberately put zero-showdown candidates in — otherwise the opponent stops bluffing you and your value finds no payoff; choose the delayed bluff from hands that unblock the opponent's folds |
| **Lead/donk** | NOT the default — ranges are symmetric, "your board" can't be established; main gate *(mostly)*: a wide call against a small c-bet + a turn that pairs the low card/comes low (a turn that pairs the top card on a paired board also opens it — 31.4-EK); ICM-lead exceptions are ring-only, they don't carry over to HU|"your board" can't be established; ICM-lead exceptions are ring-only, they don't carry over to HU |
| **Size** | Rainbow/static board = home of the big-polar size; the "dry=1/3" rule of thumb leaves equity-edge on the table in HU |
| **Tell cycle** | Projection from a single data point is legitimate: read the timing / sizing / line triad; a reasonable-medium size (half-pot) in a polar spot is a novice tell — sizes should be either tiny or big/polar; on the HU river thin value wants a second (medium) size *(borderline)* |
| **Small pair** | Rejam vs. an open from the BB stays +EV to a surprising depth — anchor roughly ~40bb *(calibrate)*: below it jam dominates, above it shifts to call/raise; the jam's justification is "the name of what I fold out" (31.7-EK) |
| **BB iso (into a limp)** | Two sizes: linear small (body) + polar big (melting premium + dip suited trash) *(calibrate)*; shallow, the strongest high-card-blocker hands jam, one tier below goes non-all-in raise *(which rank to which bucket: calibrate)* — the shallow OOP iso is wide |
| **Raise-only opponent** | Against an opponent who never limps, widen your jam range noticeably at mid depth — condition: observed narrow call to jam; kill-switch is the jam's call-eating rate; the jam's value drops as depth increases; the zero point is a plain call *(thresholds and observation window: calibrate)* |
| **Into a polar iso (IP)** | A SMALL non-all-in limp-reraise (lowest-EV limp-calls + a premium cover); a limp-jam achieves the same fold while risking the whole stack |
| **Trap test** | If you can't name the hand that gives extra action to your limp, RAISE; the trap is dead at mid depth, lives off a two-low-card iso at the shallow end, and closes again at the shallowest end *(depths: calibrate)* |
| **Three buckets** | It's not the hand that defines the bucket, it's the response the hand gives to the extra action that comes into a limp (fold/call/rais

## Chapter 32 — 3-bet Pot Postflop: Size Is Chosen by Hand Class and the Block That Whiffed, Not the Board

### 32.0 The one-sentence thesis


> **In a 3-bet pot, size is chosen not by board texture alone but by which block the texture made whiff + by hand class. The root error (one pair in a bloated pot) is the error of the one who bloated it — the 3-bettor: range + nut advantage give him a license to jam at shallow SPR and to use polar sizes at deep SPR; using the license without looking at SPR and texture (stacking off with one pair at 100bb on a connected texture where the nut advantage has melted) is the root error itself. The capped caller's one pair is already a bluff-catcher (B26).**

"The 3-bettor = rich in Ax" is incomplete: the 3-bettor's Ax is dense as a proportion but narrow in class and extreme-weighted (AA/AK/AQ + suited wheel bluffs), the caller's is middle-class, wide and dominated (AJ/AT/A9s types) — most of the suited Ax and offsuit AT-AJ stay with the caller (AQo mostly with the 3-bettor, partly with the caller at deep stacks — *calibrate*). The lion's share of the offsuit broadway segment (KQ/KJ/QJ) is also with the caller; KT/QT/JT come along at deep stacks or against a small 3-bet, at 30-40bb they mostly fold *(composition: B4, calibrate)*. Two consequences: (1) you have two fold blocks to target — offsuit broadway and suited Kx/Qx/Ax junk; on every flop the question is "which block whiffed, and which size folds it out?" (2) don't underrate the caller's Ax on an A-high board: the small bet is efficient because it is cheap; a big bet still doesn't fold out the caller's Ax, it only raises the price of your bluffs and forces your narrow/extreme Ax into a polar (nut vs air) tree — on A-high the big size is mostly unnecessary *(calibrate)*.

### 32.1 The shallow-SPR tree: jam or check


Around 40bb, middle-connected board (T-9-x / J-9-x): neither range really whiffs. The caller has middle/bottom pair + straight draws; you have top pair + overpairs + two-overcard draws. The caller's "something" is weaker than yours but carries too much equity to fold. The flop jam converts this into money: you fully realize your own equity, you fold out the behind-but-with-equity hands (suited Kx/Qx, offsuit broadway, low pairs), and you make the draws call while behind.

The tree has two branches: **jam** (top pair, overpair, two overcards + straight/backdoor-flush, strong draws) / **check** (bottom pairs and weak Ax that don't want to put money in; drawless AK — check-call/check-jam; the trap — top set). The in-between sizes are copies that play the same hands at lower frequency; when they're dropped the EV loss is ~zero *(node-lock — B27.3)*. On A-high + fd + two broadways (A-Q-T fd) the same tree: AK/AT without a flush card plays fast. **The boundary:** at SPR ~3-4 and above (≳4-5 at 100bb) the flop jam mostly ceases to exist as a class on any board — the jam is 3-4 times the pot; a two-size scheme (small + big) takes its place. The texture closest to a jam is the most connected high boards where your overcards (AK/AQ) pick up nut-gutshots (J-T-x type): big size + turn stack-off weight, borderline *(which boards, what frequency: calibrate; 32.3)*. B11.0's "SPR 1-4: one pair is a bluff-catcher" row holds for the capped caller at SPR ≳2 (at SPR ≲1.5 even his top pair mostly drops to a call-off — he doesn't start the pot but doesn't fold to the jam; 11.0-EK-2); for the nut-advantaged 3-bettor at SPR ≲2 top pair/overpair is mostly jam class *(frequency: calibrate)*, and at 100bb the row returns to apply to the 3-bettor as well (32.2).

### 32.2 The mechanism: depth and the underpair compass


**Depth determines top pair's street count.** At 40bb Ax top pair stacks off on almost every runout → bet the flop. At 100bb three streets means putting the stack in on the river with one pair; on most rivers (the B11.4 catalog runs across all three streets) the jam is not value. But the street you skip is not the flop: Ax top pair mostly continues with a bet on the flop (the underpair compass chooses the size, below), the skipped street is the river — sometimes the turn; a weak kicker checks more on turn/river *(calibrate)*. In a 100bb A-high pot, Ax top pair is mostly a "two streets + river control" hand; "bet-bet-jam" is a 40bb reflex.

**The underpair compass.** The size is chosen by value's most FRAGILE layer. Ask on the flop: "do my underpairs (TT-QQ) want to bet?"

- **A-K-x:** they don't — the caller's Kx has passed them, what they fold out is already behind, what calls them is a better pair → underpairs check; Ax/Kx + gutshot bluffs are polar → big size, frequency still high (range advantage peaks here — only the underpair layer drops out).
- **A-J-x:** they do — Kx/Qx are pairless overcards, they fold to a small bet or call from behind → underpair bets small for value, the range comes along → single small size, full-range.
- **A-J-x + fd:** this time they don't (like A-K-x) — the draws that can bluff on the turn multiply *(calibrate)*.

Rule: if the underpair wants to bet, linear small; if it doesn't, polar big + underpair checks — the compass chooses the size, range advantage chooses the frequency; the underpair sets the size's CEILING, SPR sets its floor. This is the 3-bet-pot application of B18.1-EK "value chooses the size".

### 32.3 Board classes


*(sizes/frequencies: calibrate; the direction is fixed)*

Don't memorize the classes, derive them from three drivers: **(a) who has the nut advantage?** — the range advantage is the 3-bettor's on most boards, the nut advantage only on high-card / A-high textures; if the nuts are the caller's, the size shrinks or checking grows. **(b) SPR** — at ≲2 the jam/check tree; at ~3-4 the two-size tree, flop jam mostly gone; at 100bb SPR ≳4-5, the tree changes (32.1). **(c) The turn-jam threat** — it is texture-conditional: shallow, on dynamic textures (J-T-x type) the turn-jam threat does the flop size's job and leaves the naked draw indifferent → the big-size / jam tree works; deep, the threat is preserved only by a pot-size flop bet → pot-size on J-T-x types; on thin-nut textures (three-broadway) small at every depth, the rest goes to the turn (32.5). At the same SPR, what separates two textures is (a), not (c). The six texture families are worked examples of these three drivers:

| Family | SPR ≲2 (30-45bb) | SPR ~3-4 (baseline ~55-70bb; at 100bb SPR ≳4-5, the tree changes — 32.1) | Driver / why |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Disconnected A-high** (A-6-2 r type) | Full-range small | Full-range small | (a) Nut + range are yours; the caller's broadway/suited-Kx block can't hang on, low pockets are thin value. Wheel-connected A-high (A-3-2 type) is borderline: the caller DOES have 22/33 + A5s-A4s/54s-type wheels/sets; at 100bb depth makes the caller's nut share valuable → check share grows, small-bet frequency drops; shallow, low SPR + range advantage suppress this → full-range small *(calibrate)* |
| **A + broadway** (A-J-x, A-K-x type) | Underpair compass (32.2): on A-K-x Ax polar big, underpair checks; on A-J-x linear small | Same; part of the Ax checks *(calibrate)* | (a) Kx/Qx turned into a pair → the target layer changed; on A-K-x part of the QQ/JJ/TT-type low pockets are big BLUFF candidates (they block the caller's KJ/QJ/JT), 99 and below block nothing → check. Against a sticky opponent all Ax big |
| **Two high connected cards / three broadway** (K-Q-x, J-T-x, K-Q-T type) | Jam/check; big on dynamic J-T-x types — (c) the turn jam leaves the naked draw indifferent; small on three-broadway (K-Q-T) — (a) the nut advantage is thin, a big size is committing against the caller's two-pair/straight segment *(calibrate)* | Pot-size polar (32.4). No flop jam; the texture closest to a jam is the J-T-x type where the overcards pick up nut-gutshots: big size + turn stack-off weight, borderline *(calibrate)*. Small on three-broadway (K-Q-T) — (a) the caller is dense in AJ/JT/two-pair/sets, the nut advantage is thin *(calibrate)* | (a) Nut advantage thin: the top overpairs are yours, the caller's two-pair/straight combos are dense, his overpairs few (capped except QQ). The second-pair pile calls half pot in full; dominated second pair (QJ type) checks |
| **Middle connected** (T-9-x, J-9-x, 9-8-7 type) | Jam/check (32.

### 32.3-EK-2 The IP 3-bettor version of the mono row: overpairs check back, the bluff is a flush-suit overcard — and on three-broadway, if the caller is the BB, half pot is borderline


32.3's mono row is for the OOP 3-bettor. As the IP 3-bettor (3-bet from the BTN, early opener calls, deep) on a 9-8-4 mono-type middle board three things change *(calibrate)*:

- **Overpairs predominantly check back.** The caller's range is suited-heavy (suited Ax/Kx, pairs), light on offsuit broadway → on the mono he either has a flush or air. When you put money in with an overpair, what pays is mostly a flush, what folds was already air. Even the lower overpairs and sets are half in check — a set blocks the top-pair calls (32.6-EK-2).
- **The bluff candidate is not JT but A♠x / K♠x:** it carries an overcard over villain's pockets and realizes on its own with the nut/second-nut fd. JT is neither an overcard nor the suit → check.
- **On the turn the caller's bet is polar and big** (flushes + sets + straight-draw bluffs; 9x is in check): overpairs are separated by blocker, not by rank — 26.4-EK item 3. River: if villain range-checks, your value size grows; if he block-bets, it shrinks (32.6 card-matching).

**Three-broadway note (for the 32.3 cell):** "small at every depth" is for SB-vs-BTN; at 40bb SB-vs-BB, if the caller is the BB and his range carries suited Kx/Qx/Jx junk, half pot as the single size is borderline — small doesn't fold that junk (K5s type) out, half pot does *(calibrate)*. Deep and against the BTN, small as before.

### 32.3-EK The 100bb column: the OOP 3-bettor's check share more than doubles — A-low and low boards stop being "full-range small"


The "Disconnected A-high: full-range small" and "Disconnected low: merged → small-medium, high frequency" cells of the 32.3 table are for SPR ~3–4 (55–70bb); at 100bb (SB vs BTN) both turn into a "check bucket" *(calibrate in your own sim)*:

- **Two asymmetries.** (1) Shallow, OOP's realization comes from flop jams; at 100bb, once the jam class disappears, raw equity rises a little but realization drops markedly while IP's rises — the value of position grows with SPR. (2) At 100bb IP finds a raise with AQ/AJ on an A-low board instead of slow-playing (shallow the money goes in anyway, deep he has to grow it) → the raise frequency vs A-low multiplies; on other textures the raise frequency vs the c-bet mostly does NOT change with depth — the rote "IP is aggressive everywhere when deep" is wrong.
- **Result:** on disconnected A-low the check share goes from ~zero to a third; in the low-board bucket it approaches two-thirds (calibrate disconnected-low and connected-low separately); on three-broadway and A+broadway the difference is small. On dry paired (T-7-7 / 9-4-3) half pot, on high-paired (A-A-x) it stays small.
- **Execution:** build the check range from KK/QQ/JJ + weak Ax (polar bet, middle layer checks); against IP's A-low raise plan a check-call with middle Ax, not bet-fold. Full-range small is a 40–60bb license.

Measurement for 32.7: the 100bb A-low check share and IP's raise frequency. Same direction as 29.2.

### 32.4 Sizing: the indifference price, polar/linear, fragile value


**The size is chosen by the target layer's indifference price.** First "which class do I want to fold out?", then "at which size is that class indifferent?" On a paired board where trips are plausible for the caller (9-7-7 type), the caller's middle layer (offsuit broadway, backdoor Kx/Qx, gutshots, low pockets) mostly calls a small bet and approaches indifference around half pot; on a paired board whose high single card is in your range (A-J-J type) it already folds to small. The flop twin of B11.3-EK *(calibrate)*.

**On two-high-card boards, pot-size polar — the bluff comes from BOTTOM PAIR.** On K-Q-6 half pot mostly keeps the middle pairs and low pockets in; pot-size folds out the low pockets and pushes Qx to the borderline *(calibrate)*. **Value** = strong-kicker top pair + overpairs; **bluff** = bottom pair + high gutshots (AT/AJ) + fd; **check** = second pair and low pockets — with pot-size they only isolate themselves against better hands. The bottom-pair bluff is aceless 6x (65s/64s type, if it's in the range): it blocks 66/Q6/K6 and doesn't block the 7-J pockets and Ax you want to fold out. A6s, on the other hand, blocks both the Ax that folds and the AQ/AK that continues — net effect borderline. A-K-x is the same bucket. The value count caps the bet frequency; surplus bluff candidates change not the frequency but which bluff gets chosen (the best blockers) — the difference between A-K-6 and A-K-4 is marginal *(calibrate)*.

**The fragile overpair mass: size is chosen by SPR and decay, not nut scarcity.** On 6-5-2 against 77-JJ the caller calls with two overcards + backdoors, and every overcard turn is a threat. The caller's nuts are few in absolute number but more than yours — the sets (22/55/66) are his; you have the range/overpair advantage, not the nut advantage. Shallow, the size's bigness comes from SPR already committing you + from value taken before the decay; you fold out suited Tx/9x with pot-size. At 100bb the same mass is merged → the size shrinks, the frequency grows; fragility pushes the size one notch up, not AA/KK — split by protection need *(calibrate)*. B18.1-EK "protection is a fake reason" finds its boundary here: in this mass protection IS "value taken before the decay" — SPR sets the size's floor, the underpair its ceiling (32.2).

**Turn SPR:** after pot-size, SPR <1 → turn jam/check; after half pot, SPR ~1.5 → turn half pot, river jam.

### 32.5 IP/OOP and check-node lines


**The OOP 3-bettor's full-range small bet is legitimate.** B18.5 "OOP range-betting with air is a TRAP" is for the single-raised PFR OOP; the range-advantaged 3-bettor bets full-range small on disconnected A-high; on A-less disconnected middle mono MOSTLY small (as connectivity grows and the caller's flush density (non-nut included) grows, the check share grows — the nut-flush share mostly stays yours via Axs — *calibrate*).

**The turn-jam threat.** What leaves a draw indifferent is not the flop size but the threat of a turn JAM. On dynamic textures (J-T-x type) at 40bb, flop bet + turn jam forces the naked fd / lone gutshot into a borderline call-off → the big size works. At 100bb the threat doesn't disappear, it is delayed: the turn jam becomes an overbet (turn SPR ≳1.5), and the caller carries his draw through the flop without committing → the threat is preserved only by a pot-size flop bet; on J-T-x types pot-size does that. On three-broadway (K-Q-T), though, SMALL at every depth: the nut advantage is thin (32.3 a), a big size is committing against the caller's two-pair/straight segment — collect the auto-fold block cheaply and carry the rest to the turn *(calibrate)*.

**IP check-node on high-card connected boards: the small stab folds out little.** The OOP 3-bettor's check range is nuts + a protected middle layer (second pair, middle pocket pairs, broadways with gutshots) + give-ups; a small bet folds out little of that range — only the already-weakest hands drop. Size up — around half pot *(calibrate)*. Value = top pair; bluff candidates = hands that don't block the middle pairs AND don't block villain's folding A-high block — low suited connected / gutshots that don't carry an A; low Ax is at the end of the stab list (it blocks the Ax that folds; on an A runout it can turn into a value-jam, but on two-broadway textures where the A completes a straight (A on K-Q-x → JT, A on Q-J-T) that capacity drops too, *calibrate*). A high-equity open-ended draw doesn't need fold equity: it realizes for free and is fragile against a check-raise jam → mostly check back *(calibrate)*.

**IP stab on low boards (when you are the caller).** On 7-6-5 / 6-5-4 the 3-bettor's offsuit broadway has nothing, his 9x/8x is scarce, and his overpairs don't want three streets → he mostly checks, and his check range is weak (a portion of the overpairs still bets). Don't check back calling it a "trap"; the small stab is nearly full-range — in practice the 3-bettor folds these hands even more often than theory does *(calibrate)*. The boundary question: **does the 3-bettor's overcard/broadway segment pick up a straight draw on this texture?** 8-7-6 / 9-8-7 (and boards whose top two cards are consecutive, like 9-8-2 / 8-7-3 — on 9-8-2 JT/QJ, on 8-7-3 T9/JT pick up draws) yes → selective; true disconnected 9-5-2 / 8-4-2 types no → stab freely *(calibrate the boundary boards)*. B19.4's 3-bet-pot row, the mirror of B18.5: on LOW/connected boards the OOP 3-bettor mostly

### 32.5-EK-2 On low boards, the OOP 3-bettor's line is chosen not by the board but by the BLUFF composition of the 3-bet range — with middle suited connecteds, small range-bet; without them, check


32.5's "on low connected boards OOP mostly checks, IP stabs" row is for the SB's narrow, broadway-heavy 3-bet range against the BTN. The BB's 3-bet range against the BTN takes its bluffs from middle suited connecteds (98s/T8s/T7s type) + middle pairs; with that range 7-6-5 / 6-5-4 is YOUR board *(calibrate)*:

- **Small full-range bet, high frequency:** the two-pair/straight/set + overpair mass is yours; the caller's offsuit broadway (KQo/KJo/JTo) auto-folds, and the pool also throws away its backdoor-less K9s/Q9s. A big size is unnecessary — what folds is folding anyway, and the remaining range is strong; 99/TT's big-bet EV is close to the small one's.
- **If there are NO middle suited connecteds in your range** (SB-vs-BTN, a premium-heavy BB 3-bet): 32.5 as written — check, leave it to the IP stab.
- **The boundary card:** on 8-7-6 / 9-8-7 the caller's Tx/JT gutshots open up → the frequency drops (the mirror of 32.5's "does the overcard segment pick up a draw" question).

The answer to 32.9 Drill 5 ("7-6-5 small stab") stays correct for SB-vs-BTN; BB-vs-BTN, because OOP checks less often, the stab node opens less — add "who is the 3-bettor?" to the drill. The IP stab's turn continuation is in 19.4-EK-3.

### 32.5-EK The IP 3-bettor tree (BTN 3-bet vs CO, 50–60bb): NO range-bet on low boards — high-equity middle broadway CHECKS, bet = strong + low-equity bluffs


As the IP 3-bettor, the reflex "I'm the aggressor, small range-bet on 6-5-2" is a root error. On low boards the OOP caller's range (plenty of suited connectors/small pairs at 60bb) carries natural value for a check-raise (overpairs, 54/65, 77–TT, sets) + natural bluffs (K8/A7 backdoors). When KQ/AJ/AT gets bet, no worse hand folds, better hands pay, and it folds to a bluff-raise — the hand you bet-folded was beating the villain. Checked back, on a K/Q/J turn the kicker ceiling is yours → you're the one who builds the cooler. Bet: strong hands + JT-type low-equity bluffs (if it gets raised the loss is small, and it folds out KJ/QJ).

- **A-low (A-5-2):** KK/QQ/KQs check back — 6x/Ax doesn't fold, KJ/KT folds; check-back share around one fifth *(calibrate)*.
- **Middle (9-8-3):** part of the 8x and drawless AK/AQ checks back — if it gets raised it's condemned to a pure call and to the OOP jam on the turn.
- **High board:** the reverse — small and frequent; the yardstick is "does the small size fold out suited Ax?" (on J-9-7 A5s-backdoor folds, on 9-7-3 it doesn't). On K-Q-T the nuts change on the turn → flop small, the money goes in on the turn. On mono, the overcard's value depends on "does villain bluff after check-check?" *(borderline)*.

**Counter-exploit (when you are the OOP caller):** the pool as IP is prone to range-betting on A-x-x low → use the small (~2.3×) check-raise WIDE with 5x/Ax protection + 8-7/8-6 wraps; K8s/Q9s/T7s backdoors fold *(calibrate)* — the 3-bet-pot application of 26.5's "flop 1/3 → x/r often" row.

### 32.6 Turn/river: polarization and card-matching


After the small flop bet gets called, the turn polarizes on most cards (big size, middle layer checks); on turns that add straights/middle pairs it returns to linear small *(calibrate)*. **Bluff selection = today's fold equity + tomorrow's value-jam capacity** — low-Ax-type unblockers that don't block the middle pairs come to the front; a high-equity draw doesn't need fold equity, it realizes for free. River: OOP, against an IP whose bluffs are Ax-heavy, FOLD AQ and CALL QJ — AQ blocks the bluffs (B26.4).

**Card-matching in the river overbet bluff.** Don't pick the blocker only by "does it block the nuts". Villain makes his bluff-catch decision by the cards that match your VALUE range: on a board where the flush CAME IN, if your value carries a middle card + a flush card, the bluff-catcher holding that card (Ax + flush card) says "I matched it" and CALLS, the one not holding it FOLDS. The critical property of a bluff candidate is that you too carry the card of villain's premium bluff-catcher — it reduces the "matched" call class, and the folding share of what remains mostly rises (fold equity rises, not guaranteed). That's why low pocket + flush card is the natural overbet bluff on a mono board where the flush came in; on a missed flush, bluffs carrying a flush card are mostly at the end of the list — you'd be blocking the missed draws villain was going to fold anyway; if a non-blocking candidate exists, pick it. Value in two sizes: if the top Ax is villain's, weak-middle Ax small, flush+ big. The attacking face of B11.2-EK *(calibrate)*.

### 32.6-EK-2 The strongest hand's turn check: not range protection but bluff INDUCTION — against a station the bet comes back


32.6 said the turn polarizes after the small flop bet gets called; it didn't say why the top of the range sits in check. The reason is not protection:

- **The audience for bet-bet-bet is gone.** The small flop bet folded out the caller's air; the remaining range is pair/draw-heavy. His weak pair (6x/5x) folds to bet-bet-bet despite the good price — the pool lets it go even saying "I was top pair on the flop". His strong pair (9x) bets into your check on its own — the value doesn't run away. His air/gutshots (T8/Q8/K8 type) fire big bluffs at your check — AA blocks none of them. Add the three up and AA's check EV beats its bet EV *(calibrate)*.
- **Top set on a mono board is the same bucket** (IP 3-bettor, 9-8-4 mono): 99 blocks villain's top-pair calls, few hands will pay a bet; the check-back induces villain's polar straight-draw bluffs and middle flushes (32.3-EK-2).
- **The boundary:** if villain doesn't bluff at checks (rec station) there is no induction → the bet comes back. If villain range-checks the river, your river value size grows; if he block-bets, it shrinks.

The rationale sets the boundary: "protection" says always check regardless of the opponent, "induction" brings the bet back against a station. 29.3's sentence "set slowplay = range protection" misnames the mechanism — the same bridge as 26.2-EK-3: protection is the consequence, the reason is EV.

### 32.6-EK The IP 3-bettor's turn size runs INVERSE to card height — low/blank turn BIG, high turn SMALL; the bluff comes from the "value bet on three rivers" hand


32.6 said "polarizes on most cards"; for the IP 3-bettor the direction runs inverse to the card. The small flop bet (J-9-7 / 9-8-3 / 6-5-2) got called:

- **Low/blank turn (3, 4, T):** villain's continuing range is suited-Ax backdoors, KQ/KJ, low pockets, gutshots — that layer folds to ~65% pot and above, doesn't fold to small; a small size carries it to the river for free → BIG, around a third of the effective stack *(calibrate)*. The rote "blank turn → small continuation bet" is wrong here.
- **High turn (K/Q):** your range is dense with that card (KQ/KJ/AK/QT/JT) → "nearly everything" small and frequent; villain's AQo/AJo and low pockets fold to the small bet, his pairs weren't folding anyway.
- **Bluff selection** — 32.6's "tomorrow's value-jam capacity" principle: K7/K6/K5 type (folds out KQ/KJ, value on a K river), Q9 (folds out QJ/KQ/AQ); hands like T7/98 whose "value bet is doubtful even when it arrives" don't bet the turn. The yardstick: "does this bluff turn into a value bet on at least three rivers?" *(calibrate)*. The pool does the opposite: bets JT, doesn't bet KJo/KTo — the most expensive hand-selection error in a 3-bet pot.
- **In the river jam bluff** a second list: the hands villain THINKS are your bluff class — pick the real bluff from outside it (1.6); read together with card-matching (32.6).

### 32.7 Calibration


*(calibrate: in your own solver, the 3-bettor node — jam frequency and check class (40bb T-9-x); big-size / turn stack-off share on 100bb J-T-x; underpair bet/check EV difference (A-K-x vs A-J-x); the indifference size (9-7-7); the EV loss of reducing to two sizes (B27.3); 100bb A-high top pair turn/river control rate; 100bb overpair size on disconnected low boards; the IP check-node size. In the field: the caller's fold rate to the small bet over your last 30 3-bet pots.)*

### 32.8 Cheat sheet


| Topic | Rule |
|---|---|
| **Thesis + range** | Size is chosen not by texture alone but by the whiffed block + hand class; the 3-bettor's Ax is dense as a proportion but narrow/extreme-weighted in class, the caller's is middle-class, wide and dominated; two fold blocks: offsuit broadway + suited Kx/Qx/Ax junk. The root error belongs to the one who bloated the pot: the license depends on SPR + texture |
| **The jam tree** | SPR ≲2 (30-45bb) middle-connected: jam or check, no in-between size. At SPR ~3-4 and above (≳4-5 at 100bb) no flop jam; on the most connected high boards (if the overcards pick up nut-gutshots) big size + turn stack-off weight, one notch below mostly pot-size *(which ones: calibrate)* |
| **100bb A-high** | Top pair is mostly 2 streets + river control (the skipped street is not the flop); on wheel A-high the check share grows, small-bet frequency drops — the caller's wheel/set share gains value with depth *(calibrate)* |
| **The underpair compass** | If TT-QQ wants to bet, linear small; if not, polar big + underpair checks (frequency stays high — the compass chooses the size, range advantage the frequency) — the underpair sets the ceiling, SPR the floor |
| **Two high cards** | Mostly pot-size polar *(calibrate)*: value ≈ TPTK + overpair; bluff candidates ≈ bottom pair (weak at showdown, doesn't block the pockets) + high gutshots; second pair / low pockets predominantly check |
| **Disconnected low** | The range/overpair advantage is yours, the nuts the caller's; shallow big (commit + decay), at 100bb merged → small-medium, the fragile overpair one notch bigger *(calibrate)*; 9-8-7 types check/small |
| **Paired / mono** | Trips plausible for the caller (9-7-7) → around half pot — the nuts are his but his middle layer calls small in full, the indifference price (32.4); the high single card yours (A-J-J) → small, the target layer already folds to small; on mono "who has the nut flush?" — A+low check-heavy/small mixed, A-less disconnected middle mostly small, if the caller's made flush/straight mass has grown (T-9-8 type; the nut-fd share still yours) check-heavy |
| **Three broadway** | Small at every depth (the nut advantage is thin — the caller is dense in two-pair/straights, a big size is committing against their segment; calibrate). The big-size / jam tree belongs to the dynamic high texture (J-T-x type): shallow, flop bet + turn jam leaves the naked draw indifferent, at 100bb pot-size |
| **IP check-node** | On high/broadway boards the small stab is inefficient; around half pot, bluff candidates are A-less low suited connected / gutshots (doesn't block villain's folding A-high block); low Ax at the end of the stab list *(calibrate)* |
| **Low-board stab** | On connected low (7-6-5 type) the 3-bettor's whiffed broadway block + part of the overpairs check → small stab at the check-node; on disconnected low (6-5-2) the default

### 32.9 Drill (5 questions)


1. 40bb, SB 3-bets, BTN calls, flop T♠9♦4♣, your hand A♠T♦. Line?
   → **Jam.** Shallow SPR + middle-connected board + range advantage: you fold out the behind-but-with-equity hands; the in-between size is a copy (32.1).
2. Same hand, same board, 100bb. What changed?
   → **No jam; pot-size/check.** At 100bb the flop jam is mostly not a class (SPR ≳4-5; at ~3-4 the two-size tree too). On T-9-x AK/AQ has no nut-gutshot, and on a Q the caller's KJ makes a straight → pot-size. The texture closest to the big size is the J-T-x-type board where the overcards pick up nut-gutshots *(calibrate)* (32.3).
3. 100bb, TT on A-K-4 rainbow: bet or check? On A-J-4?
   → **A-K-4 check, A-J-4 small bet.** Kx has passed you → underpair checks, the range is polar; on A-J-x Kx/Qx are pairless overcards → linear small (32.2).
4. 100bb, K-Q-6 rainbow, your hand 7♥6♥. Is it a bluff, and what size?
   → **Pot-size bluff candidate.** Aceless 6x: the 6 blocks 66/K6/Q6; it doesn't block the 88-JJ pockets you want to fold out (the 7 half-blocks 77 — negligible); Qx stays in check (32.4).
5. You called a 3-bet, 100bb, flop 7-6-5 r, the 3-bettor checks, your hand K♣J♦. Stab? What if it's 8-7-6?
   → **7-6-5 small stab; 8-7-6 selective.** On 7-6-5 the 3-bettor's check range is weak; on 8-7-6 the overcard/Tx segment picks up straight draws (32.5).

*Root-error link: B4 lowers the error's frequency, B18 gives the single-raised flop, B11 gives the bloated pot's execution; B32 gives the lines of the one who BUILDS the bloated pot. The root error doesn't change and belongs to the one who bloated it: the license is limited to shallow SPR + nut-advantaged textures; deep, on a texture where the nut advantage has melted, the 3-bettor who stacks off with one pair regardless of texture commits the root error; the capped caller's one pair is already a bluff-catcher. Guard: ask the size not of the board label but of "which block whiffed + does my underpair want to bet + how many streets does my top pair carry".*

---

## Chapter 33 — Blind-vs-Blind: The Depth Layers of Limp, Raise, and Jam

### 33.0 Thesis


> **BvB is the widest-ranged node at the table and the one that changes shape most with depth. SB is OOP, BB has position. The rule of thumb isn't "limp = weak, raise = strong" — it's "which engine is running."**

SB's limp doesn't move in a single direction with depth: the raw limp share grows as it shallows toward the jam/fold threshold, the way it does in HU, then flips over and shrinks below roughly 12–15bb as it collapses into jam *(calibrate)*; the INSIDE of the limp — the limp-reraise/limp-jam slice — mostly traces a U *(calibrate)*: deep, a slice of premium goes for the limp-reraise trap; in the middle it thins out (a thin premium cap + a blocker bluff); shallow, it's the blocker limp-jam. The two arms have different contents, and each arm has its own engine. This is the position-mirror of HU-BTN (B31.1): there, limp is made IP; here, it's made OOP — the "limp = see a cheap flop" engine over there mostly turns, over here, into an "avoid the branch where an OOP raise gets called + close the SPR" engine. The rationale changes and the curve shifts with it: the direction is the same, but at the same depth OOP SB limps more often than IP HU-BTN does. Raise-fold's preflop cost is the same on both sides; the difference shows up in the branch where the raise gets called or 3-bet: BTN grows his position with money by raising (called, he plays IP), whereas SB's raise, once called, leaves him OOP in a grown pot — fold is expensive when 3-bet, and call is out of position — while limp gives OOP a cheap flop and, via limp-reraise, a way to close the SPR. The gap opens up deep and closes shallow *(calibrate)*.

### 33.1 Depth Layers — the U-Curve and Three Engines


| Layer *(calibrate)* | Engine | SB premium | Content of SB's limp |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Deep** (roughly above 50–60bb; 45–60bb is the mid→deep transition band) | SPR: the limp-reraise cap for a slice of premium; the body stays raise-first *(calibrate)* | Mixed: mostly RAISE-first (the range doesn't stay capped), one slice LIMPS → big reraise into the iso *(calibrate)* | Narrows with depth but never hits zero; wider and more merged than HU-BTN (medium hands escaping raise-fold); the limp-reraise slice is preserved: part of premium + a few blocker bluffs *(calibrate)* |
| **Mid** (roughly 30–50bb) | BB's iso polarizes → the limp-reraise target narrows | Mostly RAISE; a thin limp-reraise cap | Limp-call body (suited/connected/small pairs/weak Ax — continuing at the price against the iso) + limp-fold tail (trash) + a small limp-reraise slice (thin premium cap + blocker bluff, non-all-in); the trap slice sits at the bottom |
| **Shallow** (roughly 20–30bb) | BB's jam over a raise is cheap → raise-fold gets expensive | Big pairs RAISE (again); AK is borderline | Limp-call body (suited/connected/small pairs/weak Ax — continuing at the price against the iso) + limp-fold tail (trash) + a limp-jam slice (blocker Ax/Kx + small pairs) returns here — not the deep arm's premium trap but a semi-bluff; the limp share is thickest |

Effective bb/SPR selects the layer; three questions shift the thresholds INSIDE the layer and decide which side you play on in the transition band (45–60bb): "Will BB call my limp-reraise with a suited hand?" (deep) · "Is BB iso'ing my limp with trash, or with a suited hand?" (mid) · "Will BB jam over my raise?" (shallow). As it gets deeper, SB shrinks his limp share and shifts to raise-first — the body of premium stays raise-first, the range doesn't get capped; within what remains of the limp, the limp-reraise share grows — OOP grows the deep pot both by raising and by the cap, and the cap share increases with depth *(calibrate)*. As it gets shallower, the limp thickens: a good hand raises-and-takes-it (a big reward relative to the blind+ante stack), everything else is limp / limp-jam. A fourth layer — very shallow (roughly ≤15bb): no raise-fold, 3-bet = jam, premium raise-call/jam, limp = mostly limp-jam, trash folds — this is B3/B30's jam-fold doctrine, and it falls outside this chapter *(calibrate)*.

> **Position mirror (B31.1 ↔ B33.1).** What mirrors is the position, not the curve. HU-BTN's limp is IP, and it's the OOP opponent who does the isolating; BvB's SB limp is OOP, and it's the IP opponent (BB) who does the isolating — and the IP iso mostly grows with depth. The direction "raise as it deepens, limp as it shallows" is the same in both places; what shifts is the level and the content: because an OOP raise, once called, leaves him out of position in a grown pot, SB mostly limps more than HU-BTN does at the same depth — the surplus is medium hands escaping raise-fold, the limp slice

### 33.1-EK 10bb BvB Push/Call Pocket Cheat Sheet — the Fourth Layer's Pocket-Sized Form


SB push *(calibrate)*: offsuit, every Kx and every Qx (Q2o ≈ zero but the pool folds too much → push); a BIG drop from Q to J: J7o+, then the parallel T7o/97o/87o; 6x offsuit and below folds. Suited, every suited hand carrying one high card (T2s+, 92s borderline); low suited hands need connectivity (74s/64s/65s push, the lowest disconnected suited folds). BB call *(calibrate)*: every Ax, every Kx; a big drop from K to Q: Q8o+, J9o+, medium suited broadway. Mnemonic anchor: the push threshold breaks at Q→J, the call threshold at K→Q — don't mix the two up. The borderline call is mostly +EV — the condition is the pool's limp-trapping: the pool's jam range holds less premium than balance (it occasionally limps AA/KK) → it's less top-heavy than the chart, so the borderline call becomes a "fist bump," and folding is the relatively bigger mistake; against an opponent who also jams his premium, that same call reverts to borderline/−EV. On its own side, the pool mostly folds the chart's borderline calls (medium Qx-o / Jx-o and weak suited broadway) → SB's thinnest pushes get through; go ahead and fire them too *(calibrate with your own pool data)*. Don't carry the HU chart over here: HU-BTN's open-jam is tight at 10bb because he has a limp (B31.1); BvB SB's jam appetite is high because he's OOP — both jam ranges, and the call threshold, are memorized separately. Below the band: at 8bb gambling widens noticeably, the 5→7bb transition is the biggest jump, ≤3bb it's any two cards (B30.6).

### 33.2 SB's Hand: Limp-Reraise Deep, Limp-Jam Shallow


**Deep: a slice of premium limps.** The body stays raise-first (the range doesn't get capped); calibrate the slice that goes to the trap. Mechanism: SB raises, BB calls, and they go to the flop at a high SPR; BB's medium suited connector hits a flop that catches one pair, and he walks away easily. Limp instead invites the **middle band** (medium suited connectors, offsuit broadway) — hands BB mostly won't 3-bet a raise with but mostly does isolate a limp with — into position; a big limp-reraise *(calibrate)* drops the SPR several times over, and that same one pair on that same flop mostly goes to stack-off. The EV comes not from folding out trash but from that band. Keep the reraise value-weighted: the target band is sticky, and stickiness pays off value, not bluffs.

**Bucket construction.** Sort every hand by the answer it will give to an iso after limping: limp-fold (the weakest offsuit, the lowest suited) · limp-call (QT/QJ-type hands that continue comfortably against an iso, medium suited broadway) · top (limp-reraise or raise). The group that doesn't fit a bucket — medium Qx/Jx offsuit, gapped medium suited — is **natural raise-fold material**. The limp is built bottom-up: first the offsuit hands with the lowest raise-EV, then the "playable but doesn't want to see a 3-bet" shield layer (low suited Kx/Qx, gapped low suited). Rule: **if you limped it suited, there's mostly no folding to a single iso** — the lowest suited hand is the exception *(calibrate)*; outside that, if you're going to fold, raise-fold instead. Don't 50/50-mix the two borderline hands: raise the better one, limp the weaker one. Because you're OOP, the shield layer should be thicker than HU-BTN's *(calibrate)*.

**Shallow: big pairs stay raise** *(calibrate)*. BB isos the limp often and small; the range he calls a limp-jam with is narrower than the range that jams over my raise. A raise brings even hands BB wouldn't call a limp-jam with into his jamming range (jam range > call range) — if a big pair limp-jams, it loses that wider call. AK is borderline: it works both as a raise and as a jam over the iso (below). Limp-jam material is the weakest offsuit Ax (wheel-kicker), low-medium offsuit Kx, and small pairs *(bounds: calibrate)*: it folds out the iso's trash, blocks BB's call range, and carries live equity when called.

**Jam over the iso.** The question isn't "is my hand strong" but "who calls, and do I dominate him." Big offsuit Ax (AJ/AQ; AK borderline, works both ways) is the base for this: the medium suited Ax / strong-broadway class in the iso both isolates and calls, and its postflop playability is low. Suited medium broadway (KTs/KQs) is the reverse: if you jam it, only better pays, and the Kx you dominate folds → limp-call instead (it's already a raise at mid-depth, 33.3) *(bounds: calibrate)*. The pool jams the iso/reraise more than theory *(calibrate)* → don't make a non-all-in reraise with a medium hand you'd be folding to a jam.

### 33.3 Mid Stack: BB's Iso Polarizes — the Premium Limp Target Narrows, the Body Shifts to Raise ("Too Deep to Trap, Too Shallow to Reraise")


Deep, BB isos the limp depolarized; the large majority of it calls a limp-reraise. At mid-depth, the same BB range splits in two: the strongest hands JAM over the reraise, offsuit trash iso-folds, and **the medium suited hands in between check instead of iso'ing**. The limp-reraise target narrows — a reraise mostly gets either a jam or a fold; a limp-call, meanwhile, leaves the pot too small for premium. For the same reason, KQs/QJs/QTs mostly shift to RAISE at mid-depth: you have to put money in against that "checking suited medium" band that won't fold to a raise but won't iso a limp either; limp it and he sees a free flop. At mid-depth the limp-reraise slice shrinks but stays a mix of value+bluff: a thin premium slice (a portion of the biggest pairs) stays in limp-reraise as a cap over a few non-all-in blocker Ax/Kx bluffs — otherwise BB's jam over the reraise becomes unbounded; the body of premium shifts to raise *(calibrate)*. Limp-jam is an overbet at this depth and belongs to the shallow layer (33.2). Big offsuit Ax fast-plays; weaker suited broadway/connectors (below QTs: JTs, J9s, T9s type) sit on the playability line — the boundary is mixed *(calibrate)*.

### 33.4 BB's Response to the Limp: Iso, Iso-Jam — and SB's Call of the Jam


**Iso.** Don't reflex-iso every hand with the same size. The most expensive group is offsuit broadway (KJo/QTo): isolating them feels natural, but that's exactly the band limp-reraise is targeting. Low suited hands are a cheap iso: they fold to a reraise, and if the iso holds they play it with position. Villain's limp-reraise frequency picks the size and the width: against a pool that reraises rarely and small, iso SMALLER and WIDER *(calibrate)*; against a competent limp-reraiser, trim your offsuit-broadway isos. As the ante bloats the pot, both the limp and the iso get cheaper — iso width runs wider than the ante-less rule of thumb *(calibrate)*. IP-BB's iso is noticeably wider than HU-OOP's iso (B31.4); don't mix the two mnemonics up.

**Iso-jam** *(~30bb, calibrate)*. The material mirrors 33.2: K5o/K6o-type blockers (fold out the Kx hands that dominate them, and live if called) and medium suited connectors (don't want to eat a limp-jam over a non-all-in iso → jam directly instead). Suited medium broadway doesn't jam. Because the jam is preflop all-in it's position-independent; non-all-in iso width depends on position.

**SB's call — the limp got jammed on.** All-in, only raw equity against the range plus pot odds count; the "dominated" instinct doesn't beat raw equity. If BB's jamming texture is Ax/pair-heavy and narrow (value-dense), a live medium suited connector (98s-type) is a better call candidate than a dominable weak suited Ax / offsuit broadway; if the texture widens with broadway and connectors, high-card equity takes over and weak Ax / offsuit broadway swings borderline-call — the threshold depends on pot odds and depth *(calibrate)*. The pool's jam starts from weak offsuit Ax and comes late to suited connectors *(calibrate)* → against the pool, a connector call is relatively good, an offsuit-broadway call relatively bad; against a narrow, value-dense jam, both classes mostly fold. The limp-call trap only applies with a hand that DOMINATES the jam range: something around K7s and above can work *(calibrate)*, K2s can't — jam the hands yourself that "don't mind going, but don't dominate." The call threshold widens roughly one notch for every bb shallower *(calibrate)* — memorize one reference point and shift from there.

### 33.4-EK The Covering BB's Response to a Short SB's Limp — Zero Jam, a POLAR Iso (Top + Pure Trash + Low Offsuit Ax), NO MEDIUM CARDS; at Similar Stacks, the Reverse: a LINEAR Iso


Root error: applying the chipEV BB-iso rule of thumb against a limp (33.4) to every stack relationship, or "I have the big stack, I'll jam the limp." The mechanism is two separate worlds. (A) You're the leading BB, SB is short and at max premium (limp range narrow: suited Kx/Ax + 7x/8x/9x connectors + a trap): jam is ZERO — a min-raise functions like a jam (SB folds KJo/Q9s/98s to the raise too; hands that continue against the raise but would fold to a jam are close to nonexistent), and on top of that it's cheaper and carries more bluffs. The iso is POLAR: top (99+/AJ+) + pure trash (62o/72o/T2o — pure *(calibrate)*) + low offsuit Ax (A3o: the A blocks AA/AK, the 3 doesn't touch SB's limp body). Medium-card hands (7x/8x/9x, K5o) are ABSENT — these are the cards SB limp-folds, so holding them means blocking a fold. The tighter SB's limp range, the narrower the value iso (TT/99 check back!): raise AJ and what folds is always what you dominate, what continues is always what dominates you. (B) Similarly big stacks, premium low-medium (~9% *(calibrate)*): SB's limp-reraise/limp-jam is nearly absent (the premium shuts that line down) → BB's iso widens LINEARLY: medium-value hands like A9o/ATo/KQo/JTs/65s/76s iso because they nearly always see the flop and don't eat aggression; the polar-trash slice also disappears (73o/83o). As premium rises (9%→14%→15% *(calibrate)*), iso frequency drops gradually and polarizes.

**Rule:** the texture of the iso against the limp is set by SB's continuing range: SB short-tight → polar iso, no medium cards, no jam; SB similar-loose → linear iso, medium suited hands included. 33.4 is the chipEV-BvB baseline; these are the two extremes at the FT.

### 33.5 Population Deviation — Three Exploits


Deep, the pool isos the limp far below theory and folds to SB's raise at roughly the same rate regardless of size *(calibrate)* — it isn't reading the price, it's saying "I play this hand / I don't." Three consequences:

1. **No fold from SB, deep** — if the bottom of the range isn't being punished, every hand gets played.
2. **After a limp, the hardest-to-play OOP bottom trash turns into a small raise-fold** *(calibrate)* — if the fold rate doesn't depend on size, there's no point in an expensive bluff; the same deviation also pays for sizing up the value raise. Only against an unknown/passive opponent; against a good player, the bucket logic stands unchanged.
3. **Narrow the value limp-reraise** — the pool isolates strong hands more often than theory and weak ones less often → the continuing range facing a limp-reraise is also strong → only premium (roughly QQ+/AK, calibrate) is reraise-value. The KQs/AJo/ATs class gets dominated by that range, and a reraise with it becomes a thin bluff, not value → take it out of the limp and move it into the opening raise (since the limp gets isolated rarely, the trap doesn't work either); if it was limped, it stays limp-call, not a reraise. Condition: this holds when the pool isolates the limp rarely; against an AGGRESSIVE isolator who covers you, it reverses in PKO — TT+/AQ+ limp-reraise widens (28.25). Calibration note: the pool's BB 3-bets SB's raise at 40–60bb at roughly half of theory's frequency; the deep/mid layer boundary drops to ~45bb in the pool — play 60bb like deep *(calibrate)*.

BB mirror: the pool is passive deep → grab the small pots yourself, build your image there; when the big pot comes, the payoff comes from that image.

### 33.5-EK Shallow Pool (15–20bb): Heavy Jamming, Light Isolating Against the Limp


Carry 33.5's deep deviation (light isolating) into the shallow range. In 15–20bb BvB, the pool jams SB's limp several times theory's frequency and non-all-in isolates it at about half of theory's rate *(calibrate)* — it picks the jam from "good hand" and the iso from almost nothing. The consequence lands on the book's lines like this:

**33.5's "no fold from SB, deep" line carries over to shallow too.** An unpunished limp realizes extra equity → at 20bb even the weakest offsuit hands don't fold, they limp.

**33.3's "medium suited shifts to raise" line reverses here.** Medium suited broadway gets squeezed both ways — too much jamming comes over a raise, too little non-all-in isolating comes over a limp (if a jam comes, you only lose your 1bb limp) → LIMP: fine postflop, carries a trap share, and a strong iso range doesn't crush its weak limp-call because it isn't isolating.

**The pool correction to 33.2's "limp-jam material is small pairs" line.** A small pair's limp-jam EV comes from the iso's fold; if the iso isn't coming, the smallest pairs revert to OPEN-JAM (fold equity + live equity), while medium pairs stay in the limp *(bound: calibrate)*.

**Jam and raise sets** *(bounds: calibrate)*. Jam: offsuit Ax, the smallest pairs, low/medium suited connectors — live against a high card when called, folds out the hands that dominate them. Non-all-in raise: the top (the pool jams wide against suited Ax, raise-call is profitable) and the high-card offsuit blocker class raise-folds; suited bluffs and medium suited connectors are borderline — raise-fold or limp. Big pairs raise rather than limp-trap: at 20bb there's no point jamming to fold out the bottom of the range (B31.1-EK2).

**A pool discount to 33.4's "one notch per bb" rule.** The pool jams the limp WIDER than theory → your call range against the pool's jam is also wider than theory, the threshold moves down *(calibrate)*.

### 33.6 Asymmetric and Mixed Stacks


ChipEV/effective-stack mechanics; no bounty here, B28's cover logic doesn't carry over to this section. Shared principle: a rejam is priced by the effective stack — the deep player's rejam neither gets cheaper nor more expensive, the short player's rejam is his whole stack. The spots are framed as opener-vs-behind; the BvB mirror: the effective stack is the short player's — a short SB plays the 20bb chart, and a deep BB plays against him with the same depth logic; a deep SB thinks open-jam instead of a small open against a short BB.

**Facing a short opener, you're deep IP, deep blinds behind → call NARROWS, mostly a small commit-3-bet.** A short player (~10–15bb, calibrate) opened, your hand is medium-good. Two things narrow the call: a 3-bet that commits the short stack is so cheap it dominates flatting; and the deep blinds crush the flat with a non-all-in squeeze and split the equity share against the short stack. The short player's range is polarized (nuts + raise-fold trash); a small 3-bet that commits him *(calibrate)* folds out the trash, and against his value a suited Ax/suited broadway's equity is enough. The short player's answer is mostly fold, jam with suited Ax, and almost never call. In position, borderline hands stay call *(calibrate)*. Hypothesis: the pool mostly over-calls here *(verify with showdown data, calibrate)* — if it over-calls, the deviation favors you.

**You're the short one, the table is deep → WIDEN your borderline RFI.** You open ~20bb *(calibrate)*, the players behind are deep. A rejam only costs them the effective ~20bb — without ICM, the deep player's rejam neither gets cheaper nor more expensive; what lowers his appetite is that the deep player prefers to call in position and play postflop, plus, with multiple deep players behind, he gets squeezed by the threat of a cold-4-bet/call from someone else *(calibrate)*. A table that leans toward calling opens up realize-equity room for small pairs, small suited connectors, and medium suited Kx/Qx → you gain EV as the opener. This happens often when the table is deep (e.g., early in the tournament). Once the effective stack drops to ~12bb, the call option disappears, and the deep player's effective-stack-priced reshove doesn't leave a small raise-fold alive → jam-or-fold (B30.0-EK).

**You're deep, shorts behind → narrow the open, add open-JAM.** A small open is exposed to the short player's jam → low suited Kx, gapped suited, weak offsuit broadway drop out; medium/small pairs, suited Ax, and the suited-broadway class become open-jams *(bounds: calibrate)* — the open-jam erases the short player's fold-equity rejam, removing raise-fold; the cost is knowingly sacrificing the value of the rejams that dominate you. That's why the top of the range (big pairs, big offsuit Ax) still plays min-raise-call: it wants to induce a dominating rejam (dominated offsuit broadway, medium Ax), not fold it out. Dominable offsuit broadway stays in the min-raise — fold if the short player jams, play it in position if he calls *(calibrate)*. Against the pool opening short, the call is almost never, the rejam is only ha

### 33.6-EK Cross-Reference Map: the Bounty Layer Is in B28, the Whale-Limp Node Is in 23.1-EK


33.6's rule of "no bounty, B28's cover logic doesn't carry over here" stands as written; what's missing is the address table. The same seats change direction once a bounty enters — the chipEV mechanism of "the effective stack is the short player's" becomes, in PKO, "who covers whom + who holds the bounty":

- **The covered SB** (facing an aggressive covering BB): the B33.2/33.3 layers shift — the covering BB doesn't jam over a raise, he flats and isolates; strong suited RAISEs, strong offsuit Ax + pairs LIMP-JAM (semi-value), the top is a big raise (28.26); the top's limp-reraise is a pump, not a trap (28.25).
- **The covering SB, shallow HU-BvB (12–20bb):** ICM's limp-heavy texture reverses — bounty equity closes the gap, the trap becomes unnecessary → the limp collapses, the range stays tight but JAM-heavy (28.3-EK).
- **The covering SB, deep OOP:** it's aggression, not VPIP, that widens — the fringe flat gets cut, a linear 3-bet opens up (28.1-EK).
- **A whale's limp, mixed stacks (no bounty):** the limp node for 33.6's "short opener, you deep IP" logic is in 23.1-EK — does my stack fit in a jam, how many players behind are jamming, am I deep against the players behind.

Rule: in BvB, first ask "is there a bounty?" — if not, 33.6; if so, the addresses above; the bounty incentive belongs only to the COVERING player, and what gets cut from the covered player's game is the bluff.

### 33.6-EK-2 In Symmetric-High-Premium BvB the Tree Is Locked to Two of the Opponent's Dials — Call Width Against the Jam + Iso Frequency Against the Limp


Root error: the rule of thumb "stone bubble → any-two jam" or "ICM → raise-only." Mechanism: with both sides at ~20% premium (small field, linear payout, one player left to bust), theory has SB jamming any-two, BB paying only with 88+/AJs/AQo. If BB pays a notch wider (77/AJo/ATs — calibrate), any-two jam collapses to nearly zero jam, and the tree reverts to LIMP (jam ~13%, limp is the body). If BB isolates the limp more than theory (doesn't check QQ/JJ/AQ — people don't), limp EV drops, medium hands (medium suited Kx/Qx, small pairs) go BACK to jam, the limp narrows, and folding rises a bit. The raise slice is only AA/AKs plus a few trash hands: at 20% premium, if a rejam comes over the raise, there's no stack-off. Strong hands that get limped: KK/AK/suited Ax limp-jam, AA limp-raise, even QQ limp-CALLS — there's no stone-bubble stack-off with 99. The covering SB, while his own premium is meaningful (~9%) and the opponent's isn't much higher, is also limp-heavy (limp 40%+ — calibrate): suited hands that don't reach raise-call but are a waste to raise-fold (KQs/QTs/K9s) go to the limp; jam = 98/T8/J9 + offsuit Ax + small pairs that "fold out the hands that dominate them"; raise = the top + K9/K8/Q8 (pays worse hands like T9/98, has the better top pair) + "one high, one low." Q9/K9 don't jam (only one combo above folds) → raise.

**Rule:** at high symmetric premium, SB tracks three dials — how wide BB pays a jam (jam width), how often he isolates a limp (limp/jam balance), while raise stays nearly closed off. B33's chipEV engine points the same direction; the engine here is the premium.

### 33.6-EK-3 FT BvB Short SB (Covered, Premium 20%+): Min-Raise Is ZERO, the Tree Is Jam-or-Limp; QQ/AK/TT Open-Jam; Limp-Jam Is Almost Absent


Root error: carrying the chipEV BvB layer (33.1–33.2) to the FT — "17bb SB: a mix of raise/limp/limp-jam." Mechanism: when a short SB's premium against a covering BB is near the ceiling (20%+ *(calibrate)*), three things break. (1) Min-raise dies: BB pays wide, over-realizes on the flop, and SB can't realize anything → raise-first goes to 0%. (2) Induction dies: 25% premium means "limp → BB jams → he showed an Ace → QQ folds," so even QQ/JJ/TT/AK/AQ go OPEN-JAM — by jamming the top, you can also bring weak hands like A4o/J9o into the jam; KK/AA stay in the limp (limp-jam/limp-click). (3) Limp-jam is almost absent: small pairs don't limp-jam — 33bb's bluffs block them, and BB finds himself with his call range; offsuit Ax doesn't limp-jam — A8o LIMP-FOLDS, A9o limp-raises; the limp-raise slice is premium plus a few offsuit-Ax bluffs. Total VPIP is small (roughly a fifth, *calibrate*), the limp body: the best suited connectors, suited Kx/Ax, offsuit broadway; Q8o/K8o fold. As it deepens (16→19bb), jam decreases and the limp body widens (AK/JJ, which had been protecting the limp, revert to the limp). Against BB's iso: suited Kx/9x call, offsuit KTo/QJo fold, pairs limp-fold (!) — only TT+/AK/KQ limp-jam. If premium is MEDIUM (SB is the shortest but the gap is small, ~9% vs 3% *(calibrate)*), everything comes back: limp drops to 0%, jam + raise (K6o raises), limp-call is wide.

**Rule:** the short SB's tree has three modes by premium: ceiling premium → jam-or-limp (raise 0, limp-jam 0); medium premium → jam-or-raise (limp 0); low premium/similar stacks → limp-heavy (33.1). 33.2's "limp-jam material is small pairs + weak offsuit Ax" is the chipEV/low-premium case; at ceiling premium that slice scatters into limp-fold/open-jam.

### 33.7 Calibration


Keep the strategy in SEGMENTS: deep / mid / shallow blocks. Don't react to every single-bb change within a block; let effective bb/SPR trigger the layer transition (the stack cost of a raise-fold/limp-fold doesn't depend on whether the opponent has jammed before), and let observation shift the thresholds INSIDE a layer (the limp-jam floor, jam call-width, iso size) — don't wait on either the chart label or the read (mostly). The pool 3-bets very little deep and jams very little at mid-shallow *(calibrate)* — it plays the two layers almost identically. In the deep phase small-pot aggression is cheap → fire test hands early (does he 3-bet the raise, does he always fold to the iso), take your read from there, and spend it in the shallow phase's jam decisions. If the opponent is aggressive/sticky in the early phase, shift your shallow-phase limp-jam and call-over-jam thresholds accordingly.

| Slot | *(calibrate)* |
|---|---|
| Layer thresholds | deep / mid / shallow bb boundaries — in your own solver |
| Limp-reraise and iso size | how many times it drops the SPR deep; a small+wide iso against a low-reraise pool |
| Limp-jam / iso-jam / call-vs-jam | blocker Ax/Kx floor, small-pair threshold, reference bb + the notch/bb shift |
| Commit-3-bet, the squeeze size that commits a short stack (33.6's multi-way spot; there's no squeeze in pure BvB) | ratio to the short opener's stack |

### 33.7-EK Layer Transition for HU-BTN: bb Picks the Default, the Opponent's First Jam/Iso Picks the Exploit


33.7's rule — "let effective bb trigger the layer transition, don't wait on the read" — is for the BvB default, and it also picks the default in HU. In HU-BTN, an exploit layer sits on top of it; its trigger isn't bb but the opponent's response: you've dropped from 40→30bb, the chart says to move to limp, but if the opponent still isn't jamming your raise and isn't attacking your limp, keep the raise strategy — "if it isn't broken, don't fix it." The real breaking point is where the opponent's response changes: once the first jam comes over the raise, pull your limp share to chart level; once the first iso-jam comes over the limp, trim your limp-fold share and pull your limp-call range to chart level — usually the wider one (31.12), because a widening jam range prices the call in with more hands. 31.6's single-data-point rule applies here: one jam is enough, don't wait for a second. Boundary: the exploit layer can't drop below the default — playing raise-only at 20bb because the opponent looks passive leaves your raise-fold expensive once jams start coming (33.1's shallow engine). With no read (a new opponent, the first few hands) play the default layer; once a read comes in, exploit; once the read breaks (the opponent starts limping/jamming), go back to default. B33.7 is for BvB, this line is for HU-BTN.

### 33.10 ICM Layer (1): the Leading SB's C-Bet Map — "Leader = Bet Every Board" Is the Most Common Mistake


B33 plays without ICM. In FT/near-bubble BvB, once SB opens as the leader and a short BB calls, playing postflop as "I hold the premium gap → range-bet" misreads the book's 21.3 "one notch up." Moving up a notch changes BOARD SELECTION, not frequency:

- **Range-bet boards:** at least two low cards, no broadway (A-3-2, 8-3-2, 7-6-4, 6-4-3). Reason: the polar RFI range carries the offsuit 3x/6x/2x trash, and BB has folded those hands → the second/third pair is on your side. Size isn't small, ~40% pot *(calibrate)* — to make JTo/J9o indifferent. On connected low boards, small-size and jam split the job (33.11).
- **Check-heavy boards:** medium-connected (J-T-7, T-8-6 — ~90% check, jam-or-check), broadway (K-T-6, K-J-8, Q-9-5), T-5-2 (BB has all the suited Tx). **A-K-9 is deceptive:** you hold AK, but your 74o/J3o trash pulls the equity down, while BB's Kx/9x pulls it up — your equity is at its lowest here *(calibrate)*; if you bet, go big-and-polar (Ax/Kx value + zero-equity backdoor bluffs).
- **Medium boards** (9-7-5, J-9-4, Q-8-4, J-6-2): medium frequency; dry → small, connected → big.
- **At equal stacks** the map is the same: low boards get a small range-bet; because of the Tx you limped, T-T-8 / J-T-7 / 9-8-8 range-check; broadway bets 70%/checks.
- **Pool deviation:** on dry-disconnected boards (T-7-3, J-6-2), shift toward a small range-bet — the ICM pool folds J5s/K8o/Q9o. On connected boards there's no deviation, continuances are natural.

> **A few points of premium gap don't buy every board. The leading SB saves the range-bet for low, broadway-free boards (~40% size); medium-connected and broadway boards — A-K-9 included — are check-heavy. BB carries the second-pair and top-pair mass on those boards.**

### 33.11 ICM Layer (2): SB's Flop-Jam — Melting Value, the One-High-Card Bluff, the Folding Flush Draw


21.5 gave the short stack's flop-jam at a shallow SPR; in BvB-ICM, the LEADER or an equal stack throws the jam, and the size can run up to 3x the pot *(calibrate)*. On connected low/medium boards (7-6-4, T-9-5, 8-7-5, T-9-5) there are two reasons: your value melts street by street, and on a scary river you don't get paid anyway.

- **Value:** a medium hand that wants protection — 99–QQ, 7x, AT/JJ, KT/QT. An overpair carrying a flush card checks (you have a flush-out, no need to rush), one that doesn't carries a jam.
- **Bluff, not a draw:** a flush draw doesn't jam (21.2-EK-4). The bluff logic is one high card plus the right flush card: AJ/AQ WITHOUT the flush card — dominate BB's A-high-flush-draw call, and let that card sit with the opponent; A2 WITH the flush card — that card should not sit with the opponent; K8/Q8/J8 as a flush-carrying gutshot. On low boards, add straight draws with an overcard (T3/J3/Q3).
- **BB's answer (the other seat on the same line):** top pair calls; second/third pair without backup folds; a NAKED flush draw folds — including the A-high ones *(calibrate)*; combo draws and two overcards + a flush draw call. A straight-draw K4-fd calls, a naked K3-fd folds. The blocker line is in 21.1-EK: KT folds, Q9 calls.
- **In response:** if SB is also betting the small size, BB should raise that small bet rarely and call it widely — the small-size range is either nuts or trash (the jam already took the medium hands), so a raise has no target.

> **In BvB-ICM the flop-jam buys protection: a medium hand whose value is melting, a bluff of one high card plus flush-card logic (AQ without the flush card, A2 with it). As BB, fold the naked flush draw into a jam; call combo draws and two overcards.**

### 33.8 Cheat Card


| Topic | Rule |
|---|---|
| **Layer selection** | Effective bb/SPR selects it; three questions set the within-layer thresholds and the transition band (45–60bb): is there a suited call for my reraise (deep) / who's isolating my limp (mid) / is there a jam over my raise (shallow) |
| **Deep premium** | Mostly RAISE-first (the range doesn't stay capped); one slice LIMPS → a big reraise trap against the iso *(calibrate)*; a small raise keeps the pot/SPR high, a big reraise drops the SPR → reraise value-weighted |
| **Mid premium + KQs/QJs** | Mostly RAISE — BB's iso polarizes, the limp-reraise target is narrow; a thin premium cap + a blocker bluff stay in limp-reraise *(calibrate)*; limp-jam belongs to the shallow layer |
| **Shallow premium / limp-jam** | Big pairs RAISE (jam range > limp-jam-call range); AK is borderline — raise or jam-over-the-iso; limp-jam material is the weakest offsuit Ax/Kx + small pairs *(calibrate)* |
| **Suited limp** | Mostly NO folding to a single iso (the lowest suited is the exception, calibrate) — if you're going to fold, raise-fold instead |
