| **Raise-fold / limp-fold material** | Medium Qx/Jx offsuit and gapped suited that don't fit a bucket → raise-fold; of two borderline hands, raise the better one, limp the weaker — no mixing |
| **BB iso** | Offsuit broadway is expensive, low suited is cheap; small+wide against a low-reraise pool; the ante widens it; wider than HU-OOP's iso |
| **Jam over the iso** | Finds a dominated call (big offsuit Ax); suited medium broadway doesn't jam |
| **Limp got jammed on** | Raw equity + pot odds decide *(calibrate)*: against a narrow, Ax/pair-heavy (value-dense) jam, a live medium suited connector is good, a dominated weak Ax / offsuit broadway is bad; against a wide jam, high cards take over; the trap is only for a hand that dominates the jam range |
| **Pool** | No fold from SB deep; small raise-fold on bottom trash; NARROW the value limp-reraise (premium only), KQs/AJo/ATs go into the opening raise |
| **Short opener, you're deep IP** | Call NARROWS — mostly a small commit-3-bet or fold; borderline hands in position stay call |
| **Deep blinds + short opener** | A calling regime + small 3-bet-fold; a squeeze size that commits the short player (≈half the BB's stack, calibrate) forces SB into jam-or-fold, and for the short player, calling = a stack-off |

### 33.9 Drill (5 Questions)


1. **BV1.** Deep BvB *(calibrate; roughly 70bb+)*, AA in SB. While the body stays raise-first, which slice goes into the limp-reraise trap, and why shouldn't the range stay capped? Which of BB's bands calls the raise, and isolates the limp — where does the EV come from there?
2. **BV2.** ~40bb, KQs in SB. Limp or raise? How does BB's iso range split at this depth, and why does that decide it?
3. **BV3.** ~25bb, A3o in SB. Limp-call/limp-fold, limp-jam, or raise-fold — fold isn't on the table? If it were AA, which lines stay open; why is A3o's limp-jam a borderline line that leans mostly on fold equity (only live equity if called), and how does BB's jam frequency price the raise-fold?
4. **BV4.** You limped from SB, BB jammed for ~15–20bb *(calibrate)*. 98s and KTo — if the jamming texture is a narrow, Ax/pair-heavy (value-dense) range, which one calls; if the texture widens with broadway, how does the decision flip? Work it out from raw equity + pot odds.
5. **BV5.** SB opened min-raise ~12bb, you're deep in BB with ATs. Call or jam? Against the short player's polar range (nuts + raise-fold trash), a jam folds out trash and carries equity against his value; a call leaves the opener committed on every flop — which line, and why? (The BvB mirror of 33.6's deep-IP logic.)

*Root-error link: in BvB the root error is born preflop — raising premium deep and then stacking off with one pair in a bloated OOP pot (the mistake isn't the raise itself, it's clinging to the pair), or, shallow, gifting BB's jam a raise-fold via the "limp = weak" rule of thumb. This is B31.1's position-mirror, conditioned by B5.1 and B17.3; postflop stickiness ties back to B23.12.*

### 33.2-EK SB's Bottom Slice — If the Next Hand Moves You to a Cheap, Profitable Seat, Pull Back a Notch

22.10's rule "if YOUR blind is approaching, WIDEN" prices an approaching COST (paying the blind). The same engine's mirror runs in SB: the hand after SB is BTN — the seat that pays no blind, the most profitable seat at the table. When picking SB's bottom marginal slice (the most marginal jam/call/limp combinations), the bust risk puts at stake not only that hand's own EV but also the value of being carried "for free" into BTN next hand — this concerns only SB's bottom slice, the body of the range is unaffected. *(borderline — future-game EV, in the flavor of edge/ICM, outside pure chipEV; in ICM, the future hand's weight should be read together with 22.2-EK-2's "arrival role"; calibrate.)*

**Rule:** before picking SB's bottom marginal slice, ask: "where does the next hand move me?" If you're moving to an expensive seat (your own blind), WIDEN the slice (22.10); if you're moving to a cheap, profitable seat (BTN), pull that same bottom slice back a notch — the two are mirror faces of the same future-hand logic.

### 33.12 In an Iso-Raise Pot, BB's Best Equity Bucket Is High Cards

In an SB-limp / BB-iso-raise pot, SB's call range is both topless (premium doesn't stay in SB, it goes to raise-first) and stripped of its bottom (33.2's bucket construction: the weakest offsuit/suited hands drop to limp-fold) — a pure middle-band, CAPPED range. BB's iso-raise range, by contrast, is top-heavy: dense in Ax/Kx and broadway, big pairs (see 33.4). This structure confirms the intuition: on an A-high/K-high board, BB's density in Ax/Kx/broadway fits the board perfectly, while SB's capped middle-band range (76s/54s/33-type) COMPLETELY whiffs that board often — being capped doesn't guarantee matching high cards, a kicker alone doesn't produce equity. That's why A-high/K-high is BB's HIGHEST equity bucket. BB's LOWEST equity bucket, conversely, is low/disconnected boards: SB's middle-band range (small pairs, connectors) hits there often, while BB's Ax/broadway-heavy range whiffs there.

**Rule:** in an iso-raise pot, read board equity by "whose range is denser on this board," not "whose range is topless or bottomless" — BB's top-heavy iso-raise range is strongest on high-card boards, weakest on the low-disconnected boards that SB's capped middle-band range hits.
